  2 Samuel 22:44 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines join rescue from conflict with peoples, leadership of nations and service from an unknown people. The psalm speaks of disputes and appointment, while Samuel speaks of battle and preservation as head.

Psalms 17:45
At the hearing of the ear, it obeyed me; foreigners lied to me.
  2 Samuel 22:45 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines combine hearing followed by obedience with foreigners lying to the speaker. The clauses appear in opposite order, and neither is changed into a claim of sincere submission.

Psalms 17:46
Foreigners grew old and limped away from their paths.
  2 Samuel 22:46 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: The corresponding foreigner lines use different images of failure and departure. The psalm says they grew old and limped away from their paths; Samuel says they will be cast away and stumble out of enclosures.

Psalms 17:47
The Lord lives! Blessed be my God! Let the God of my salvation be exalted,
  2 Samuel 22:47 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines proclaim that the Lord lives, bless God and connect exaltation with salvation. The psalm exhorts that the God of salvation be exalted, while Samuel uses Guard language and says He will be exalted.

Psalms 17:48
the God who gives me acts of vengeance and subjected peoples beneath me,
  2 Samuel 22:48 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines attribute vengeance and authority over peoples beneath the speaker to God. The psalm says He subjected peoples, while Samuel says He disciplines them; the stated actions remain distinct.

Psalms 17:49
my rescuer from my wrathful enemies. Thou wilt raise me above those who rise against me; Thou wilt rescue me from an unjust man.
  2 Samuel 22:49 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines combine rescue from enemies, being raised above attackers and rescue from an unjust man or man of wrongs. The repeated three-part deliverance description supplies the particular correspondence.

Psalms 17:50
Therefore I will thank Thee among the nations, Lord, and sing praise to Thy name,
  Romans 15:9 — Quotation of praise among the nations: Paul explicitly quotes giving thanks among the nations and singing praise to God's name while explaining Gentiles' glorifying God for mercy. The source line also has a parallel in 2 Samuel 22:50; this comparison does not decide between the two song forms as the quotation's immediate source.
  2 Samuel 22:50 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both lines promise acknowledgment or thanksgiving among the nations and singing praise to the Lord's name. Their shared words also appear in the quotation recorded in Romans 15:9.

Psalms 17:51
magnifying the salvations of His king and showing mercy to His anointed, to David and to his seed for ever.
  2 Samuel 7:12-16 — Mercy to David's royal seed: The song ends with mercy to God's anointed, David and his seed for ever. Nathan's message promises David a royal descendant, enduring kingdom and mercy that will not be withdrawn; those specific royal-seed and mercy promises correspond to the song's conclusion.
  2 Samuel 22:51 — Parallel forms of David’s rescue song: Both conclusions name great deliverance for God's king and mercy toward His anointed, explicitly David and his descendants for ever. The matching royal identity and enduring lineage formula are supplied by both texts themselves.

Psalms 18:2
The heavens recount God’s glory, and the firmament announces the work of His hands.
  Romans 1:19-20 — Creation discloses the Creator: The heavens announce God's glory and the work of His hands. Romans says God's invisible power and divinity are understood through what He made; both make created things witnesses to their Creator, using different formulations.

Psalms 18:5
Their sound went out into all the earth, and their utterances to the limits of the inhabited world. In the sun He set His dwelling.
  Romans 10:18 — Quotation of the worldwide sound: Romans quotes the sound going through all the earth and words reaching the inhabited world's ends in its argument about hearing. The comparison concerns the source unit's opening clauses; its additional statement that God set His dwelling in the sun is not part of the quotation.

Psalms 18:6
And he is like a bridegroom coming out of his bridal chamber; he will exult like a giant to run his way.
  Joel 2:16 — The bridegroom emerging from a chamber: The psalm uses the simile of a bridegroom coming out of his bridal chamber. Joel's summons to communal fasting calls the bridegroom out of his bedroom and the bride out of her bridal chamber; the comparison concerns that concrete wedding-room image rather than an identity of the subjects.

Psalms 18:7
His going out is from heaven’s edge, and his arrival reaches heaven’s edge; there is no one who will be hidden from his heat.
  Ecclesiastes 1:5 — The sun's course: Following its mention of the sun, the psalm depicts movement from heaven's edge to heaven's edge and heat from which none is hidden. Ecclesiastes describes the sun rising, setting and drawing toward its place; the passages present its course in praise and in reflection on recurring activity respectively.

Psalms 18:8
The Lord’s law is blameless, turning souls; the Lord’s testimony is trustworthy, making infants wise.
  Psalms 118:130 — Divine words give understanding: The source says the Lord's testimony makes infants wise. Psalm 118 says the disclosure of His words gives light and understanding to the simple; the shared instructional effect is retained while the different descriptions of its recipients remain explicit.

Psalms 18:9
The Lord’s ordinances are upright, rejoicing the heart; the Lord’s commandment shines afar, giving light to the eyes.
  Proverbs 6:23 — Commandment as illumination: The Lord's commandment shines afar and gives light to the eyes. Proverbs calls the commandment of law a lamp and a light, giving a close counterpart to the instructional light image.

Psalms 18:10
The fear of the Lord is pure, enduring for the age of the age; the Lord’s judgments are true, justified together,
  Psalms 118:160 — True words and righteous judgments: The source calls the Lord's judgments true and justified together, while Psalm 118 joins the truth of His words with His righteous judgments. The source attributes endurance to the fear of the Lord; the target expressly attributes it to the judgments.

Psalms 18:11
desirable beyond gold and much precious stone, and sweeter than honey and honeycomb.
  Psalms 118:72 — Divine law valued beyond precious metal: The source values the Lord's judgments beyond gold and much precious stone. Psalm 118 similarly places the law of His mouth above thousands of gold and silver; both make divine instruction more desirable than material wealth.
  Psalms 118:103 — Divine sayings sweeter than honey: The honey-and-honeycomb comparison for the Lord's judgments closely corresponds to Psalm 118's comparison of His sayings with honey and honeycomb in the speaker's mouth. Both use sweetness to express the delight of divine instruction.

Psalms 18:13
Who will understand transgressions? Cleanse me of my hidden things.
  Leviticus 4:27-31 — Cleansing faults beyond present awareness: The prayer asks cleansing from hidden things while questioning who understands transgressions. Leviticus provides atonement for an unintentional offence once it becomes known, showing that lack of awareness does not remove the need for cleansing or forgiveness; the respective forms are prayer and a prescribed offering.

Psalms 18:15
And the sayings of my mouth will be pleasing, and the meditation of my heart will be before Thee continually, Lord, my helper and my redeemer.
  Psalms 103:34 — The speaker's meditation before God: The source anticipates pleasing sayings and continual meditation before God. Psalm 103 asks that the speaker's meditation be sweet to Him; the passages respectively express expectation and petition concerning the acceptability of the speaker's own worship.

Psalms 19:2
May the Lord hear you in the day of affliction; may the name of Jacob’s God protect you.
  Genesis 35:3 — Jacob's God hears in the day of affliction: The blessing asks that the Lord hear in the day of affliction and invokes Jacob's God. Jacob himself describes God as the One who heard him in the day of affliction, providing a specific narrative counterpart to both parts of the invocation.

Psalms 19:3
May He send you help from the sanctuary and support you from Zion.
  Psalms 133:1-3 — Divine benefit from Zion and the sanctuary: The prayer asks for help from the sanctuary and support from Zion. Psalm 133 addresses servants in the Lord's house and declares that the Lord will bless from Zion, sharing the sanctuary setting and the stated origin of divine benefit.

Psalms 19:4
May He remember every sacrifice of yours and make your whole burnt offering fat. Diapsalma.
  1 Samuel 7:9-10 — Whole burnt offering and a plea for deliverance: The blessing asks God to remember every sacrifice and make the whole burnt offering fat. At Mizpah Samuel offers a whole burnt offering and cries for Israel, and the Lord hears him as the enemy approaches; this compares sacrifice joined with a plea for aid in distinct settings.

Psalms 19:5
May He give you according to your heart and fulfill all your counsel.
  Psalms 20:3 — Royal petition and granted desire: The blessing asks God to grant according to the recipient's heart and fulfill his counsel. Psalm 20 reports the gift of the king's soul's desire and the wish of his lips, furnishing a corresponding statement of granted desire in another royal prayer.

Psalms 19:6
We will exult in your salvation, and in our God’s name we will be magnified. May the Lord fulfill all your petitions.
  Psalms 20:2 — Rejoicing in royal salvation: The community promises to exult in the addressed person's salvation; the closing request for the king supplies the royal context. Psalm 20 explicitly says the king will rejoice in God's power and exult in His salvation, shifting the stated rejoicer from community to king.

Psalms 19:7
Now I know that the Lord saved His anointed; He will hear him from His holy heaven; in mighty acts is the salvation of His right hand.
  Psalms 88:21-25 — The anointed ruler upheld by God's hand: The source joins salvation of the Lord's anointed with the saving power of His right hand. Psalm 88 names David as God's anointed and promises support from God's hand, strength from His arm and defeat of enemies; the comparison does not identify a single battle.

Psalms 19:8
These rely on chariots, and those on horses, but we will be magnified in the name of the Lord our God.
  Isaiah 31:1-3 — Chariots and horses contrasted with the Lord: The psalm says some rely on chariots or horses, while its speakers will be magnified in the Lord's name. Isaiah condemns reliance on Egypt's horses and chariots while failing to trust Israel's Holy One; the texts contrast military resources with God using different positive responses.

Psalms 19:10
Lord, save Thy king, and hear us on whatever day we call upon Thee.
  1 Timothy 2:1-2 — Prayer for rulers: The psalm asks the Lord to save His king and hear the community. The letter expressly directs petitions and intercessions for kings and people in high positions, giving an explicit instruction corresponding to this kind of royal prayer.

Psalms 20:2
Lord, the king will rejoice in Thy power and exult greatly in Thy salvation.
  Psalms 19:6 — King and community exult in salvation: The king's promised rejoicing in God's power and salvation corresponds to the preceding royal prayer's communal promise to exult in the addressed person's salvation. The passages name different participants as rejoicing and do not require one historical occasion.

Psalms 20:3
Thou gavest him the desire of his soul and didst not deprive him of the wish of his lips. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 19:5 — Granted desire beside a royal petition: The source reports that God granted the king's soul's desire and did not deprive him of his lips' wish. Psalm 19 asks God to grant according to the heart and fulfill counsel; the particular comparison is between petitioned and reported royal desire.

Psalms 20:4
For Thou didst meet him with blessings of kindness; Thou didst place upon his head a crown of precious stone.
  2 Samuel 12:30 — A royal crown with precious stone: The source describes a crown of precious stone placed upon the king's head. Samuel recounts a crown containing gold and precious stone being placed on David's head at Rabbah; the material and royal placement correspond, without establishing that this is the same crown or occasion.

Psalms 20:5
He asked Thee for life, and Thou gavest it to him, length of days for the age of the age.
  Psalms 60:7-8 — Length of days for the king: The source reports the king asking for life and receiving length of days for the age of the age. Psalm 60 anticipates added days and years for the king across generations and his remaining before God, providing a particular royal-longevity counterpart.

Psalms 20:7
For Thou wilt give him blessing for the age of the age; Thou wilt gladden him with joy in Thy presence.
  2 Samuel 7:29 — Enduring royal blessing before God: The source joins enduring blessing for the king with joy in God's presence. David's prayer asks that his house be blessed and remain before God for ever; the link concerns continuing royal blessing and presence, with individual king and household distinguished.

Psalms 20:8
For the king hopes in the Lord, and through the Most High’s mercy he will certainly not be shaken.
  Psalms 88:29-30 — Royal stability grounded in divine mercy: The king's hope of remaining unshaken rests on the Most High's mercy. Psalm 88 promises enduring mercy, a faithful covenant and an established royal seed and throne, giving a royal-covenant counterpart to that stated basis of confidence.

Psalms 20:10
You will make them like a fiery furnace at the time of your presence; the Lord in His anger will throw them into turmoil, and fire will devour them.
  Malachi 3:19 — Oven imagery for fiery judgment: The source pictures enemies made like a fiery furnace and devoured by fire in the Lord's anger. Malachi depicts the coming day burning like an oven and the lawless as ignited straw; both use furnace or oven images for judgment while assigning the comparison to different subjects.

Psalms 20:11
You will destroy their fruit from the earth and their seed from the sons of men,
  Hosea 9:16 — Fruit and offspring removed in judgment: The source predicts destruction of the enemies' fruit and seed. Hosea expressly combines Ephraim's dried roots and absent fruit with the loss of offspring, supplying a particular counterpart to the use of fruit and descendants in a judgment description.

Psalms 20:12
because they directed evils against you; they devised a counsel they will certainly be unable to establish.
  Psalms 32:10-11 — Hostile counsel unable to stand: The enemies devise evil counsel that they cannot establish. Psalm 32 contrasts the Lord's nullifying peoples' and rulers' counsels with the permanence of His own counsel; the correspondence concerns frustrated human designs rather than an identified shared conspiracy.

Psalms 20:13
For you will make them turn their backs; among your remaining ones you will prepare their face.
  Psalms 17:41 — Enemies made to turn their backs: The opening clause anticipates enemies being made to turn their backs. David's rescue song likewise recounts his enemies' backs being turned toward him while addressing God; only that clear shared action supplies this connection.

Psalms 21:2
God, my God, attend to me; why didst Thou abandon me? Far from my salvation are the words of my transgressions.
  Matthew 27:46 — Quotation in the Passion: Jesus repeats the opening question about God's abandonment. Matthew quotes that question, without repeating the psalm's following clause about the speaker's transgressions.
  Mark 15:34 — Quotation in the Passion: Mark translates Jesus' cry with the psalm's opening address and question about abandonment; the remaining transgressions clause is outside the quoted words.

Psalms 21:3
My God, I will cry by day, and Thou wilt not hear, and by night, and not to folly for me.
  Job 30:20 — Complaint of unanswered prayer: Both speakers describe crying to God without being heard. The psalm's day-and-night persistence and Job's complaint retain their distinct settings.

Psalms 21:5
In Thee our fathers hoped; they hoped, and Thou didst rescue them.
  Psalms 43:2-4 — Recollection of ancestral deliverance: The trust and rescue of the fathers is compared with the remembered divine work for the fathers, whose deliverance is explicitly attributed to God's arm rather than their own.

Psalms 21:6
To Thee they cried out and were saved; in Thee they hoped and were not put to shame.
  Exodus 3:7-8 — Narrative illustration of ancestral rescue: The psalm remembers ancestors crying to God and being saved; Exodus recounts God hearing Israel's cry and coming to rescue them. This supplies an example, not an exclusive identification of every remembered rescue.

Psalms 21:7
But I am a worm and not a human being, a human reproach and an object of the people’s contempt.
  Isaiah 53:3 — Dishonored sufferer: The psalm's speaker is a human reproach and object of contempt; Isaiah's afflicted figure is dishonored and held of no account. The worm image remains particular to the psalm.

Psalms 21:8
All who watch me sneered at me; they spoke with their lips and shook their heads:
  Matthew 27:39-40 — Passion correspondence: The spectators' mocking speech and head-shaking recur among those reviling the crucified Jesus. Matthew gives the particular taunt concerning the sanctuary and self-rescue.
  Mark 15:29-30 — Passion correspondence: Mark likewise describes passersby reviling Jesus while shaking their heads, corresponding to the psalm's joined gestures and hostile speech.

Psalms 21:9
“He hoped in the Lord; let Him rescue him; let Him save him, since He desires him.”
  Matthew 27:43 — Repeated taunt: The mockers' challenge that God rescue the one who trusts Him, if He desires him, closely repeats the psalm's taunt concerning trust and divine rescue.

Psalms 21:10
For Thou art the One who drew me from the belly, my hope from my mother’s breasts.
  Psalms 70:6 — Divine care from birth: Both prayers trace divine support to the mother's womb; this source adds being drawn from the belly and hope from the mother's breasts.

Psalms 21:11
Upon Thee I was cast from the womb; from my mother’s belly Thou art my God.
  Psalms 138:13 — Divine support from the womb: The speaker's lifelong relation to God beginning in the mother's belly corresponds to the other psalm's explicit divine support from the mother's womb.

Psalms 21:12
Do not stand away from me, for affliction is near, for there is no one to help.
  2 Timothy 4:16-17 — Human abandonment and divine help: The plea that God stay near when no helper is present is compared with Paul's report that everyone deserted his defense but the Lord stood beside him. No shared historical incident is claimed.

Psalms 21:14
They opened their mouth against me like a lion seizing prey and roaring.
  Psalms 34:17 — Hostile lion imagery: The enemies' open mouths are likened to a predatory lion; the other prayer asks restoration from hostile wickedness and rescue of the speaker's only life from lions.

Psalms 21:15
I was poured out like water, and all my bones were scattered. My heart became like wax melting in the midst of my belly.
  Psalms 57:9 — Melting wax image: The afflicted speaker's heart melts like wax; the other psalm uses melting wax for the removal of the wicked. The shared image is applied to different subjects.

Psalms 21:16
My strength dried up like a potsherd; my tongue is stuck to my throat, and Thou broughtest me down into the dust of death.
  Lamentations 4:4 — Tongue adhering in thirst: The dried-up speaker's tongue sticks to his throat; Lamentations uses the same bodily image explicitly for nursing children suffering thirst.

Psalms 21:17
For many dogs surrounded me; a gathering of evildoers encompassed me; they dug into my hands and feet.
  John 20:25 — Comparison of wounded hands: The psalm says enemies dug into the speaker's hands and feet; Thomas speaks specifically of nail marks in Jesus' hands. The link compares injured hands while preserving the different wording and not supplying feet to John.

Psalms 21:19
They divided my garments among themselves, and cast a lot for my clothing.
  John 19:23-24 — Explicit fulfillment quotation: John explicitly quotes the division of garments and casting a lot for clothing, applying it to the soldiers' division and decision concerning Jesus' tunic.
  Matthew 27:35 — Passion correspondence: Matthew narrates the division of Jesus' garments by casting a lot, matching both actions in the psalm.
  Mark 15:24 — Passion correspondence: Mark describes the crucifiers dividing Jesus' garments and casting a lot to determine each person's portion, corresponding to the psalm's two linked actions.

Psalms 21:20
But Thou, Lord, do not put my help far away; attend to my support.
  Psalms 70:12 — Repeated appeal for nearby help: Both prayers ask God not to remain far away and to attend to the speaker's help, preserving the paired distance-and-assistance formulation.

Psalms 21:21
Rescue my soul from the sword and my only one from the dog’s hand.
  Psalms 34:17 — Rescue of the soul and only one: Both pleas pair the speaker's soul with an additional expression: the only one here and the only life there. Both request rescue using hostile animal imagery, with the dog and sword here distinct from the lions there.

Psalms 21:22
Save me from a lion’s mouth and my lowliness from the horns of one-horned animals.
  2 Timothy 4:17 — Rescue from a lion's mouth: The psalm asks rescue from a lion's mouth; Paul reports being rescued from a lion's mouth. This verbal correspondence does not identify Paul's danger as a literal animal encounter.

Psalms 21:23
I will recount Thy name to my brothers; in the midst of the assembly I will sing hymns unto Thee:
  Hebrews 2:11-12 — Explicit quotation: Hebrews introduces the announcement of God's name to brothers and praise in the assembly as words of the One who sanctifies, directly quoting this verse.

Psalms 21:24
You who fear the Lord, praise Him! All Jacob’s seed, glorify Him! Let all Israel’s seed fear Him,
  Psalms 134:19-20 — Israel and those fearing God summoned to praise: Both calls address Israel and those who fear the Lord, summoning them to bless or praise Him. The target additionally names the houses of Aaron and Levi.

Psalms 21:25
because He did not despise or resent the poor man’s petition, nor turn His face away from me, and when I cried out to Him, He heard me.
  Psalms 101:18 — The lowly petition not despised: Both affirm that God regards a lowly person's prayer without treating the petition as nothing; this source also reports that the cry was heard.

Psalms 21:26
From Thee comes my praise in a great assembly; I will repay my vows before those who fear Him.
  Psalms 49:14 — Praise and repayment of vows: Both join praise offered to God with payment of vows. The source locates this repayment before those fearing Him in the great assembly.

Psalms 21:27
The needy will eat and be filled, and those who seek the Lord will praise Him; their hearts will live for the age of the age.
  Psalms 33:10-11 — Seekers of God supplied with good: The needy eat and are filled while seekers praise God; the target promises that those fearing and seeking Him will lack no good thing, contrasting the hungry rich.

Psalms 21:28
All the limits of the earth will remember and turn to the Lord, and all the families of the nations will bow down before Thee,
  Psalms 85:9 — All nations coming to worship: Both envision all nations bowing before the Lord; the source emphasizes earth's limits remembering and turning, while the target describes the nations God made coming to glorify Him.

Psalms 21:29
because kingship belongs to the Lord, and He is master of the nations.
  Psalms 46:8-9 — Universal divine kingship: The Lord's kingship and mastery of nations correspond to God's being King of the whole earth and reigning over nations upon His holy throne.

Psalms 21:30
All the prosperous of the earth ate and bowed down; before Him all who descend into the earth will fall, and my soul lives for Him.
  Philippians 2:10 — Universal bodily homage: The psalm depicts those descending into the earth falling before God; Philippians includes those under the earth among all who bend the knee at Jesus' name. The source's eating and living soul remain distinct details.

Psalms 21:31
And my seed will serve Him; the coming generation will be announced to the Lord,
  Psalms 101:19 — Worship transmitted to a coming generation: The serving seed and announced coming generation correspond to a record for another generation and a people being created who will praise the Lord.

Psalms 21:32
and they will announce His righteousness to a people yet to be born, because the Lord has done it.
  Psalms 77:6-7 — Announcement to people yet unborn: Both explicitly anticipate children or a people still to be born receiving an announcement of God's deeds, so that another generation will know and hope in Him.

Psalms 22:1
A psalm of David. The Lord shepherds me, and nothing shall fail me.
  John 10:11 — Divine shepherd image: The psalm presents the Lord as the speaker's shepherd; Jesus calls Himself the good Shepherd who lays down His life for the sheep. The target develops the shepherd image through self-giving.
  Isaiah 40:11 — The Lord shepherding His flock: Isaiah directly describes the Lord shepherding, gathering and comforting His flock, corresponding to the psalm's claim that the Lord shepherds the speaker.

Psalms 22:2
He made me dwell in a place of green growth; He nourished me beside water of rest.
  Ezekiel 34:14-15 — Good pasture and rest: The green place and restful water belong to the psalm's shepherd image; Ezekiel promises God's sheep rich pasture, nourishment and rest. The geographical details remain distinct.

Psalms 22:3
He turned my soul back; He guided me upon paths of righteousness for His name’s sake.
  Psalms 30:4 — Guidance for God's name: Both attribute personal guidance to God for His name's sake. The source speaks of righteous paths and turning the soul back; the target joins guidance with nourishment.

Psalms 22:4
For even if I walk in the midst of death’s shadow, I will fear no evils, for Thou art with me; Thy rod and Thy staff comforted me.
  Isaiah 43:2 — Divine presence through danger: The speaker fears no evils while walking in death's shadow because God is with him; Isaiah promises God's presence while passing through water and fire. The dangers remain different images.

Psalms 22:5
Thou didst prepare a table before me opposite those who afflict me; Thou didst enrich my head with oil, and Thy cup is intoxicating—how excellent!
  Psalms 35:9 — Divine abundance as intoxicating drink: The excellent intoxicating cup corresponds to intoxication from the richness of God's house and drinking the torrent of His delight. The table, enemies and head-oil belong to this source's fuller image.

Psalms 22:6
And Thy mercy will pursue me all the days of my life, and I will dwell in the Lord’s house for length of days.
  Psalms 26:4 — Lifelong dwelling in God's house: The expectation of dwelling in the Lord's house for length of days corresponds closely to the petition to dwell there all the days of one's life.

Psalms 23:1
A psalm of David, for the first day of the week. The earth and its fullness belong to the Lord, the inhabited world and all who dwell in it.
  1 Corinthians 10:26 — Explicit quotation: Paul quotes the Lord's ownership of the earth and its fullness in explaining food and conscience, directly reusing the psalm's declaration.

Psalms 23:2
He founded it upon seas and prepared it upon rivers.
  Psalms 135:6 — Earth established upon waters: Both describe God making the earth firm upon waters; this source names seas and rivers in the foundation image.

Psalms 23:3
Who will ascend the Lord’s mountain, and who will stand in His holy place?
  Psalms 14:1 — Question about access to the holy mountain: Both ask who may enter or remain in God's holy dwelling and mountain, introducing ensuing ethical qualifications.

Psalms 23:4
One innocent in hands and pure in heart, who did not take up his soul for emptiness and did not swear deceitfully to his neighbor.
  Psalms 14:2-4 — Ethical qualifications for holy access: Both answers require integrity of heart and conduct, especially truthful treatment of neighbors and fidelity in swearing. They answer the respective questions about God's holy dwelling.
  Matthew 5:8 — Purity of heart and divine access: Purity of heart qualifies the person who can stand in God's holy place; Jesus' blessing promises the pure in heart that they will see God. The settings of holy access and promised sight remain distinct.

Psalms 23:6
This is the generation of those who seek Him, who seek the face of Jacob’s God. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 104:3-6 — Seeking the face of Jacob's God: Both describe seeking the Lord and His face. This source identifies Him as Jacob's God, while the target addresses Abraham's offspring and Jacob's sons and adds continual seeking and remembrance of wonders.

Psalms 23:7
Your rulers—lift up gates! And be raised, eternal gates, and the King of glory will enter.
  Psalms 117:19-20 — Gates opened for sacred entry: Both address gates to be opened for entry associated with the Lord. Here the King of glory enters; there a thankful righteous worshiper enters the Lord's gate.

Psalms 23:8
Who is this King of glory? The Lord, strong and powerful; the Lord, powerful in war.
  Exodus 15:1-3 — The Lord's power over war: The King of glory is powerful in war; the victory song praises the Lord who cast horse and rider into the sea and shatters wars. The target's actual description is preserved.

Psalms 23:9
Your rulers—lift up gates! And be raised, eternal gates, and the King of glory will enter.
  Psalms 117:19-20 — Repeated sacred-entry summons: This repeated summons for the King of glory's entrance is compared with the petition to open the Lord's gates for a righteous worshiper. The entrants and gate descriptions are distinguished.

Psalms 24:1
A psalm of David. To Thee, Lord, I lifted my soul, my God.
  Psalms 142:8 — Lifting the soul to God: Both prayers explicitly state that the speaker lifted his soul to the Lord; the target places this act within a request to learn the way to walk.

Psalms 24:2
In Thee I have trusted; may I not be put to shame, and let my enemies not laugh at me.
  Psalms 70:1 — Trust without disgrace: Both pair personal hope or trust in God with a plea never to be put to shame. This source additionally asks that enemies not laugh.

Psalms 24:3
For indeed, all who wait for Thee will certainly not be put to shame; let all who act lawlessly without cause be ashamed.
  Romans 10:11 — Trust and freedom from shame: The promise that those waiting for God will not be ashamed corresponds to Paul's scriptural assurance that everyone believing in Him will not be put to shame. This is a thematic comparison, not a claim that Paul identifies this exact verse as his quotation.

Psalms 24:4
Make Thy ways known to me, Lord, and teach me Thy paths.
  Psalms 142:8-10 — Prayer to learn God's way: Both ask God to make known the way to walk and to teach and guide the speaker. The target adds instruction in doing God's will and guidance by His good Spirit.

Psalms 24:5
Guide me into Thy truth and teach me, for Thou art God my Savior, and I waited for Thee the whole day.
  Psalms 42:3 — Truth as a guide toward God: The source asks to be guided into divine truth; the target asks God to send light and truth and reports that they guided the speaker to His holy dwelling.

Psalms 24:6
Remember Thy compassions, Lord, and Thy mercies, for they are from eternity.
  Psalms 102:17 — Mercy from eternity: The plea to remember compassions and mercies from eternity corresponds to the declaration that the Lord's mercy is from eternity to eternity toward those who fear Him.

Psalms 24:7
Do not remember the sins of my youth or my acts of ignorance; according to Thy mercy, remember me Thyself, for Thy kindness’ sake, Lord.
  Job 13:26 — Sins of youth remembered or charged: The psalm asks God not to remember youthful sins; Job complains that God recorded evils and placed the sins of youth around him. The request and complaint are contrasted without equating their circumstances.

Psalms 24:8
The Lord is kind and upright; therefore He will give sinners instruction in the way.
  Psalms 118:68 — Divine goodness and instruction: The Lord's kindness is the basis for His instruction of sinners; the other prayer likewise appeals to His goodness while asking Him to teach His ordinances.

Psalms 24:10
All the Lord’s ways are mercy and truth for those who seek His covenant and His testimonies.
  Psalms 102:17-18 — Mercy and covenant keeping: Both connect the Lord's mercy with those keeping or seeking His covenant and testimonies or commandments. The source's truth and the target's generations remain distinct emphases.

Psalms 24:11
For Thy name’s sake, Lord, Thou wilt be gracious to my sin, for it is great.
  Psalms 78:9 — Forgiveness for the Lord's name: Both appeal to the Lord's name as the ground for gracious dealing with sin. The source acknowledges its greatness; the target asks communal rescue and propitiation.

Psalms 24:12
Who is the human being who fears the Lord? He will give him instruction in a way he chose.
  Psalms 110:10 — Reverence and instruction: The God-fearing person receives instruction in a way; the target likewise links fear of the Lord with wisdom and understanding practiced in conduct.

Psalms 24:13
His soul will lodge among good things, and his seed will inherit land.
  Psalms 36:9-11 — Promised inheritance of land: The source promises land to the God-fearing person's seed; the target promises land to those waiting for the Lord and to the meek. The stated recipients are preserved rather than equated in every detail.

Psalms 24:14
The Lord is the stronghold of those who fear Him, and the Lord’s name belongs to those who fear Him, and His covenant is to make things known to them.
  Psalms 110:9-10 — Covenant, divine name and reverence: Both join the Lord's covenant and His name with reverence and knowledge or understanding. The source's actual stronghold wording is retained and is not replaced with a secret-friendship formula.

Psalms 24:15
My eyes are continually toward the Lord, for He will pull my feet out of a trap.
  Psalms 123:7 — Release from a hunter's trap: God's pulling the speaker's feet from a trap corresponds to the soul's rescue like a bird from a shattered hunters' trap. The bodily and bird images remain distinct.

Psalms 24:16
Look upon me and have mercy on me, for I am alone and poor.
  Psalms 85:1-3 — Poverty as the basis of petition: Both speakers identify themselves as poor when asking God to attend and show mercy. The source adds aloneness; the target adds need and continual crying.

Psalms 24:17
The afflictions of my heart widened; bring me out of my distresses.
  Psalms 142:11 — Being brought out of distress: The petition to be brought out of expanding heart-afflictions corresponds to the other prayer's assurance that God will bring the soul out of distress in His righteousness.

Psalms 24:19
See my enemies, for they multiplied and hated me with unjust hatred.
  Psalms 34:19 — Unjust hostility: Both prayers name unjust or causeless hatred from enemies. The target additionally asks that these enemies not rejoice over the speaker.

Psalms 24:20
Guard my soul and rescue me; may I not be put to shame, for I hoped in Thee.
  Psalms 70:1-2 — Hope, rescue and avoidance of shame: Both appeals combine hope in God, rescue and freedom from shame; the target explicitly invokes divine righteousness in requesting deliverance.

Psalms 24:22
Redeem Israel, God, from all its afflictions.
  Psalms 129:7-8 — Redemption of Israel: Both speak of God redeeming Israel: this source names all its afflictions, while the target names all its lawless deeds. The objects of redemption remain distinct.

Psalms 25:1
Of David. Judge me, Lord, for I walked in my innocence, and hoping in the Lord, I will certainly not grow weak.
  Psalms 7:9 — Judgment according to innocence: Both speakers ask the Lord to judge them while appealing to their innocence or righteousness, making the ethical basis of the petition explicit.

Psalms 25:2
Test me, Lord, and try me; try my kidneys and my heart by fire.
  Jeremiah 17:10 — Testing hearts and kidneys: The requested testing of heart and kidneys corresponds directly to the Lord's declaration that He examines hearts and tests kidneys before recompensing conduct.
  Psalms 65:10 — Divine testing by fire: The speaker asks to be tried by fire; the target recalls God trying the community by fire as silver is tested. Individual inward examination and communal testing remain distinct.

Psalms 25:4
I did not sit with a council of emptiness, and I will certainly not enter with transgressors.
  Psalms 1:1 — Refusal of corrupt company: Both refuse participation in ungodly company. This source names sitting with a council of emptiness and entering with transgressors; Psalm1 distinguishes walking in ungodly counsel, standing in sinners' way and sitting with pestilent men.

Psalms 25:5
I hated an assembly of evildoers, and I will certainly not sit with the ungodly.
  Psalms 100:5-7 — Exclusion of evildoing companions: The rejection of an evildoers' assembly corresponds to refusing fellowship or dwelling to arrogant and unjust speakers while favoring faithful companions.

Psalms 25:6
I will wash my hands among the innocent and go around Thine altar, Lord,
  Exodus 30:19-21 — Washing before the altar: The psalm joins washed hands among the innocent with going around the altar; Exodus prescribes hand-and-foot washing when priests approach the altar. The ritual comparison does not identify the psalm's speaker as an Aaronic priest.

Psalms 25:8
Lord, I loved the beauty of Thy house and the place of Thy glory’s dwelling.
  Psalms 83:2-3 — Love for the divine dwelling: Love of the house's beauty and place of glory corresponds to the beloved dwellings and longing for the Lord's courts in the target.

Psalms 25:9
Do not destroy my soul together with the ungodly, or my life with men of bloodshed,
  Psalms 54:24 — Doom of men of bloodshed: The plea not to perish together with men of bloodshed is compared with their being brought down to a well of corruption. The first speaker asks to be spared their fate.

Psalms 25:10
in whose hands are lawless deeds; their right hand was filled with gifts.
  Psalms 14:5 — Gifts in unjust hands: Hands filled with gifts occur among lawless men of bloodshed; the target explicitly praises refusal of gifts against innocent people. The contrast concerns corrupt gifts rather than all giving.

Psalms 25:12
For my foot stood in uprightness; in assemblies I will bless Thee, Lord.
  Psalms 34:18 — Thanksgiving in an assembly: The promise to bless the Lord in assemblies corresponds to the promise of thanksgiving and praise in a great assembly and among the people.

Psalms 26:1
Of David, before being anointed. The Lord is my light and my Savior; whom will I fear? The Lord is the protector of my life; of whom will I be afraid?
  Psalms 17:29 — God as personal light: The Lord as the speaker's light corresponds to God lighting the speaker's lamp and darkness. Both portray divine aid through illumination.
  Psalms 45:2-3 — Divine protection and fearlessness: The speaker's refusal to fear rests on God as protector; the target likewise reasons from God as refuge and strength to fearlessness amid upheaval.

Psalms 26:3
Even if an army draws up against me, my heart will not fear; even if war rises against me, in this I hope.
  2 Kings 6:15-17 — Confidence before a threatening army: The psalm maintains confidence even before an army; Elisha tells his fearful attendant not to fear the encircling force because of the greater protection with them. The narrative illustrates the stance without dating the psalm to that event.

Psalms 26:4
One thing I asked from the Lord; this I will seek: that I may dwell in the Lord’s house all the days of my life, that I may behold the Lord’s loveliness and visit His temple.
  Psalms 83:2-5 — Desire to dwell in God's house: The lifelong desire to dwell in and behold the Lord's house corresponds to longing for His courts and the blessedness of those who dwell in His house.

Psalms 26:5
For He hid me in a tent in the day of my troubles; He sheltered me in the hiding place of His tent; He raised me upon a rock.
  Psalms 60:3-5 — Rock and divine dwelling as refuge: Both combine being raised on a rock with protection in God's dwelling. The images express security in trouble rather than identifying a single literal site.

Psalms 26:8
My heart said unto Thee, “My face sought Thee.” Thy face, Lord, I will seek.
  Psalms 104:4 — Seeking the Lord's face: The personal resolve to seek God's face corresponds directly to the summons to seek His face continually.

Psalms 26:9
Do not turn Thy face away from me; do not turn aside in anger from Thy servant. Become my helper; do not reject me or abandon me, God my Savior.
  Psalms 142:7 — Plea against the hidden divine face: Both ask that God not turn His face away from the distressed speaker; the target states the feared consequence as becoming like those descending to a pit.

Psalms 26:10
For my father and my mother abandoned me, but the Lord took me to Himself.
  Isaiah 49:14-15 — Parental abandonment contrasted with God: The Lord receives the one abandoned by father and mother; Isaiah contrasts even a mother's possible forgetting with God's refusal to forget Zion. The human relationships remain distinct.

Psalms 26:11
Give me instruction in Thy way, Lord, and guide me on an upright path because of my enemies.
  Psalms 5:9 — Guidance because of enemies: Both request instruction or guidance along God's upright way specifically because of enemies, preserving the same motive and path image.

Psalms 26:12
Do not hand me over to the souls of those who afflict me, for unjust witnesses rose against me, and injustice lied to itself.
  Matthew 26:59-60 — Hostile false witnesses: The psalm's unjust witnesses are compared with false witnesses sought and appearing against Jesus before the council. This is a correspondence of hostile testimony, not a claim that the psalm narrates that hearing.

Psalms 26:13
I believe that I will see the Lord’s good things in the land of the living.
  Psalms 141:6 — Hope in the land of the living: Expectation of seeing the Lord's good things in the land of the living corresponds to calling Him one's hope and portion in that land.

Psalms 26:14
Wait for the Lord; act courageously, and let your heart be strong, and wait for the Lord.
  Psalms 30:25 — Repeated exhortation to courage: Both exhort those waiting or hoping for the Lord to act courageously and let their hearts be strong, sharing the distinctive paired commands.

Psalms 27:1
Of David. To Thee, Lord, I cried; my God, do not keep silence toward me, lest Thou keep silence toward me and I become like those descending into a pit.
  Psalms 142:7 — Divine withdrawal and descent to the pit: Both fear becoming like those descending to a pit if God withholds attention. Silence toward the petitioner and turning the face away are the respective descriptions.

Psalms 27:2
Hear the voice of my petition as I petition Thee, as I lift my hands toward Thy holy temple.
  2 Chronicles 6:12-13 — Hands extended in temple prayer: The psalm depicts hands lifted toward the holy temple; Solomon spreads his hands before the altar in the temple court and toward heaven. The posture and sanctuary context correspond while the directions remain explicit.

Psalms 27:3
Do not drag my soul away together with sinners, and do not destroy me together with those working injustice, who speak peace with their neighbors while evils are in their hearts.
  Psalms 34:20 — Peaceful words concealing hostility: Both describe people speaking peaceful things while inwardly devising evil or deceit, exposing the discrepancy between speech and intention.

Psalms 27:4
Give them according to their works and according to the wickedness of their practices; give them according to the works of their hands; repay their recompense to them.
  Romans 2:6 — Recompense according to works: The plea that evildoers receive according to their works corresponds to the declaration that God repays each according to works. The enemy-directed petition and universal judgment statement remain distinct.
  Jeremiah 17:10 — Recompense for conduct: The requested repayment according to practices corresponds to the Lord's declaration that He gives each according to ways and the fruits of practices.

Psalms 27:5
Because they did not understand the Lord’s works or the works of His hands, Thou wilt pull them down and certainly not build them up.
  Isaiah 5:12-13 — Disregard of God's works and judgment: Both connect failure to consider the Lord's works or the works of His hands with judgment. The source describes being pulled down; Isaiah describes captivity and death.

Psalms 27:6
Blessed be the Lord, because He heard the voice of my petition.
  Psalms 65:19-20 — Blessing God for hearing prayer: Both bless God immediately in connection with His hearing the sound of a petition and not rejecting prayer.

Psalms 27:8
The Lord is the stronghold of His people and the protector of the salvations of His anointed.
  Psalms 19:7 — Salvation of the anointed: Both explicitly attribute the anointed one's salvation to the Lord; the target describes the saving strength of His right hand.

Psalms 27:9
Save Thy people and bless Thine inheritance; shepherd them and lift them up for ever.
  Psalms 79:2-4 — Shepherding and saving Israel: The plea to save and shepherd God's people corresponds to the invocation of Israel's shepherd and the call for Him to come and save.

Psalms 28:1
A psalm of David, for the departure of the tent. Bring to the Lord, sons of God; bring to the Lord young rams; bring to the Lord glory and honor.
  Psalms 95:7 — Glory and honor brought to the Lord: Both repeat the summons to bring glory and honor to the Lord. This source addresses sons of God and mentions young rams; the target addresses families of nations.

Psalms 28:2
Bring to the Lord glory for His name; bow down to the Lord in His holy courtyard.
  Psalms 95:8-9 — Glory for the name and worship in the holy court: The invitation to give glory for the Lord's name and bow in His holy courtyard closely recurs in the target's adjoining calls.

Psalms 28:3
The Lord’s voice is over the waters; the God of glory thundered; the Lord is over many waters.
  Psalms 76:17-19 — Divine thunder over troubled waters: The Lord's voice over many waters and the God of glory's thunder correspond to waters fearing God and His thundering voice amid lightning and trembling earth.
  1 Samuel 7:10 — Narrated divine thunder: The description of divine thunder corresponds to the Lord thundering with a great voice against Israel's attackers. The narrative supplies an example of the voice's power, not the identity of the psalm's waters.

Psalms 28:4
The Lord’s voice is in strength; the Lord’s voice is in magnificence.
  Job 37:4-5 — Powerful divine voice: The Lord's strong and magnificent voice is compared with Elihu's description of the Strong One thundering wonders with His voice. The attribution to Elihu is retained.

Psalms 28:5
The voice of the Lord, who crushes cedars; and the Lord will crush Lebanon’s cedars,
  Isaiah 2:12-13 — Lebanon's cedars under divine power: The Lord crushing Lebanon's cedars corresponds to the Lord's day against the high cedars of Lebanon among the proud and elevated. No particular storm or campaign is identified.

Psalms 28:8
The voice of the Lord, who shakes the wilderness; and the Lord will shake the wilderness of Kadesh.
  Hebrews 12:26 — Divine voice shaking the world: The Lord's voice shakes the wilderness of Kadesh; Hebrews describes His voice shaking the earth and promising a further shaking. The geographic scope differs, and this is not labeled Hebrews' quoted source.

Psalms 28:10
The Lord will settle the flood, and the Lord sits as King for ever.
  Psalms 9:8 — Enduring divine kingship: The Lord's sitting as King for ever corresponds to His remaining for ever with a throne prepared for judgment. The flood statement is an additional feature of this source.

Psalms 28:11
The Lord will give strength to His people; the Lord will bless His people with peace.
  Numbers 6:24-26 — Blessing the people with peace: The psalm's promise that the Lord bless His people with peace corresponds to the priestly blessing culminating in the Lord giving peace.

Psalms 29:2
I will exalt Thee, Lord, for Thou tookest me up and didst not gladden my enemies over me.
  Psalms 34:19 — Enemies denied rejoicing: The speaker thanks God for not gladdening his enemies; the other prayer asks that unjust enemies not rejoice over him. The answered thanksgiving and request correspond.

Psalms 29:3
Lord my God, I cried out unto Thee, and Thou didst heal me.
  Psalms 6:3 — Prayer and divine healing: The report of crying to God and being healed is compared with the direct plea for the Lord to heal the weak speaker. No shared illness or chronology is assumed.

Psalms 29:4
Lord, Thou broughtest my soul up from Hades; Thou didst save me from those descending into a pit.
  Psalms 15:10 — Soul delivered from Hades: Being brought up from Hades corresponds to confidence that God will not abandon the speaker's soul to Hades. The images of deliverance retain their own temporal forms.

Psalms 29:5
Sing praise to the Lord, His holy ones, and give thanks at the remembrance of His holiness,
  Psalms 96:12 — Thanksgiving at remembrance of holiness: Both summon God's people to rejoice or sing and give thanks at the remembrance of His holiness, sharing this specific thanksgiving formula.

Psalms 29:6
because anger is in His wrath, and life in His will; weeping will lodge in the evening, and exultation comes toward morning.
  Psalms 125:5-6 — Tears giving way to exultation: Evening weeping followed by morning exultation is compared with sowing in tears and returning with exultation. The comparison concerns the reversal and does not rewrite the source's wrath clause as a statement of duration.

Psalms 29:7
But I said in my prosperity, “I will certainly never be shaken.”
  Psalms 9:27 — Contrasting speakers claiming stability: The speaker recalls saying in prosperity that he would never be shaken; the sinner in Psalm9 says he will not be shaken across generations. Their similar boasts have different speakers and contexts.

Psalms 29:8
Lord, in Thy will Thou didst supply strength to my beauty; but Thou didst turn Thy face away, and I became troubled.
  Psalms 103:29 — Trouble when God turns His face away: Both explicitly connect God turning His face away with the recipients becoming troubled. Here the individual speaks; the target describes dependent creatures.

Psalms 29:10
“What benefit is there in my blood, in my descending into corruption? Will dust give thanks unto Thee, or announce Thy truth?”
  Psalms 6:6 — Question about thanksgiving among the dead: Both appeal for life by asking whether death or Hades permits thanksgiving to God. The source uses dust and corruption as its images.
  Isaiah 38:18-19 — Life as the setting of praise: The question whether dust can thank God corresponds to the contrast between the dead who do not praise Him and the living who bless Him.

Psalms 29:11
The Lord heard and had mercy on me; the Lord became my helper.
  Psalms 117:21 — Heard prayer and divine aid: The Lord hearing and becoming the speaker's helper corresponds to thanksgiving that He heard and became the speaker's salvation.

Psalms 29:12
Thou didst turn my lament into dancing for me; Thou didst tear open my sackcloth and gird me with gladness,
  Isaiah 61:2-3 — Mourning garments exchanged for gladness: The torn sackcloth and girding with gladness correspond to mourners receiving an ointment of joy and a robe of glory instead of despondency. The texts use distinct clothing images for the reversal.

Psalms 30:2
In Thee, Lord, I hoped; may I never be put to shame; in Thy righteousness rescue me and bring me out.
  Psalms 70:1-2 — Repeated rescue formula: Both combine hope in God, avoidance of disgrace and rescue in divine righteousness, with closely corresponding requests to be brought out.

Psalms 30:3
Incline Thine ear to me; hasten to bring me out; be for me a protecting God and a house of refuge to save me.
  Psalms 70:2-3 — Inclined ear and protecting refuge: Both ask God to bend His ear, save the speaker and become a defending or protecting place of refuge.

Psalms 30:4
For Thou art my stronghold and my refuge, and for Thy name’s sake Thou wilt guide me and nourish me.
  Psalms 22:3 — Guidance for the divine name: Both attribute the speaker's guidance to the Lord for His name's sake; nourishment here accompanies the target's righteous paths and restored soul.

Psalms 30:5
Thou wilt bring me out of this trap they hid for me, for Thou art my protector.
  Psalms 123:7 — Rescue from a concealed trap: The promise of release from a hidden trap corresponds to the rescued soul likened to a bird escaping when hunters' trap is shattered.

Psalms 30:6
Into Thy hands I will entrust my spirit; Thou didst redeem me, Lord, God of truth.
  Luke 23:46 — Words used by Jesus at death: Jesus entrusts His spirit into the Father's hands using the psalm's distinctive formula. Luke gives this as His final spoken prayer before death.
  Acts 7:59 — Entrusting the spirit at death: Stephen asks the Lord Jesus to receive his spirit while being stoned, corresponding to the psalm's act of entrusting the spirit to the Lord. The addressee and wording are preserved.

Psalms 30:7
Thou didst hate those who guard vanities to no purpose; but I hoped in the Lord.
  Jonah 2:9 — Guarding futile vanities: Both negatively describe those guarding vain things; the psalm contrasts them with hope in the Lord, while Jonah says such people abandoned their mercy.

Psalms 30:8
I will exult and rejoice in Thy mercy, because Thou didst look upon my humiliation; Thou didst save my soul from constraints
  Exodus 3:7-8 — God sees affliction and rescues: The report that God looked upon humiliation and saved from constraints is compared with His seeing Israel's affliction and coming to rescue them from Egypt. This is an illustrative correspondence without assigning the psalm to that event.

Psalms 30:9
and didst not enclose me in an enemy’s hands; Thou didst set my feet in a spacious place.
  Psalms 17:18-20 — From enemies into a spacious place: Both describe divine rescue from enemies through the image of being brought or set in a spacious place.

Psalms 30:10
Have mercy on me, Lord, for I am afflicted; my eye was troubled with anger, my soul and my belly too.
  Psalms 6:8 — An eye troubled by anger: Both use the specific description of the speaker's eye being troubled from anger. This source additionally names soul and belly.

Psalms 30:11
For my life failed in pain and my years in groaning; my strength grew weak in poverty, and my bones were troubled.
  Psalms 6:3-7 — Groaning, weakness and troubled bones: Both describe bodily weakness, troubled bones and prolonged groaning. The source expressly locates weakness in poverty, without replacing that word with sin.

Psalms 30:12
Beyond all my enemies I became a reproach, and greatly so to my neighbors, and a fear to my acquaintances; those who watched me outside fled from me.
  Psalms 37:12 — Distress and nearby relationships: The speaker becomes a reproach to neighbors and a fear to acquaintances, while outside observers flee; the target distinguishes friends and neighbors standing opposite from the nearest standing far away. The comparison preserves these different reactions within nearby relationships.

Psalms 30:13
I was forgotten from the heart like a dead person; I became like a lost vessel.
  Psalms 87:5-6 — Living speaker likened to forgotten dead: The speaker describes being forgotten like a dead person; the target similarly likens the speaker to helpless dead in a grave who are remembered no more.

Psalms 30:14
For I heard the reproach of many dwelling all around; as they gathered together against me, they planned to take my soul.
  Jeremiah 20:10 — Reproach and conspiracy all around: Both speakers report hearing the reproach of many gathering all around and plotting against them. The close verbal correspondence preserves their different settings.

Psalms 30:16
My seasons are in Thy hands; rescue me from the hand of my enemies and from those who pursue me.
  Job 14:5 — Human times in God's keeping: The declaration that the speaker's seasons are in God's hands corresponds to human months being numbered with God and a limit set by Him.

Psalms 30:17
Make Thy face shine upon Thy servant; save me in Thy mercy.
  Numbers 6:25 — The shining divine face and mercy: The plea that God's face shine upon His servant and save in mercy corresponds to the blessing that His face shine and He show mercy.

Psalms 30:19
Let deceitful lips become speechless, those that speak lawlessness against the righteous in arrogance and contempt.
  Psalms 11:3-5 — Judgment on deceitful lips: Both prayers oppose deceitful lips and arrogant speech: one asks that they become speechless, the other that the Lord destroy them. The source specifically names their attacks on the righteous.

Psalms 30:21
Thou wilt hide them in the hiding place of Thy presence from human turmoil; Thou wilt shelter them in a tent from the contradiction of tongues.
  Psalms 60:4-5 — Divine shelter and dwelling imagery: Both depict divine protection with dwelling imagery: the source promises shelter in a tent from human strife, while the target joins sojourning in God's dwelling with shelter beneath His wings.

Psalms 30:23
But I said in my alarm, “So I have been cast away from before Thine eyes.” For this reason Thou heardest the voice of my petition when I cried unto Thee.
  Jonah 2:3-5 — Cast from God's eyes yet heard: Both prayers report the speaker's claim of being cast from God's eyes alongside divine hearing of the cry. The psalm's alarm and Jonah's descent into the sea remain separate settings.

Psalms 30:25
Act courageously, and let your heart be strong, all who hope in the Lord.
  1 Corinthians 16:13 — Paired exhortation to courage and strength: The exhortation to those hoping in the Lord to be courageous and strong corresponds to the paired commands to be courageous and strong while standing firm in faith.

Psalms 31:1
Of David. Of understanding. Blessed are those whose lawless deeds are forgiven and whose sins are covered.
  Romans 4:6-7 — Explicit quotation: Paul introduces David's declaration of blessedness and directly quotes forgiveness of lawless deeds and covering of sins.

Psalms 31:2
Blessed is the man whose sin the Lord will certainly not reckon, and in whose mouth there is no deceit.
  Romans 4:8 — Explicit quotation: Paul directly quotes the blessedness of the man whose sin the Lord does not reckon. The psalm's additional no-deceit clause is outside that quotation.

Psalms 31:3
Because I kept silent, my bones grew old from my crying out all day long.
  Psalms 31:5 — Same-chapter movement from silence to confession: Silence accompanies bodily decline and crying; the later acknowledgment of sin accompanies reported forgiveness. The source's simultaneous silence and crying are preserved, while the comparison follows the psalm's change to open confession.

Psalms 31:4
For day and night Thy hand weighed heavily upon me; I was turned to misery when a thorn became lodged. Diapsalma.
  Job 13:21 — Affliction under the divine hand: The psalm describes God's hand weighing heavily day and night; Job asks that the divine hand and terror be removed. The source's thorn and misery are not supplied to Job.

Psalms 31:5
I made my sin known, and I did not cover my lawlessness. I said, “I will confess my lawlessness against myself to the Lord,” and Thou forgavest the impiety of my sin. Diapsalma.
  1 John 1:9 — Confession and forgiveness: The speaker acknowledges rather than covers lawlessness and reports forgiveness; the letter likewise connects confession of sins with God's forgiving and cleansing.

Psalms 31:6
For this every holy one will pray unto Thee at a fitting time; yet in a flood of many waters they will not approach him.
  Psalms 45:2-4 — Divine refuge amid threatening waters: The praying person's safety from a flood is compared with God as refuge even when seas roar and mountains are displaced into them. The particular water images remain distinct.

Psalms 31:8
I will give you understanding and instruct you in this way in which you will go; I will fix my eyes upon you.
  Psalms 24:4-5 — Instruction and prayer for guidance: The promise of instruction in the way corresponds to the other prayer's request that God teach His paths and guide in truth. The speaker of this promise is not assigned an identity beyond the verse.

Psalms 31:9
Do not become like a horse and mule that have no understanding; with bridle and muzzle tighten their jaws— those that do not approach you.
  James 3:3 — A horse controlled through its mouth: Both employ the horse's bridle or bit as an image of guidance and restraint. The psalm warns against uncomprehending behavior; James uses the animal illustration in discussing control of speech.

Psalms 31:10
Many are the scourges of the sinner, but mercy will surround the one who hopes in the Lord.
  Psalms 33:20-23 — Contrasting bearers of many afflictions: This source assigns many scourges to the sinner and mercy to the hopeful; the target assigns many afflictions to the righteous yet promises rescue. The comparison preserves the different subjects rather than treating suffering as proof of guilt.

Psalms 31:11
Rejoice in the Lord and exult, righteous ones; boast, all upright in heart.
  Psalms 32:1 — Repeated summons to the righteous: Both summon the righteous and upright to exult in the Lord, linking upright character with the appropriateness of praise.

Psalms 32:1
Of David. Exult in the Lord, righteous ones; praise is fitting for the upright.
  Psalms 31:11 — Repeated summons to the righteous: The exhortation to righteous and upright people to exult in the Lord closely corresponds to the previous psalm's concluding summons to those groups.

Psalms 32:2
Give thanks to the Lord with the lyre; make music to Him with a ten-stringed psaltery.
  Psalms 91:2-4 — Thanksgiving with named string instruments: Both join thanksgiving and music to the Lord with the lyre and a ten-stringed psaltery, preserving the specified instruments.

Psalms 32:3
Sing Him a new song; make music well with a shout of triumph.
  Psalms 95:1 — A new song to the Lord: Both command singing a new song to the Lord; this source adds skilled music and a triumphal shout, while the target addresses all the earth.

Psalms 32:4
For the Lord’s word is upright, and all His works are in faithfulness.
  Deuteronomy 32:4 — Righteous and faithful divine works: The Lord's upright word and faithful works correspond to the proclamation that God's works are true and He is faithful, righteous and without unrighteousness.

Psalms 32:5
He loves mercy and judgment; the earth is full of the Lord’s mercy.
  Psalms 118:64 — The earth full of divine mercy: Both explicitly declare the earth full of the Lord's mercy. The target turns this declaration into a request for instruction.

Psalms 32:6
By the Lord’s word the heavens were established, and by the spirit of His mouth all their power.
  Genesis 1:6-8 — Heaven established through divine speech: The psalm attributes the heavens to the Lord's word; Genesis narrates God speaking the firmament into being and naming it Heaven. The spirit of His mouth and all their power remain this source's additional expressions.

Psalms 32:7
He gathers the sea’s waters like a wineskin; He places the deeps in storehouses.
  Genesis 1:9-10 — Gathering the sea's waters: God's gathering the waters in the psalm corresponds to His command that waters gather and the resulting seas in Genesis. The wineskin and storehouses belong to the psalm's imagery.
  Job 38:8-11 — Divine containment of the sea: The collected waters and stored deeps correspond to the Lord's account of bounding the sea with gates and bars. The containment images are compared without making them identical structures.

Psalms 32:8
Let all the earth fear the Lord; let all who inhabit the world be shaken before Him.
  Psalms 95:9 — The whole earth trembling before God: Both call the entire earth and its inhabitants to be shaken before the Lord; the target places this call alongside worship in His holy court.

Psalms 32:9
For He spoke, and they came to be; He commanded, and they were created.
  Genesis 1:3 — Creation by command: The declaration that God spoke and things came to be is illustrated directly by His command for light and the appearance of light.

Psalms 32:10
The Lord scatters the counsels of nations; He nullifies the plans of peoples and nullifies the counsels of rulers.
  Psalms 2:1-5 — National counsels confronted by God: The nullification of nations', peoples' and rulers' counsels is compared with their concerted opposition meeting divine laughter and wrath in Psalm2.

Psalms 32:11
But the Lord’s counsel remains for ever, the plans of His heart from generation to generation.
  Proverbs 19:21 — Enduring divine counsel: Both contrast human plans with the Lord's counsel remaining for ever, using the same enduring-counsel formulation.

Psalms 32:12
Blessed is the nation whose God is the Lord, the people He chose as an inheritance for Himself.
  Deuteronomy 32:9 — A people as God's inheritance: The chosen people called the Lord's inheritance correspond to Jacob and Israel explicitly designated His portion and measured lot of inheritance.

Psalms 32:13
From heaven the Lord looked down; He saw all the sons of men.
  Psalms 13:2 — Looking from heaven upon humanity: Both describe the Lord looking down from heaven upon the sons of men, preserving the vantage point and the people observed.

Psalms 32:14
From His prepared dwelling place He looked upon all who inhabit the earth.
  Psalms 10:4 — Heavenly dwelling and human observation: The Lord surveys the earth's inhabitants from His dwelling; the target joins His heavenly throne and holy temple with eyes toward the needy and eyelids examining the sons of men.

Psalms 32:15
He formed their hearts individually; He understands all their deeds.
  Psalms 138:2-5 — Formation and knowledge of persons: The maker of individual hearts understands deeds; the other prayer joins God's understanding of personal reasonings and ways with His forming the speaker.

Psalms 32:16
A king is not saved through a great force, and a giant will not be saved by the abundance of his strength.
  1 Samuel 17:47 — Salvation beyond military force: The denial that great force or a giant's strength saves corresponds to David's declaration that the Lord does not save by sword and spear because the battle belongs to Him.

Psalms 32:17
A horse is false for salvation, and by the abundance of its power it will not be saved.
  Proverbs 21:31 — A war horse cannot supply salvation: The horse's power is denied as a source of salvation; the proverb likewise distinguishes a horse prepared for war from the help that comes from the Lord.
  Psalms 19:8-9 — Horses contrasted with reliance on God: The horse's inability to save corresponds to the contrast between reliance on chariots and horses and reliance on the Lord's name, followed by their different outcomes.

Psalms 32:18
Behold, the Lord’s eyes are upon those who fear Him, those who hope in His mercy,
  Psalms 33:16 — The Lord's eyes upon His faithful: Both explicitly place the Lord's eyes upon those devoted to Him: those fearing and hoping here, and the righteous there.

Psalms 32:19
to rescue their souls from death and nourish them in famine.
  Psalms 36:18-19 — Nourishment during famine: The Lord nourishes the hopeful in famine; the target likewise promises that the blameless will be filled during famine rather than shamed in adversity.

Psalms 32:20
Our soul waits for the Lord, for He is our helper and defender.
  Psalms 113:17-19 — Hope in the divine helper and defender: The waiting soul calls the Lord helper and defender; the target repeatedly joins Israel's, Aaron's and God-fearers' hope with those same two descriptions.

Psalms 32:21
For our heart will rejoice in Him, and we have hoped in His holy name.
  Psalms 5:12 — Rejoicing joined to hope in the divine name: The community's rejoicing in God and hope in His holy name correspond to Psalm 5's call for those hoping in Him to rejoice and those loving His name to boast in Him. The combined name, hope and rejoicing language supplies the specific reciprocal comparison.

Psalms 33:2
I will bless the Lord at every time; His praise is continually in my mouth.
  Hebrews 13:15 — worship parallel: The promise to keep the Lord's praise continually in the mouth corresponds to Hebrews' continual sacrifice of praise, explicitly described as the fruit of lips confessing His name.

Psalms 33:3
My soul will be praised in the Lord; let the meek hear and rejoice.
  Psalms 68:33 — literary parallel: Here the meek are invited to hear and rejoice while the speaker's soul is to be praised in the Lord; Psalm 68 similarly invites the poor to see and rejoice while seeking God. The groups and sensory verbs retain their different wording.

Psalms 33:4
Magnify the Lord with me; let us exalt His name together.
  Romans 15:6 — communal worship parallel: The invitation to magnify and exalt the Lord together corresponds to Paul's prayer that the community glorify God with one accord and one mouth. Both emphasize shared praise rather than an isolated speaker.

Psalms 33:5
I sought the Lord, and He heard me; from all my sojourns He rescued me.
  Genesis 35:3 — personal deliverance testimony: The psalmist says the Lord heard him and rescued him from all his sojourns. Jacob recalls God hearing him in affliction and preserving him on the way he traveled; the comparison concerns divine hearing and help in these distinct journey or residence settings.

Psalms 33:6
Approach Him and be enlightened, and your faces will certainly not be put to shame.
  Exodus 34:29-35 — poetic and narrative imagery parallel: The psalm connects approaching God with enlightenment and unashamed faces. Exodus describes Moses' face being glorified through speaking with God; this is a related image of divine encounter affecting the face, in a different narrative setting.

Psalms 33:7
This poor man cried out, and the Lord heard him; from all his afflictions He saved him.
  Psalms 106:6 — literary parallel: The poor man's cry, the Lord's hearing, and rescue from afflictions correspond to the distressed wanderers crying to the Lord and being rescued from their distresses in Psalm106.

Psalms 33:8
An angel of the Lord will encamp around those who fear Him and will rescue them.
  Acts 12:7-11 — narrative illustration: The psalm promises rescue through an angel of the Lord. Peter's prison deliverance provides a particular angelic rescue: the angel leads him past guards, and Peter identifies the Lord as sending the angel to rescue him.

Psalms 33:9
Taste and see that the Lord is kind; blessed is the man who hopes in Him.
  1 Peter 2:3 — quotation parallel: Peter's statement about having tasted that the Lord is good closely repeats the psalm's invitation to taste and see that the Lord is kind. The supplied translations retain good and kind respectively.

Psalms 33:10
Fear the Lord, His holy ones, for there is no deficiency for those who fear Him.
  Psalms 111:1-3 — wisdom promise parallel: The absence of deficiency among those fearing the Lord corresponds to the blessing of the God-fearing person whose household has riches in Psalm111. Both associate reverence for the Lord with His provision.

Psalms 33:11
Rich people became poor and hungry, but those who seek the Lord will not lack any good thing. Diapsalma.
  Luke 1:53 — reversal parallel: The psalm contrasts rich people becoming poor and hungry with good things available to those seeking the Lord. Mary's song likewise joins hungry people receiving good things with rich people being sent away empty.
  Matthew 6:31-33 — wisdom promise parallel: Those seeking the Lord will not lack good things here; Jesus tells hearers concerned about food, drink and clothing to seek God's kingdom and righteousness, with these necessities added to them.

Psalms 33:12
Come, children, listen to me; I will teach you the fear of the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 6:1-2 — instruction parallel: The invitation to children to learn the fear of the Lord corresponds to Moses' teaching of commandments so that the hearers, their sons and their sons' sons may fear the Lord throughout life.

Psalms 33:13
Who is the person who desires life, who loves to see good days?
  1 Peter 3:10 — quotation: Peter's quotation begins with the person wishing to love life and see good days, matching this opening question about desiring life and seeing good days before the instructions on speech.

Psalms 33:14
Stop your tongue from evil and your lips from speaking deceit.
  1 Peter 3:10 — quotation: The latter part of Peter's quoted verse repeats the injunction to keep the tongue from evil and the lips from speaking deceit, the two speech restrictions given here.

Psalms 33:15
Turn aside from evil and do good; seek peace and pursue it.
  1 Peter 3:11 — quotation: Peter repeats the complete sequence of turning from evil, doing good, seeking peace and pursuing it. These four actions are the distinctive shared instruction.

Psalms 33:16
The Lord’s eyes are upon the righteous, and His ears toward their supplication.
  1 Peter 3:12 — quotation: Peter's quotation preserves the Lord's eyes upon the righteous and His ears attending their petition, matching the two images of attentive divine regard in this verse.

Psalms 33:17
But the Lord’s face is against those who do evil, to destroy their remembrance from the earth.
  1 Peter 3:12 — quotation: The closing clause in Peter's quotation repeats the Lord's face being against evildoers. The psalm's additional destruction of their remembrance is not included in Peter's quoted sentence.

Psalms 33:18
The righteous cried out, and the Lord heard them; from all their afflictions He rescued them.
  2 Timothy 4:17-18 — deliverance testimony parallel: The psalm testifies that the Lord rescued the righteous from all their afflictions. Paul recounts a past rescue and then expects the Lord to rescue him from every evil work and save him into His heavenly kingdom; the comparison preserves the comprehensive language and Paul's expressly heavenly horizon.

Psalms 33:19
The Lord is near to the brokenhearted, and He will save the humble in spirit.
  Isaiah 57:15 — promise parallel: The Lord's saving nearness to the brokenhearted corresponds to Isaiah's Lord Most High giving life to the brokenhearted. The Isaiah text describes His heavenly dwelling while also giving patience and life to the distressed.

Psalms 33:20
Many are the afflictions of the righteous, and He will rescue them from them all.
  2 Timothy 3:11-12 — affliction and rescue parallel: The psalm acknowledges many afflictions of the righteous while promising the Lord's rescue. Paul similarly recounts multiple persecutions and rescue from all of them, immediately adding that godly people will be persecuted.

Psalms 33:21
The Lord guards all their bones; not one of them will be shattered.
  John 19:33-36 — fulfillment wording parallel: John connects Jesus' unbroken legs with Scripture saying that a bone of His will not be broken. This corresponds to the psalm's protection of all the righteous person's bones; the comparison does not identify this as John's exclusive scriptural source.
  Exodus 12:46 — bone protection correspondence: The psalm's statement that no bone will be shattered has a concrete wording counterpart in the Passover rule forbidding the breaking of a bone. The psalm speaks of the righteous, while Exodus regulates the Passover animal.

Psalms 33:23
The Lord will redeem the souls of His servants, and none of those who hope in Him will incur guilt.
  Psalms 129:6-8 — redemption and hope parallel: Both passages join hope in the Lord with redemption: His servants' souls are redeemed here, while Psalm129 calls Israel to hope and promises redemption from all its lawless deeds.

Psalms 34:1
Of David. Judge, Lord, those who wrong me; make war on those who make war on me.
  Exodus 14:14 — divine combat parallel: David asks the Lord to fight those fighting him. Moses' assurance that the Lord will fight for Israel supplies a narrative counterpart to the request for God to undertake His people's battle.

Psalms 34:2
Take hold of weapon and shield, and rise to my aid.
  Isaiah 59:16-18 — divine warrior imagery: The petition for the Lord to take weapon and shield and rise to help corresponds to Isaiah's divine intervention with arm, breastplate, helmet and vengeance. The armor pieces differ, while both portray God acting as an armed defender.

Psalms 34:3
Draw the sword and close the way against those pursuing me; say to my soul, “I am your salvation.”
  Isaiah 12:2 — salvation confession parallel: The requested reassurance, I am your salvation, corresponds to Isaiah's confession that the Lord is his Savior and became his salvation, producing trust rather than fear.

Psalms 34:4
Let those who seek my soul be ashamed and abashed; let those devising evils for me turn backward and be put to shame.
  Psalms 69:3 — literary parallel: Both petitions ask that people seeking the speaker's life be ashamed and that those wishing him evil turn backward in disgrace. The combined sequence gives a close verbal parallel.

Psalms 34:5
Let them become like dust before the wind, with an angel of the Lord pressing them out.
  Psalms 1:4 — poetic imagery parallel: The enemies are compared to dust before the wind here; Psalm1 compares the ungodly to chaff driven away by wind. Both use windblown material for their instability, retaining the distinct dust and chaff terms.

Psalms 34:6
Let their way become darkness and a slippery path, with an angel of the Lord pursuing them.
  Proverbs 4:19 — path imagery parallel: The enemy's way is requested to become dark and slippery. Proverbs describes the ungodly walking dark ways without knowing how they stumble, combining darkness of the path with insecure footing.

Psalms 34:7
For without cause they hid for me the destruction of their trap; without purpose they reproached my soul.
  Lamentations 3:52 — unprovoked persecution parallel: The hidden trap is described as without cause and the reproach as without purpose. Lamentations likewise describes enemies hunting the sufferer like a sparrow without cause, linking unprovoked hostility with capture imagery.

Psalms 34:8
Let a trap they do not know come upon them; let the snare they hid catch them, and into that trap they will fall.
  Psalms 7:16 — retributive trap imagery: The hidden snare is requested to catch its own maker. Psalm7 similarly depicts someone falling into the pit he dug, reversing the intended victim and trap-maker.

Psalms 34:9
