  Malachi 1:11 — God's name honored across the earth: Psalm 47 extends God's praise, like His name, to the earth's limits. Malachi says His name has been glorified among nations from sunrise to sunset, with incense and pure sacrifice in every place. Both give the honor of God's name a worldwide scope, with different forms of worship specified.

Psalms 47:12
Let Mount Zion rejoice; let Judea’s daughters exult because of Thy judgments, Lord.
  Psalms 96:8 — Zion and Judea's daughters rejoicing at judgments: Both verses join Zion's rejoicing with the daughters of Judea exulting because of God's judgments. Psalm 47 voices the desired response and Psalm 96 describes it as heard and enacted; the distinctive combination is shared.

Psalms 47:14
set your hearts upon her strength and divide her citadels, so that you may tell another generation.
  Psalms 77:4-6 — Telling the next generation: After commands concerning the city's strength and citadels, Psalm 47 gives the purpose of telling another generation. Psalm 77 repeatedly directs the transmission of God's deeds and instruction to children and the generation to come. The shared purpose is deliberate intergenerational testimony, with different material being described.

Psalms 47:15
For this is God, our God for ever and for age upon age; He will shepherd us through the ages.
  Psalms 22:1 — God shepherding His people: Psalm 47 identifies the community's God forever and says He will shepherd them through the ages. Psalm 22 confesses that the Lord shepherds the individual speaker. Both assign shepherding to God; the communal duration is not rewritten as shepherding only until death.

Psalms 48:3
both the earthborn and the sons of men, rich and poor together.
  Proverbs 22:2 — Rich and poor considered together: Psalm 48 explicitly includes rich and poor together in its audience for teaching on wealth and mortality. Proverbs brings rich and poor together and says the Lord made both. The paired economic groups are jointly placed under a condition beyond their wealth, with mortality and common creation remaining different emphases.

Psalms 48:5
I will incline my ear to a parable; with a psaltery I will open my riddle.
  Psalms 77:2 — Parables and riddles as modes of instruction: Psalm 48 says the speaker will incline his ear to a parable and open a riddle with a psaltery. Psalm 77 says he will open his mouth in parables and utter riddles from the beginning. The two named forms of enigmatic instruction correspond, without making listening and speaking the same action.

Psalms 48:7
Those trusting in their strength and boasting in the abundance of their wealth,
  Jeremiah 9:22 — Confidence in strength and wealth: The psalm identifies those trusting their strength and boasting in abundant wealth. Jeremiah forbids the strong from boasting in strength and the rich from boasting in wealth. Both pair these particular grounds of human confidence in a critical setting.
  1 Timothy 6:17 — Wealth as an uncertain ground of confidence: Psalm 48 introduces people boasting in wealth before discussing redemption prices and the passing of possessions. Timothy is told to forbid the rich from arrogance and hope in uncertain wealth, directing hope to God. The connection concerns misplaced confidence, not a claim that every wealthy person has the same conduct.

Psalms 48:8
A brother does not redeem; will a person redeem? He will not give God his atonement price
  Mark 8:36-37 — Questions of redemption and life's exchange price: Psalm 48 says a brother does not redeem, asks whether a person will redeem and says he will not give God his atonement price, continuing with the soul's redemption price in the next unit. Jesus asks what gain of the world benefits someone who forfeits life and what a person can give in exchange for life. Both question material means of securing life without resolving Psalm 48:10's difficult syntax.

Psalms 48:9
and the price of the redemption of his soul.
  Mark 8:37 — The soul's redemption price and exchange for life: This native continuation unit explicitly names the price of the redemption of a person's soul, continuing the atonement-price statement in verse 8. Jesus asks what a person can give in exchange for his life. The particular price-for-life comparison is supported without turning verse 9 into an independent sentence or resolving verse 10's difficult continuation.

Psalms 48:11
The foolish and senseless will perish together and leave their wealth to strangers.
  Ecclesiastes 2:16-19 — Wise and foolish dying while wealth passes to others: Psalm 48 says foolish and senseless people perish and leave wealth to strangers after mentioning the wise dying. Ecclesiastes asks how the wise dies with the fool and laments leaving toil to a successor whose wisdom is unknown. Both join common mortality with loss of control over accumulated goods.

Psalms 48:12
Their tombs are their houses for ever, their dwellings from generation to generation; they invoked their names upon their lands.
  Job 17:13 — The grave or Hades described as a house: Psalm 48 calls tombs the enduring houses of those who invoked their names upon their lands. Job conditionally describes Hades as his house and gloom as his bedding. Both use domestic lodging for death's dwelling, while Job's personal lament is distinct from the psalm's observation about the wealthy.

Psalms 48:13
A person, though in honor, did not understand; he was compared to senseless beasts and made like them.
  Ecclesiastes 3:18-20 — Human beings compared with beasts: The psalm says the honored human who did not understand became like senseless beasts. Ecclesiastes compares humans and beasts through their shared death, breath and return to dust. The comparison is explicit in both, but the psalm emphasizes failed understanding rather than merely creaturely mortality.

Psalms 48:15
Like sheep they were placed in Hades; death shepherds them. The upright will rule over them in the morning, and their help will grow old in Hades, away from their glory.
  Psalms 22:1-4 — Contrasting shepherds in the presence of death: Psalm 48 pictures people as sheep placed in Hades with death shepherding them. Psalm 22 names the Lord as shepherd and refuses fear while walking in death's shadow because He is present. The shepherd imagery and death setting create a precise contrast between the guides; no identical afterlife account is imposed.

Psalms 48:16
But God will redeem my soul from Hades’ hand when He receives me. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 15:10 — Hope that God will not leave the soul to Hades: Psalm 48 expects God to redeem the soul from Hades' hand when He receives the speaker. Psalm 15 says God will not abandon the speaker's soul to Hades. Both contrast Hades with God's saving action for the soul, with redemption and non-abandonment expressed differently.
  Hosea 13:14 — Divine rescue from Hades' hand: The psalm anticipates God redeeming the soul from Hades' hand. Hosea gives God's promise to rescue people from Hades' hand and redeem them from death. The unusual combination of redemption and Hades' hand corresponds, with the individual and plural recipients kept distinct.

Psalms 48:18
For when he dies he will not take everything, nor will his glory descend with him.
  1 Timothy 6:7 — Limits on carrying possessions beyond life: Psalm 48 says the dying person will not take everything and his glory will not descend with him. Timothy states that people brought nothing into the world and cannot carry anything out. Both limit the retention of possessions beyond life; the psalm's 'not everything' is not silently changed into the letter's 'anything.'

Psalms 48:20
He will enter as far as the generation of his fathers; he will never see light.
  Job 10:21-22 — Death pictured as enduring darkness: Psalm 48 says the person will enter the generation of his fathers and never see light. Job anticipates going without return to a land of everlasting darkness with no brightness. Both portray departure into death as a lasting loss of light, without identifying the individual experiences.

Psalms 48:21
A person, though in honor, did not understand; he was compared to senseless beasts and made like them.
  Psalms 48:13 — same-chapter refrain of uncomprehending human honor: The final verse repeats the statement that the honored human failed to understand and became like senseless beasts. Its repetition after the discussion of wealth and death closes the warning with the same judgment voiced earlier.

Psalms 49:1
A psalm of Asaph. The God of gods, the Lord, spoke and summoned the earth from the sun’s risings to its settings.
  Psalms 112:3 — The span from sunrise to sunset: Psalm 49 says God spoke and called the earth from the sun's risings to its settings. Psalm 112 uses the same span to command praise of the Lord's name. The geographic or daily formula is shared, with divine summons and human praise remaining different actions.

Psalms 49:3
our God, and He will not keep silent; fire will burn before Him, and around Him is a mighty tempest.
  Psalms 96:3 — Fire before God: Psalm 49 describes fire burning before the coming God and a great tempest around Him. Psalm 96 describes fire going ahead before Him and setting enemies aflame. Both place fire before God in divine manifestation; the enemy-burning object belongs expressly to Psalm 96.

Psalms 49:4
He will summon heaven above and the earth, to judge His people.
  Deuteronomy 31:28 — Heaven and earth summoned for testimony concerning the people: Psalm 49 calls heaven above and earth to the judging of God's people. Moses gathers the people's officers and calls heaven and earth as witnesses concerning them. The cosmic summons has a judicial setting in both, with God and Moses as different speakers.

Psalms 49:5
Gather His holy ones to Him, those who establish His covenant over sacrifices.
  Exodus 24:5-8 — Covenant established over sacrifices: Psalm 49 calls for God's holy ones who establish His covenant over sacrifices to be gathered. Exodus narrates burnt offerings and calves, the reading of the covenant book, and covenant blood sprinkled on the people. It supplies a concrete covenant-and-sacrifice comparison without claiming the psalm's gathering is the same ceremony.

Psalms 49:6
The heavens will announce His righteousness, for God is judge. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 96:6 — Heavens declaring God's righteousness: Psalm 49 says the heavens will announce His righteousness because God is judge. Psalm 96 says the heavens announced His righteousness and all peoples saw His glory. The distinctive heavenly proclamation is shared, with future and completed forms preserved.

Psalms 49:7
“Hear, My people, and I will speak unto you; Israel, I testify unto you. I am God, your God.
  Exodus 20:2 — God's covenant self-identification to Israel: God addresses Israel with 'I am God, your God' while testifying to it. The commandments open with the Lord identifying Himself as Israel's God who brought it from Egypt. The repeated self-identification frames divine address to the same covenant people without adding the exodus clause to the psalm.

Psalms 49:11
I know all the birds of heaven, and the field’s beauty is with Me.
  Matthew 10:29 — Divine knowledge and oversight of birds: God says He knows all heaven's birds in the psalm. Jesus says no sparrow falls to the ground apart from the Father. Both explicitly extend divine attention to birds, with the psalm asserting knowledge and the Gospel specifying their falling under the Father's oversight.

Psalms 49:12
If I become hungry, I will certainly not tell you, for the inhabited world and its fullness are Mine.
  Psalms 23:1 — The world and its fullness belong to God: Psalm 49 explains that God would not tell humans if hungry because the inhabited world and its fullness are His. Psalm 23 declares that the earth, its fullness, the inhabited world and its inhabitants belong to the Lord. The ownership statement is the particular basis shared by the two passages.

Psalms 49:13
Will I eat bulls’ flesh or drink goats’ blood?
  Acts 17:25 — God is not sustained by human offerings: The psalm rhetorically asks whether God will eat bulls' flesh or drink goats' blood. Paul says God is not served by human hands as though needing anything, since He supplies life and everything. Both reject treating divine life as dependent on human provision, with sacrificial food specified only in the psalm.

Psalms 49:14
Sacrifice a sacrifice of praise to God and pay your vows to the Most High.
  Hebrews 13:15 — A sacrifice of praise: The psalm commands a praising sacrifice to God as well as repayment of vows. Hebrews calls for a continual sacrifice of praise to God and explains it as lips confessing His name. The praise offering corresponds directly; Hebrews's verse does not restate the vow command.

Psalms 49:15
Call on Me in the day of affliction; I will rescue you, and you will glorify Me.” Diapsalma.
  Psalms 106:6-8 — Calling in distress, rescue and grateful acknowledgment: God invites a call in affliction, promises rescue and says the rescued person will glorify Him. Psalm 106 recounts people crying in affliction and being rescued, then calls them to acknowledge God's mercies and wonders. The sequence links appeal, divine rescue and the human response of praise.

Psalms 49:16
But to the sinner God said, “Why do you recount My decrees and take up My covenant through your mouth?
  Romans 2:17-23 — Religious instruction contradicted by conduct: God asks a sinner why he recites divine decrees and carries the covenant in his mouth; the following rebuke includes running with a thief and sharing a portion with adulterers. Romans asks whether one who teaches against theft and adultery practices them. Both challenge a mismatch between professed instruction and conduct, with association and the questioned acts expressed differently.

Psalms 49:17
But you hated instruction and cast My words behind you.
  Nehemiah 9:26 — Casting God's words behind the body: The psalm accuses the sinner of hating instruction and casting God's words behind him. The prayer recorded in Nehemiah says the people cast God's law behind their bodies while withdrawing from Him. The unusual bodily image expresses deliberate rejection of divine instruction in both.

Psalms 49:18
If you saw a thief, you would run along with him, and you would place your portion with adulterers.
  Proverbs 1:10-15 — Joining those who steal: The psalm rebukes someone who runs with a thief and also has a portion with adulterers. Proverbs depicts people inviting the son to join in seizing property and sharing a common purse, then forbids walking with them. The concrete comparison is participation with thieves; the adultery clause is not transferred into Proverbs's scene.

Psalms 49:19
Your mouth multiplied wickedness, and your tongue was weaving deception.
  Psalms 33:14 — Evil and deceitful speech: Psalm 49 accuses the mouth of multiplying wickedness and the tongue of weaving deceit. Psalm 33 commands restraint of the tongue from evil and lips from deceit. The paired evils and organs of speech correspond in accusation and admonition.

Psalms 49:20
Sitting, you would speak against your brother, and against your mother’s son you would place a stumbling block.
  Psalms 68:9 — Hostility involving brothers and a mother's sons: Psalm 49 describes speech against one's brother and a stumbling block against one's mother's son. Psalm 68 describes the speaker estranged from brothers and a foreigner to his mother's sons. Both use the paired kinship expressions for fractured family relations, without equating slander with estrangement or identifying the speakers.

Psalms 49:21
These things you did, and I kept silent; you conceived the lawless thought that I would be like you. I will rebuke you and set it before your face.
  Isaiah 57:11-12 — Divine forbearance followed by exposure of wrongdoing: Psalm 49 says God's silence was mistaken for likeness to the sinner, before He promises rebuke and exposure. Isaiah says God sees and overlooks the addressee, who did not fear Him, then promises to proclaim His righteousness and the person's evils. Both join withheld immediate response with disregard and coming disclosure, using different descriptions of that response.

Psalms 49:22
Understand these things, you who forget God, lest He seize you and there be no rescuer.
  Deuteronomy 32:39 — No rescuer from divine seizure: The psalm warns those forgetting God to understand lest He seize them with no one to rescue. Deuteronomy declares God's power to kill, make alive, wound and heal, and that no one can deliver from His hands. The threat's lack of an alternative deliverer is explicit in both.

Psalms 49:23
A sacrifice of praise will glorify Me, and there is a way by which I will show him God’s salvation.”
  Psalms 49:14 — same-chapter return to the praising sacrifice: The closing verse says a praising sacrifice glorifies God and connects the way with divine salvation. It returns to the command to offer a praising sacrifice in verse 14, enclosing the intervening rebuke with the poem's positive account of worship.

Psalms 50:2
when Nathan the prophet came to him, when he had gone in to Bathsheba.
  2 Samuel 12:1 — Nathan's visit named in the Psalm heading: The heading places the psalm when Nathan the prophet came to David after the Bathsheba encounter. Samuel specifically narrates the Lord sending Nathan to David and Nathan coming in to him. The shared named visit is grounded in the surrounding narrative of David's wrongdoing.
  2 Samuel 11:3-4 — The Bathsheba encounter named in the heading: The heading explicitly situates Nathan's visit after David went in to Bathsheba. Samuel identifies Bathsheba as Uriah's wife and narrates David taking her and lying with her. The comparison follows the heading's named event rather than a general association with David.

Psalms 50:3
Have mercy on me, God, according to Thy great mercy, and according to the abundance of Thy compassions blot out my lawless deed.
  Isaiah 43:25 — God wiping out lawless acts: Psalm 50 appeals to divine mercy and compassion for the blotting out of the speaker's lawless deed. Isaiah presents God as the One who wipes out lawless acts and will not remember. The erasure of wrongdoing corresponds as requested mercy and divine declaration.

Psalms 50:4
Wash me more and more from my lawlessness, and cleanse me from my sin.
  Isaiah 1:16 — Washing and cleansing from evil: The psalm asks God to wash the speaker from lawlessness and cleanse him from sin. Isaiah commands people to wash themselves, become clean and remove evils. Both use washing and cleansing for moral evil, while the petition to God and command to the people have different immediate agents.

Psalms 50:6
Against Thee alone I sinned and did evil before Thee, so that Thou mayest be justified in Thy words and prevail when Thou art judged.
  Romans 3:4 — Quotation about God justified in His words: Romans explicitly cites the psalm's statement that God may be justified in His words and prevail when He is judged. Paul uses it to uphold God's truth despite human unfaithfulness. Both retain God as the one described as being judged, rather than silently reversing that wording.

Psalms 50:7
For behold, I was conceived in lawless deeds, and in sins my mother conceived me.
  Job 14:4-5 — Impurity associated with life's beginning: The psalmist speaks of being conceived in lawless deeds and sins. Job asks who can be clean from filth and answers no one, even if life is one day. Both place impurity at the outset of human life; neither comparison specifies an unnamed act by the psalmist's mother or settles a theory of inherited guilt.

Psalms 50:8
For behold, Thou didst love truth; Thou madest known to me the unseen and hidden things of Thy wisdom.
  1 Corinthians 2:7-10 — Hidden divine wisdom made known: The psalm says God revealed unseen and hidden things of His wisdom to the speaker. Paul describes God's hidden wisdom and says God revealed the prepared things through the Spirit. Concealed wisdom becomes known by divine disclosure in both; the psalm is not said to name Paul's particular proclamation.

Psalms 50:9
Thou wilt sprinkle me with hyssop, and I will be cleansed; Thou wilt wash me, and I will be whitened beyond snow.
  Leviticus 14:4-7 — Hyssop and sprinkling for cleansing: Psalm 50 anticipates sprinkling with hyssop and becoming clean. Leviticus includes hyssop in the cleansing rite and commands sprinkling the person seven times. The distinctive material and cleansing action correspond, without asserting that the penitent's sin is a diagnosis of leprosy.
  Isaiah 1:18 — Sins cleansed to snow's whiteness: The psalm anticipates washing and becoming whiter than snow. Isaiah promises to whiten purple-red sins like snow and scarlet sins like wool. Both use snow's whiteness for removal of moral stain, while the psalm's comparative 'whiter' differs from Isaiah's simile.

Psalms 50:10
Thou wilt make me hear exultation and gladness; humbled bones will exult.
  Psalms 34:9-10 — Joy and bones expressing praise: Psalm 50 anticipates hearing exultation and gladness, and humbled bones will exult. Psalm 34 anticipates the soul's exultation and all the bones asking who is like the rescuing Lord. Both give the bones a voice or action within joyful praise, with distinct statements about what the bones do.

Psalms 50:11
Turn Thy face away from my sins, and blot out all my lawless deeds.
  Isaiah 38:17 — All sins moved out of view: The psalm asks God to turn His face away from sins and blot out all lawless acts. Hezekiah's prayer says God cast all the speaker's sins behind him. Both express removal of all wrongdoing through spatial or erasure language; the target's actual 'behind me' is not changed to 'behind God.'

Psalms 50:12
Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew an upright spirit in my inward parts.
  Ezekiel 36:25-27 — Cleansing, a new heart and renewed spirit: The psalm asks God to create a clean heart and renew an upright spirit within the speaker. Ezekiel promises cleansing, a new heart and new spirit, and also God's own Spirit placed within the people. Both locate inward renewal in divine action, while the psalm's upright spirit is not simply equated with every spirit expression in Ezekiel.

Psalms 50:13
Do not cast me away from Thy face, and do not remove Thy holy Spirit from me.
  1 Samuel 16:14 — The feared removal of God's Spirit: Psalm 50 asks God not to remove His holy Spirit from the speaker. Samuel describes the Lord's Spirit departing from Saul. The narrative gives a specific contrast to the prayer against removal; it does not identify Saul as this psalm's speaker.

Psalms 50:14
Restore to me the exultation of Thy salvation, and strengthen me with a governing spirit.
  Psalms 12:6 — Joy in divine salvation: Psalm 50 asks restoration of the exultation of God's salvation. Psalm 12 anticipates the heart exulting in God's salvation after hope in mercy. The particular joy is tied to salvation in both; the request for restoration and the anticipated response remain distinct.

Psalms 50:15
I will teach the lawless Thy ways, and the ungodly will turn back unto Thee.
  Psalms 24:8-9 — Teaching God's way to those needing correction: Psalm 50 anticipates teaching God's ways to the lawless so the ungodly turn back to Him. Psalm 24 says the Lord gives sinners instruction in the way and teaches the meek His ways. Both connect divine ways and instruction directed toward those needing guidance, with the penitent and God as different teachers.

Psalms 50:16
Rescue me from bloodshed, God, God of my salvation; my tongue will exult in Thy righteousness.
  2 Samuel 12:9 — Bloodshed in the Nathan and David context: Psalm 50 asks rescue from bloodshed, and its heading names Nathan's visit after Bathsheba. In that visit Nathan specifically accuses David of killing Uriah through the sons of Ammon. The narrative supplies concrete bloodshed within the named context; the psalm's request itself does not name Uriah or specify the exact sense of deliverance.

Psalms 50:17
Lord, Thou wilt open my lips, and my mouth will announce Thy praise.
  Ezekiel 3:27 — God opening the mouth for speech: The psalm says God will open the speaker's lips and the mouth will announce His praise. God tells Ezekiel He will open his mouth so that he speaks a divine message. Both place the opening of speech organs under divine agency, while praise and prophetic address remain distinct utterances.

Psalms 50:18
For if Thou hadst desired sacrifice, I would have given it; Thou wilt not delight in burnt offerings.
  Psalms 39:7 — Sacrifices and burnt offerings not desired: Psalm 50 says a desired sacrifice would have been given, but God will not delight in burnt offerings. Psalm 39 says God did not desire sacrifice and offering or request burnt and sin offerings. The explicit negative statements concerning these offerings correspond; Psalm 50's later restored offerings are still read in context.
  1 Samuel 15:22 — Ritual offering measured against another response to God: The psalm denies divine delight in burnt offerings before identifying a broken spirit and humbled heart as sacrifice. Samuel asks whether burnt offerings and sacrifices are as desirable as listening, and values obedience above sacrifice. Both refuse to treat ritual offerings as sufficient, while humility and obedience are distinct positive responses.

Psalms 50:19
A sacrifice to God is a broken spirit; a broken and humbled heart God will not despise.
  Isaiah 57:15 — God's favorable regard for the brokenhearted: Psalm 50 calls a broken spirit God's sacrifice and says He will not despise a broken and humbled heart. Isaiah describes the Most High giving patience to the fainthearted and life to the brokenhearted. Both affirm divine regard for brokenhearted people, without changing Isaiah's resting among holy ones into a different dwelling claim.

Psalms 50:20
Do good to Zion, Lord, in Thy good pleasure, and let Jerusalem’s walls be built.
  Nehemiah 2:17-18 — Jerusalem's wall and God's favorable action: The psalm asks God to do good to Zion and asks that Jerusalem's walls be built. Nehemiah calls for rebuilding Jerusalem's wall and reports God's good hand upon him. The particular city, wall-building and favorable divine action correspond, without assigning the psalm's request to Nehemiah's historical occasion.

Psalms 50:21
Then Thou wilt delight in a sacrifice of righteousness, an offering and burnt offerings; then they will bring calves upon Thine altar.
  Psalms 4:6 — A sacrifice of righteousness: Psalm 50 anticipates God's delight in a sacrifice of righteousness after the prayer for Zion and Jerusalem. Psalm 4 commands offering a sacrifice of righteousness and hoping in the Lord. The specific description of acceptable sacrifice connects the passages and qualifies any reading of Psalm 50 as rejecting every offering without distinction.

Psalms 51:2
when Doeg the Idumean came and reported to Saul, and said to him, “David came to Abimelech’s house.”
  1 Samuel 22:9-10 — Doeg's report about David and Abimelech: The heading names Doeg reporting to Saul that David came to Abimelech's house. Samuel records Doeg reporting Jesse's son's arrival with Abimelech at Nomba. The named report corresponds closely, while the heading calls Doeg Idumean and Samuel calls him Syrian; those ethnic descriptions are not harmonized.

Psalms 51:3
Why do you boast in wickedness, mighty one, in lawlessness all day long?
  Psalms 93:3-4 — Boasting by workers of lawlessness: The accusation of boasting in wickedness and lawlessness all day long corresponds to the question about sinners boasting and workers of lawlessness speaking injustice. Both challenge the wrongdoers' boastful speech rather than merely describing their power.

Psalms 51:4
Your tongue devised injustice; like a sharpened razor you worked deceit.
  Psalms 63:4 — The tongue as a sharpened weapon: The source joins a tongue devising injustice with deceit worked like a sharpened razor; Psalm 63 describes tongues sharpened like a sword. The particular comparison joins harmful speech and cutting blades, with razor and sword remaining different weapons.

Psalms 51:5
You loved wickedness more than goodness, injustice more than speaking righteousness. Diapsalma.
  John 3:19-20 — Loving evil rather than good: The preference for wickedness over goodness corresponds to John's account of people loving darkness more than light because their deeds are evil. The connection concerns the stated preference for evil, without identifying the psalm's addressee with a particular person in John's account.

Psalms 51:7
Therefore God will pull you down completely; may He pluck you out and remove you from your dwelling, and your root from the land of the living. Diapsalma.
  Proverbs 12:3 — Uprooted wickedness and enduring righteous roots: The petition that God remove the wrongdoer's root from the land of the living contrasts with the assurance that righteous roots will not be uprooted. The common root-and-uprooting image gives opposite outcomes for the wicked and righteous.

Psalms 51:8
The righteous will see and fear; they will laugh at him and say:
  Psalms 57:11 — The righteous observe the wrongdoer's judgment: The righteous seeing, fearing and laughing at the wrongdoer's overthrow compares with the righteous rejoicing when he sees vengeance in Psalm 57. The responses differ, and the latter's washing hands in sinners' blood is not imported into this source.

Psalms 51:9
“Look at the person who did not make God his helper, but hoped in the abundance of his wealth and became strong in his vanity.”
  1 Timothy 6:17 — Trust in abundant wealth instead of God: The person who failed to make God his helper and hoped in abundant wealth exemplifies the misplaced hope against which 1 Timothy warns the wealthy. The instruction expressly contrasts hope in uncertain wealth with hope in God.

Psalms 51:10
But I am like a fruitful olive tree in God’s house; I hoped in God’s mercy for ever and for age upon age.
  Psalms 91:13-14 — A flourishing tree in God's house: The speaker's fruitful olive tree in God's house corresponds to the righteous trees planted in the Lord's house and flourishing in His courts. The olive remains distinct from the palm and cedar named in Psalm 91.

Psalms 52:1
For the end. Concerning Maeleth. Of understanding, of David.
  Psalms 87:1 — The Maeleth designation in headings: Both headings preserve the designation Maeleth. Psalm 87 adds answering and names Heman the Israelite; the shared designation is retained without assigning it an unprovided musical meaning or equating the two headings in full.

Psalms 52:2
A fool said in his heart, “There is no God.” They became corrupt and abominable in lawless deeds; there is no one doing good.
  Psalms 13:1 — Parallel fool and corruption declaration: The fool's denial of God and the account of corrupt, abominable conduct correspond to the opening running text of Psalm 13. The complete target also contains its heading; this is a comparison of the actual declaration, not a renumbering of that composite unit.

Psalms 52:3
God bent down from heaven toward the sons of men, to see whether anyone understands or seeks God.
  Psalms 13:2 — Parallel divine inspection for understanding: The source says God bent down from heaven to see whether anyone understands or seeks God; Psalm 13 says the Lord looked down for that purpose. The inspection and its question correspond, while bent down and looked down, and God and Lord, remain the respective formulations.

Psalms 52:4
All turned aside; together they became useless; there is no one doing good, not a single one.
  Psalms 13:3 — Parallel universal departure and absence of good: Both declarations say all turned aside and became useless. The source then says no one is doing good, while the opening of the complete expanded Psalm 13:3 unit says none shows kindness. The target's additional throat, tongue, feet and fear clauses remain part of that unit and are not attributed to this source verse.
  Romans 3:12 — The doing-good declaration and Romans' kindness wording: Romans' scriptural citation includes all turning aside and becoming useless, then says no one shows kindness, corresponding to the psalm's no one doing good. Psalm 13 also contains the declaration, so this connection does not claim that Psalm 52 is the exclusive source of Romans' wording.

Psalms 52:5
Will all who work lawlessness not learn? Those eating my people like the eating of bread did not call on God.
  Psalms 13:4 — Parallel devouring of the people as bread: Both passages question workers of lawlessness who devour the people as a meal of bread and fail to call on God. The distinctive meal-of-bread image and the accompanying failure to invoke God establish the literary correspondence.

Psalms 52:6
There they will fear greatly where there was no fear, for God scattered the bones of people-pleasers. They were put to shame, for God despised them.
  Psalms 13:5 — Parallel fear where there is no fear, with different explanations: The prospect of fear where there is no fear corresponds to Psalm 13's report of cowering there. This source explains the result through scattered bones of people-pleasers and God's rejection, while Psalm 13 says God is with a righteous generation; the explanations and tense are preserved distinctly.

Psalms 52:7
Who will give Israel’s salvation from Zion? When the Lord turns back His people’s captivity, Jacob will exult and Israel will rejoice.
  Psalms 13:7 — Parallel salvation from Zion and restored captivity: The question about Israel's salvation from Zion and the rejoicing of Jacob and Israel when captivity is turned back correspond directly. The passage is retained as a literary parallel without assigning either declaration to a particular historical restoration.

Psalms 53:2
When the Ziphites came and said to Saul, “Behold, is David not hidden among us?”
  1 Samuel 23:19-20 — The Ziphites' question to Saul about hidden David: The heading's question about David being hidden among the Ziphites closely corresponds to their question to Saul in 1 Samuel 23. The named speakers, addressee and report supply the connection; the later Ziphite report in chapter 26 does not establish a unique date for the heading.

Psalms 53:5
For outsiders rose against me, and powerful men sought my soul; they did not set God before themselves. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 85:14 — Attackers who do not set God before themselves: Both passages describe attackers rising against the speaker, strong men seeking his soul, and their failure to set God before themselves. The source calls them outsiders while Psalm 85 calls them lawless people; the shared sequence is not an identification of an individual enemy.

Psalms 53:7
He will turn the evils back upon my enemies; in Thy truth, utterly destroy them.
  Psalms 7:17 — Evil returning upon its perpetrators: The expectation that evils will be turned back upon enemies corresponds to the wrongdoer's trouble returning upon his own head. The comparison concerns the reversal of inflicted evil, with the source's further petition for destruction preserved separately.

Psalms 53:8
I will sacrifice unto Thee willingly; I will give thanks to Thy name, Lord, for it is good.
  Psalms 115:8 — Sacrifice joined to thanksgiving: The source promises willing sacrifice and thanksgiving to God's good name; Psalm 115 explicitly promises a sacrifice of praise. Both join sacrificial offering with praise, without turning the source's willing offering into a specified mandatory rite.

Psalms 53:9
For Thou didst rescue me from every affliction, and my eye has looked upon my enemies.
  1 Samuel 26:24 — Rescue from every affliction: The psalm reports rescue from every affliction, while David's speech in Samuel asks for shelter and expresses the assurance that the Lord will rescue him from every affliction. The distinctive formula connects testimony with future assurance without dating the psalm to that encounter.

Psalms 54:6
Fear and trembling came upon me, and darkness covered me.
  Genesis 15:12 — Fear accompanied by darkness: The speaker's fear, trembling and covering darkness compare with the great dark fear falling on Abram during his sunset trance. The source's covering darkness and Genesis' description of a dark fear remain distinct formulations in different circumstances.

Psalms 54:7
I said, “Who will give me wings like a dove’s, so that I may fly away and rest?”
  Isaiah 60:8 — Flying like a dove: The wish for a dove's wings to fly away and rest compares with Isaiah's question about those flying like doves with their young. The bird-flight image is shared, but Isaiah's arriving company is not identified with the psalmist's desired escape.

Psalms 54:8
Behold, I went far away in flight and lodged in the wilderness. Diapsalma.
  Jeremiah 9:1 — Retreat to a remote wilderness lodging: The speaker's distant flight and lodging in the wilderness correspond to Jeremiah's wish for a remote wilderness station where he could leave his faithless people. One reports retreat and the other desires it; no common physical lodging is asserted.

Psalms 54:9
I waited for the one saving me from faintheartedness and tempest.
  Psalms 106:26-30 — Rescue from inward distress and a storm: The speaker reports waiting for rescue from faintheartedness and tempest. Psalm 106 recounts mariners whose souls melted in distress before they cried to God and He quieted the storm. The combined distress-and-storm rescue provides the comparison without making this speaker one of those sailors.

Psalms 54:10
Drown them, Lord, and divide their tongues, for I saw lawlessness and contradiction in the city.
  Genesis 11:7-9 — Divided tongues in a troubled city: The appeal to divide their tongues amid the city's lawlessness corresponds to God's confounding languages and interrupting the city-building project in Genesis. The link concerns divine disruption of human speech; it does not identify the psalm's city as Babel.

Psalms 54:12
and interest-taking and deceit did not depart from its squares.
  Ezekiel 22:12 — Interest-taking within a corrupt city: The source places interest-taking and deceit in the city's squares; Ezekiel accuses his addressed city of taking interest and increase amid oppression. The particular economic wrong in an urban indictment supplies the connection.

Psalms 54:14
But you, a man of like soul, my leader and my acquaintance,
  Micah 7:5 — A trusted companion and leader becomes unsafe: The address to a man of like soul, the speaker's leader and acquaintance, stands within the complaint about intimate betrayal. Micah warns against confidence in friends and hope in leaders. The shared intimate and leadership roles support the comparison without naming this companion.

Psalms 54:15
you who made food sweet together with me: in God’s house we walked in concord.
  Psalms 40:10 — Shared food and violated fellowship: The recollection of making food sweet together and walking in concord in God's house corresponds to the trusted man who ate the speaker's loaves and magnified treachery in Psalm 40. The source's food is preserved as food, rather than replaced with a familiar wording about sweet counsel.

Psalms 54:16
Let death come upon them, and let them descend alive into Hades, for wicked things are in their sojournings, within them.
  Numbers 16:30-33 — Going down alive to Hades: The appeal that the wicked descend alive to Hades uses the distinctive fate narrated of Korah's company when the earth swallowed them. The parallel concerns that extraordinary living descent, not an identification of the psalm's enemies as the same people.

Psalms 54:18
Evening, morning and noon I will recount; I will proclaim, and He will hear my voice.
  Daniel 6:11 — Three daily occasions of prayer: The source names evening, morning and noon for recounting and proclaiming before God. Daniel continues making supplication three times a day despite the royal restriction. The threefold daily practice corresponds, but Daniel's target verse does not name the psalm's three hours.

Psalms 54:20
God will hear and humble them, He who exists before the ages. Diapsalma. For they have no exchange, and they did not fear God.
  Psalms 89:2 — God existing before the ages: The description of God as existing before the ages corresponds to the prayer addressing Him as God before the mountains and from age to age. The connection is the explicit assertion of God's existence beyond created time, not an explanation of the source's separate no-exchange clause.

Psalms 54:21
He stretched out his hand in making repayment; they profaned his covenant.
  Psalms 88:35 — Profaning a covenant contrasted with God's pledge: The source says they profaned his covenant; Psalm 88 records God's pledge not to profane His covenant. The repeated covenant-profanation expression supports a contrast, without resolving the source's pronouns into the parties of Psalm 88's covenant.

Psalms 54:22
They were divided by the anger of his face, and his heart drew near; his words became softer than oil, yet they are missiles.
  Proverbs 5:3-4 — Oily pleasantness and sharp harm: The source calls words softer than oil yet missiles. Proverbs moves from honey-dripping lips and the temporary oiling of the listener's throat to what proves bitter and sharper than a blade. Both juxtapose pleasant oral imagery with weapon-like harm, while preserving whose throat appears in Proverbs.

Psalms 54:23
Cast your care upon the Lord, and He will nourish you; He will never give the righteous a shaking.
  1 Peter 5:7 — Casting one's care upon God: The instruction to cast one's care upon the Lord corresponds closely to 1 Peter's instruction to cast all anxiety upon Him because He cares. The source emphasizes nourishment and the righteous person's stability, while Peter explicitly gives God's care as the reason.

Psalms 54:24
But Thou, God, wilt bring them down into a well of corruption; men of bloodshed and deceit shall certainly not live out half their days. But I will hope in Thee, Lord.
  Proverbs 10:27 — The shortened life of the wicked: The declaration that men of blood and deceit will not live half their days corresponds to Proverbs' contrast between the fear of the Lord adding days and impious years becoming few. Proverbs does not specify the source's fraction of half a lifetime.

Psalms 55:2
Have mercy on me, Lord, for a man trampled me; fighting all day, he afflicted me.
  Psalms 55:3 — Trampling and persistent fighting (same-chapter): The source joins a man's trampling with his all-day fighting and afflicting the speaker. Verse 3 joins enemies' all-day trampling with many fighters from a height. This same-chapter recurrence varies the singular and plural attackers and the placement of the all-day modifier.

Psalms 55:3
My enemies trampled me all day, for many are those fighting me from a height.
  Psalms 55:2 — Trampling and persistent fighting (same-chapter): The source's enemies trampled all day and are many fighters from a height; the preceding verse has one man trampling, fighting all day and afflicting the speaker. This same-chapter recurrence preserves the different subjects and the placement of the all-day modifier.

Psalms 55:4
By day I will fear, but I will hope in Thee.
  Psalms 55:12 — Fear and hope answered by confidence (same-chapter): This verse says that by day the speaker will fear but will hope in God; verse 12 joins hope in God with the declaration that he will not fear. The same-chapter contrast preserves the positive fear statement here rather than silently negating it.

Psalms 55:5
In God I will praise my words all day. In God I have hoped; I will not fear. What will flesh do to me?
  Hebrews 13:6 — Fearlessness and the question of human harm: The source's hope in God, refusal to fear and question about what flesh can do correspond to Hebrews' cited confidence in the Lord as helper, refusal to fear and question about what a person will do. The comparison concerns that closing confidence, while the source's opening phrase remains my words.

Psalms 55:7
They will sojourn and conceal themselves; they will watch my heel, just as they waited for my soul.
  Genesis 3:15 — Watching for the heel: The concealed enemies watching the speaker's heel compare with God's statement that the serpent will watch for the woman's seed's heel. The uncommon heel-watching expression supplies the link; it does not identify these human enemies as the serpent.

Psalms 55:9
I declared my life unto Thee; Thou didst set my tears before Thee, as also in Thy promise.
  2 Kings 20:5 — Tears set before and seen by God: The source says God set the speaker's tears before Himself; God's message to Hezekiah says He has seen his tears. Both explicitly place a distressed person's tears before divine notice, without introducing a bottle or equating the two sufferers.

Psalms 55:10
My enemies will turn backward on whatever day I call upon Thee. Behold, I know that Thou art my God.
  Psalms 17:41 — Enemies turned backward by God: The expectation that enemies will turn backward on the day the speaker calls on God corresponds to Psalm 17's report that God made enemies turn their backs. Future expectation and reported victory remain distinct; no common battle is asserted.

Psalms 55:12
In God I have hoped; I will not fear. What will a human being do to me?
  Hebrews 13:6 — Trust without fear of a human being: The source's hope in God, refusal to fear and question about what a human can do correspond closely to the confidence cited in Hebrews. The connection is the shared fear-and-human-harm formula, without claiming this psalm is Hebrews' exclusive quotation source.

Psalms 55:13
Within me, God, are the vows of praise that I will pay unto Thee,
  Deuteronomy 23:22-24 — Paying vows made to God: The speaker's vows of praise and promise to pay them correspond to Deuteronomy's requirement not to delay payment of a vow to the Lord. The law also distinguishes not making a vow from making one and then failing to fulfill it.

Psalms 55:14
because Thou didst rescue my soul from death and my feet from slipping, that I may be pleasing before God in the light of the living.
  Psalms 114:8-9 — Soul rescued from death and feet from slipping: Both passages combine rescue of the soul from death and feet from slipping with being well pleasing before God. The source names the light of the living, while Psalm 114 names the land of the living and additionally mentions eyes rescued from tears.

Psalms 56:2
Have mercy on me, God, have mercy on me, for my soul has trusted in Thee; in the shadow of Thy wings I will hope until lawlessness passes.
  Psalms 90:4 — Hope beneath God's wings: The source seeks shelter in the shadow of God's wings until lawlessness passes; Psalm 90 assures the addressee that he will hope under God's wings. The shared wing-shelter image is specific, while the source's stated duration remains its own.

Psalms 56:4
He sent from heaven and saved me; He gave those trampling me over to reproach. Diapsalma. God sent His mercy and His truth
  Psalms 42:3 — Divine qualities sent to guide and rescue: The source reports God sending His mercy and truth; Psalm 42 asks God to send His light and truth and recounts their guidance to His holy mountain and dwellings. Both portray divine qualities as sent aid, with mercy and light remaining distinct.

Psalms 56:5
and rescued my soul from among young lions. I lay down troubled. Sons of men—their teeth are a weapon and arrows, and their tongue a sharp sword.
  Psalms 63:4 — The human tongue as a sharp sword: The source compares human tongues to a sharp sword alongside weapon-like teeth. Psalm 63 likewise describes tongues sharpened like a sword. The connection rests on this exact speech-weapon image, not on changing Psalm 63's bitter deed into a bitter word.

Psalms 56:6
Be exalted above the heavens, God, and Thy glory above all the earth.
  Psalms 107:6 — Parallel exaltation above heaven and earth: The appeal for God to be exalted above the heavens and for His glory over all the earth corresponds directly to the refrain in Psalm 107. The parallel is the complete heaven-and-earth exaltation formula.

Psalms 56:7
They prepared a trap for my feet and bowed my soul down; they dug a pit before me and fell into it. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 7:16 — The trap-maker falls into his own pit: The source reports enemies preparing a trap and digging a pit into which they fell. Psalm 7 describes the wrongdoer digging a pit and predicts his fall into the hole he made. Their reversal of the intended victim and trap-maker supplies the connection.

Psalms 56:8
My heart is ready, God; my heart is ready. I will sing and make music.
  Psalms 107:2 — Parallel ready heart and resolve to sing: The repeated declaration that the heart is ready and the resolve to sing and make music correspond to Psalm 107's opening song declaration. The latter joins the music to my glory, which is not silently added to this source's wording.

Psalms 56:9
Awake, my glory; awake, psaltery and lyre! I will awake at dawn.
  Psalms 107:3 — Parallel awakening of instruments and dawn: The summons to awaken, the psaltery and lyre, and the speaker's awakening at dawn correspond closely to Psalm 107. The source's address to his glory is preserved distinctly from the target's distribution of glory in its preceding verse.

Psalms 56:10
I will give thanks unto Thee among peoples, Lord; I will make music unto Thee among nations,
  Psalms 107:4 — Parallel praise among peoples and nations: Both passages promise thanksgiving among peoples and music among nations. The matched two-part public praise formula belongs to the larger shared song sequence rather than merely a general command to worship.

Psalms 56:11
for Thy mercy was magnified to the heavens, and Thy truth to the clouds.
  Psalms 107:5 — Mercy and truth reaching heaven and clouds: Both passages associate God's great mercy with heaven and His truth with the clouds. The source's extent to the heavens and Psalm 107's above the heavens remain distinct formulations within the same paired image.

Psalms 56:12
Be exalted above the heavens, God, and Thy glory above all the earth.
  Psalms 56:6 — Closing reprise of the exaltation refrain (same-chapter): The closing appeal for exaltation above the heavens and glory over all the earth repeats verse 6. This same-chapter refrain frames the intervening account of the enemies' pit and the speaker's readiness to sing.

Psalms 57:1
For the end. Do not destroy. Of David, for an inscription on a pillar.
  Psalms 74:1 — The Do not destroy heading designation: Both headings explicitly contain Do not destroy. The source names David and the target Asaph; the shared designation is retained without identifying a musical tune, common occasion or author beyond what each heading says.

Psalms 57:2
Do you then truly speak righteousness? Do you judge uprightly, sons of men?
  Micah 3:1 — A question challenging human justice: The question about speaking righteousness and judging uprightly among the sons of men corresponds to Micah's question whether Israel's authorities should know justice. The questioning form challenges their moral responsibility, while the groups addressed remain distinct.

Psalms 57:3
For indeed in your heart you work lawless deeds on earth; your hands weave injustice together.
  Isaiah 59:5-6 — Weaving injustice and weaving lawless works: The source says hands weave injustice; Isaiah describes weaving a spider's web and explains that the works are works of lawlessness. The textile-making image gives concrete form to deliberate wrongdoing in both passages.

Psalms 57:4
Sinners were estranged from the womb; from the belly they went astray and spoke lies.
  Isaiah 48:8 — Wrongdoing described from the womb: The source describes sinners as estranged from the womb and going astray from the belly with lies. Isaiah addresses a faithless people called lawless even from the belly. The shared birth-origin language is preserved as accusation in each context, not generalized into a new claim about every infant.

Psalms 57:5
Their fury is in the likeness of a serpent, like that of a deaf asp that stops up its ears,
  Deuteronomy 32:33 — Serpent and asp imagery for harmful wickedness: The source likens their fury to a serpent and describes a deaf asp; Deuteronomy calls their wine the venom of serpents and asps. The paired serpent-and-asp imagery corresponds, but fury is not rewritten as venom and the target's wine remains its own image.

Psalms 57:6
which will not hear the voice of enchanters, or a spell being cast by a wise person.
  Jeremiah 8:17 — Snakes beyond the charmer's control: The asp's refusal to hear enchanters corresponds to Jeremiah's deadly snakes that cannot be charmed. The distinctive failure of snake-charming provides the connection without identifying the metaphorical wicked people with Jeremiah's sent snakes.

Psalms 57:7
God shattered their teeth in their mouth; the Lord crushed the lions’ grinders.
  Psalms 3:8 — God crushes the teeth of the wicked: The broken teeth and crushed grinders of lions correspond to Psalm 3's declaration that God crushed sinners' teeth. The source's lion imagery intensifies the same tooth-breaking representation of disabling hostile power.

Psalms 57:9
Like wax that melted, they will be taken away; fire fell, and they did not see the sun.
  Psalms 67:3 — Wickedness melting like wax before fire: The source's melting wax and falling fire compare with Psalm 67's wish that sinners perish as wax melts before fire. The common wax-and-fire destruction image does not erase the source's further statement about not seeing the sun.

Psalms 57:11
A righteous person will rejoice when he sees vengeance upon the ungodly; he will wash his hands in the sinner’s blood.
  Psalms 67:24 — Blood imagery in the aftermath of vengeance: The righteous person's washing hands in sinners' blood compares with Psalm 67's foot dipped in blood and dogs' tongues associated with enemies. Both portray the aftermath of divine vengeance through bodily contact with enemy blood, while hands, foot and dogs remain distinct.

Psalms 57:12
A human being will say, “Then there is indeed fruit for the righteous; then there is God judging them on earth.”
  Malachi 3:14-18 — A visible distinction between righteous and wicked: The concluding affirmation of fruit for the righteous and God judging them on earth compares with Malachi's challenge about the advantage of serving God and the promised visible distinction between righteous and lawless people. The psalm's verdict and Malachi's future vindication address the particular question of whether righteousness has a discernible result.

Psalms 58:1
For the end. Do not destroy. Of David, for an inscription on a pillar, when Saul sent and watched his house to put him to death.
  1 Samuel 19:11-12 — Saul's watchers at David's house: The heading's account of Saul sending and watching the house to kill David corresponds to Samuel's account, which explicitly supplies messengers at David's house, Michal's warning and her escape assistance. The named sender, house watch and intended killing establish the narrative link.

Psalms 58:3
Deliver me from those working lawlessness, and save me from men of bloodshed.
  Psalms 25:9-10 — Separation from lawless people and men of blood: The plea for rescue from workers of lawlessness and men of blood corresponds to the plea not to be destroyed with the ungodly and men of blood whose hands hold lawless deeds. Rescue from these people and avoidance of their fate remain different petitions.

Psalms 58:4
For behold, they hunted my soul; powerful men attacked me. Neither my lawlessness nor my sin, Lord—
  Psalms 7:4-6 — Innocence and pursuit of the soul: The source protests that the pursuit of his soul is not for his lawlessness or sin. Psalm 7 places injustice in the speaker's hands and repayment to those who repay him evils within a conditional oath leading to enemy pursuit of his soul. Both relate pursuit to the speaker's conduct; Psalm 7:5 does not expressly supply the object of his repayment.

Psalms 58:5
without lawlessness they ran and made straight. Awake to meet me, and see!
  Psalms 43:24-27 — Awakening God to meet the afflicted: The appeal that God awake, meet the speaker and see compares with Psalm 43's calls to awake, arise, help and redeem during affliction. The specific awakening appeal is shared without identifying the individual and communal situations as one event.

Psalms 58:6
And Thou, Lord, God of powers, God of Israel, attend to visiting all the nations; have no compassion on any who work lawlessness. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 81:8 — God's judgment extending to all nations: The invocation of the God of Israel to attend to all nations corresponds to the call for God to rise and judge the earth, taking inheritance among all nations. The particular movement from Israel's God to the whole range of nations connects the petitions.

Psalms 58:7
They will return toward evening, hunger like a dog, and circle a city.
  Isaiah 56:11 — Human opponents pictured as hungry dogs: The source compares the evening city-circlers to hungry dogs; Isaiah describes shameless dogs who know no satisfaction. The canine appetite image is shared, while the source's hunger does not by itself assert Isaiah's explicit insatiability.

Psalms 58:8
Behold, they will speak out with their mouths, and a sword is on their lips; for who has heard?
  Psalms 56:5 — A sword in hostile speech: The sword on their lips corresponds to the sharp sword of the human tongue in Psalm 56. Both place a cutting weapon in an organ of speech, preserving lips here and tongue in the target.

Psalms 58:9
And Thou, Lord, wilt laugh them to scorn; Thou wilt reduce all nations to nothing.
  Psalms 2:4 — God laughs at hostile nations: The Lord's laughter and treatment of nations as nothing correspond to the heavenly One laughing and sneering at the hostile coalition in Psalm 2. The laughter of God at collective opposition is the particular shared response.

Psalms 58:10
My strength—I will keep watch toward Thee, for Thou, God, art my supporter.
  Psalms 58:18 — Supporter confession with a changed address (same-chapter): The address to my strength and resolve to keep watch toward God return in verse 18 as an address to my helper and resolve to make music. The repeated confession of God as supporter links this same-chapter modified refrain without making the different addresses identical.

Psalms 58:13
The sin of their mouth, the word of their lips— let them be caught in their arrogance; and from curse and falsehood consummations will be announced
  Proverbs 12:13 — Sinful lips and entrapment: The source joins sins of the mouth and lips with a petition that they be caught in their arrogance. Proverbs states that a sinner falls into traps through the sin of lips. The shared mouth-sin and entrapment pattern compares a petition for judgment with a stated consequence of wrongdoing.

Psalms 58:14
in the anger of consummation, and they shall certainly not exist; and they will know that God rules Jacob and the ends of the earth. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 82:17-19 — Judgment leading to recognition of worldwide divine rule: The source predicts that they will know God rules Jacob and the ends of the earth. Psalm 82 petitions for humiliating judgment and recognition that the Lord alone is Most High over all the earth. Both connect judgment with acknowledgment of God's worldwide rule, preserving prediction here and petition there.

Psalms 58:15
They will return toward evening, hunger like a dog, and circle a city.
  Psalms 58:7 — Return of the evening dog refrain (same-chapter): The return at evening, hunger like dogs and circling the city repeat verse 7. This same-chapter recurrence follows the petitions for judgment and precedes the further description of their search for food.

Psalms 58:17
But I will sing to Thy power and exult in Thy mercy in the morning, for Thou becamest my supporter and a refuge in the day of my affliction.
  Psalms 91:3 — Morning proclamation of divine mercy: The speaker resolves to sing to God's power and exult in His mercy in the morning; Psalm 91 calls for declaring His mercy in the morning. The specified morning celebration of mercy supplies the connection, while Psalm 91 additionally assigns truth to the night.

Psalms 58:18
My helper, I will make music unto Thee, for Thou, God, art my supporter, my God, my mercy.
  Psalms 58:10 — Closing supporter confession with music (same-chapter): The closing address to my helper and promise to make music reprise verse 10's confession of God as supporter. This same-chapter variation changes the earlier address my strength and watchfulness into helper and song, while adding the addresses my God and my mercy.

Psalms 59:1
For the end. For those who will yet be changed. For an inscription on a pillar, of David, for instruction.
  Psalms 44:1 — The heading concerning those who will be changed: The source's heading concerning those who will yet be changed corresponds to Psalm 44's heading concerning those who will be changed. The shared supplied designation is preserved without replacing it with a different musical or floral title.

Psalms 59:3
God, Thou didst reject us and pull us down; Thou wast angry and hadst compassion on us.
  Isaiah 12:1 — Anger and renewed divine compassion: The source places God's anger beside His compassion after rejection and overthrow. Isaiah's thanksgiving explicitly remembers God's anger turning away and His mercy. The connection is this movement between anger and compassion; Isaiah states the turning away more expressly.

Psalms 59:4
Thou didst shake the earth and trouble it; heal its fractures, for it was shaken.
  Isaiah 30:26 — Healing a fracture after divine affliction: The appeal to heal the earth's fractures after God shook it compares with Isaiah's announcement that the Lord will heal His people's fracture and wounded pain. The healing-of-fracture expression is shared, while earth and people remain different immediate objects.

Psalms 59:5
Thou didst show Thy people hard things; Thou madest us drink wine of stupor.
  Isaiah 51:17-22 — Judgment pictured as an intoxicating drink: The source says God gave the people wine of stupor. Isaiah describes Jerusalem drinking the Lord's cup of anger and falling, then expressly calls her drunk but not from wine. The judgment-as-intoxication imagery corresponds without changing that qualification.

Psalms 59:7
So that Thy beloved may be rescued, save by Thy right hand and hear me.
  Psalms 107:7 — Parallel rescue of the beloved by God's right hand: Both passages ask for salvation by God's right hand and a hearing so that His beloved ones may be rescued. The ordered beloved-rescue, right-hand and hearing clauses provide a close literary parallel.

Psalms 59:8
God spoke in His holy place: “I will exult and divide Shechem, and measure out the Valley of Tents.
  Psalms 107:8 — Parallel divine allotment of Shechem and the valley of tents: Both report God speaking in His holy place before the division of Shechem and measurement of the valley of tents. Within that divine speech the source says I will exult and the target I will be exalted; those different clauses remain distinct within the shared allotment sequence.

Psalms 59:9
Gilead is Mine, and Manasseh is Mine; Ephraim is the strengthening of My head; Judah is My king.
  Psalms 107:9 — Parallel claims over Gilead, Manasseh, Ephraim and Judah: The same ordered territorial declarations culminate in Ephraim's relation to the divine speaker's head and Judah as My king. The source edition's king wording is preserved instead of replacing it with a familiar lawgiver wording.

Psalms 59:10
Moab is the basin of My hope; upon Idumea I will extend My sandal; foreigners were subjected to Me.”
  Psalms 107:10 — Parallel Moab, Idumea and subjected foreign peoples: Both combine Moab as a vessel of My hope, the sandal extended over Idumea and foreign peoples subjected. The source calls Moab a basin and Psalm 107 a cauldron; neither is rewritten as a washbasin.

Psalms 59:11
Who will lead me away into an enclosed city? Who will guide me as far as Idumea?
  Psalms 107:11 — Parallel questions about the enclosed city and Idumea: The paired questions about who will lead to an enclosed city and who will guide as far as Idumea correspond directly. Neither passage names that city, so the connection does not supply an identification such as Petra.

Psalms 59:12
Is it not Thou, God, who didst reject us? And Thou wilt not go out, God, with our forces.
  Psalms 107:12 — Parallel declaration that God will not go out with the forces: Both passages ask about the God who rejected them and then state that He will not go out with their forces. The negative is preserved; the connection is not rewritten as a promise that God will accompany the army.

Psalms 59:13
Give us help from affliction, for human salvation is vain.
  Psalms 107:13 — Parallel help in affliction and futile human salvation: Both ask for help from affliction because human salvation is futile. The matched petition and its reason connect the two passages as part of the repeated song section.

Psalms 59:14
But through God we will do a mighty deed, and He will reduce our afflicters to nothing.
  Psalms 107:14 — Parallel power in God and the enemies' reduction to nothing: The resolve to act mightily in God and His bringing hostile people to nothing correspond to Psalm 107's closing resolve and assurance. The source's afflicting people and the target's enemies remain the respective descriptions.

Psalms 60:3
From the ends of the earth I cried unto Thee when my heart grew weary; Thou didst raise me upon a rock,
  Psalms 26:5 — God raises the distressed speaker upon a rock: The account of crying from the ends of the earth with a weary heart and being raised upon a rock corresponds to Psalm 26's protection in trouble and raising upon a rock. The distinctive upward placement on the rock supplies the link without adding a higher-than-I phrase.

Psalms 60:5
I will sojourn in Thy dwelling for the ages; I will be sheltered under the shelter of Thy wings. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 22:6 — Enduring residence in God's dwelling: The resolve to sojourn in God's dwelling for ages compares with dwelling in the Lord's house for length of days in Psalm 22. Both anticipate enduring divine hospitality, while the source also names shelter under God's wings.

Psalms 60:6
For Thou, God, heardest my vows; Thou gavest an inheritance to those who fear Thy name.
  Psalms 24:12-13 — Inheritance associated with fear of God: The source associates God's gift of inheritance with those fearing His name. Psalm 24 describes the person who fears the Lord and declares that his seed will inherit land. The target specifies descendants and land beyond what this source specifies.

Psalms 60:7
Thou wilt add days upon the king’s days, his years until the day of generation upon generation.
  Psalms 20:5 — God adds length of life to the king: The future addition of days to the king's days and years through generations corresponds to Psalm 20's king asking for life and receiving length of days. The source's future assurance and the target's reported gift are preserved distinctly.

Psalms 60:8
He will remain for ever before God; who will seek out his mercy and truth?
  2 Samuel 7:29 — Royal permanence before God: The king remaining before God for ever corresponds to David's prayer that the house of God's servant remain before God for ever. The common royal permanence formula does not equate the individual king with the entire house or explain away the source's following question about mercy and truth.

Psalms 60:9
So I will make music to Thy name for the age of the age, that I may pay my vows day by day.
  Psalms 49:14 — Praise joined to payment of vows: The promise of enduring music to God's name and daily fulfillment of vows corresponds to Psalm 49's paired commands to offer praise and pay vows to the Most High. The shared praise-and-vow combination connects promise with instruction.

Psalms 61:1
For the end. Concerning Jeduthun. A psalm of David.
  Psalms 38:1 — Jeduthun named in both headings: Both headings explicitly name Jeduthun with a David attribution. This is a comparison of the exact supplied heading name, without extending it to differently spelled names elsewhere or assigning an unprovided office.

Psalms 61:2
Will my soul not submit to God? For from Him is my salvation.
  Psalms 61:6 — Question and command for the soul's submission (same-chapter): The question about the soul submitting to God is resumed in verse 6 as a command to the soul. This same-chapter variation gives salvation as the reason here and endurance in verse 6, rather than making the two reasons identical.

Psalms 61:3
For He is my God and my Savior, my supporter; I shall certainly not be shaken any further.
  Psalms 61:7 — Savior and supporter refrain with a changed result (same-chapter): The confession that God is savior and supporter recurs in verse 7. The result here is not being shaken any further, while verse 7 says not migrating away; this same-chapter variation preserves that actual difference.

Psalms 61:4
How long do you attack a human being? All of you murder, as against a leaning wall and a fence thrust down.
  Isaiah 30:13-14 — A vulnerable wall and collapse imagery: The assault on a human is compared to a leaning wall and a fence thrust down. Isaiah likens sin to a wall falling in a captured fortified city and then to shattered pottery. The unstable-wall image corresponds, while its human and moral referents remain distinct.

Psalms 61:5
Nevertheless they planned to reject my honor; they ran in falsehood; they blessed with their mouths but cursed with their hearts. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 27:3 — Peaceful or blessing mouths with evil hearts: The source contrasts blessing with the mouth and cursing with the heart. Psalm 27 describes people speaking peace to neighbors while evils are in their hearts. The particular outward-speech and inward-intention opposition supplies the connection.

Psalms 61:6
Nevertheless submit to God, my soul, for my endurance is from Him.
  Psalms 61:2 — Command resuming the soul's submission question (same-chapter): The command that the soul submit to God resumes verse 2's question. This same-chapter response grounds submission in endurance from God, whereas the earlier verse names salvation.

Psalms 61:7
For He is my God and my Savior, my supporter; I shall certainly not migrate away.
  Psalms 61:3 — Repeated supporter confession preserving migration wording (same-chapter): The savior-and-supporter confession repeats verse 3, but its concluding assurance is that the speaker will not migrate away. The earlier assurance that he will not be shaken is a related yet differently worded same-chapter result and does not replace this clause.

Psalms 61:9
Hope in Him, all the assembly of the people; pour out your hearts before Him. God is our helper. Diapsalma.
  1 Samuel 1:15 — Pouring the inner self before God: The instruction to pour out hearts before God corresponds to Hannah's explanation that she is pouring out her soul before the Lord while praying. The shared pouring-out image connects exhortation with enacted prayer, preserving hearts here and soul there.

Psalms 61:10
Nevertheless the sons of men are vain; the sons of men are false upon scales for doing injustice, they themselves together from vanity.
  Proverbs 11:1 — Falsehood, scales and injustice: The source says the sons of men are false upon scales for doing injustice; Proverbs calls deceitful scales an abomination and a just weight acceptable. The comparison follows the supplied falsehood-and-scales language, without substituting conduct for the sons of men or claiming they weigh less than breath.

Psalms 61:11
Do not hope in injustice or long for plunder; if wealth flows, do not attach your heart.
  1 Timothy 6:17 — Refusing to attach the heart to increasing wealth: The warning not to attach the heart when wealth flows corresponds to the instruction that the rich not set hope on uncertain wealth but on God. The source also condemns injustice and plunder, but it does not therefore label every increase of wealth as plunder.

Psalms 61:13
that power belongs to God, and mercy unto Thee, Lord, for Thou wilt repay each one according to his works.
  Romans 2:6 — Repayment to each according to works: The declaration that God will repay each according to his works corresponds directly to Romans' statement within its account of impartial divine judgment. The formula is shared without asserting an exclusive quotation source for Romans.

Psalms 62:2
God, my God, toward Thee I rise at dawn; my soul thirsted for Thee. How greatly my flesh longs for Thee in a desert, trackless and waterless land!
  Psalms 41:2-3 — The soul thirsting for God: The source's thirsty soul and longing flesh in a waterless land correspond to Psalm 41's deer-water comparison and thirst of the soul for the living God. The particular metaphor directs physical thirst toward desire for God in both passages.

Psalms 62:5
So I will bless Thee in my life; in Thy name I will lift my hands.
  Psalms 133:2 — Blessing God with lifted hands: The promise to bless God during life and lift hands in His name corresponds to Psalm 133's instruction to lift hands toward the holy places and bless the Lord. The gestures and blessing correspond, while the target explicitly situates its instruction by night.

Psalms 62:6
May my soul be filled as with fat and richness, and my mouth will praise with lips of exultation.
  Psalms 35:9 — Divine abundance pictured as rich nourishment: The wish that the soul be filled as with fat and richness compares with Psalm 35's promised intoxication from the richness of God's house and drink from His delight. The abundant nourishment imagery connects a wish with an assurance without making both passages descriptions of the same literal meal.

Psalms 62:7
If I remembered Thee upon my bed, in the dawn hours I meditated upon Thee.
  Psalms 118:148 — Remembrance on the bed and dawn meditation: The source conditionally speaks of remembrance on the bed and meditation on God at dawn. Psalm 118 describes eyes awake before dawn to meditate on His sayings. The connection preserves the condition, the different timing and the distinction between God and His sayings as meditation's object.

Psalms 62:8
For Thou becamest my helper, and in the shelter of Thy wings I will exult.
  Psalms 16:8 — Exultation in the shelter of God's wings: The speaker promises to exult beneath God's wings because God became his helper. Psalm 16 appeals for protection and declares that God will shelter the speaker beneath His wings. The shared wing-shelter image connects promised exultation with protective petition and assurance.

Psalms 62:9
My soul clung behind Thee; Thy right hand supported me.
  Psalms 17:36 — God's right hand supports the speaker: The declaration that God's right hand supported the speaker corresponds directly to the same assurance in Psalm 17. The source's soul clinging behind God and the target's further discipline clauses remain their respective surrounding descriptions.

Psalms 62:10
But they sought my soul in vain; they will enter the lowest places of the earth.
  Ezekiel 32:18-21 — Hostile people descending to the earth's depth: The enemies who sought the speaker's soul will enter the lowest earth. Ezekiel describes nations bringing Egypt's daughters down dead into the earth's depth with those descending to a pit. The descent image connects distinct judgment scenes without identifying their victims.

Psalms 62:11
They will be handed over into the hands of a sword; they will be portions for foxes.
  Jeremiah 7:33 — The slain become portions for animals: The source joins delivery to the sword with becoming portions for foxes. Jeremiah says the people's dead will become food for birds and wild beasts. The aftermath of death as animal food corresponds, while the animals and judged groups remain different.

Psalms 62:12
But the king will rejoice in God; everyone swearing by Him will be praised, for the mouth of those speaking unjust things was stopped.
  Psalms 106:42 — Righteous joy and the stopped mouth of injustice: The source links the king's future rejoicing in God with the past statement that the mouth speaking unjust things was stopped. Psalm 106 predicts the upright seeing and rejoicing and all lawlessness stopping its mouth. The paired joy and silencing pattern corresponds while the past and future stopping remain distinct.

Psalms 63:3
Thou didst shelter me from a conspiracy of evildoers, from a multitude working lawlessness,
  Psalms 30:21 — Shelter from hostile human speech and plotting: Both describe God sheltering people from hostile human activity. Psalm 30 joins concealment in God's presence with shelter from contradicting tongues; Psalm 63 follows its conspiracy with tongue-as-weapon imagery.

Psalms 63:4
who sharpened their tongues like a sword and stretched their bow—a bitter deed—
  Psalms 56:5 — The tongue pictured as a sharp sword: Both turn the enemy's tongue into a sharp sword and place it alongside weapon imagery. Psalm 63 describes sharpening tongues; Psalm 56 identifies the tongue itself as the sword.

Psalms 63:5
to shoot down a blameless one in hidden places; suddenly they will shoot him down and will not fear.
  Psalms 10:2 — Concealed archery against the upright: Both describe enemies shooting an upright person from concealment: hidden places here and moonless darkness in Psalm 10. The bow, hidden attack and morally described victim form the particular parallel.

Psalms 63:6
They strengthened an evil word for themselves; they discussed concealing traps; they said, “Who will see them?”
  Psalms 93:7-9 — Wrongdoers suppose their actions are unseen: The plotters conceal traps and ask who will see them. Psalm 93 records evildoers denying that the Lord sees, then answers by appealing to the Maker of the eye. The texts compare presumed invisibility without identifying their offenders.

Psalms 63:7
They searched out lawless deeds; they failed while searching with a search. A human being will approach, and a deep heart,
  Jeremiah 17:9-10 — The deep human heart and divine examination: Psalm 63 places a human being and a deep heart after the failed search for lawless deeds. Jeremiah likewise calls the heart deep and a human hard to know, then declares the Lord's examination. The comparison preserves Psalm 63's unusual sequence without replacing it with another edition's wording.

Psalms 63:9
and their tongues grew powerless against them. All who saw them were troubled,
  Psalms 11:4-5 — The defeat of a tongue that claimed power: Psalm 63 says the adversaries' tongues grew powerless. Psalm 11 prays for the destruction of deceitful lips and the boasting tongue that claimed mastery. The correspondence is the reversal of hostile verbal power.

Psalms 63:10
and every human being feared. They announced God’s works and understood His acts.
  Exodus 14:30-31 — Witnessed divine deliverance produces fear: After the enemies' defeat, observers fear and announce God's acts. Exodus describes Israel seeing the Lord's defeat of Egypt and consequently fearing and trusting Him. This is a narrative illustration of that response, without identifying the psalm's unnamed conflict as the sea crossing.

Psalms 63:11
A righteous person will rejoice in the Lord and hope in Him, and all the upright in heart will be praised.
