Elihu describes a sick person's soul drawing near death and his life being in Hades. Both place severe suffering beside death and Hades, while the psalm's approach and Elihu's statement that life was in Hades remain different clauses and states.

Psalms 87:5
I was counted with those descending into a pit; I became like a person without help, free among the dead,
  Psalms 27:1 — Being counted with those descending into a pit: Psalm 87 says the speaker was counted with those descending into a pit and became like a person without help. Psalm 27 fears that God's silence will make the speaker like those descending into a pit. The distinctive comparison with the pit's descending company connects a lamented condition with a feared consequence.

Psalms 87:6
like wounded people cast down, sleeping in a grave, whom Thou didst remember no more, and who were thrust away from Thy hand.
  Psalms 30:13 — The dead as an image of being forgotten: Psalm 87 describes wounded people sleeping in a grave whom God remembered no more. Psalm 30 says the speaker was forgotten from the heart like a dead person. The forgotten dead provide the shared image, while God is explicitly addressed as the forgetter only in the source.

Psalms 87:7
They put me in the lowest pit, in dark places and in death’s shadow.
  Lamentations 3:6 — Dark places associated with the dead: The source says they put the speaker in the lowest pit, dark places and death's shadow. Lamentations says He made the speaker dwell in dark places like those long dead. Darkness and the company of the dead connect the laments; the source's plural 'they' is not replaced with the target's singular agent.

Psalms 87:8
Thy fury rested firmly upon me, and Thou broughtest all Thy surges upon me. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 41:8 — God's overwhelming surges upon the speaker: Psalm 87 says God's fury rested upon the speaker and He brought all His surges upon him. Psalm 41 says all God's billows and waves passed over the speaker. Both describe suffering through overwhelming waters attributed to God, without making the surrounding images or occasions identical.

Psalms 87:9
Thou didst remove my acquaintances far from me; they made me an abomination to themselves. I was handed over and did not go out.
  Job 19:13-19 — Estrangement from acquaintances and close relations: Psalm 87 attributes the removal of acquaintances to God and says they made the speaker an abomination. Job describes brothers withdrawing, people who knew him forgetting him, his becoming a foreigner before household members, and former associates abhorring him. The extended social estrangement is comparable, while the grammatical agency differs and Job's speech is not assigned to the psalmist.

Psalms 87:10
My eyes grew weak from poverty. I cried unto Thee, Lord, all day; I spread my hands toward Thee:
  Psalms 142:6 — Hands spread toward God in distress: Psalm 87 combines weakened eyes and persistent crying with hands spread toward the Lord. Psalm 142 similarly says the speaker spread his hands toward God and compares his soul to waterless land. The explicit bodily gesture toward God connects the distressed prayers; the accompanying images remain different.

Psalms 87:11
Surely Thou wilt not work wonders for the dead? Or will physicians raise them, and will they give Thee thanks?
  Isaiah 38:18-19 — The dead and the possibility of thanksgiving: The source asks about wonders for the dead, whether physicians will raise them, and whether they will give thanks. Hezekiah's prayer says the dead will not bless God whereas the living will bless Him. The comparison concerns praise from the dead and living; it preserves 'physicians' and does not turn the lament into a comprehensive denial of divine resurrection.

Psalms 87:12
Surely no one will recount Thy mercy in a grave and Thy truth in destruction?
  Psalms 6:6 — Praise questioned in the grave and death: Psalm 87 asks whether anyone will recount God's mercy in a grave or His truth in destruction. Psalm 6 says no one in death remembers God and asks who will give thanks in Hades. Both press the question of honoring God from death's realm, with their distinct terms and lament settings preserved.

Psalms 87:13
Surely Thy wonders will not be known in darkness, or Thy righteousness in a forgotten land?
  Job 10:21-22 — Death's land depicted as darkness: Psalm 87 asks whether God's wonders and righteousness will be known in darkness and a forgotten land. Job anticipates departure to a dark land without return and describes its lack of brightness. The dark land image connects the laments, without making Job's speech a direct explanation of every question in the psalm.

Psalms 87:14
And I cried unto Thee, Lord, and in the morning my prayer will reach Thee first.
  Psalms 5:4 — Prayer directed to God in the morning: Psalm 87 anticipates the speaker's prayer reaching God first in the morning. Psalm 5 says God will hear the speaker's voice in the morning, when the speaker will stand before Him and look on. Both give the morning a particular place in anticipated prayer, while the actions of prayer, hearing and standing differ.

Psalms 87:15
Why, Lord, dost Thou thrust away my soul and turn Thy face away from me?
  Psalms 43:25 — Questioning God's averted face: Psalm 87 asks why the Lord thrusts away the soul and turns His face away. Psalm 43 asks why God turns His face away and forgets the people's poverty and affliction. The question about God's averted face connects individual and communal lament without resolving their suffering as the same event.

Psalms 87:17
Thy wraths passed over me, and Thy threats utterly troubled me;
  Psalms 89:7 — Divine anger and the sufferers' trouble: The speaker says God's wraths passed over him and His threats utterly troubled him. Psalm 89 says the community failed in God's anger and was troubled in His wrath. The paired divine anger and human trouble are shared, while threats and failure, and singular and communal voices, remain distinct.

Psalms 87:18
they encircled me like water all day; together they closed around me.
  Psalms 68:2-3 — Affliction imagined as engulfing water: In Psalm 87 the wraths and threats just described encircled the speaker like water and closed around him. Psalm 68 cries for rescue because waters have entered to the soul; the speaker entered the sea's depths and a tempest drowned him. The surrounding or engulfing water conveys mortal distress, without identifying literal floods or the speakers.

Psalms 87:19
Thou didst remove friend and neighbor far from me, and my acquaintances because of misery.
  Psalms 87:9 — same-chapter return to estranged acquaintances: The final verse returns to God's removal of close people and acquaintances after the earlier complaint that acquaintances were removed and treated the speaker as an abomination. This repeated social loss frames the intervening death questions and unanswered prayer; the ending retains acquaintances removed because of misery, not darkness as a friend.

Psalms 88:2
I will sing of Thy mercies, Lord, for ever; from generation to generation I will proclaim Thy truth with my mouth,
  Psalms 99:5 — Enduring mercy and truth through generations: Psalm 88 promises enduring song of God's mercies and proclamation of His truth from generation to generation. Psalm 99 confesses God's mercy for ever and His truth through generations. The paired qualities and their duration supply a direct correspondence between praise and its stated grounds.

Psalms 88:3
for Thou saidst, “Mercy will be built for ever; in the heavens Thy truth will be prepared.”
  Psalms 118:89-90 — Divine truth enduring with a heavenly reference: Psalm 88 reports God's promise that mercy will be built for ever and His truth prepared in the heavens. Psalm 118 places the Lord's enduring word in heaven and declares His truth through generations. Both join divine reliability and heavenly permanence, while word and truth are not made the identical grammatical subject in both passages.

Psalms 88:4
“I made a covenant with My chosen ones; I swore to David My servant:
  2 Samuel 23:5 — David and an enduring covenant: The source records a covenant with God's chosen ones and an oath to David His servant. In David's last words, 2 Samuel records his confidence in God's everlasting covenant with him. The named David and divine covenant make the connection particular, while the source's plural chosen ones is preserved.

Psalms 88:5
‘I will prepare your seed for ever and build your throne from generation to generation.’” Diapsalma.
  2 Samuel 7:12-13 — David's seed and an enduring throne: God promises David enduring seed and a throne built through generations. Through Nathan, God promises to raise David's seed and establish that offspring's royal throne for ever. Both join David's descendants and an enduring throne, with the target also assigning the building of God's house to the offspring.

Psalms 88:6
The heavens will acknowledge Thy wonders, Lord, and Thy truth in an assembly of holy ones.
  Psalms 148:1-4 — Heavenly praise of God: Psalm 88 says the heavens will acknowledge God's wonders and His truth in an assembly of holy ones. Psalm 148 summons praise from the heavens and names angels, powers, heavenly lights and the heavens of heavens. The heavenly praise setting is shared, without identifying the source's holy ones with every group in the target's summons.

Psalms 88:7
For who in clouds will be equaled to the Lord, and who among sons of God will be likened to the Lord?
  Exodus 15:11 — The Lord incomparable among gods and sons of God: The source asks who in clouds or among sons of God can be likened to the Lord. Exodus asks who among gods is like Him, calling Him glorified among holy ones and working wonders. The rhetorical assertion of divine incomparability is shared, while clouds, sons of God and gods remain the texts' distinct expressions.

Psalms 88:8
God is glorified in the council of holy ones, great and fearsome above all around Him.
  Psalms 81:1 — God's preeminence within an assembly: Psalm 88 describes God glorified in the council of holy ones and fearsome above those around Him. Psalm 81 places God in an assembly of gods, judging in their midst. Both portray God's preeminence within a surrounding assembly, with glorification and judgment as different actions and the groups' identities not equated.

Psalms 88:10
Thou rulest the sea’s might, and Thou quietest the surge of its waves.
  Mark 4:39-41 — The power to quiet a raging sea: The psalm praises God for ruling the sea's might and quieting its waves. Mark narrates Jesus rebuking the wind and commanding the sea, followed by calm and the disciples' question about His identity. The specific mastery over violent water grounds the comparison without claiming that Mark explicitly quotes this verse.

Psalms 88:11
Thou didst humble a proud one like a wounded man, and with the arm of Thy power Thou didst scatter Thine enemies.
  Exodus 15:6-7 — Divine power defeating enemies: Psalm 88 describes the arm of God's power scattering enemies, beside the humbling of an unnamed proud one. The sea song praises God's right hand shattering enemies and His shattering adversaries in the abundance of His glory. The divine limb and defeated enemies are comparable; the psalm's proud one is not supplied with a name or identified as Pharaoh.

Psalms 88:12
The heavens are Thine, and the earth is Thine; the inhabited world and its fullness—Thou didst found them.
  Psalms 23:1-2 — God's ownership and founding of the world: Psalm 88 declares the heavens and earth God's, then says He founded the inhabited world and its fullness. Psalm 23 likewise declares the earth and its fullness the Lord's and says He founded it. The linked ownership and founding statements form an extended correspondence, with the passages' additional cosmic details kept distinct.

Psalms 88:13
The north and seas—Thou didst create them; Tabor and Hermon will exult in Thy name.
  Psalms 97:8 — Mountains personified in exultation: Psalm 88 anticipates Tabor and Hermon exulting in God's name after mentioning His creation of the north and seas. Psalm 97 says rivers will clap their hands together and mountains will exult. The mountains' human act of rejoicing is shared, without changing the source's seas into south or identifying the target's unnamed mountains.

Psalms 88:14
Thine is the arm with dominion; let Thy hand be strengthened; let Thy right hand be raised.
  Psalms 9:33 — An appeal for God's hand to be raised: Psalm 88 asks that God's hand be strengthened and His right hand raised. Psalm 9 likewise asks the Lord God to arise and let His hand be raised, then asks Him not to forget the needy. The particular raised-hand petition is shared, while the source's right-hand specification and the target's concern for the needy remain distinct.

Psalms 88:15
Righteousness and judgment are the preparation of Thy throne; mercy and truth will go ahead before Thy face.
  Psalms 96:2 — Righteousness and judgment at God's throne: Psalm 88 names righteousness and judgment as the preparation of God's throne. Psalm 96 uses the same paired qualities as His throne's establishment amid cloud and gloom. The divine throne and its moral basis correspond, while mercy and truth proceeding before God belong to the source's additional clause.

Psalms 88:16
Blessed are the people who know the cry of triumph; Lord, they will walk in the light of Thy face,
  Numbers 6:25-26 — Blessing and the light of God's face: The psalm blesses the people and says they will walk in the light of the Lord's face. The priestly blessing asks the Lord to make His face shine upon the recipient and lift His face toward him. The particular connection of divine facial light and blessing is shared, with communal walking and priestly petition as different expressions.

Psalms 88:17
and in Thy name they will exult all day, and in Thy righteousness they will be raised up.
  Psalms 32:21 — Joy in God's name: Psalm 88 anticipates people exulting in God's name throughout the day. Psalm 32 joins the community's heart rejoicing in Him with its having hoped in His holy name. Both associate communal joy with God's name, while the target mentions hope and the source also mentions being raised in righteousness; the target does not expressly state a causal connection.

Psalms 88:18
For Thou art the boast of their power, and by Thy favor our horn will be raised.
  1 Samuel 2:10 — Strength and a horn raised by God: Psalm 88 names God the boast of the people's power and says 'our horn' will be raised by His favor. Hannah's song says God gives strength to 'our kings' and will raise the horn of His anointed. The paired divine strength and raised horn are shared, with the community and the expressly royal recipients distinguished.

Psalms 88:19
For support belongs to the Lord, and to the Holy One of Israel, our King.
  Isaiah 44:6 — God as Israel's King: The psalm places support with the Lord and the Holy One of Israel, our King. Isaiah introduces God as Israel's King and rescuer before declaring His uniqueness. The particular attribution of Israel's kingship to God supports the link; Isaiah 44:6 does not itself repeat the Holy One designation.

Psalms 88:20
Then Thou spakest in a vision to Thy holy ones and saidst: “I placed help upon a powerful man; I raised a chosen one from My people.
  2 Samuel 7:8 — David raised from among the people to royal service: The source recalls a vision in which God raised a chosen one from His people, identified as David in the following unit. Nathan's message recalls God taking His servant David from the sheepfold to lead Israel; its surrounding account calls the message a vision. Both describe David's elevation by God, with the sheepfold detail supplied only by the account in 2 Samuel.

Psalms 88:21
I found David My servant; with My holy oil I anointed him,
  1 Samuel 16:12-13 — David's anointing with oil: The divine speech says God found David His servant and anointed him with holy oil. 1 Samuel narrates the Lord identifying the youngest son and commanding his anointing, then Samuel taking the horn of oil and anointing him. The named David and actual oil-anointing correspond, with God as authorizer and Samuel as executor distinguished.

Psalms 88:22
for My hand will support him, and My arm will strengthen him.
  Isaiah 41:10 — Divine strength and supporting hand: God promises His supporting hand and strengthening arm to David. In Isaiah He tells Israel not to fear and says He strengthened, helped and secured the addressee with His righteous right hand. Strength and divine bodily support connect the assurances, while David and Israel, and promised and stated accomplished help, remain distinct.

Psalms 88:23
An enemy will not gain by him, and a son of lawlessness will not go on to harm him.
  2 Samuel 7:10 — Protection from lawless harm in the Davidic promise: Psalm 88 promises that no son of lawlessness will go on to harm David. Nathan's message promises a place for Israel and says no unjust man will again humble the people. Both belong to promises of protection in the Davidic setting, but the psalm's royal individual and the people Israel in 2 Samuel are different recipients.

Psalms 88:24
I will cut down his enemies before him and put those who hate him to flight.
  2 Samuel 7:9 — David's enemies removed before him: Psalm 88 promises that God will cut down David's enemies before him and put his haters to flight. Nathan's message recalls God's presence with David and destruction of his enemies before him. The named beneficiary and enemies removed before him connect promise and retrospective declaration without erasing the tense difference.

Psalms 88:25
My truth and My mercy will be with him, and in My name his horn will be raised.
  Psalms 131:17 — The raising of David's horn: God promises David His truth and mercy and says his horn will be raised in His name. Psalm 131 says God will make a horn sprout for David and prepared a lamp for His anointed. The divine raising or growth of David's horn is a specific royal image, while the source's name and the target's lamp remain different accompanying elements.

Psalms 88:26
I will set his hand in the sea and his right hand in rivers.
  Psalms 71:8 — Royal reach expressed through sea and river: Psalm 88 says God will set the king's hand in the sea and his right hand in rivers. Psalm 71 describes the king ruling from sea to sea and from the river to the inhabited world's extremities. The waters express royal reach in both, with David's promise and the poem headed for Solomon kept in their respective settings.

Psalms 88:27
He will call on Me, ‘Thou art my Father, my God and the supporter of my salvation.’
  2 Samuel 7:14 — God as Father in a royal promise: Psalm 88 anticipates the king calling God his Father, God and supporter of salvation. Nathan's oracle promises that God will be a father to David's offspring and that the offspring will be His son. The royal father-son relationship is explicit in both, while David's role in the psalm and the promised offspring in 2 Samuel are not collapsed into one immediate addressee.

Psalms 88:28
And I will make him firstborn, high among the kings of the earth.
  Colossians 1:15-18 — Firstborn status associated with preeminence: Psalm 88 promises to make the king firstborn and high among earth's kings. Colossians calls the Son firstborn of all creation and firstborn from the dead so that He may have preeminence in everything. The firstborn and preeminence pattern is comparable, while royal rank, creation and resurrection are distinct scopes and an identity claim is not inferred from the term alone.

Psalms 88:29
For ever I will keep My mercy for him, and My covenant is faithful to him.
  2 Samuel 7:15-16 — Mercy retained and the royal promise made secure: God promises to keep mercy for the king for ever and calls His covenant faithful to him. Nathan's oracle promises that mercy will not be withdrawn and that the house, kingdom and throne will be made secure for ever. The continued mercy and secure royal promise connect the passages, without inserting a name for the target's unnamed removed predecessors.

Psalms 88:30
I will establish his seed for an age of an age and his throne like the days of heaven.
  2 Samuel 7:16 — An enduring dynasty and throne: Psalm 88 promises the king's seed for an age of an age and his throne like heaven's days. The divine oracle in 2 Samuel promises the house and kingdom made secure for ever and the throne established for ever. The enduring descendants or house and throne correspond, while the heaven-days comparison belongs expressly to the psalm.

Psalms 88:31
If his sons abandon My law and do not walk in My judgments,
  1 Kings 2:3-4 — Royal sons and keeping God's ways: The source introduces the condition that the king's sons abandon God's law and judgments, leading to discipline in the following units. David's charge to Solomon joins keeping the Lord's commands with the promise concerning sons guarding their ways and continuing on Israel's throne. Both connect the royal descendants with obedience, without equating the distinct consequences stated in their contexts.

Psalms 88:32
if they profane My ordinances and do not keep My commandments,
  1 Kings 9:6-7 — Royal descendants forsaking commandments: Psalm 88 continues the condition of descendants profaning ordinances and failing to keep commandments. God's warning to Solomon names him and his children turning away and not keeping commandments, followed by judgment on Israel and the house. The stated failure of royal descendants is comparable; the collective judgment in Kings is not substituted for the psalm's subsequent rod and scourges.

Psalms 88:33
I will visit their lawless acts with a rod and their sins with scourges;
  2 Samuel 7:14 — Royal wrongdoing disciplined with a rod: Psalm 88 promises to visit the sons' lawless acts with a rod and sins with scourges. Nathan's oracle promises fatherly correction of the royal offspring's wrongdoing with a rod of men and afflictions of human beings. The particular rod of discipline within the royal promise is shared, while scourges and afflictions remain the texts' different wording.

Psalms 88:34
but I shall certainly not scatter My mercy away from him, nor act unjustly in My truth,
  2 Samuel 7:15 — Mercy not withdrawn after royal discipline: After its threat of discipline, Psalm 88 promises not to scatter mercy away from the king or act unjustly in truth. The oracle in 2 Samuel likewise follows the disciplinary promise with assurance that God's mercy will not be withdrawn. The conjunction of correction and retained mercy is specific; the target's unnamed removed predecessors are not silently named.

Psalms 88:35
nor shall I profane My covenant, and what goes out through My lips I shall certainly not set aside.
  Numbers 23:19 — God's spoken word not set aside: The psalm promises that God will not profane His covenant or set aside what goes out through His lips. Balaam's oracle asks whether God speaks without doing or says without abiding by it. The reliability of divine speech is shared; Numbers's preceding descriptions of being led astray and threatened are not replaced with a different familiar wording.

Psalms 88:36
Once I swore by My holiness: I will not lie to David.
  Psalms 131:11 — God's truthful oath to David: God says He once swore by His holiness and will not lie to David. Psalm 131 says the Lord swore truth to David and will not annul it, then mentions a descendant on his throne. The named recipient, oath and truthfulness directly correspond, with holiness and non-annulment as their respective additional expressions.

Psalms 88:37
His seed will remain for ever, and his throne will be like the sun before Me,
  Psalms 71:5 — Royal endurance measured with the sun: Psalm 88 promises enduring royal seed and a throne like the sun before God. Psalm 71 says the king will remain beside the sun and before the moon through generations. Solar endurance within royal language is shared, while a throne and the royal figure are the differently expressed subjects.

Psalms 88:38
and like the moon, established for ever; and the witness in heaven is faithful.” Diapsalma.
  Jeremiah 38:36-37 — The moon's order and enduring divine commitment: Psalm 88 compares the promised royal endurance to the moon established for ever and a faithful heavenly witness. Jeremiah names God's ordering of sun, moon and stars and says Israel's family would cease being a nation if those laws ceased. Celestial stability supports an enduring commitment in both, with David's line and Israel's national existence distinguished.

Psalms 88:39
But Thou didst reject and treat as nothing; Thou didst put off Thine anointed.
  Psalms 88:21 — same-chapter contrast between anointing and rejection: The lament says God put off His anointed, whereas the earlier divine speech names David and says God anointed him with holy oil. The return to the anointed makes the lament's tension with the recalled favor explicit, without identifying the unnamed lamented king as the historical David.

Psalms 88:40
Thou didst overturn Thy servant’s covenant; Thou didst profane his consecrated thing to the ground.
  Psalms 88:35 — same-chapter tension over the covenant: The lament says God overturned His servant's covenant, after the promise that He would not profane His covenant or set aside His words. This direct covenant contrast structures the complaint. The source's accompanying profaned consecrated thing is preserved as stated rather than renamed a crown.

Psalms 88:41
Thou didst tear down all his fences; Thou madest his fortifications cowardice.
  Psalms 79:13 — God removing protective fences: Psalm 88 laments God tearing down the king's fences and making his fortifications cowardice. Psalm 79 asks why God tore down the vine's fence, exposing it to those passing along the road. The removal of protection under divine agency is shared, with king and national vine as different images and cowardice not changed into ruins.

Psalms 88:42
All who travel the road plundered him; he became a reproach to his neighbors.
  Psalms 79:13 — Road travelers taking from an exposed possession: Psalm 88 says all who travel the road plundered the king. Psalm 79 says everyone traveling the road harvests the vine after its fence was removed. The distinctive road-passers taking from what has been exposed connects the images, while plundering a king and harvesting the national vine remain distinct actions.

Psalms 88:43
Thou didst raise the right hand of his enemies; Thou didst gladden all his enemies.
  Psalms 88:24 — same-chapter reversal concerning the king's enemies: The lament says God raised the enemies' right hand and gladdened them. Earlier He promised to cut the king's enemies down and put his haters to flight. Their rise and joy reverse the expected defeat within the poem's own promise-and-lament sequence.

Psalms 88:44
Thou didst turn back the help of his sword and didst not support him in war.
  Psalms 43:10 — God's support absent in warfare: Psalm 88 says God turned back the help of the king's sword and did not support him in war. Psalm 43 laments divine rejection and shame and says God will not go out with the forces. Both describe military weakness through absent divine accompaniment, with a royal individual and communal forces, and past and future expression, distinguished.

Psalms 88:45
Thou didst remove him from purification; Thou didst dash his throne down to the ground.
  Psalms 88:5 — same-chapter contrast over the throne: The lament says God dashed the king's throne to the ground, whereas His earlier promise was to build David's throne through generations. The opposed treatment of the throne supplies a direct internal contrast. This connection does not attempt to explain the source's separate statement about removal from purification.

Psalms 88:46
Thou didst shorten the days of his time; Thou didst pour shame over him. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 20:5 — Shortened royal days contrasted with granted length of life: Psalm 88 laments the shortening of the king's days and the pouring of shame upon him. Psalm 20 says the king asked for life and received length of days for an age of an age. The treatment of royal lifespan forms a particular contrast without identifying the two unnamed kings or changing shame into a different royal attribute.

Psalms 88:47
How long, Lord? Wilt Thou turn away to the end? Will Thy wrath blaze like fire?
  Psalms 78:5 — The question of enduring wrath like fire: Both passages ask 'How long' and describe divine wrath or jealous anger burning like fire. Psalm 88 asks whether God will turn away to the end, while Psalm 78 questions anger to the end. The extended fire-and-duration complaint is shared in royal and communal laments.

Psalms 88:48
Remember what my existence is; for surely Thou didst not create all the sons of men in vain?
  Job 7:7 — A plea to remember fragile human existence: Psalm 88 asks God to remember what the speaker's existence is before questioning whether all humans were created in vain. Job likewise asks God to remember that his life is a breath and says his eye will no longer return to see good. Both address human transience to God through a remembrance plea; the psalm's creation question remains a question.

Psalms 88:49
Who is the person who will live and not see death, who will deliver his soul from the hand of Hades? Diapsalma.
  Ecclesiastes 8:8 — Human inability to control death: The psalm asks who will live without seeing death and deliver his soul from Hades' hand. Ecclesiastes denies human authority over the spirit and over the day of death. Both confront human inability to secure life against death; neither comparison turns that human limitation into a denial of God's power to rescue.

Psalms 88:50
Where are Thine ancient mercies, Lord, which Thou didst swear to David in Thy truth?
  Psalms 88:4 — same-chapter appeal back to the oath to David: The closing question about ancient mercies invokes what God swore to David in truth. The opening divine speech explicitly records His oath to David His servant alongside the covenant with His chosen ones. The later appeal returns to that particular oath as the ground for questioning the present experience.

Psalms 88:51
Remember, Lord, the reproach of Thy servants, which I bore in my bosom from many nations,
  Psalms 68:10 — Bearing reproach associated with God: Psalm 88 asks God to remember His servants' reproach borne by the speaker from many nations. Psalm 68 says reproaches of those reproaching God fell upon the speaker. Both describe personally bearing reproach within a relationship to God, with the servants' reproach and reproach aimed at God kept as different objects.

Psalms 88:53
Blessed be the Lord for ever. May it be; may it be.
  Psalms 40:14 — An enduring blessing closed by repeated assent: Psalm 88 closes with blessing the Lord for ever and twice saying 'May it be.' Psalm 40 likewise closes by blessing the Lord, Israel's God, from age to age and repeating 'May it be.' The extended closing doxology and doubled assent form a particular literary correspondence.

Psalms 89:1
A prayer of Moses, the man of God. Lord, Thou becamest our refuge through every generation.
  Deuteronomy 33:1 — Moses called the man of God in a prayer and a blessing: The psalm heading calls Moses the man of God. Deuteronomy uses the same title to introduce his blessing of Israel before his death. The connection concerns the two texts' Mosaic framing; it does not date the psalm to that final blessing.

Psalms 89:2
Before the mountains came to be and the earth and inhabited world were formed, from age to age Thou art.
  Psalms 101:26-28 — The Creator endures beyond the created world: Psalm 89 places God's existence before mountains and the formed world. Psalm 101 contrasts the founded earth and heavens, which will perish, with the Lord who remains and whose years do not fail. The creation and divine-duration contrast is explicit in both.

Psalms 89:4
For a thousand years in Thine eyes are like yesterday, which passed, and a watch in the night.
  2 Peter 3:8 — A thousand years and a day in the Lord's reckoning: The psalm compares a thousand years in God's eyes to yesterday and a night watch. Second Peter repeats the thousand-years/day comparison in both directions while addressing the apparent delay of the promise. The night-watch image remains distinctive to the psalm.

Psalms 89:5
Their things counted as nothing will be years. In the morning, like green grass, may it pass away;
  Isaiah 40:6-8 — Passing human life pictured as grass: The psalm's morning grass image belongs to its account of passing years. Isaiah explicitly calls all flesh grass and contrasts its withering with God's enduring word. The connection uses the grass image; it does not replace the psalm's unusual opening clause with another edition's flood language.

Psalms 89:6
in the morning may it blossom and pass away; in the evening may it fall, grow hard and dry up.
  Psalms 102:15-16 — Blossoming and vanishing life as grass: Psalm 89 pictures morning blossoming followed by evening drying. Psalm 102 likens human days to grass and a field flower, followed by a passing wind and disappearance. Both develop the short course of flourishing vegetation as an image of mortality, with different descriptions of its passing.

Psalms 89:8
Thou didst set our lawless deeds before Thee; our age is in the light of Thy face.
  Jeremiah 16:17 — Wrongdoing exposed before God's sight: The psalm says God set the community's lawless deeds before Him. Jeremiah says their wrongdoings were not hidden before His eyes. This concerns the visibility of wrongdoing to God; the psalm's separate clause about our age in the light of His face is preserved as supplied.

Psalms 89:10
The days of our years—in them are seventy years, or, with strength, eighty years; the greater part of them is toil and pain, for gentleness came upon us, and we shall be disciplined.
  2 Samuel 19:35-36 — Eighty years and diminished human capacities: The psalm names seventy years, or eighty with strength, amid toil and pain. Barzillai asks how many years remain and describes declining taste and hearing at eighty. His individual account provides a concrete comparison of advanced age, without turning the psalm into a universal lifespan rule.

Psalms 89:11
Who knows the might of Thine anger, and Thy wrath from the fear of Thee?
  Nahum 1:6 — Questions concerning the force of divine anger: The psalm asks who knows the might of God's anger. Nahum asks who can stand before or resist that anger and depicts its destructive force. The rhetorical questions concern human limits before divine wrath, while knowing its might and withstanding it remain different questions.

Psalms 89:13
Return, Lord—how long? Be moved concerning Thy servants.
  Exodus 32:11-14 — Mosaic intercession for relief from divine anger: The Moses-attributed psalm asks the Lord to return and be moved concerning His servants after its description of wrath. Exodus narrates Moses asking God to cease fierce anger and show mercy concerning His people. The prayers share the intercessory movement without establishing one historical occasion.

Psalms 89:14
We were filled in the morning with Thy mercy, and we exulted and rejoiced all our days.
  Psalms 58:17 — Morning mercy accompanied by exultation: Psalm 89 reports being filled with God's mercy in the morning and rejoicing. Psalm 58 resolves to exult in His mercy in the morning. The distinctive morning-mercy-and-joy combination corresponds, while the reported past and prospective resolve remain different.

Psalms 89:15
We were glad in return for the days Thou didst humble us, the years when we saw evils.
  Psalms 89:7-10 — Same-chapter reversal of afflicted days and years: The report of gladness in return for days of humiliation and years of evils answers the earlier anger, failed days and painful years. Repeated days-and-years language organizes this internal reversal within the prayer; its supplied past formulations are retained.

Psalms 90:1
A praise of a song, of David. The one dwelling in the help of the Most High will lodge in the shelter of the God of heaven.
  Psalms 26:5 — Dwelling within God's protective shelter: Psalm 90 joins dwelling in the Most High's help with lodging in divine shelter. Psalm 26 describes being hidden and sheltered in God's tent during trouble. Both portray divine protection through a sheltered dwelling, without identifying the psalm's lodging with a particular physical tent.

Psalms 90:2
He will say to the Lord, Thou art my Helper and my refuge, my God; I will hope in Him.
  Psalms 17:3 — God as helper, refuge and object of hope: The confession in Psalm 90 combines my Helper, my refuge, my God and hope in Him. Psalm 17 combines refuge, rescuer, my God, helper and hope in Him in its longer list of protective titles. The cluster of titles and hope formula supplies the correspondence.

Psalms 90:3
For He will rescue me from the hunters’ trap and from a disturbing word.
  Psalms 123:7 — Rescue from the hunters' trap: Psalm 90 promises rescue from the hunters' trap. Psalm 123 pictures the rescued community's soul as a bird escaping a shattered hunters' trap. The trap and rescue correspond; the bird and broken-trap details belong to the target.

Psalms 90:4
With His shoulders He will overshadow you, and under His wings you will hope; His truth will surround you with a weapon.
  Psalms 16:8 — Protection pictured beneath God's wings: Psalm 90 promises overshadowing and hope beneath divine wings. Psalm 16 asks to be guarded and says God will shelter the speaker in the shelter of His wings. Both use the wing-shelter image, while the request and assurances retain their distinct forms.

Psalms 90:5
You will not fear terror by night, the missile flying by day,
  Proverbs 3:24-26 — Fearless rest and approaching threats: Psalm 90 speaks of freedom from night terror and the day's missile. Proverbs promises pleasant sleep and freedom from approaching fright or ungodly assault under the Lord's support. The comparison joins protected rest with threatened attack; the target does not supply the missile image.

Psalms 90:8
You will only observe with your eyes and see the repayment of sinners.
  Psalms 36:34 — Seeing the outcome of sinners' wrongdoing: Psalm 90 says the protected person will observe sinners' repayment with his eyes. Psalm 36 says the one waiting for the Lord will see when sinners are destroyed. Both expressly describe witnessing judgment; the target additionally speaks of inheriting the land.

Psalms 90:9
For Thou, Lord, art my hope. You have made the Most High your refuge.
  Psalms 90:1-2 — Same-chapter return to refuge and hope: After the descriptions of danger, verse 9 returns to the opening Most High, refuge and hope vocabulary. This repeated confession links the opening shelter statement to the ensuing protection promises. The verse's changes of address are preserved without assigning every pronoun an inferred speaker.

Psalms 90:10
Evils will not approach you, and a scourge will not come near your dwelling.
  Exodus 12:23 — A protected dwelling spared the destructive blow: The psalm promises that a scourge will not approach the dwelling. Exodus describes the Lord preventing the destroyer from entering the blood-marked houses to strike. This is a concrete comparison of protected dwellings, without identifying the psalm's promise with that Passover occasion.

Psalms 90:11
For He will command His angels concerning you, to guard you in all your ways.
  Matthew 4:6 — The angel-command promise quoted in the temptation: The tempter cites the psalm's command concerning angels when urging Jesus to throw Himself down. Matthew's citation omits the source's all-your-ways clause. Jesus' following refusal to test God governs this narrative use; the quotation is not an endorsement of the tempter's proposed action.

Psalms 90:12
On their hands they will lift you, lest you strike your foot against a stone.
  Luke 4:11 — The lifted hands and stone promise quoted: Luke's tempter quotes being lifted on angels' hands lest the foot strike a stone. The wording closely corresponds to this psalm unit. The complete temptation context includes Jesus' rejection of putting God to the test, rather than making deliberate danger the promise's application.

Psalms 90:13
You will step on an asp and a basilisk, and trample a lion and a dragon.
  Luke 10:19 — Treading dangerous serpents: The psalm describes stepping on an asp and basilisk and trampling a lion and dragon. Jesus gives the disciples authority to tread snakes and scorpions. The serpent-treading image corresponds, while the creature lists and the disciples' narrative setting remain distinct.

Psalms 90:14
Because he hoped in Me, I will rescue him; I will shelter him, because he knew My name.
  Psalms 9:11 — Knowing God's name and hoping in Him: Psalm 90 joins the protected person's hope with knowing God's name. Psalm 9 calls those who know His name to hope in Him and says He has not abandoned His seekers. The shared knowing-name and hope combination is more specific than a general rescue theme.

Psalms 90:15
He will call upon Me, and I will hear him; I am with him in affliction, and I will take him out and glorify him.
  Psalms 49:15 — Calling in affliction and receiving rescue: Both join calling upon God in affliction with His rescue. Their final glorifying clauses differ: Psalm 90 has God glorify the rescued person, while Psalm 49 has the rescued person glorify God. The correspondence preserves that reversal of agency.

Psalms 90:16
With length of days I will fill him and show him My salvation.
  Psalms 20:5 — Length of days granted by God: Psalm 90 promises to fill the protected person with length of days. Psalm 20 reports the king asking God for life and receiving length of days. Both explicitly present prolonged life as divinely given, with different beneficiaries and forms of speech.

Psalms 91:2
It is good to give thanks to the Lord and make music to Thy name, Most High,
  Psalms 7:18 — Thanksgiving and music to the name of the Most High: Psalm 91 joins thanks to the Lord with music to the name of the Most High. Psalm 7 likewise joins thanks to the Lord with singing praise to the name of the Lord Most High. The combined acts and divine title form a close verbal parallel.

Psalms 91:3
to declare Thy mercy in the morning and Thy truth by night,
  Psalms 89:14 — Morning mercy in declaration and experience: Psalm 91 makes declaring divine mercy in the morning part of its thanksgiving. Psalm 89 reports being filled with that mercy in the morning and rejoicing. The morning-mercy wording corresponds, while Psalm 91 adds its distinct declaration of truth by night.

Psalms 91:4
on a ten-stringed psaltery, with song on a lyre.
  Psalms 32:2 — The ten-stringed psaltery and lyre: Both specify a ten-stringed psaltery together with a lyre in music offered to God. This is a correspondence of the named instrument combination, without identifying a particular performance or equating every additional musical expression.

Psalms 91:5
For Thou didst gladden me, Lord, by Thy making, and at the works of Thy hands I will exult.
  Psalms 103:31 — Rejoicing in God's works with different celebrants: Psalm 91 says God's making gladdened the speaker, who will exult at His handiwork. Psalm 103 says the Lord will rejoice in His works. The shared joy-in-divine-works pattern preserves the difference between the human celebrant and God Himself.

Psalms 91:6
How great Thy works have become, Lord; Thy reasonings have grown very deep.
  Romans 11:33-34 — The depth of divine thought and wisdom: The psalm calls God's reasonings very deep. Romans praises the depth of His wisdom and knowledge and asks who knew His mind. Both portray divine understanding through depth; Romans adds explicit questions about the limits of human understanding.

Psalms 91:8
when sinners spring up like grass and all who work lawlessness emerge, it is so that they may be utterly destroyed for the age of the age.
  Psalms 36:1-2 — Lawless flourishing pictured as short-lived grass: Psalm 91 pictures sinners springing up like grass before destruction. Psalm 36 warns against envying evildoers because they will dry up like grass and fall like green plants. The same vegetation image interprets the apparent flourishing of the lawless.

Psalms 91:10
For behold, Thine enemies will perish, and all who work lawlessness will be scattered.
  Psalms 67:2 — The scattering of God's opponents: Psalm 91 predicts the perishing of God's enemies and the scattering of workers of lawlessness. Psalm 67 asks that God arise and His enemies be scattered. The dispersal language corresponds while each verse's subjects and prediction or petition remain intact.

Psalms 91:11
My horn will be raised like a one-horned creature’s, and my old age in rich oil.
  1 Samuel 2:1 — The raised horn as a personal image of strength: Psalm 91 says the speaker's horn will be raised like a one-horned creature's. Hannah's song says her horn is exalted in her God. The personal horn-exaltation metaphor corresponds; the psalm's animal comparison and old-age-in-oil clause are not supplied to Hannah's words.

Psalms 91:13
A righteous person will blossom like a palm tree; like the cedar in Lebanon he will multiply.
  Psalms 1:3 — The righteous person as a flourishing tree: Psalm 91 compares the righteous to a blossoming palm and a multiplying cedar of Lebanon. Psalm 1 depicts the person who delights in the law as a planted, fruitful tree. Both portray righteous life through sustained tree growth, with distinct species and settings.

Psalms 91:14
Planted in the Lord’s house, they will blossom in the courts of our God.
  Psalms 51:10 — A fruitful tree within God's house: Psalm 91 places the flourishing trees in the Lord's house and courts. Psalm 51 compares the speaker to a fruitful olive tree in God's house. The shared divine-house and fruitful-tree combination is explicit, while the olive remains distinct from the preceding palm and cedar.

Psalms 91:15
They will still multiply in a rich old age and be flourishing,
  Proverbs 16:31 — A favorable old age associated with righteousness: Within its portrait of the righteous, Psalm 91 describes multiplying and flourishing in a rich old age. Proverbs calls old age a crown of boasting found in ways of righteousness. Both give a positive image of age in relation to righteous living, without equating their tree and crown metaphors.

Psalms 91:16
to declare that the Lord my God is upright, and there is no injustice in Him.
  Deuteronomy 32:4 — God's righteousness excludes injustice: Psalm 91 declares the Lord upright with no injustice in Him. Deuteronomy calls Him faithful, without unrighteousness, righteous and holy. The explicit denial of injustice alongside divine righteousness forms the comparison.

Psalms 92:1
For the day before the Sabbath, when the earth has been inhabited; a praise of a song, of David. The Lord reigned, clothed Himself in comeliness; the Lord clothed Himself in power and girded Himself. For He established the inhabited world, which will not be shaken.
  Psalms 95:10 — Divine kingship and the unshaken world: Both announce the Lord's reign and connect it with His establishing the inhabited world so that it will not be shaken. Psalm 92 adds clothing and girding with power; Psalm 95 adds the announcement among nations and upright judgment.

Psalms 92:2
Thy throne is ready from then; Thou art from the age.
  Lamentations 5:19 — The enduring divine throne: Psalm 92 joins the throne's readiness from then with God's existence from the age. Lamentations confesses that the Lord will dwell for ever and His throne endures through generations. Both affirm divine rule's duration amid otherwise different poetic settings.

Psalms 92:3
The rivers lifted up, Lord; the rivers lifted up their voices.
  Psalms 97:8 — Rivers personified with voices and hands: Psalm 92 pictures rivers lifting their voices. Psalm 97 pictures rivers clapping their hands as mountains exult. Both give rivers human expressive action in proclamations of the Lord's rule, one vocal and the other applause; the source's emotion is not otherwise specified.

Psalms 92:4
From the voices of many waters, wondrous are the sea’s surges; wondrous on high is the Lord.
  Psalms 28:3 — Many waters in a portrayal of divine majesty: Psalm 92 speaks of the voices of many waters, wondrous sea surges and the Lord wondrous on high. Psalm 28 describes the Lord's voice over waters and the Lord over many waters. The combination of many waters, voice language and divine majesty corresponds, with the waters' voices distinct from God's voice.

Psalms 92:5
Thy testimonies were made very sure; sanctity befits Thy house, Lord, for length of days.
  1 Corinthians 3:16-17 — Holiness belonging to God's dwelling: The psalm says sanctity befits the Lord's house. Paul says God's temple is holy and addresses the community as that temple in which His Spirit dwells. The shared holiness of divine dwelling remains distinct from Paul's application of the temple image to people.

Psalms 93:1
A psalm of David, for the fourth day of the week. The God of vengeance is the Lord; the God of vengeance spoke openly.
  Romans 12:19 — Vengeance belongs to the Lord: Psalm 93 calls the Lord the God of vengeance. Romans forbids private vengeance and cites the Lord's claim that vengeance and repayment are His. The divine ownership of vengeance connects the texts; Paul's corresponding instruction to believers is stated in its own context.

Psalms 93:2
Be exalted, Judge of the earth; give repayment to the arrogant.
  Genesis 18:25 — Appeal to the Judge of all the earth: Psalm 93 invokes the Judge of the earth to repay the arrogant. Abraham appeals to the One judging all the earth to do justice in distinguishing righteous and ungodly. Both appeal to God's universal judicial authority, while requesting different judicial outcomes.

Psalms 93:3
How long, Lord, will sinners— how long will sinners boast,
  Psalms 51:3 — Questioning the boasting of wrongdoers: Psalm 93 asks how long sinners will boast. Psalm 51 asks why the mighty one boasts in wickedness and lawlessness. Both make wrongful boasting the object of a direct rhetorical challenge, without identifying the psalm's sinners with the person addressed in Psalm 51.

Psalms 93:5
They humbled Thy people, Lord, and mistreated Thine inheritance.
  Isaiah 47:6 — Oppression of God's people and inheritance: Psalm 93 says opponents humbled God's people and mistreated His inheritance. Isaiah addresses the daughter of the Chaldeans for defiling His inheritance and showing His people no mercy. The paired people-and-inheritance description and oppressive treatment correspond without identifying the unnamed opponents.

Psalms 93:6
They killed the widow and newcomer and murdered orphans.
  Exodus 22:20-23 — Violence against the stranger, widow and orphan: The psalm charges opponents with killing widows, newcomers and orphans. Exodus forbids afflicting the resident foreigner, widow and orphan, and announces divine wrath against violators. The complete vulnerable-group combination and threatened accountability make the legal relation particular.

Psalms 93:7
They said, The Lord will not see, nor will the God of Jacob understand.
  Ezekiel 8:12 — Wrongdoers claim the Lord does not see: Psalm 93 reports the claim that the Lord will not see or understand. Ezekiel reports elders acting secretly and saying the Lord does not see and has abandoned the land. The explicit denial of divine sight corresponds, without equating the two groups or their acts.

Psalms 93:9
Does the One who planted the ear not hear, or the One who formed the eye not perceive?
  Proverbs 20:12 — The Creator of hearing and sight: The psalm asks whether the One who planted the ear and formed the eye can fail to hear or perceive. Proverbs says an ear hears and an eye sees and calls both works of the Lord. The paired organs and divine creation support the psalm's rhetorical argument.

Psalms 93:11
The Lord knows human reasonings, that they are futile.
  1 Corinthians 3:20 — Human reasonings quoted as the thoughts of the wise: Paul explicitly cites the Lord's knowledge that thoughts are futile. The psalm says human reasonings; Paul's quoted form says the thoughts of the wise. The quotation is retained with that wording difference and its argument against boasting in human wisdom.

Psalms 93:12
Happy the person Thou disciplinest, Lord, whom Thou teachest from Thy law,
  Job 5:17 — A beatitude for the person God disciplines: The psalm calls happy the person the Lord disciplines and teaches. Eliphaz likewise calls happy the person the Lord reproved. This is a comparison of their beatitude wording, not an endorsement of Eliphaz's application to Job; the friends' speech is qualified by the reproof in Job 42:7-8.

Psalms 93:14
For the Lord will not reject His people, nor abandon His inheritance,
  1 Samuel 12:22 — The Lord will not reject His people: The psalm's assurance that the Lord will not reject His people closely matches Samuel's assurance to Israel. Samuel grounds it in the Lord's great name and gracious choice. The shared refusal-to-reject formula is explicit in both.
  Romans 11:1-2 — The refusal to reject God's people reaffirmed: Paul asks whether God rejected His people and answers that He did not reject those He foreknew. This closely corresponds to the psalm's assurance that the Lord will not reject His people. Paul's discussion of Israel and the remnant supplies the target's particular application.

Psalms 93:17
Had the Lord not helped me, my soul would almost have sojourned in Hades.
  Psalms 29:4 — Divine rescue and the soul's relation to Hades: Psalm 93 says that without the Lord's help the speaker's soul would almost have sojourned in Hades. Psalm 29 thanks the Lord for bringing his soul up from Hades. Both use the soul-and-Hades image for mortal danger and divine rescue, in counterfactual and retrospective forms.

Psalms 93:18
If I said, My foot has been shaken, Thy mercy, Lord, helps me.
  Psalms 72:2-3 — Personal images of a shaken foot: Psalm 93 presents the shaken-foot statement conditionally and follows it with the Lord's helping mercy. Psalm 72 confesses that the feet were almost shaken while envying sinners' peace. The personal foot-instability image corresponds, with the target supplying its own occasion of envy.

Psalms 93:20
Will a throne of lawlessness join Thee, one that shapes toil by command?
  Isaiah 10:1-2 — Command or writing used for injustice: The psalm questions whether a throne of lawlessness shaping toil by command can join God. Isaiah condemns writers of wickedness who turn aside the poor's judgment and exploit widows and orphans. The comparison concerns command or writing associated with injustice, without supplying a particular written ordinance to Isaiah.

Psalms 93:21
They will hunt a righteous person’s soul and condemn innocent blood.
  Proverbs 1:11-16 — Collective plotting against righteous life: Psalm 93 describes hunting a righteous person's soul and condemning innocent blood. Proverbs exposes a group's invitation to bloodshed and to hiding a righteous man unjustly in the earth. The shared targeting of righteous life is retained, with judicial condemnation and the criminal invitation distinguished.

Psalms 93:22
The Lord became my refuge, and my God the Helper of my hope.
  Psalms 90:2 — The refuge, helper and hope confession repeated: Psalm 93 joins the Lord as refuge with God as helper of the speaker's hope. Psalm 90 combines my Helper, my refuge, my God and hope in Him. The repeated cluster of protective titles supplies the correspondence while preserving the different construction of hope.

Psalms 93:23
He will repay them their lawlessness, and according to their wickedness the Lord our God will obliterate them.
  Psalms 27:4 — Repayment measured by wrongdoing: Psalm 93 predicts repayment of lawlessness according to wickedness. Psalm 27 petitions for repayment according to opponents' works and the wickedness of their practices. The parallel joins repayment, wrongdoing and the according-to formulation, while prediction and request remain distinct.

Psalms 94:1
A praise of a song, of David. Come, let us exult in the Lord; let us shout to God our Savior.
  Psalms 80:2 — Paired calls to exult and raise a shout: Psalm 94 calls the community to exult in the Lord and shout to God its Savior. Psalm 80 pairs exultation in God its helper with a shout to the God of Jacob. The paired acclamations correspond without equating their distinct headings or festival contexts.

Psalms 94:2
Let us come before His face with thanksgiving, and with psalms let us shout to Him.
  Psalms 99:4 — Approaching God with thanksgiving and psalms: Psalm 94 calls for coming before God's face with thanksgiving and psalms. Psalm 99 calls for entering His gates with thanksgiving and courts with hymns. The shared approach-plus-thanksgiving-and-music pattern is explicit, with different descriptions of the place of approach.

Psalms 94:3
For the Lord is a great God and a great King above all gods.
  Psalms 95:4 — Divine greatness above all gods: Psalm 94 calls the Lord a great God and great King above all gods. Psalm 95 likewise declares the Lord great and fearsome above all gods. The closely repeated greatness-and-above-all-gods formula receives a royal designation in Psalm 94 and a fearsome designation in Psalm 95.

Psalms 94:4
For in His hand are the earth’s ends, and the mountain heights are His.
  Isaiah 40:12 — The earth and mountains in God's hand: The psalm places earth's ends in God's hand and assigns Him the mountain heights. Isaiah asks who measured earth with a handful and weighed the mountains. The earth-and-mountain pairing with divine handling corresponds; possession in the psalm differs from Isaiah's measurement imagery.

Psalms 94:5
For the sea is His, and He made it, and His hands formed the dry land.
  Jonah 1:9 — The maker of sea and dry land: The psalm says God made the sea and formed dry land. Jonah identifies the God he worships as the One who made sea and dry land. The paired creation confession is nearly the same, spoken in praise in the psalm and during Jonah's sea voyage in the narrative.

Psalms 94:7
For He is our God, and we are the people of His pasture and the sheep of His hand. Today, if you hear His voice,
  Ezekiel 34:30-31 — God's people described as His flock: The psalm joins He is our God with the people of His pasture and sheep of His hand. Ezekiel identifies Israel as His people, His sheep and flock, and names Himself their God. The people-flock-God combination corresponds while the hand and pasture wording remains the psalm's own.
  Hebrews 3:7 — The Today appeal quoted by Hebrews: Hebrews introduces the Holy Spirit's speech with the psalm's closing appeal to hear His voice today. This citation takes up the final clause of the source unit; the preceding people-and-sheep confession remains the psalm's immediate context.

Psalms 94:8
do not harden your hearts as in the provocation, on the day of testing in the wilderness,
  Hebrews 3:8 — The wilderness hardening warning quoted: Hebrews quotes the command not to harden hearts as in the provocation and wilderness testing. The close wording links its exhortation against unbelief to this psalm warning, while the larger Hebrews argument supplies the new audience and application.

Psalms 94:9
where your ancestors tested, put to the proof, and saw My works.
  Hebrews 3:9 — The ancestors' testing and sight of God's works quoted: Hebrews repeats the ancestors testing God and seeing His works. Its first-person Me makes the divine object of the testing explicit. This is part of its sustained quotation of the psalm rather than an independently identified new wilderness incident.

Psalms 94:10
For forty years I was vexed with that generation and said, They always stray in heart, and they did not know My ways,
  Hebrews 3:10 — Forty years and the wandering generation in the quotation: Hebrews retains forty years, divine indignation, wandering hearts and ignorance of God's ways. In its sequence forty years completes the preceding saw-My-works clause before Therefore I was indignant; the psalm directly places the forty years with God's vexation. The shared citation preserves that difference.
  Numbers 14:33-35 — Forty years of wilderness judgment: The psalm remembers forty years of divine vexation with the generation that strays in heart. Numbers announces forty years in the wilderness bearing sins and knowing God's anger. The stated duration, wilderness generation and divine anger provide the narrative correspondence without making every clause an exact quotation.

Psalms 94:11
as I swore in My anger, They shall not enter My rest.
  Hebrews 3:11 — The oath excluding entry into God's rest quoted: Hebrews repeats the sworn declaration that the rebellious will not enter God's rest. It subsequently discusses those whose bodies fell in the wilderness and their unbelief. The target explicitly receives the psalm's oath into that argument.
  Hebrews 4:3-5 — The rest oath reused alongside creation's Sabbath: Hebrews repeats the psalm's exclusion oath while discussing believers entering rest and God's seventh-day rest. The connection is this explicit reuse and interpretation; it does not reduce all of Hebrews' rest language to a single geographical destination.

Psalms 95:1
When the house was being built after the captivity; a song of David. Sing to the Lord a new song; sing to the Lord, all the earth.
  1 Chronicles 16:23 — The all-earth singing summons in the Chronicles song: The psalm's call for all the earth to sing to the Lord corresponds to the same summons in David's appointed praise before the ark. The psalm adds a new song and a post-captivity house-building heading; those different settings are preserved rather than identified.
  Ezra 3:8-11 — House-building after captivity accompanied by praise: The heading places the song when the house was being built after captivity. Ezra narrates returned captives beginning work on God's house and communal praise when the foundation was laid. The building, return and praise setting corresponds, without proving this exact ceremony is the heading's occasion.

Psalms 95:2
Sing to the Lord; bless His name. Proclaim the good news of His salvation day after day.
  1 Chronicles 16:23 — Salvation proclaimed from day to day: The psalm calls for announcing the good news of God's salvation day after day. Chronicles' song likewise calls for proclaiming His salvation from day to day. The distinctive salvation-and-daily-proclamation phrase forms the comparison.

Psalms 95:3
Announce His glory among the nations, His wonders among all the peoples.
  1 Chronicles 16:8-9 — Wonders recounted among all peoples: The psalm calls for God's glory to be announced among nations and His wonders among all peoples. Chronicles calls for His works to be known among peoples and His wonders recounted to everyone. These are parallel universal proclamation directives; glory and works remain distinct words.

Psalms 95:4
For great is the Lord and greatly to be praised; He is fearsome above all gods.
  1 Chronicles 16:25 — The great and fearsome Lord in the parallel song: Both declare the Lord great, greatly to be praised and fearsome above all gods. The close multi-clause wording belongs to the extended correspondence between this psalm and Chronicles' song, without identifying their stated occasions.

Psalms 95:5
For all the gods of the nations are demons, but the Lord made the heavens.
  1 Chronicles 16:26 — The nations' gods contrasted with the heavenly Creator: Both contrast the gods of the nations with the God who made heaven. The psalm calls those gods demons; Chronicles calls them idols. The creator contrast is retained with the different descriptions of the nations' gods made explicit.

Psalms 95:6
Thanksgiving and beauty are before Him; holiness and magnificence are in His sanctuary.
  1 Chronicles 16:27 — Qualities before God and in His place: Both arrange qualities before God and qualities in His place. The psalm gives thanksgiving and beauty, then holiness and magnificence in His sanctuary; Chronicles gives glory and praise, then strength and boasting in His place. The parallel structure preserves these lexical differences.

Psalms 95:7
Bring to the Lord, families of nations; bring to the Lord glory and honor.
  1 Chronicles 16:28 — Families of nations summoned to bring glory: Both address families of nations and repeat the summons to bring or give to the Lord. The psalm pairs glory with honor; Chronicles pairs glory with strength. The shared address and repeated imperative are retained without substituting one second object for the other.

Psalms 95:8
Bring to the Lord glory for His name; take up sacrifices and enter His courts.
  1 Chronicles 16:29 — Name-glory, offerings and approach to God: Both join glory for God's name with bringing offerings and approaching Him. The psalm says sacrifices and entry to His courts; Chronicles says gifts brought before Him and then worship in His holy courts. The ordered ritual actions correspond with their supplied differences.

Psalms 95:9
Bow down to the Lord in His holy court; let all the earth be shaken before Him.
  1 Chronicles 16:29-30 — Worship in holy courts and the earth's response: The psalm commands bowing in God's holy court and that all earth be shaken. Chronicles likewise places bowing in His holy courts beside a call for all earth to fear before Him. Court worship and worldwide response correspond, while shaking and fear are different formulations.

Psalms 95:10
Say among the nations, “The Lord has become King!” For He also established the inhabited world, which will not be shaken. He will judge peoples with uprightness.
  1 Chronicles 16:30-31 — The stable world and announcement of divine reign: The psalm joins announcing the Lord's kingship among nations with the established world that will not be shaken. Chronicles places the earth being set right and unshaken beside saying among nations that the Lord reigns. The matching clauses occur in a different order and mood.

Psalms 95:11
Let the heavens rejoice and the earth exult; let the sea be shaken, and all that fills it.
  1 Chronicles 16:31-32 — Heaven, earth and the full sea responding: Both summon or describe heaven's gladness, earth's exultation and the sea with its fullness. The psalm says the sea should be shaken; Chronicles says it shall resound. The sequence of cosmic participants is shared without making those sea verbs identical.

Psalms 95:12
The plains will rejoice, and everything in them; then all the trees of the woodland will exult
  1 Chronicles 16:32-33 — Field and forest in the parallel praise sequence: The psalm moves from rejoicing plains and their contents to exulting woodland trees. Chronicles moves from the tree of the field and everything in it to glad forest trees. Their field-to-forest sequence corresponds while the supplied plains and tree-of-field wording remains distinct.

Psalms 95:13
before the Lord, for He is coming, for He is coming to judge the earth. He will judge the inhabited world with righteousness and peoples with His truth.
  1 Chronicles 16:33 — The Lord's coming to judge the earth: Both explain nature's rejoicing by the Lord's coming to judge the earth. The psalm repeats that He is coming and specifies righteousness and truth; Chronicles says He has come and does not include those final qualifications in this unit.

Psalms 96:1
Of David, when his land is being established. The Lord has become King! Let the earth exult; let many islands rejoice.
  Isaiah 42:10-12 — Islands included in worldwide rejoicing and praise: Psalm 96 calls the earth and many islands to rejoice at the Lord's kingship. Isaiah's new hymn addresses the earth's extremity and island inhabitants and announces praise in the islands. Both expressly extend the celebration to islands, without assigning the psalm's land-establishment heading a historical event.

Psalms 96:2
Cloud and gloom surround Him; righteousness and judgment are the establishment of His throne.
  Psalms 88:15 — Righteousness and judgment establish the divine throne: Both place righteousness and judgment in the establishment or preparation of God's throne. Psalm 96 surrounds Him with cloud and gloom; Psalm 88 has mercy and truth go before His face. The shared throne formula is distinguished from their surrounding imagery.

Psalms 96:3
Fire will go ahead before Him and set His enemies aflame all around.
  Psalms 49:3 — Fire preceding the divine approach: Psalm 96 describes fire going before God and burning His enemies. Psalm 49 describes God coming manifestly with fire burning before Him and a surrounding tempest. Both place fire before the approaching God, with different descriptions of its surroundings and effects.

Psalms 96:4
His lightning flashes shone upon the inhabited world; the earth saw and was shaken.
  Psalms 76:19 — World-illuminating lightning and the shaken earth: Both say divine lightning shone upon the inhabited world and that the earth shook. Psalm 76 additionally describes the voice of thunder in the wheel. The lightning-and-earth response is a close verbal parallel, with that additional image left in the target.

Psalms 96:5
The mountains melted like wax before the Lord, before the Lord of all the earth.
  Micah 1:3-4 — Wax-melting imagery at the Lord's advent: The psalm has mountains melt like wax before the Lord. Micah describes the Lord descending, mountains shaken beneath Him and valleys melting like wax before fire. The wax-melting image accompanies divine approach in both; mountains and valleys are not conflated.

Psalms 96:6
The heavens announced His righteousness, and all the peoples saw His glory.
  Psalms 49:6 — The heavens announce God's righteousness: Both expressly give the heavens the action of announcing God's righteousness. Psalm 49 connects this with God as judge; Psalm 96 joins it to all peoples seeing His glory. The shared announcement clause retains its different neighboring explanations.

Psalms 96:7
Let all who bow down to carved images be ashamed, those who boast in their idols. Bow down to Him, all His angels!
  Hebrews 1:6 — The command for angelic worship echoed in Hebrews: The psalm commands all His angels to bow down. Hebrews commands all God's angels to worship when the firstborn is brought into the inhabited world. The angelic worship summons closely corresponds; this does not claim it is the only related scriptural formulation.

Psalms 96:8
Zion heard and rejoiced, and the daughters of Judea exulted because of Thy judgments, Lord.
  Psalms 47:12 — Zion and Judea's daughters rejoice at God's judgments: Both join Zion's rejoicing with the exultation of Judea's daughters because of the Lord's judgments. Psalm 96 reports that Zion heard and rejoiced; Psalm 47 frames the shared response as a summons. The location, participants and reason all correspond.

Psalms 96:9
For Thou art the Lord, the Most High over all the earth; Thou wast greatly exalted above all gods.
  Psalms 82:19 — The Most High over all the earth: Both identify the Lord as Most High over all the earth. Psalm 96 adds His exaltation above all gods, while Psalm 82 asks opponents to recognize His name and unique height. The shared worldwide Most High formula is explicit.

Psalms 96:10
You who love the Lord, hate evil! The Lord guards the souls of His holy ones; He will rescue them from the hand of sinners.
  Amos 5:14-15 — Love and hatred in moral allegiance: The psalm commands those loving the Lord to hate evil. Amos quotes We have hated evil things and loved good things while urging restoration of judgment. Both pair love and hatred with moral allegiance; Amos's reported words are not converted into the psalm's imperative, and the objects of love differ.

Psalms 96:11
Light has dawned for the righteous, and gladness for the upright in heart.
  Proverbs 4:18 — Light associated with righteous life: Psalm 96 says light has dawned for the righteous. Proverbs describes righteous ways shining like light and advancing toward the day. Both portray righteous life through illumination, with a light dawning for people distinguished from their ways themselves shining.

Psalms 96:12
Rejoice in the Lord, you righteous, and give thanks at the remembrance of His holiness.
  Psalms 29:5 — Thanksgiving at the remembrance of holiness: Both call for thanks at the remembrance of God's holiness, addressed to righteous or holy people in a context of praise. The distinctive remembrance-of-holiness thanksgiving formula is repeated, with differing surrounding commands.

Psalms 97:1
A psalm of David. Sing to the Lord a new song, for the Lord has done wonders. His right hand and His holy arm won salvation for Him.
  Isaiah 52:10 — The holy arm and divine salvation: Psalm 97 says God's right hand and holy arm won salvation for Him. Isaiah says the Lord will uncover His holy arm before nations and the earth's ends will see salvation from God. The holy-arm and salvation combination is explicit, with achieved salvation and future manifestation distinguished.

Psalms 97:2
The Lord made His salvation known; before the nations He revealed His righteousness.
  Isaiah 52:10 — Salvation made visible before the nations: Psalm 97 reports salvation made known and righteousness revealed before the nations. Isaiah promises the unveiling of God's holy arm before all nations and salvation seen at earth's ends. Both present divine rescue publicly to nations; righteousness and the holy arm remain their distinct revealed objects.

Psalms 97:3
He remembered His mercy to Jacob and His truth to the house of Israel. All the ends of the earth saw the salvation of our God.
  Isaiah 52:10 — Earth's ends seeing God's salvation: The psalm reports all earth's ends seeing the salvation of our God. Isaiah predicts all earth's ends seeing salvation from God. The close concluding formula corresponds, while the psalm additionally names remembered mercy to Jacob and truth to Israel.

Psalms 97:4
Shout to God, all the earth; sing and exult and make music.
  Psalms 65:1-2 — All the earth summoned to shout and make music: Both address all the earth with a shout to God and an accompanying call to music. Psalm 97 adds singing and exultation; Psalm 65 specifies music to His name and glory to His praise. The repeated worldwide acclamation-and-music pattern supplies the link.

Psalms 97:5
Make music to the Lord with a lyre, with a lyre and the sound of a psalm.
  Psalms 146:7 — Music to God with a lyre: Psalm 97 repeats the lyre in its command to make music to the Lord. Psalm 146 likewise commands music to God with a lyre after beginning with thanksgiving. The particular instrument and God-directed musical command correspond without identifying a single performance.

Psalms 97:6
With hammered trumpets and the sound of a horn trumpet, shout before the King, the Lord.
  Numbers 10:2 — Trumpets made of hammered work: The psalm names hammered trumpets in its acclamation before the Lord as King. Numbers orders two silver trumpets made of hammered work, within instructions that include their use at feasts and offerings. The manufacture and instrument type correspond; the psalm's horn trumpet is a separate named instrument.

Psalms 97:7
Let the sea be shaken, and all that fills it, the inhabited world and those who dwell in it.
  Psalms 95:11 — The sea and its fullness in the creation summons: Both call for the sea and all that fills it to be shaken. Psalm 97 adds the inhabited world and its dwellers, whereas Psalm 95 first calls heaven and earth to rejoice. The closely repeated sea clause links the two creation-wide summonses.

Psalms 97:8
Rivers will clap their hands together; the mountains will exult,
  Isaiah 55:12 — Clapping nature and rejoicing mountains: Psalm 97 has rivers clap their hands and mountains exult. Isaiah has mountains and hills leap in joy and field trees clap their branches. Both combine joyful mountains with clapping nonhuman creation, preserving rivers versus trees and hands versus branches.

Psalms 97:9
for He has come to judge the earth. He will judge the inhabited world with righteousness and peoples with uprightness.
  Psalms 95:13 — The repeated closing announcement of world judgment: Both close their creation rejoicing with the Lord's coming to judge the earth and world in righteousness. Psalm 97 says He has come and peoples will be judged with uprightness; Psalm 95 says He is coming and gives His truth. The parallel ending preserves tense and final-attribute differences.

Psalms 98:1
A psalm of David. The Lord has become King! Let peoples be angry. He sits upon the cherubim; let the earth be shaken.
  1 Samuel 4:4 — The Lord seated upon the cherubim: Psalm 98 describes the reigning Lord seated upon the cherubim. The account in 1 Samuel uses the same throne description when the Lord's ark is carried from Shiloh. The shared divine title does not identify the psalm with that battle or endorse the Israelites' expectation that bringing the ark guarantees victory.

Psalms 98:3
Let them give thanks to Thy great name, for it is fearsome and holy.
  Psalms 110:9 — The divine name as holy and fearsome: Psalm 98 calls for thanks to the great name because it is fearsome and holy. Psalm 110 concludes its redemption-and-covenant statement by calling His name holy and fearsome. The paired attributes explicitly qualify the divine name in both.

Psalms 98:4
The King’s honor loves judgment. Thou didst prepare uprightness; judgment and righteousness in Jacob Thou hast done.
  Proverbs 16:12 — Kingship, righteousness and preparation: Psalm 98 says the King's honor loves judgment and that God prepared uprightness. Proverbs says a ruling throne is prepared with righteousness and evil is abhorrent to a king. Both connect kingship, preparation and righteous order: uprightness is prepared in the psalm, while a throne is prepared with righteousness in the proverb.

Psalms 98:5
Exalt the Lord our God and bow down at His footstool, for He is holy.
  Psalms 131:7 — Bowing toward the place of God's feet: Psalm 98 commands bowing at God's footstool. Psalm 131 resolves to enter His dwellings and bow toward the place where His feet stood. The location associated with divine feet and the act of bowing correspond; no independent object identity is asserted for the footstool.

Psalms 98:6
Moses and Aaron were among His priests, and Samuel among those who call on His name. They called upon the Lord, and He heard them,
  1 Samuel 7:8-9 — Samuel calls upon the Lord and is heard: The psalm names Samuel among those who called on God and were heard. The narrative reports Israel requesting his intercession, his cry for Israel and the Lord hearing him. The named person and call-and-hearing action provide the concrete narrative link; the psalm also names Moses and Aaron.

Psalms 98:7
He spoke to them in a pillar of cloud. They kept His testimonies and the ordinances He gave them.
  Exodus 33:9-11 — Divine speech in the pillar of cloud: The psalm describes speech to them in a pillar of cloud. Exodus describes the pillar descending at the tent while God speaks with Moses. The cloud-pillar and speech correspond; Moses is the target's named recipient, and Samuel is not inserted into that scene through the psalm's collective pronoun.

Psalms 98:8
Lord our God, Thou heardest them; God, Thou wast gracious to them and tookest vengeance for all their practices.
  Numbers 14:20-23 — Graciousness together with judgment: The psalm joins God's graciousness with vengeance for practices.
