Numbers places God's gracious answer to Moses beside exclusion from the land for those who repeatedly tested Him. Both hold graciousness and consequences together, without equating all referents of the psalm's pronouns with that congregation.

Psalms 98:9
Exalt the Lord our God and bow down toward His holy mountain, for holy is the Lord our God.
  Psalms 98:5 — Same-chapter framing by exaltation and bowing: Verses 5 and 9 repeat Exalt the Lord our God, the call to bow, and the reason of holiness. Footstool and holy mountain give the two locations. The refrain frames the intervening account of mediators calling upon God and His answer and judgment.

Psalms 99:1
A psalm for thanksgiving. Shout to the Lord, all the earth.
  Psalms 97:4 — The all-earth acclamation repeated: Psalm 99 opens its thanksgiving with the command that all the earth shout to the Lord. Psalm 97 uses the same all-earth shout to God before further musical commands. The concise worldwide acclamation formula links these neighboring praise psalms.

Psalms 99:2
Serve the Lord with gladness; enter before Him with exultation.
  Deuteronomy 28:47 — Serving the Lord with gladness: Psalm 99 commands service to the Lord with gladness. Deuteronomy identifies failure to serve Him with gladness and a good heart as grounds for the ensuing judgment. The same service-and-gladness combination appears as positive summons and reproach for its absence.

Psalms 99:3
Know that the Lord—He is God. He made us, and not we; His people and the sheep of His pasture.
  Psalms 94:6-7 — The Creator's people and the sheep of His pasture: Psalm 99 joins God's making us with His people and the sheep of His pasture. Psalm 94 likewise joins the Lord who made us with our God, the people of His pasture and sheep of His hand. The combined creation-and-flock confession corresponds, including the differing hand clause.

Psalms 99:4
Enter His gates with thanksgiving, His courts with hymns. Give thanks to Him; praise His name.
  Psalms 117:19-20 — Entering the Lord's gates with thanksgiving: Psalm 99 commands entry through God's gates with thanksgiving. Psalm 117 requests the opening of righteousness's gates and says the entrant will thank the Lord, then names the Lord's gate. The gate, entry and thanksgiving sequence is explicit in both.

Psalms 99:5
For the Lord is kind; His mercy is for ever, and His truth through generation after generation.
  Psalms 116:2 — Enduring mercy and truth in praise: Psalm 99 joins everlasting mercy with truth through generations. Psalm 116 joins mercy grown strong over the people with the Lord's truth remaining for ever. Both pair mercy and truth in praise; the duration clauses are retained with the attributes to which each text attaches them.

Psalms 100:1
Of David. A psalm. I will sing mercy and judgment unto Thee, Lord;
  Psalms 32:5 — Mercy and judgment paired: The speaker will sing mercy and judgment to the Lord. Psalm 32 explicitly says God loves mercy and judgment. The same ethical pair is the song's subject in one passage and the object of divine love in the other.

Psalms 100:2
I will make music and understand in a blameless way. When wilt Thou come to me? I used to walk in innocence of my heart within my house.
  1 Kings 9:4 — Davidic walking with an upright heart: The Davidic psalm reports walking in innocence of heart within the house. God tells Solomon to walk as David walked, with holiness of heart and uprightness. The named Davidic frame and walking-heart combination correspond, without making the household setting the same as Solomon's temple scene.

Psalms 100:3
I did not set a lawless matter before my eyes; I hated those who commit transgressions.
  Psalms 25:4-5 — Personal refusal of wrongdoing and hatred of evildoers: Psalm 100 says the speaker did not set a lawless matter before his eyes and hated transgressors. Psalm 25 says he did not sit with emptiness and hated an assembly of evildoers. The paired avowal of refusal and hatred corresponds while their specified acts remain different.

Psalms 100:5
The one secretly speaking against his neighbor I used to drive away; with a proud eye and an insatiable heart, with that one I did not eat.
  Psalms 14:3 — Speech against the neighbor excluded: Psalm 100 reports driving away one speaking secretly against his neighbor. Psalm 14's qualified dweller does not act deceitfully with his tongue or take up reproach against those nearest him. Both exclude damaging neighbor-directed speech from accepted conduct, with their different actions and agents preserved.

Psalms 100:6
My eyes were on the faithful of the land, that they might sit with me; the one walking in a blameless way used to minister to me.
  Psalms 14:1-2 — Blameless walking as a qualification for dwelling and service: Psalm 100 chooses faithful people to sit with the speaker and the blameless walker to minister to him. Psalm 14 asks who may dwell with the Lord and answers with one walking blamelessly. The shared admission criterion is explicit; the speaker's attendants and God's dwelling guests are distinct roles.

Psalms 100:7
One practicing arrogance did not dwell within my house; one speaking unjust things did not prosper before my eyes.
  Psalms 5:5-6 — Wrongdoers excluded from dwelling and presence: Psalm 100 says a practitioner of arrogance did not dwell in the speaker's house and a speaker of unjust things did not prosper before his eyes. Psalm 5 says an evildoer will not sojourn with God and the lawless will not remain before His eyes. The dwelling-and-before-the-eyes pattern is shared, with human household and divine presence distinguished.

Psalms 100:8
In the mornings I used to kill all the sinners of the land, to destroy from the Lord’s city all who practice lawlessness.
  Jeremiah 21:11-12 — Morning administration in Davidic settings: The Davidic psalm reports morning killing of sinners to remove lawlessness from the Lord's city. Jeremiah commands the house of David to judge in the morning and rescue the plundered. The morning and Davidic public-justice setting corresponds; reported killing and commanded rescue are not equated.

Psalms 101:1
A prayer of a poor person when he becomes despondent and pours out his petition before the Lord.
  Psalms 141:3-4 — Pouring out a plea in despondency: The heading describes a poor person becoming despondent and pouring out a petition before the Lord. Psalm 141 resolves to pour out a plea before Him and recalls the spirit failing within. The pouring-prayer and inward-collapse combination corresponds without identifying the anonymous sufferer with David's cave setting.

Psalms 101:3
Do not turn Thy face away from me. On whatever day I am afflicted, incline Thine ear to me; on whatever day I call upon Thee, hear me quickly.
  Psalms 68:18 — The face, affliction and swift-hearing plea: Both ask God not to turn His face away and join affliction with a plea to hear quickly. Psalm 101 additionally specifies the ear inclined in affliction and quick hearing when called. The shared face-affliction-hearing sequence makes the parallel particular.

Psalms 101:4
For my days vanished like smoke, and my bones were scorched like kindling.
  Job 30:30 — Bones scorched by heat in a lament: The psalm describes bones scorched like kindling. Job says his bones are scorched from heat within his own lament. The particular heated-bones image corresponds, while the kindling comparison and vanished days like smoke remain the psalm's distinct images.

Psalms 101:5
My heart was struck like grass and dried up, for I forgot to eat my bread.
  Psalms 101:12 — Same-chapter recurrence of grass drying up: The struck and dried heart is likened to grass in verse 5; verse 12 again says the speaker dried up like grass while describing declining days. The repeated drying-grass simile links bodily affliction with the poem's later statement of temporal frailty.

Psalms 101:6
From the sound of my groaning, my bone clung to my flesh.
  Lamentations 4:8-9 — The wasted body with flesh or skin against bones: The psalm says the bone clung to the speaker's flesh amid groaning and forgotten food. Lamentations describes skin clinging to bones, drying like wood, in a famine context. Both depict bodily wasting through the bone-covering relationship; flesh and skin and the respective circumstances remain distinct.

Psalms 101:7
I became like a wilderness pelican; I became like a night raven on a house site.
  Isaiah 34:10-11 — Bird imagery with wilderness or deserted land: The psalm compares the speaker to a wilderness pelican and a night raven on a house site. Isaiah places birds including ravens in land destined for enduring desolation. The bird imagery and wilderness or deserted settings offer a limited poetic comparison; the source's house site is not declared identical with Isaiah's deserted land. Night raven and raven are not asserted to be identical species, nor is the sufferer's situation identified with that judgment.

Psalms 101:10
For I ate ashes like bread and mixed my drink with weeping,
  Psalms 79:6 — Tears incorporated into food and drink imagery: Psalm 101 reports ashes eaten like bread and drink mixed with weeping. Psalm 79 asks whether God will feed bread of tears and give tears to drink by measure. Both combine bread, drink and grief, with ashes and the measure kept in their respective texts.

Psalms 101:11
because of Thine anger and wrath, for after lifting me up Thou didst dash me down.
  Psalms 72:18 — Being cast down after elevation: The afflicted speaker says God lifted him and then dashed him down. Psalm 72 describes God casting down deceitful people when they were lifted up. The vertical reversal corresponds; comparison of the image does not assign the target's moral characterization of the wicked to the source's complainant.

Psalms 101:12
My days bent like a shadow, and I dried up like grass.
  Job 14:1-2 — Brief life pictured with vegetation and a shadow: The psalm joins days bending like a shadow with the speaker drying like grass. Job describes short mortal life as a fallen flower and a fleeing shadow. Both combine vegetation and shadow images for mortality, retaining the different plants and verbs.

Psalms 101:13
But Thou, Lord, remainest for ever, and Thy memorial is from generation to generation.
  Exodus 3:15 — God's memorial through generations: The psalm confesses the Lord's perpetual endurance and memorial from generation to generation. God's speech to Moses names His everlasting name and memorial from generation to generation. The repeated memorial-and-generations formula supplies the close verbal connection.

Psalms 101:14
Thou wilt arise and have compassion on Zion, for it is time to have compassion on her; the time has come.
  Zechariah 1:12-17 — Renewed compassion for Zion and Jerusalem: Psalm 101 announces that the time for compassion on Zion has come. Zechariah moves from asking how long Jerusalem will lack mercy to God's promise to return in compassion and again have mercy on Zion. The shared focus is compassion for Zion and its announced arrival; Zechariah supplies the explicit preceding complaint and its stated year.

Psalms 101:15
For Thy servants took pleasure in her stones and will have compassion on her dust.
  Nehemiah 2:3-5 — Concern for the material remains of Jerusalem: The psalm says God's servants took pleasure in Zion's stones and will have compassion on her dust. Nehemiah grieves over the desolated ancestral city and asks to rebuild it. Their concern for the city's material setting supplies the comparison; stones and dust remain distinct from the narrative's tombs and burned gates.

Psalms 101:16
Nations will fear the Lord’s name, and all the kings of the earth Thy glory,
  Psalms 137:4-5 — All earth's kings respond to the Lord's glory: Psalm 101 anticipates nations fearing the Lord's name and all earth's kings His glory. Psalm 137 asks all earth's kings to thank Him for heard words and to sing in His ways because His glory is great. The shared worldwide royal response to divine glory takes different forms: fear in the source and song in the target.

Psalms 101:17
for the Lord will build Zion and will appear in His glory.
  Psalms 146:2 — The Lord builds Zion or Jerusalem: Psalm 101 says the Lord will build Zion; Psalm 146 says the Lord builds Jerusalem and will gather Israel's dispersed. The divine builder and the holy city correspond. The source additionally describes His appearing in glory rather than the target's gathering clause.

Psalms 101:18
He looked upon the prayer of the lowly and did not treat their petition as nothing.
  Psalms 68:34 — Regard for prayer and hearing of the needy: Psalm 101 pairs regard for the lowly's prayer with not treating their petition as nothing. Psalm 68 pairs hearing the needy with not despising His bound ones. The paired divine attention and refusal of disregard correspond, with lowly petitioners, needy people and bound ones kept as each passage names them.

Psalms 101:19
Let this be written for another generation, and the people being created will praise the Lord,
  Psalms 77:4-7 — Divine acts transmitted to a future generation: The psalm commands a written record for another generation and says the people being created will praise. Psalm 77 transmits divine works to sons yet to be born so they may hope in God. Both address a future audience, with writing and repeated telling, and created people and future sons, distinguished.

Psalms 101:20
for He leaned out from His holy height; the Lord looked from heaven upon the earth,
  Deuteronomy 26:15 — Looking from the holy heavenly dwelling: Psalm 101 says the Lord looked from His holy height and from heaven upon earth. Deuteronomy petitions Him to look from His holy house, from heaven, and bless Israel and the land. The holy heavenly vantage and downward attention correspond, in report and petition respectively.

Psalms 101:21
to hear the groaning of those in bonds, to release the sons of those put to death,
  Psalms 78:11 — Groaning prisoners and the sons of those put to death: Psalm 101 joins hearing the groaning of those in bonds with releasing sons of those put to death. Psalm 78 asks that the same groaning enter God's presence and that He preserve sons of those put to death. The two-part formulation is close; the sons are not replaced with people merely sentenced to death.

Psalms 101:22
to announce the Lord’s name in Zion and His praise in Jerusalem,
  Isaiah 12:4-6 — The Lord's name proclaimed in Zion's praise: Psalm 101 states the purpose of announcing the Lord's name in Zion and His praise in Jerusalem. Isaiah calls for His name and deeds to be proclaimed and for Zion's inhabitants to rejoice. The name-proclamation and Zion-praise combination corresponds without identifying a single occasion.

Psalms 101:23
when peoples are gathered together, and kingdoms, to serve the Lord.
  Zechariah 8:20-22 — Peoples gathered to seek and serve God in Jerusalem: The psalm places gathered peoples and kingdoms serving the Lord after the proclamation in Zion and Jerusalem. Zechariah describes many peoples and nations coming to seek His presence in Jerusalem. The multinational gathering and city-directed worship correspond, while serving and seeking remain distinct expressions.

Psalms 101:24
He answered him in the way of his strength: Tell me the fewness of my days.
  Psalms 38:5 — Asking to know the brief measure of one's days: The psalm's direct speech asks to be told the fewness of my days. Psalm 38 asks God to make known life's limit and number of days. The requests concerning life's measure correspond; the source's opening he and him are not assigned unexpressed identities.

Psalms 101:25
Do not take me up at the halfway point of my days; Thy years extend through a generation of generations.
  Isaiah 38:9-10 — The fear of losing the remaining span of life: Psalm 101 asks not to be taken up at the halfway point of the speaker's days. Hezekiah's prayer speaks of leaving remaining years behind at the height of his days and Hades' gates. The untimely interruption of life corresponds without identifying the psalm's sufferer as Hezekiah.

Psalms 101:26
At the beginning Thou, Lord, didst found the earth, and the heavens are works of Thy hands.
  Hebrews 1:10 — The foundation of earth and heavens quoted in Hebrews: Hebrews quotes the Lord founding earth in the beginning and the heavens as works of His hands. The passage belongs to its scriptural presentation concerning the Son. The source's creation statement and the target's reception of it are both explicit.

Psalms 101:27
They will perish, but Thou wilt remain; all will grow old like a garment. Like a cloak Thou wilt change them, and they will be changed.
  Hebrews 1:11-12 — The perishing heavens and garment image quoted: Hebrews repeats the created things perishing while the Lord remains, their growing old like a garment and their change. The psalm says He will change them like a cloak; Hebrews says He will roll them up like a cloak and they will be changed. Those verbs are preserved.

Psalms 101:28
But Thou art the same, and Thy years will not fail.
  Hebrews 1:12 — Unchanging identity and unfailing years quoted: Hebrews reproduces the concluding assertion that Thou art the same and Thy years will not fail. It follows the quoted garment imagery in the target and forms part of the contrast between the enduring Son and created things in that chapter.

Psalms 101:29
The children of Thy servants will make their dwelling, and their offspring will be established for ever.
  Psalms 68:36-37 — Servants' offspring dwelling in restored Zion: Psalm 101 ends with servants' children dwelling and their offspring established for ever. Psalm 68 joins God's saving Zion and rebuilding Judea's cities with His servants' offspring possessing it and lovers of His name dwelling there. The restoration, servants' offspring and continuing dwelling cluster corresponds.

Psalms 102:1
Of David. Bless the Lord, my soul, and all that is within me, His holy name.
  Psalms 103:1 — The soul summoned to bless the Lord: Both psalms open with a Davidic designation and the speaker summoning his own soul to bless the Lord. Psalm 102 adds all within him and the holy name; Psalm 103 proceeds to God's greatness and clothing.

Psalms 102:2
Bless the Lord, my soul, and do not forget all His repayments:
  Deuteronomy 8:10-14 — Blessing God without forgetting His benefits: The psalm joins blessing the Lord to not forgetting His repayments. Deuteronomy likewise joins blessing God for the good land to warnings against forgetting Him amid received abundance; its material examples remain its own.

Psalms 102:3
who is gracious toward all your lawless deeds, who heals all your diseases;
  Mark 2:5-12 — Mercy toward sins and bodily healing: The psalm joins graciousness toward lawless deeds with healing diseases. Mark joins Jesus' explicit forgiveness of the paralytic's sins with healing. The paired acts of mercy and healing correspond, with the wording and setting distinct.

Psalms 102:4
who redeems your life from corruption, who crowns you with mercy and compassions;
  Psalms 48:16 — Redemption from corruption and Hades: Psalm 102 blesses the one redeeming life from corruption; Psalm 48 expects God to redeem the speaker's soul from Hades' hand. Both describe divine rescue in relation to death while preserving corruption and Hades as different expressions.

Psalms 102:5
who fills your desire with good things; your youth will be renewed like an eagle’s.
  Isaiah 40:31 — Renewal pictured through an eagle: The psalm describes youth renewed like an eagle. Isaiah describes those enduring for God exchanging strength and growing wings like eagles. The shared renewal and eagle imagery does not make youth and strength identical wording.

Psalms 102:6
The Lord does deeds of mercy and judgment for all who are wronged.
  Psalms 145:7 — God's justice for the wronged: Psalm 102 joins mercy and judgment for all the wronged; Psalm 145 says the Lord does justice for those wronged. The common recipients and judicial action provide the particular correspondence.

Psalms 102:7
He made His ways known to Moses, His wishes to the sons of Israel.
  Deuteronomy 4:5 — Divine instruction through Moses to Israel: The psalm says God made His ways known to Moses and His wishes to Israel's sons. Moses in Deuteronomy says he showed Israel ordinances and judgments as the Lord commanded him. The comparison concerns that named channel of instruction, with the different terms preserved.

Psalms 102:8
The Lord is compassionate and merciful, patient and abundant in mercy.
  Exodus 34:6 — The compassionate and merciful Lord: The psalm calls the Lord compassionate, merciful, patient and abundant in mercy. Exodus's proclamation contains compassionate, merciful, slow to anger, abundant in mercy and true. The closely shared cluster is retained with patient and slow to anger distinguished.

Psalms 102:9
He will not be angry to the end, nor maintain wrath for ever.
  Isaiah 57:16 — Anger that does not last forever: The psalm denies enduring anger and perpetual wrath; Isaiah's divine speech denies taking vengeance forever or always being angry. The parallel concerns the stated temporal limit, without equating every recipient or surrounding circumstance.

Psalms 102:11
for according to heaven’s height above the earth, the Lord strengthened His mercy toward those who fear Him.
  Psalms 56:11 — Heavenly height and the greatness of mercy: Psalm 102 uses heaven's height above earth to describe mercy strengthened toward those fearing God. Psalm 56 says mercy was magnified to the heavens. The height imagery corresponds while their prepositions and statements about recipients remain distinct.

Psalms 102:12
As far as the east is distant from the west, He removed our lawless deeds from us.
  Micah 7:19 — Sins removed through a spatial image: The psalm measures removal of lawless deeds by the distance of east from west. Micah depicts sins thrown into the sea's depths. Both use great spatial separation to portray removal of sins, with horizontal distance and depth kept distinct.

Psalms 102:13
As a father has compassion on children, the Lord had compassion on those who fear Him,
  Isaiah 63:15-16 — Fatherhood and divine compassion: The psalm compares the Lord's compassion with a father's compassion for children. Isaiah's prayer asks about withheld compassion and confesses God as Father. The psalm states compassion; the prayer seeks it amid felt deprivation.

Psalms 102:14
for He knew our formation. Remember that we are dust.
  Genesis 2:7 — Human formation and dust: The psalm connects knowledge of human formation with the imperative to remember that we are dust. Genesis narrates God forming the human from earth's dust. The source's imperative is preserved rather than changed into a statement that God remembers dust.

Psalms 102:15
A man—his days are like grass; like a flower of the field, so he will flower,
  Isaiah 40:6-8 — Human life as grass and a field flower: Both passages compare human life or glory with grass and a flower. Isaiah explicitly describes their withering and contrasts God's enduring word; Psalm 102 places the flower image within its account of short human life.

Psalms 102:16
for a wind passed through him, and he will not exist, and he will no longer recognize his place.
  Job 7:9-10 — Vanishing life and the unrecognized place: The psalm's mortal no longer recognizes his place after passing away; Job says the dead person's place will no longer recognize him. The unusual place-recognition image corresponds, but its subject and object are reversed and remain distinct.

Psalms 102:17
But the Lord’s mercy is from eternity to eternity toward those who fear Him, and His righteousness toward children’s children,
  Luke 1:50 — Mercy toward those fearing God: The psalm sets God's mercy from eternity to eternity toward those fearing Him and then mentions righteousness for children's children. Luke sets mercy for generations upon those fearing Him. The mercy-and-fear formula is shared without merging the different temporal clauses.

Psalms 102:18
to those who keep His covenant and remember His commandments to do them.
  Deuteronomy 7:9 — Mercy and commandment keeping within covenant: The psalm describes covenant keepers who remember commands to do them. Deuteronomy describes God keeping covenant and mercy with those loving Him and keeping His commands. The actors of covenant keeping differ, while the recipients' obedience corresponds.

Psalms 102:19
The Lord prepared His throne in heaven, and His kingdom has dominion over all.
  Psalms 10:4 — The Lord's heavenly throne: Both locate the Lord's throne in heaven. Psalm 102 connects it with universal kingship; Psalm 10 proceeds to His examination of human beings. The shared throne location supports the comparison without merging the following claims.

Psalms 102:20
Bless the Lord, all His angels, mighty in strength, doing His word, so as to hear the voice of His words.
  Psalms 148:2 — Angels summoned to praise God: Psalm 102 commands the Lord's angels to bless Him and describes their obedience. Psalm 148 commands all His angels to praise Him. The common summons and named recipients correspond; the obedience description belongs to Psalm 102.

Psalms 102:21
Bless the Lord, all His powers, His ministers who do His will.
  Psalms 148:2 — All God's powers summoned to praise: Psalm 102 summons all the Lord's powers to bless Him, describing ministers doing His will. Psalm 148 likewise summons all His powers to praise Him. The shared group and act support the link without interpreting powers beyond the wording.

Psalms 102:22
Bless the Lord, all His works, in every place of His dominion. Bless the Lord, my soul.
  Psalms 102:1 — Same-chapter closing return to the soul's blessing: After summoning angels, powers and all works, the final unit returns to the opening summons for the speaker's soul to bless the Lord. This same-chapter comparison identifies the psalm's closing return to its opening address.

Psalms 103:1
Of David. Bless the Lord, my soul. Lord my God, Thou hast become very great; Thou hast clothed Thyself in praise and dignity,
  Psalms 102:1 — The soul summoned to bless the Lord: The two openings share a Davidic designation and the summons for the speaker's soul to bless the Lord. Their following praise differs: God's greatness and clothing here, the holy name and all within the speaker in Psalm 102.

Psalms 103:2
wrapping Thyself in light like a garment, stretching out heaven like a hide curtain;
  Isaiah 40:22 — Heaven stretched like a covering: The psalm describes heaven stretched like a hide curtain; Isaiah says God stretched it like a tent for dwelling. The covering comparison and divine action correspond, with Isaiah's vault and the psalm's light garment retained as separate details.

Psalms 103:3
He roofs His upper chambers in waters, makes clouds His means of ascent, and walks on the wings of winds.
  Psalms 17:11 — Divine movement on the wings of winds: Both describe God moving on the wings of winds. Psalm 17 also names cherubim and flight; Psalm 103 speaks of upper chambers and clouds as a means of ascent. These additional images are not collapsed into one description.

Psalms 103:4
He makes His angels winds, and His ministers a burning fire.
  Hebrews 1:7 — Angels and fiery ministers in a cited saying: Hebrews introduces a saying concerning angels that closely follows this psalm's angels and fiery ministers. The psalm says winds and burning fire, while Hebrews says spirits and a flame of fire; the textual differences are preserved within the quotation relationship.

Psalms 103:5
He founded the earth on its stability; it will not be tilted for an age of ages.
  Psalms 92:1 — The established earth that will not be shaken: Psalm 103 describes the earth founded in stability and not tilted for ages. Psalm 92 describes the inhabited world established so it will not be shaken. Both connect divine establishment with lasting stability, preserving their different nouns and verbs.

Psalms 103:6
The deep is His covering like a garment; waters will stand upon the mountains.
  Genesis 7:19-20 — Waters above the mountains: The psalm pictures the deep as a covering and says waters will stand upon mountains; Genesis narrates floodwaters covering high mountains. The particular water-over-mountains image corresponds without establishing that the psalm identifies the same historical event or tense.

Psalms 103:7
At Thy rebuke they will flee; at the voice of Thy thunder they will become afraid.
  Psalms 17:14-16 — Thunder and divine rebuke before the waters: Psalm 103 depicts waters fleeing at God's rebuke and fearing His thunderous voice. Psalm 17 joins heavenly thunder with waters' springs appearing at His rebuke. The shared rebuke, thunder and water imagery is retained without equating fleeing with appearing.

Psalms 103:9
Thou didst set a boundary they will not pass, nor will they return to cover the earth.
  Job 38:10-11 — The boundary waters cannot cross: The psalm says God set a boundary the waters will not pass. Job's divine speech describes setting sea boundaries and declaring that it will not pass beyond them. The imposed limit is particular; the psalm's further prevention of earth-covering remains its own.

Psalms 103:10
He sends springs into the ravines; waters will pass between the mountains.
  Deuteronomy 8:7 — Springs flowing through varied terrain: The psalm describes springs sent into ravines and water passing between mountains. Deuteronomy describes the good land's torrents and deep springs flowing through plains and hills. The shared springs and terrain pattern is compared without identifying the psalm's entire scene with that land.

Psalms 103:13
He waters mountains from His upper chambers; the earth will be filled with the fruit of Thy works.
  Deuteronomy 11:11-12 — Hills watered from above: The psalm describes God watering mountains from His upper chambers. Deuteronomy describes a land of hills and plains drinking heaven's rain and receiving God's care. Both connect elevated terrain with water from above, while the upper-chamber image remains the psalm's.

Psalms 103:14
He makes grass spring up for livestock and green plants for the service of humans, to bring bread out of the earth;
  Deuteronomy 11:14-15 — Grass for cattle and food for people: Both passages connect God's provision of grass for cattle with human food. Deuteronomy names grain, wine and oil and then grass and human eating; the psalm names green plants for human service and bread from earth.

Psalms 103:15
and wine gladdens the human heart, to brighten the face with oil, and bread makes the human heart firm.
  Judges 9:13 — Wine as human gladness: The psalm says wine gladdens the human heart. In Jotham's tree parable the vine calls its wine human beings' gladness from God. The shared function of wine is particular; the parable does not supply the psalm's separate oil and bread statements.

Psalms 103:16
The trees of the plain will be filled, the cedars of Lebanon that He planted;
  Ezekiel 17:22-23 — God planting a cedar: The psalm names Lebanon's cedars as trees God planted. Ezekiel describes taking a part from a cedar and planting it on Israel's mountain, where it will produce a shoot and become a great cedar. The shared cedar-and-planting image does not identify the natural description with the prophetic scene.

Psalms 103:17
there sparrows will nest; the heron’s house leads them.
  Psalms 83:4 — A sparrow and the provision of a home: Psalm 103 describes sparrows nesting; Psalm 83 describes a sparrow finding a house alongside a turtledove's nest at God's altars. The nesting and housing comparison preserves the different settings and birds and does not interpret the separate heron's-house clause.

Psalms 103:18
The high mountains are for deer; a rock is a refuge for rock rabbits.
  Proverbs 30:26 — Rock as an animal refuge and dwelling: The psalm calls rock a refuge for rock rabbits; Proverbs describes rock badgers making houses in rocks. The particular rocky dwelling imagery corresponds. The different animal names are preserved, and their zoological identity is not established by this retained comparison.

Psalms 103:19
He made the moon for seasons; the sun knew its setting.
  Genesis 1:14-16 — Heavenly lights marking time: The psalm names the moon's seasons and the sun's setting. Genesis describes heavenly lights for seasons, days and years, with greater and lesser lights ruling day and night. The shared ordering of time is retained with their different naming and functions explicit.

Psalms 103:20
Thou didst set darkness, and night came to be; in it all the beasts of the forest will pass,
  Genesis 1:4-5 — Darkness and the ordering of night: The psalm addresses God as setting darkness so that night comes; Genesis describes God dividing light from darkness and naming darkness Night. The relation concerns the divine ordering of darkness and night; the forest beasts occur only in the psalm.

Psalms 103:21
young lions roaring to seize prey and to seek food for themselves from God.
  Job 38:39 — Lions and provision of their food: The psalm says young lions seek their food from God. The Lord asks Job whether he will hunt food for lions, also mentioning dragons. The particular feeding question is compared with the psalm's statement, without turning Job's question into an identical assertion.

Psalms 103:24
How great Thy works have become, Lord! Thou madest all things in wisdom; the earth was filled with Thy possession.
  Proverbs 3:19 — The earth's making through wisdom: Psalm 103 says God made all His works in wisdom and the earth was filled with His possession. Proverbs says God founded the earth by wisdom. The shared wisdom-and-earth creation claim supports the link while retaining the psalm's wider scope.

Psalms 103:25
Here is the sea, great and spacious; there are creeping things without number, living creatures small with great.
  Genesis 1:20-21 — The sea's multitude of living creatures: The psalm portrays a spacious sea containing innumerable moving creatures, small and great. Genesis narrates the waters bringing forth moving life and God making great sea creatures. The marine-life description corresponds without identifying every creature or equating the inventories.

Psalms 103:26
There ships pass, and this dragon, which Thou didst form to play with it.
  Job 41:25 — A remarkable creature formed for play: The psalm's sea dragon is formed to play with it; Job's creature, identified as a dragon in the preceding discourse, is made to be played with by God's angels. The unusual formation-and-play relation is retained without importing the angels into the psalm or settling zoological identity.

Psalms 103:27
All wait for Thee to give them food at the right time.
  Psalms 144:15 — All creatures awaiting food at the right time: Both describe all looking or waiting toward God and receiving food at the right time. Psalm 144 specifies their eyes hoping; Psalm 103 follows its account of the sea's creatures. The shared dependence and timing form the close comparison.

Psalms 103:28
When Thou givest to them, they will gather; when Thou openest Thy hand, all things will be filled with goodness.
  Psalms 144:16 — God's opened hand filling living creatures: Both associate God's opened hand with creatures being filled. Psalm 103 says all are filled with goodness and also gather what He gives; Psalm 144 names every living creature and good pleasure. The distinctions remain within the close shared image.

Psalms 103:29
When Thou turnest Thy face away, they will be troubled. Thou wilt take back their breath, and they will fail and return to their dust.
  Ecclesiastes 12:7 — Death and return to dust: The psalm describes withdrawal of breath, failure and return to dust. Ecclesiastes describes dust returning to earth and spirit returning to God who gave it. The bodily return corresponds, while breath and spirit and the direction of their movement are not made identical.

Psalms 103:30
Thou wilt send Thy Spirit, and they will be created, and Thou wilt renew the face of the earth.
  Job 33:4 — Divine spirit and the making of life: The psalm says creatures are created when God sends His spirit. Elihu says divine spirit made him. The common creative role of spirit supports the comparison; his additional statement that the Almighty's breath teaches him is not rewritten as giving life.

Psalms 103:31
Let the Lord’s glory be for ever; the Lord will rejoice in His works.
  Isaiah 65:18-19 — God rejoicing in what He makes: The psalm states that the Lord will rejoice in His works. Isaiah joins God's making Jerusalem an exultation and His people joy with His rejoicing over them. The creative delight corresponds, with the psalm's broad works and Isaiah's specific people and city kept distinct.

Psalms 103:32
He looks upon the earth and makes it tremble; He touches the mountains, and they smoke.
  Psalms 143:5 — Mountains smoking at God's touch: Both connect God's touching mountains with their smoking. Psalm 103 describes this within praise of His power; Psalm 143 asks Him to descend and touch them. The uncommon action and effect correspond despite statement and petition differing.

Psalms 103:33
I will sing to the Lord in my life; I will make music to my God as long as I exist.
  Psalms 145:2 — Lifelong praise and music to God: Both promise praise to the Lord throughout life and music to God while the speaker exists. The paired lifelong commitments provide a close verbal and structural correspondence.

Psalms 103:34
May my meditation be sweet to Him; as for me, I will rejoice in the Lord.
  Psalms 18:15 — Pleasing expression and meditation before God: Psalm 103 desires meditation sweet to God. Psalm 18 describes pleasing sayings of the mouth and heart-meditation continually before Him. The paired inward reflection and pleasing expression correspond, while the grammatical objects of sweetness and pleasure remain different.

Psalms 103:35
May sinners vanish from the earth, and lawless people, so that they no longer exist. Bless the Lord, my soul.
  Proverbs 2:22 — Removal of the lawless from the earth: Psalm 103 desires sinners to vanish from earth and the lawless no longer to exist. Proverbs says ungodly ways perish from the land and the lawless are driven out. The lawless and their removal correspond; ways and people, and vanishing and expulsion, remain distinct.
  Psalms 103:1 — Same-chapter return to the opening blessing: The last unit returns to the opening summons for the speaker's soul to bless the Lord after the creation praise and wish concerning sinners. This same-chapter comparison marks the deliberate framing summons.

Psalms 104:1
Hallelujah. Give thanks to the Lord and call upon His name; announce His works among the nations.
  1 Chronicles 16:8 — Thanksgiving and making God's works known: Both summon thanksgiving, calling on God's name and making His works known among peoples or nations. Chronicles places the closely corresponding summons in the praise David appointed through Asaph and his brethren.

Psalms 104:2
Sing to Him and make music to Him; recount all His wonders.
  1 Chronicles 16:9 — Singing and recounting divine wonders: Both command song or music to God and recounting His wonders. Chronicles additionally says to recount them to everyone and identifies them as what the Lord has done.

Psalms 104:3
Be praised in His holy name; let the heart of those seeking the Lord rejoice.
  1 Chronicles 16:10 — The holy name and a rejoicing seeker: Both join the holy name with a seeking heart's rejoicing. The psalm says be praised and speaks of seeking the Lord; Chronicles says praise and speaks of seeking His good pleasure. Those differences remain explicit.

Psalms 104:4
Seek the Lord and be strengthened; seek His face continually.
  1 Chronicles 16:11 — Seeking the Lord and His face continually: Both join seeking the Lord, being strengthened and seeking His face continually. This shared sequence is the particular correspondence.

Psalms 104:5
Remember His wonders that He did, His portents and the judgments of His mouth,
  1 Chronicles 16:12 — Remembering wonders, portents and judgments: Both command remembrance of God's wonders, portents and the judgments of His mouth. The three-part sequence supports a close parallel within the two praise passages.

Psalms 104:6
offspring of Abraham, His servants, sons of Jacob, His chosen ones.
  1 Chronicles 16:13 — The addressed offspring and chosen sons of Jacob: Both address His servants and the sons of Jacob as His chosen ones. The psalm calls the offspring Abraham's, whereas Chronicles calls them Israel's. The corresponding address is retained without silently replacing either ancestral name.

Psalms 104:7
He is the Lord our God; His judgments are in all the earth.
  1 Chronicles 16:14 — Our God and His judgments throughout earth: Both confess Him as the Lord our God and locate His judgments throughout the earth. The corresponding confession follows the addressed ancestral offspring in each passage.

Psalms 104:8
He remembered His covenant for ever, the word He commanded for a thousand generations,
  1 Chronicles 16:15 — The remembered covenant and a thousand generations: Both connect God's remembrance of His covenant with the word commanded for a thousand generations. The psalm says He remembered; Chronicles says He remembers. Their closely shared content is retained with tense distinguished.

Psalms 104:9
which He covenanted with Abraham, and His oath to Isaac;
  1 Chronicles 16:16 — Covenant with Abraham and oath to Isaac: Both name Abraham in the covenant statement and Isaac in the oath statement. The matching named recipients and parallel arrangement provide the direct correspondence.

Psalms 104:10
He established it for Jacob as an ordinance and for Israel as an everlasting covenant,
  1 Chronicles 16:17 — The ordinance for Jacob and everlasting covenant for Israel: Both describe establishment for Jacob as an ordinance and for Israel as an everlasting covenant. This closely matching two-part formulation is retained without adding a different covenant episode.

Psalms 104:11
saying, To you I will give the land of Canaan, the measured portion of your inheritance,
  1 Chronicles 16:18 — Canaan as the measured inheritance: Both quote the promise of Canaan as the measured allotment of inheritance. The named land and measured-inheritance language make the connection particular.

Psalms 104:12
when they were small in number, very few, and sojourners in it,
  1 Chronicles 16:19 — Few and small strangers in the land: Both describe the ancestral group as few, small and dwelling as strangers. The corresponding combination occurs at the same point after the promise of the land.

Psalms 104:13
and passed from nation to nation, from a kingdom to another people.
  1 Chronicles 16:20 — Travel from nation to nation: Both describe movement from nation to nation and from a kingdom to another people. The paired travel formula connects the accounts of ancestral wandering.

Psalms 104:14
He allowed no person to wrong them and rebuked kings on their behalf:
  1 Chronicles 16:21 — Protection from wrong and rebuke of kings: Both describe divine protection of the wandering group and rebuke or reproof of kings on their account. The psalm's wronging and Chronicles' overpowering are preserved as different descriptions of the prevented harm.
  Genesis 20:3-7 — A king warned in protection of Abraham's household: The psalm recalls kings rebuked on the ancestors' behalf. Genesis narrates God's warning to Abimelech concerning Sarah and identifies Abraham as a prophet. This is a concrete instance within that ancestral history, not a claim that every rebuke refers only to this event.

Psalms 104:15
Do not touch My anointed ones, and do not act wickedly against My prophets.
  1 Chronicles 16:22 — The prohibition on touching anointed ones and harming prophets: Both give the prohibition on touching God's anointed ones and acting wickedly against His prophets. The matching two-part command is retained without identifying additional named individuals beyond their contexts.

Psalms 104:16
He called a famine upon the land; He shattered every support of bread.
  Genesis 41:29-32 — The famine of Joseph's history under God's determination: The psalm says God called famine upon the land before recounting Joseph. Genesis presents Joseph's announcement of the coming severe famine as what God will make true and hasten. The psalm's shattered support of bread remains its own image.

Psalms 104:17
He sent a man ahead of them; Joseph was sold as a slave.
  Genesis 45:5 — Joseph sold yet sent ahead by God: The psalm joins Joseph's sale into slavery with God's sending a man ahead. Joseph tells his brothers that they sold him but God sent him before them to preserve life. The named person and both actions provide the close narrative correspondence.

Psalms 104:18
They humbled his feet in fetters; his soul passed through iron,
  Genesis 39:20 — Joseph's confinement: The psalm describes Joseph's fettered feet and soul passing through iron. Genesis names Joseph's placement among the king's prisoners. The connection is the same named prisoner's confinement; Genesis does not supply the fetter or iron descriptions.

Psalms 104:20
A king sent and released him; a ruler of peoples let him go.
  Genesis 41:14 — The ruler sending for Joseph from confinement: The psalm describes the king sending and releasing Joseph. Genesis records Pharaoh sending for him and Joseph being brought out of the fortress. The royal summons and departure from confinement correspond without importing the psalm's wording into Genesis.

Psalms 104:21
He appointed him lord of his house and ruler of all his possession,
  Genesis 41:40-41 — Joseph appointed over the ruler's house: The psalm describes Joseph's appointment over the house and possessions. Pharaoh places him over his house and all Egypt. The house appointment and extended authority correspond, with possessions and territory retained as different formulations.

Psalms 104:23
Israel entered Egypt, and Jacob sojourned in the land of Ham.
  Genesis 46:5-7 — Jacob and Israel entering Egypt: The psalm names Israel's entrance into Egypt and Jacob's sojourn in Ham's land. Genesis narrates Jacob and his descendants entering Egypt. The named migration supplies the link; Ham's designation belongs to the psalm.

Psalms 104:24
He greatly increased His people and made them stronger than their enemies.
  Exodus 1:7-9 — Israel's multiplication and strength in Egypt: The psalm says God increased His people and made them stronger than their enemies. Exodus describes Israel multiplying and growing strong, followed by the Egyptian king calling them stronger than his nation. The growth and relative strength correspond.

Psalms 104:25
He turned their heart to hate His people, to deal deceitfully with His servants.
  Exodus 1:10-12 — Crafty hostility toward the multiplying people: The psalm attributes the turn of hearts toward hatred and deceit to God. Exodus records the king proposing crafty treatment and the Egyptians loathing Israel. The hostility and deceit correspond; Exodus does not explicitly attribute that heart-turning to God in these units.

Psalms 104:26
He sent out Moses His servant, Aaron whom He chose.
  Exodus 6:26-27 — Moses and Aaron commissioned for Israel's departure: The psalm names the sending of Moses and chosen Aaron. Exodus identifies Aaron and Moses as those God commanded to bring Israel out and who spoke to Pharaoh. The named commission supports the connection without transferring every title between accounts.

Psalms 104:27
He set among them the words of His signs and portents in the land of Ham.
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Signs and wonders in Egypt: The psalm says He set among them the words of His signs and portents in Ham's land. Exodus's divine speech promises multiplied signs and wonders in Egypt. The setting and signs correspond without resolving the psalm's pronoun or replacing its words-of-signs formulation with an account of the pair's actions.

Psalms 104:28
He sent out darkness and made it dark, and they provoked His words.
  Exodus 10:21-23 — The Egyptian darkness: Both describe the divinely sent darkness in Egypt. Exodus specifies its duration and the Israelites' light. The psalm's positive clause that they provoked His words is preserved and is not rewritten as an assertion that they did not rebel.

Psalms 104:29
He turned their waters into blood and killed their fish.
  Exodus 7:20-21 — Water turned to blood and fish killed: Both join waters turning to blood with the death of fish in Egypt. Exodus narrates the striking of the river; the psalm condenses the two linked effects.

Psalms 104:30
Their land teemed with frogs in the chambers of their kings.
  Exodus 7:28 — Frogs reaching the ruler's chambers: The psalm describes frogs in the land and kings' chambers. The warning to Pharaoh describes frogs entering houses, bedchambers and beds. The unusual invasion of sleeping chambers connects the plague descriptions, with plural kings and Pharaoh's address distinguished.

Psalms 104:31
He spoke, and dog-flies came, and gnats throughout all their borders.
  Exodus 8:13 — Gnats across Egypt: The psalm's command brings gnats within their borders alongside dog-flies. Exodus narrates gnats arising throughout Egypt after Aaron strikes the dust. This comparison concerns the gnat component, not a claim that both plagues occurred simultaneously in Exodus.
  Exodus 8:20 — Dog-flies across Egypt: The psalm names dog-flies alongside gnats. Exodus narrates a multitude of dog-flies entering Pharaoh's and his attendants' houses and ruining the land. This comparison concerns the distinct dog-fly component of the psalm's compressed account.

Psalms 104:32
He made their rains hail, consuming fire in their land,
  Exodus 9:23-24 — Hail accompanied by fire: Both join hail in Egypt with fire. Exodus explicitly describes fire flaming amid hail, while the psalm describes rain made hail and consuming fire. The combined phenomena provide the particular correspondence.

Psalms 104:33
and struck their vines and fig trees and shattered every tree of their border.
  Exodus 9:25 — The destruction of Egypt's trees: The psalm names struck vines and fig trees and shattered trees. Exodus describes hail shattering trees and striking plants in the plains. The destructive effect on trees corresponds; the specific vines and figs are not supplied by the target unit.

Psalms 104:34
He spoke, and locusts came, and locust larvae without number,
  Exodus 10:13-14 — The arrival of locusts: The psalm reports locusts coming at God's word and adds locust larvae without number. Exodus narrates the Lord's wind bringing locusts in unprecedented numbers over Egypt. The locust arrival and multitude correspond, with larvae remaining an additional psalm term.

Psalms 104:35
and devoured all the grass in their land and devoured the fruit of their land.
  Exodus 10:15 — Locusts consuming vegetation and fruit: Both describe locust devastation of the land's vegetation and fruit. Exodus specifies what the hail had left and the absence of remaining greenery; the psalm compresses the consumption into its account of the plague.

Psalms 104:36
He struck every firstborn in their land, the firstfruits of all their toil,
  Exodus 12:29 — The striking of Egypt's firstborn: Both describe the striking of every firstborn in Egypt. Exodus names the range from Pharaoh's firstborn to a captive woman's and livestock; the psalm uses the additional expression firstfruits of their toil.

Psalms 104:37
and brought them out with silver and gold, and none among their tribes was weak.
  Exodus 12:35-36 — Silver and gold in Israel's departure: The psalm describes bringing Israel out with silver and gold. Exodus narrates Israel asking for these articles and receiving Egyptian favor. The valuables connect the departure accounts; the psalm's absence of weakness among the tribes is not asserted by the target.

Psalms 104:38
Egypt rejoiced at their departure, for fear of them had fallen upon them.
  Exodus 12:33 — Egypt's fear and eagerness for Israel's departure: The psalm says Egypt rejoiced at Israel's going because fear had fallen upon them. Exodus says Egyptians pressed for rapid departure, fearing that all were dying. Their desire for departure and fear correspond, while joy and the quoted fear of death remain distinct.

Psalms 104:39
He spread a cloud as a shelter for them and fire to light the night for them.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — Cloud and night fire during the departure: Both recall cloud and night fire accompanying Israel. The psalm calls the cloud a shelter and the fire illumination; Exodus describes guiding pillars of cloud and fire. Their respective functions and forms are preserved.

Psalms 104:40
They asked, and quail came, and He filled them with bread from heaven.
  Exodus 16:13-15 — Quail and bread in the wilderness: The psalm pairs quail with bread from heaven. Exodus narrates quail in the evening and the next morning's fine substance identified as bread given by the Lord. The paired foods connect these wilderness-provision accounts.

Psalms 104:41
He split a rock, and waters flowed; rivers traveled through waterless places.
  Exodus 17:6 — Water coming from the rock: The psalm describes a split rock and waters flowing in a waterless place. Exodus commands the striking of the rock at Horeb so water will come out for the people, and records Moses doing so. The rock-water provision corresponds without equating every descriptive detail.

Psalms 104:42
For He remembered His holy word to Abraham His servant,
  Exodus 2:24 — Divine remembrance involving Abraham: The psalm says God remembered His holy word to Abraham. Exodus says God remembered His covenant with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob upon hearing Israel's groaning. The named ancestor and remembrance within the deliverance history provide the connection, with word and covenant distinguished.

Psalms 104:43
and brought His people out in exultation and His chosen ones in gladness.
  Exodus 15:20-21 — Celebration within Israel's deliverance from Egypt: The psalm recalls bringing the people out in exultation and gladness. Exodus depicts Miriam leading the women with timbrels, dances and song after the sea rescue. That particular celebration illustrates joy within the deliverance history without making all participants or timing identical.

Psalms 104:44
He gave them countries of nations, and they inherited the toils of peoples,
  Deuteronomy 6:10-11 — Receiving what others had built and cultivated: The psalm says Israel inherited the nations' toils. Deuteronomy describes receiving cities, houses, cisterns, vineyards and olive groves Israel did not build, fill, hew or plant. Those concrete unearned works illuminate the psalm's inheritance description.

Psalms 104:45
so that they might keep His ordinances and diligently seek His law.
  Deuteronomy 4:5 — Keeping ordinances in the inherited land: The psalm gives keeping ordinances and seeking God's law as the purpose following the land gift. Moses describes ordinances and judgments to be carried out in the land Israel will inherit. The shared land-and-obedience connection is retained with their different temporal perspectives.

Psalms 105:1
Hallelujah. Give thanks to the Lord, for He is good; His mercy is for ever.
  1 Chronicles 16:34 — Thanksgiving and enduring mercy: Both summon thanksgiving to the Lord and affirm His mercy forever. The psalm says He is good; Chronicles says it is good. The closely matching refrain is preserved without silently replacing the different pronoun.

Psalms 105:2
Who will speak the Lord’s mighty deeds, make all His praises heard?
  Psalms 39:6 — Speaking of God's deeds and the extent of their recounting: Psalm 105 asks who will speak the Lord's mighty deeds and make all His praises heard. Psalm 39 describes announcing God's wonders, which multiplied beyond number. The comparison concerns the extent of recounting, preserving question and declaration as different forms.

Psalms 105:3
Blessed are those who keep judgment and do righteousness at every time.
  Isaiah 56:1-2 — Blessedness joined to justice and righteousness: The psalm blesses those keeping judgment and doing righteousness at all times. Isaiah commands keeping justice and doing righteousness, then pronounces happiness on the person doing these things. The paired actions and blessing closely correspond.

Psalms 105:4
Remember us, Lord, with the favor of Thy people; visit us with Thy salvation,
  Luke 1:68 — God visiting His people in salvation: The psalm petitions God to remember the speakers with the favor of His people and to visit them with His salvation. Zechariah blesses God for having visited and accomplished redemption for His people. The shared divine visitation connects the communal petition with reported accomplishment, while the descriptions of the recipients remain distinct.

Psalms 105:5
that we may see the goodness of Thy chosen ones, rejoice in the gladness of Thy nation, and be praised with Thine inheritance.
  Psalms 32:12 — God's chosen nation as His inheritance: Psalm 105 seeks the good and joy of God's chosen ones, nation and inheritance. Psalm 32 blesses the nation whose God is the Lord and the people He chose as His inheritance. The distinctive chosen-people and inheritance cluster supports the comparison.

Psalms 105:6
We sinned with our fathers; we acted lawlessly and unjustly.
  1 Kings 8:47 — The communal confession of sin and wrongdoing: The psalm confesses sin, lawlessness and injustice, associating the speakers with their fathers. Solomon's prayer gives a closely corresponding threefold confession for captives returning to God. The shared confession is retained without adding the fathers clause to the target.

Psalms 105:7
Our fathers in Egypt did not understand Thy wonders; they did not remember the abundance of Thy mercy and provoked Thee while going up in the Red Sea.
  Exodus 14:11-12 — Israel's complaint in the Red Sea deliverance: The psalm recalls ancestral provocation while going up in the Red Sea. Exodus records the people's complaint about leaving Egypt before the crossing. The comparison locates complaint within that deliverance history while preserving the different placement in the accounts.

Psalms 105:8
He saved them for His name’s sake, to make His might known.
  Ezekiel 20:9 — Deliverance from Egypt for God's name: The psalm says God saved for His name's sake to make His power known. Ezekiel says He brought Israel out of Egypt so His name would not be profaned before the nations. The divine-name rationale corresponds, with manifestation of power and avoidance of profanation distinguished.

Psalms 105:9
He rebuked the Red Sea, and it dried up; He guided them in the deep as in a wilderness.
  Exodus 14:21-22 — A dry passage through the sea: Both describe the sea made dry and Israel led through it. The psalm describes God's rebuke and passage as through wilderness; Exodus describes Moses' outstretched hand and a violent south wind. The means are not replaced with each other's wording.
  Isaiah 63:12-13 — Passage through the deep pictured as wilderness travel: The psalm describes guidance through the depths as through wilderness. Isaiah joins leading Moses, overpowering water and passage through the abyss like a horse through wilderness. The unusual deep-and-wilderness travel comparison is retained without importing the horse into the psalm or resolving the target's pronoun before Him.

Psalms 105:10
He saved them from the hand of those hating them and redeemed them from the hand of the enemy.
  Exodus 14:30 — Israel rescued from the enemy's hand: The psalm describes rescue and redemption from the hands of haters and an enemy. Exodus states that the Lord rescued Israel from the Egyptians' hand on the sea-deliverance day. The shared hand-rescue statement is anchored in the same narrated setting.

Psalms 105:11
Water covered those afflicting them; not one of them was left.
  Exodus 14:28 — The pursuers covered with none remaining: Both say water covered the hostile force and not one remained. Exodus describes water returning and names chariots, riders and Pharaoh's force; the psalm calls them those afflicting Israel.

Psalms 105:12
They believed His words and sang His praise.
  Exodus 14:31 — Trust following the sea deliverance: The psalm says the people believed His words after the waters covered their enemies. Exodus says they trusted God and Moses after seeing what the Lord had done. The response at the same point in the narrative corresponds, with the stated objects of trust distinguished.
  Exodus 15:1 — Israel's song after the sea rescue: The psalm pairs belief with singing God's praise. Exodus narrates Moses and Israel singing to God after the sea deliverance. This link concerns the song component of the psalm's paired response.

Psalms 105:13
They hurried and forgot His works; they did not wait for His counsel.
  Psalms 77:11 — Forgetting divine works within Israel's history: Psalm 105 says the people hurried and forgot God's works and did not wait for His counsel. Psalm 77 recounts forgetting His benefits and the wonders He showed them. Both historical recitals identify forgetting divine acts, without importing the timing or counsel clause into the target.

Psalms 105:14
They desired a desire in the wilderness and tested God in a waterless place.
  Numbers 11:4-6 — The wilderness craving for food: The psalm describes an intense desire in the wilderness and testing God. Numbers narrates intense craving and Israel's weeping for meat while remembering Egyptian foods. The distinctive craving within the wilderness provision history connects the accounts.

Psalms 105:15
He gave them their request and sent fullness into their souls.
  Numbers 11:31-32 — The desired food supplied in abundance: The psalm says God gave their request and sent fullness to their souls. Numbers describes quail supplied by the Lord's wind and gathered in great quantities after the request for meat. Fullness is preserved; the following plague is not substituted for the psalm's actual wording.

Psalms 105:16
They angered Moses in the camp, and Aaron, the Lord’s holy one.
  Numbers 16:3 — Opposition to Moses and Aaron: Both name Moses and Aaron as targets of opposition. The psalm calls Aaron the Lord's holy one; Numbers quotes the challengers calling the whole congregation holy. These different attributions of holiness are preserved.

Psalms 105:17
The earth was opened and swallowed Dathan and covered Abiram’s assembly.
  Numbers 16:31-33 — The earth swallowing the rebel households: The psalm names Dathan and Abiram's assembly as swallowed or covered by earth. Numbers narrates the ground splitting and swallowing those just identified with Dathan and Abiram in the surrounding account. The shared event is retained without replacing Numbers' additional Korah designation.

Psalms 105:18
Fire was kindled in their assembly; a flame burned up sinners.
  Numbers 16:35 — Fire consuming the rebellious assembly: The psalm describes fire in the assembly and a flame burning sinners. Numbers narrates fire from the Lord consuming the two hundred fifty incense presenters in that rebellion. The particular fiery judgment corresponds, while the number and incense belong to Numbers.

Psalms 105:19
They made a calf at Horeb and bowed down to a carved image.
  Exodus 32:4-8 — The calf and worship of the made image: The psalm describes the calf at Horeb and bowing to a carved image. Exodus narrates making a cast calf and later explicitly recounts bowing and sacrificing to it. The calf worship corresponds, while carved and cast and the source's Horeb name are preserved.

Psalms 105:20
They exchanged their glory for the likeness of a calf that eats grass.
  Romans 1:23 — Glory exchanged for a creature's likeness: The psalm describes exchanging glory for the likeness of a grass-eating calf. Romans describes exchanging God's incorruptible glory for an image's likeness involving humans and animals. The exchange and likeness formula is particular, with its objects and scope distinguished.

Psalms 105:21
They forgot the God who saved them, who did great things in Egypt,
  Deuteronomy 8:14 — Forgetting the God who brought Israel from Egypt: The psalm accuses the people of forgetting the God who saved them and did great things in Egypt. Deuteronomy warns against forgetting the Lord who brought them from Egypt's bondage. The named deliverance and forgetting connect accusation with warning.

Psalms 105:22
wonders in the land of Ham, fearful things at the Red Sea.
  Psalms 77:12-13 — Wonders in Egypt and passage through the sea: Psalm 105 recalls wonders in Ham's land and fearful acts at the Red Sea. Psalm 77 joins Egyptian wonders with the sea torn apart for passage. The two-part historical sequence corresponds, while Tanis and the skin-bag image remain the target's details.

Psalms 105:23
He said He would destroy them, had not Moses His chosen one stood in the breach before Him, to turn His anger away so He would not destroy.
  Exodus 32:10-14 — Moses interceding against threatened destruction: The psalm says Moses stood in the breach to turn away anger when destruction was threatened. Exodus narrates that threat, Moses' plea to cease fierce anger and the Lord's being appeased. The intercession corresponds; the breach is the psalm's image.

Psalms 105:24
They treated the desirable land as nothing; they did not believe His word.
  Numbers 14:7-11 — The good land refused in unbelief: The psalm says the desirable land was treated as nothing and God's word not believed. Numbers pairs the explorers' declaration of the land's goodness with the congregation's rejection and God's accusation of unbelief. The land and unbelief together identify the particular episode.

Psalms 105:25
They grumbled in their dwellings; they did not hear the Lord’s voice.
  Numbers 14:2-4 — The congregation's wilderness grumbling: The psalm describes grumbling in dwellings and not hearing God's voice. Numbers narrates the congregation grumbling against Moses and Aaron and proposing return to Egypt in the land-refusal episode. The complaint is connected without adding the dwelling location to Numbers.

Psalms 105:26
He lifted His hand to them, to cast them down in the wilderness,
  Numbers 14:28-30 — The judgment of falling in the wilderness: The psalm describes God lifting His hand to the people to cast them down in the wilderness. Numbers announces that their bodies will fall there and that they will not enter the land. Its hand stretched toward settlement is distinguished from the psalm's hand raised to the people for judgment.

Psalms 105:27
and to cast their offspring down among the nations, and scatter them in the countries.
  Ezekiel 20:23 — Dispersion among nations and countries: The psalm speaks of casting offspring among nations and scattering them through countries. In Ezekiel, God recounts His wilderness address to the children and raising His hand to scatter them among nations and countries. The paired dispersion language and wilderness context are particular.

Psalms 105:28
They were initiated to Baal-peor and ate sacrifices of the dead.
  Numbers 25:2-3 — Baal-Peor and eating idol sacrifices: Both connect participation in Baal-Peor's worship with eating sacrifices. Numbers calls them sacrifices of idols; the psalm calls them sacrifices of the dead. The shared named cult and meal correspond without resolving that difference by substitution.

Psalms 105:29
They provoked Him with their practices, and the falling multiplied among them.
  Numbers 25:9 — The punitive aftermath of Baal-Peor: The psalm says provocation through their practices was followed by multiplied falling. Numbers counts twenty-four thousand deaths in the plague after Baal-Peor. The named episode's harmful aftermath connects the accounts, with the psalm's falling and Numbers' deaths stated distinctly.

Psalms 105:30
Phinehas stood and made atonement, and the breaking ceased.
  Numbers 25:7-8 — Phinehas rising and the harm ceasing: The psalm describes Phinehas standing, making atonement and the breaking ceasing. Numbers names Phinehas rising, narrates his violent intervention and says the plague stopped. The person, intervention and cessation correspond without replacing the psalm's terms with the target's details.

Psalms 105:31
It was reckoned to him as righteousness, from generation to generation for ever.
  Genesis 15:6 — An act reckoned as righteousness: The psalm says Phinehas' intervention was reckoned to him as righteousness; Genesis says Abram believed God and it was reckoned to him as righteousness. The distinctive reckoning formula is shared, while the persons and preceding acts remain different.
  Numbers 25:12-13 — Enduring recognition concerning Phinehas: The psalm extends the reckoning of righteousness through generations forever. Numbers promises Phinehas and his seed an everlasting priestly covenant following his atonement. The lasting recognition of the named person's act corresponds without equating righteousness with priesthood.

Psalms 105:32
They angered Him at the water of contradiction, and Moses suffered harm because of them,
  Numbers 20:12-13 — The disputed waters and harm to Moses: The psalm connects provocation at the water of contradiction with harm to Moses. Numbers names the Water of Dispute and announces that Moses and Aaron will not lead the congregation into the land. The site and consequence correspond while Numbers' accusation of unbelief remains explicit.

Psalms 105:33
for they embittered his spirit, and he issued a command with his lips.
  Numbers 20:10 — Speech at the disputed waters: The psalm says his spirit was embittered and he issued a command with his lips after naming Moses. Numbers records the address Listen to me to the disobedient people at the rock. This connects speech within the episode without rewriting the psalm as an explicit statement about rash words.

Psalms 105:34
They did not destroy the nations of which the Lord had spoken to them,
  Judges 1:28-29 — The nations remaining in Israel's land: The psalm says Israel did not destroy the nations of which the Lord had spoken to them. Judges records Canaanites subjected to tribute but not completely removed and remaining among Israel. The unremoved population connects the historical descriptions, while destroy and remove are kept distinct.

Psalms 105:35
and mixed with the nations and learned their works.
  Judges 3:5-6 — Living among nations and adopting their worship: The psalm joins mixing with the nations to learning their works. Judges describes Israel dwelling among named nations, intermarrying and serving their gods. The coexistence and adopted practice provide the connection without reducing every learned work to intermarriage.

Psalms 105:36
They served their carved images, and it became a stumbling block to them.
  Exodus 23:33 — Serving other gods as a stumbling block: The psalm says serving carved images became a stumbling block. Exodus warns that serving the resident peoples' gods will be a stumbling block. The unusual service-and-stumbling formulation connects the accusation with the warning.

Psalms 105:37
They sacrificed their sons and their daughters to demons,
  Deuteronomy 32:17 — Sacrificing to demons: Both describe sacrifices offered to demons. The psalm explicitly names sons and daughters as the victims; Deuteronomy contrasts demons with God and discusses unfamiliar gods. The common recipients of sacrifice support the link without adding child victims to Deuteronomy.
  Jeremiah 7:31 — Sons and daughters in destructive cult practice: The psalm condemns sacrificing sons and daughters to demons. Jeremiah condemns an altar at Topheth for burning sons and daughters. The shared child-directed cult practice is compared without equating all occasions or importing Topheth, fire and the explicit prohibition into the psalm.

Psalms 105:38
and shed innocent blood, the blood of their sons and daughters whom they sacrificed to Canaan’s carved images; the land was murdered with blood,
  Numbers 35:33 — Shed blood staining the land with murder: The psalm says the land was murdered with innocent blood from child sacrifice. Numbers says shed blood stains the land with murder and gives a legal remedy. The unusual land, blood and murder formulation corresponds; the psalm does not state that remedy here.

Psalms 105:39
and was defiled by their works, and they prostituted themselves through their practices.
  Hosea 5:3 — Cultic prostitution and defilement: The psalm continues the land's defilement from the preceding unit and then says the people prostituted themselves through their practices. Hosea describes Ephraim as prostituted and Israel as defiled. The paired images correspond with land and people distinguished as the objects of defilement.

Psalms 105:40
The Lord was wrathfully angry against His people and abhorred His inheritance.
  Judges 2:14 — Wrath following Israel's idolatry: The psalm reports the Lord's anger against His people after their idolatry. Judges' overview likewise reports His anger against Israel after their service of other gods. The corresponding stage in that historical cycle is the link; abhorring the inheritance is the psalm's additional description.

Psalms 105:41
He handed them over into nations’ hands, and those hating them became their masters.
  Judges 2:14 — Israel handed into enemy power: Both describe God handing Israel over into hostile power. The psalm speaks of nations and hateful masters; Judges names plunderers and surrounding enemies. The divine handover and enemy domination correspond with the descriptions distinguished.

Psalms 105:42
Their enemies afflicted them, and they were humbled beneath their hands.
  Judges 2:14-15 — Hostile hands and Israel's affliction: The psalm describes enemies afflicting Israel and humbling them under their hands. Judges joins sale into enemies' hands with the Lord's hand bringing harm and great affliction. The subjection and affliction correspond, while the different immediate agents are preserved.

Psalms 105:43
Many times He rescued them, but they provoked Him in their counsel and were humbled in their lawless deeds.
  Judges 2:16-19 — Repeated rescue and renewed disobedience: The psalm places many rescues alongside provocation in the people's counsel and humiliation in their lawless deeds. Judges describes rescue through judges and renewed corruption when a judge died. The recurring rescue-and-disobedience pattern is particular; the explicit timing after a judge's death belongs to Judges.

Psalms 105:44
He saw when they were afflicted, when He heard their petition.
  Judges 2:18 — Affliction heard and relief granted: The psalm says God saw the people's affliction when He heard their petition. Judges says He was moved by groaning before those besieging and harming them and saved through the judge. The response to oppressed people's cry corresponds, with petition and groaning distinguished.

Psalms 105:45
He remembered His covenant and changed His purpose according to His abundant mercy,
  Leviticus 26:44-45 — Covenant remembrance amid enemy captivity: The psalm connects covenant remembrance and changed purpose with abundant mercy. Leviticus describes not disregarding the people in enemies' land so as to consume them or dissolve the covenant, and promises remembrance of the former covenant. The remembrance and mercy correspond without inserting changed-purpose wording into Leviticus.

Psalms 105:46
and granted them compassions before all who had taken them captive.
  1 Kings 8:50 — Compassion before the captors: The psalm says God granted the people compassion before those taking them captive. Solomon's prayer asks God to grant compassion before captors so they will show compassion. The specific captor-directed mercy corresponds as report and petition.

Psalms 105:47
Save us, Lord our God, and gather us together from the nations, to give thanks to Thy holy name and boast in Thy praise.
  1 Chronicles 16:35 — Rescue from nations and praise of the holy name: Both ask God's saving rescue in relation to the nations so the people may praise His holy name and glory in His praise. The psalm explicitly asks gathering; Chronicles says rescue. Their closely corresponding petitions retain that distinction.

Psalms 105:48
Blessed be the Lord, the God of Israel, from eternity to eternity. All the people will say, May it be, may it be.
  1 Chronicles 16:36 — The closing blessing and the people's response: Both bless the Lord, Israel's God, through the ages and state the people's response. The psalm has May it be twice, while Chronicles has Amen once and then reports praise. The shared closing form is retained with those differences explicit.

Psalms 106:1
Hallelujah. Give thanks to the Lord, for He is good; His mercy is for ever.
  1 Chronicles 16:34 — Shared thanksgiving formula: Both call for thanksgiving to the Lord and give His enduring mercy as the reason. The psalm says He is good; Chronicles uses the wording it is good, which is preserved without making the two clauses identical.

Psalms 106:2
Let those redeemed by the Lord say so, those He redeemed from the enemy’s hand.
  Luke 1:71-74 — Redemption from an enemy's hand: The psalm addresses those the Lord redeemed from an enemy's hand. Zechariah's prophecy describes salvation from enemies and rescue from their hand in connection with God's remembered covenant.

Psalms 106:3
He gathered them from the countries, from east and west and north and the sea.
  Isaiah 43:5-6 — Gathering from distant directions: Both describe God's gathering a dispersed people from named directions. East, west and north correspond directly; the psalm's sea and Isaiah's southwest retain their different wording.

Psalms 106:4
They wandered in the wilderness, in a waterless place; they did not find a road to a city of habitation,
  Deuteronomy 8:15 — Waterless wilderness travel: The wanderers' waterless wilderness corresponds to the fearful wilderness through which the Lord led Israel, explicitly marked by thirst and absence of water. The psalm's unnamed travelers are not assigned a particular journey.

Psalms 106:5
hungry and thirsty; their soul failed within them.
  Nehemiah 9:15 — Hunger and thirst in the wilderness: The psalm's hungry and thirsty travelers correspond to the needs recalled in Nehemiah's prayer: shortage of food and thirst answered by heavenly bread and water from a rock.

Psalms 106:6
They cried to the Lord when they were afflicted, and He rescued them from their distresses,
  Psalms 106:13 — Same-chapter rescue refrain: The cry to the Lord in affliction and rescue from distress recur in the prison scene. This same-chapter refrain connects the wandering and imprisoned groups without identifying them as the same people.

Psalms 106:7
and guided them into a straight way to travel to a city of habitation.
  Isaiah 35:8-10 — A safe road toward settlement: God guides the travelers along a straight way to an inhabited city. Isaiah describes a holy road on which the scattered do not wander and the redeemed travel toward Zion; both join guided travel to arrival, while only Isaiah names Zion.

Psalms 106:8
Let them acknowledge to the Lord His mercies and His wonders to the sons of men,
  Psalms 106:15 — Same-chapter thanksgiving refrain: The call to acknowledge the Lord's mercies and wonders to human beings recurs after the prisoners' rescue. The identical refrain links gratitude for finding a way with gratitude for release from bonds.

Psalms 106:9
