for He filled an empty soul and filled a hungry soul with good things.
  Luke 1:53 — Hungry people filled with good things: Mary's song repeats the combination of hungry people, filling and good things. The psalm describes satisfying an empty and hungry soul, while Mary's song also contrasts the rich sent away empty.

Psalms 106:10
Those sitting in darkness and death’s shadow, fettered in poverty and iron,
  Isaiah 42:7 — Prisoners in darkness: Both depict people in bonds and darkness. The psalm describes their condition, including death's shadow and iron; Isaiah names bringing bound people and those sitting in darkness out of prison.

Psalms 106:11
because they provoked God’s oracles and spurned the Most High’s counsel,
  Proverbs 1:25-30 — Spurned counsel followed by distress: The prisoners' rejection of God's oracles and counsel corresponds to Wisdom's accusation that her counsel and reproof were disregarded. Both place that rejection before distress; Proverbs' speaker is personified Wisdom.

Psalms 106:12
their heart was humbled in toils; they grew weak, and there was no helper.
  Psalms 21:12 — Affliction without a helper: The exhausted prisoners have no helper, matching the other psalm's plea that God remain near because affliction is near and no one helps. Both make helplessness part of an appeal for divine rescue.

Psalms 106:13
They cried to the Lord when they were afflicted, and He saved them from their distresses,
  Psalms 106:19 — Same-chapter rescue refrain: The prisoners' cry and deliverance repeat in the following scene of people nearing death. The same-chapter refrain connects the distinct dangers through prayer answered by the Lord.

Psalms 106:14
and brought them out of darkness and death’s shadow and broke their bonds apart.
  Isaiah 49:9 — Release from bonds and darkness: The Lord brings people out of darkness and breaks their bonds in the psalm; Isaiah addresses those in bonds with Come out and those in darkness with a command to be revealed.

Psalms 106:15
Let them acknowledge to the Lord His mercies and His wonders to the sons of men,
  Psalms 106:21 — Same-chapter thanksgiving refrain: The thanksgiving refrain following liberation from prison returns after healing. Both name the Lord's mercies and wonders to human beings, linking these separate rescue scenes.

Psalms 106:16
for He shattered bronze gates and broke iron bars.
  Isaiah 45:2 — Bronze doors and iron bars broken: The distinctive paired images of shattered bronze gates or doors and broken iron bars occur in both passages. Isaiah addresses Cyrus in context, while the psalm celebrates the Lord's liberation without naming him.

Psalms 106:17
He helped them out of their lawless way, for because of their lawless deeds they were humbled.
  Psalms 118:67 — Wrongdoing, humiliation and correction: Both connect wrongdoing with being humbled and a changed way. The psalm reports help out of a lawless way, while Psalm 118's speaker says he did wrong before being humbled and then kept God's saying.

Psalms 106:18
Their soul abhorred every food, and they drew near the gates of death.
  Job 33:19-22 — Failure to eat near death: Both describe disruption of eating while death approaches. Their symptoms differ: the psalm's sufferers abhor food, whereas Elihu's described sufferer desires food but cannot receive it; that difference is not erased.

Psalms 106:19
They cried to the Lord when they were afflicted, and He saved them from their distresses.
  Psalms 106:28 — Same-chapter rescue refrain: The endangered sufferers' cry to the Lord and salvation from distress recur among the storm-tossed sailors. The refrain links rescue from approaching death with rescue at sea.

Psalms 106:20
He sent His word and healed them and rescued them from their destructions.
  Wisdom of Solomon 16:12 — Healing through God's word: The psalm says God sent His word and healed the sufferers. Wisdom identifies the Lord's word, rather than herb or poultice, as what healed, making the word's healing action the particular connection.
  Matthew 8:8 — Healing by a spoken word: The centurion's request that Jesus only speak a word and his servant will be healed corresponds to the psalm's word sent to heal. The later narrative supplies a particular instance of trust in effective healing speech.

Psalms 106:21
Let them acknowledge to the Lord His mercies and His wonders to the sons of men,
  Psalms 106:31 — Same-chapter thanksgiving refrain: The call to acknowledge the Lord's mercies and wonders returns after the sea rescue. It connects thanksgiving by the healed sufferers with thanksgiving by the sailors.

Psalms 106:22
and let them sacrifice a sacrifice of praise and proclaim His works in exultation.
  Hebrews 13:15 — Sacrifice of praise: Both explicitly name a sacrifice of praise. The psalm joins it to joyful proclamation of God's works; Hebrews describes it as the fruit of lips confessing His name through Jesus.

Psalms 106:23
Those going down into the sea in ships, doing work on many waters,
  Ezekiel 27:26 — Maritime labor before a storm: The psalm introduces people working aboard ships on many waters before describing the storm. Ezekiel pictures Tyre's rowers leading her into much water before a wind shatters her; the working voyage and storm setting correspond without identifying the vessels.

Psalms 106:24
they saw the Lord’s works and His wonders in the depth.
  Jonah 1:15-16 — Witnessing divine power at sea: The psalm says the sailors saw the Lord's wonders in the deep. Jonah narrates a particular sea marvel, the cessation of its tossing, followed by the sailors' fear of the Lord, sacrifice and vows.

Psalms 106:25
He spoke, and a storm wind stood, and its waves were raised.
  Jonah 1:4 — The Lord raises a storm at sea: Both attribute the dangerous wind and rising sea to the Lord's action. The psalm describes His speech and raised waves; Jonah reports the wind He stirred and the ship threatened with destruction.

Psalms 106:26
They rise as far as the heavens and descend as far as the deeps; their soul melted in evils.
  Acts 27:20 — Despair during a violent sea storm: The sailors' melting soul amid the heaving sea corresponds to the voyagers' loss of all remaining hope while the storm continued in Acts. The passages describe comparable distress without identifying their voyages or equating every wave image.

Psalms 106:27
They were troubled and shaken like a drunken man, and all their wisdom was swallowed up.
  Isaiah 29:9-10 — Drunkenness as an image of disorientation: The sailors are shaken like a drunken man and their wisdom fails. Isaiah describes loss of sense and a hangover without wine, followed by a spirit of stupor; both use intoxication language for impaired capacity in different settings.

Psalms 106:28
They cried to the Lord when they were afflicted, and He brought them out of their distresses,
  Mark 4:38-39 — An appeal in a storm answered by calm: The sailors cry to the Lord and are brought out of distress. Mark's disciples appeal to Jesus while fearing they will perish, and He answers by rebuking the wind and sea.

Psalms 106:29
and commanded the storm, and it settled into a breeze, and its waves fell silent.
  Mark 4:39 — Commanded wind and silent waves: The psalm's command quiets the storm and silences waves. Mark narrates Jesus commanding the sea to be silent and still, followed by the wind's cessation and a great calm.

Psalms 106:30
They rejoiced because they became quiet, and He guided them to the harbor of their desire.
  John 6:20-21 — Arrival at the desired destination: The psalm's sailors are guided to their desired harbor after danger. After Jesus reassures the frightened disciples, John's boat is at the land toward which they were going; the correspondence is safe arrival at the intended destination, not a claim that John narrates the same calming command.

Psalms 106:31
Let them acknowledge to the Lord His mercies and His wonders to the sons of men,
  Psalms 104:1-2 — Thanksgiving for God's wonders: The psalm calls for acknowledgment to the Lord of His mercies and His wonders to human beings. Psalm 104 joins thanksgiving with announcing God's works among nations and recounting His wonders. The shared connection is thanksgiving and wonders; the source is not rewritten as an express command to tell them.

Psalms 106:32
let them exalt Him in an assembly of the people and praise Him where the elders sit.
  Psalms 21:23 — Praise within the assembly: Exalting the Lord in the people's assembly corresponds to the vow to sing hymns in the midst of the assembly. Both place the testimony of divine help in communal worship.

Psalms 106:33
He made rivers a wilderness and outlets of waters thirst,
  Isaiah 50:2 — Rivers turned into dry land: Both attribute the drying of rivers to the Lord. Isaiah's threat expressly turns rivers to deserts and names thirst and lack of water, corresponding to the psalm's rivers made wilderness and water outlets made thirst.

Psalms 106:34
a fruitful land brine, because of the wickedness of those inhabiting it.
  Deuteronomy 29:22-26 — Salted land as judgment for wrongdoing: The fruitful land becomes brine because of its inhabitants' wickedness. Deuteronomy describes salt-covered land that no longer grows crops and explains its ruin by abandonment of the covenant and service to other gods.

Psalms 106:35
He made a wilderness lakes of waters and waterless land outlets of waters,
  Isaiah 41:18 — Water supplied to a dry wilderness: Both describe divine transformation of dry land into places of water. The psalm's lakes and water outlets correspond to Isaiah's marshes and watercourses in the wilderness and thirsty land.

Psalms 106:36
and settled hungry people there, and they established a city of habitation,
  Psalms 106:4-7 — Same-chapter settlement motif: The newly established city of habitation recalls the earlier wanderers' unsuccessful search and guided arrival. This same-chapter repetition traces the contrast between homelessness and settled life without asserting that every scene concerns one group.

Psalms 106:37
and sowed fields and planted vineyards and produced a fruitful crop.
  Isaiah 65:21-22 — Settled people planting and enjoying produce: The psalm's fields and vineyards yield a crop after settlement. Isaiah describes people inhabiting their own houses, planting vineyards and eating their own produce; both connect restored dwelling with fruitful labor.

Psalms 106:38
He blessed them, and they multiplied greatly, and He did not diminish their livestock.
  Deuteronomy 7:13-14 — Blessing, multiplication and livestock: The psalm joins God's blessing to human multiplication and preservation of livestock. Deuteronomy similarly promises blessing and multiplication, explicitly including human offspring, cattle and sheep.

Psalms 106:39
They became few and suffered harm from the affliction of evils and pain.
  Leviticus 26:22 — A people reduced in number through affliction: The psalm describes people becoming few and suffering harm. Leviticus' threatened affliction likewise makes the people few through destructive animals; it names a particular means that the psalm does not specify.

Psalms 106:40
Contempt was poured out upon rulers, and He made them wander in an impassable place, not a road.
  Job 12:21-24 — Humiliated rulers and lost direction: Job's speech pairs dishonor poured on rulers with their being led astray on an unknown road. The psalm similarly joins contempt on rulers to wandering in an impassable place, while preserving the difference between an unknown road and no road.

Psalms 106:41
He helped the poor out of poverty and made families like sheep.
  Ezekiel 36:37-38 — Human families pictured as sheep: The psalm pictures families as sheep after help for the poor. Ezekiel uses the human-flock image explicitly for multiplication and the filling of deserted cities, supplying a particular parallel to the pastoral image without inserting the verb multiply into the source unit.

Psalms 106:42
The upright will see and rejoice, and all lawlessness will stop its mouth.
  Job 22:19 — The righteous see and rejoice or laugh: The upright see and rejoice in the psalm. Eliphaz describes the righteous seeing and laughing at the overthrow of the unjust; the resemblance concerns their response, and his speech is not treated as a new divine pronouncement about Job.

Psalms 106:43
Who is wise and will keep these things, and will they understand the Lord’s mercies?
  Hosea 14:10 — A closing wisdom challenge: Both conclude by asking who is wise enough to understand these things. The psalm directs understanding to the Lord's mercies; Hosea directs it to His straight ways and the different responses of righteous and impious people.

Psalms 107:2
My heart is ready, God; my heart is ready. I will sing and make music in my glory.
  Psalms 56:8 — Repeated readiness to sing: The doubled declaration that the heart is ready and the resolve to sing and make music recur in Psalm 56. Psalm 107 adds in my glory; the shared wording does not establish that the psalms describe the same occasion.

Psalms 107:3
Awake, psaltery and lyre! I will awake at dawn.
  Psalms 56:9 — Repeated summons to instruments at dawn: Both summon the psaltery and lyre to awake and declare that the speaker will awake at dawn. Psalm 56 also addresses my glory; the distinctive instrumental and dawn sequence is retained in both.

Psalms 107:4
I will thank Thee among peoples, Lord, and make music unto Thee among nations,
  Psalms 56:10 — Repeated praise among peoples and nations: Both promise thanksgiving to the Lord among peoples and music among nations. The paired audiences and paired acts of praise form a close literary parallel.

Psalms 107:5
for Thy mercy is great above the heavens, and Thy truth reaches the clouds.
  Psalms 56:11 — Mercy reaching heaven and truth the clouds: Both describe the scale of God's mercy and truth through heavens and clouds. Psalm 107 says mercy is great above the heavens, while Psalm 56 says it was magnified to the heavens.

Psalms 107:6
Be exalted above the heavens, God, and Thy glory over all the earth.
  Psalms 56:12 — Repeated exaltation refrain: Both ask that God be exalted above the heavens and His glory extend over or above all the earth. This repeats Psalm 56's closing refrain in Psalm 107's praise sequence.

Psalms 107:7
So that Thy beloved ones may be rescued, save with Thy right hand and hear me.
  Psalms 59:7 — Repeated petition for the beloved: The appeal for the beloved to be rescued, salvation by God's right hand and an answer recurs together in Psalm 59. The shared sequence is more specific than a general request for help.

Psalms 107:8
God spoke in His holy place: I will be exalted and divide Shechem and measure out the valley of tents.
  Psalms 59:8 — Dividing Shechem and measuring the valley: Both introduce God's speech in His holy place and name dividing Shechem and measuring the Valley of Tents. The opening I will be exalted and I will exult retain their respective wording.

Psalms 107:9
Gilead is Mine, and Manasseh is Mine; Ephraim is the support of My head; Judah is My king.
  Psalms 59:9 — Repeated territorial declaration: Both declare Gilead and Manasseh Mine, describe Ephraim in relation to the head, and name Judah My king. The shared sequence supports the parallel without replacing king with another traditional rendering.

Psalms 107:10
Moab is the cauldron of My hope; over Idumea I will extend My sandal; foreign peoples were subjected to Me.
  Psalms 59:10 — Repeated Moab and Idumea imagery: Both call Moab a vessel of hope, extend a sandal over Idumea, and say foreign peoples were subjected. Cauldron and basin remain distinct source wordings within the close parallel.

Psalms 107:11
Who will lead me to a city of enclosure? Who will guide me as far as Idumea?
  Psalms 59:11 — Repeated questions about the enclosed city: Both ask who will lead the speaker into an enclosed city and guide him as far as Idumea. Neither passage names the city, so the correspondence does not supply a geographical identification.

Psalms 107:12
Is it not Thou, God, who didst reject us? Thou wilt not go out, God, with our forces.
  Psalms 59:12 — Repeated rejection and absence from the forces: Both question whether God who rejected the people is their guide, then state that He will not go out with their forces. The negative statement is preserved in both.

Psalms 107:13
Give us help from affliction, for human salvation is futile.
  Psalms 59:13 — Repeated contrast with human salvation: Both ask God for help from affliction because human salvation is futile or vain. The request and its stated reason recur together.

Psalms 107:14
In God we will act with power, and He will bring our enemies to nothing.
  Psalms 59:14 — Power through God against opponents: Both conclude that through or in God the people will act mightily and He will reduce their opponents to nothing. Enemies and afflicters retain their respective wording.

Psalms 108:1
For the end. Of David. A psalm. God, do not pass over my praise in silence,
  Psalms 82:2 — Petition against divine silence: Both directly ask God not to remain silent. Psalm 108's appeal concerns the speaker's praise amid deceitful opposition, while Psalm 82's following context concerns God's enemies.

Psalms 108:2
for a sinner’s mouth and a deceitful person’s mouth were opened against me; they spoke against me with a deceitful tongue,
  Psalms 26:12 — Hostile speech and false accusation: The deceitful mouths speaking against the psalmist correspond to unjust witnesses rising against the other speaker. Both combine personal opposition with falsehood, without changing Psalm 26's wording that injustice lied to itself.

Psalms 108:3
and surrounded me with words of hatred and fought me without cause.
  John 15:25 — Hatred without cause: John's cited statement that they hated Jesus without cause corresponds to the psalm's hatred and fighting without cause. The shared wording is clear, but this link does not claim Psalm 108 is the citation's exclusive source.

Psalms 108:4
Instead of loving me, they were slandering me, but I was praying.
  Luke 6:27-28 — Prayer amid hostility, with differing responses: The psalmist responds to slander by praying, while Luke commands prayer for mistreaters and blessing for those who curse. The shared recourse to prayer amid hostility does not erase Psalm 108's following imprecations or assert that it prays for the opponents' benefit.

Psalms 108:5
They set evils against me in exchange for good things and hatred in exchange for my love.
  Psalms 34:12 — Evil repaid for good: Both speakers complain that good deeds receive evil in return. Psalm 108 adds hatred in exchange for love, while Psalm 34 adds the distinct phrase childlessness to my soul.

Psalms 108:6
Appoint a sinner over him, and let a slanderer stand at his right hand.
  Zechariah 3:1 — A slanderer at the right hand: The requested slanderer standing at the accused person's right hand corresponds to Zechariah's slanderer standing at Joshua's right hand to oppose him. The positional and adversarial imagery is shared without identifying the accused people.

Psalms 108:8
Let his days become few, and may another take his oversight.
  Acts 1:20 — Explicit quotation concerning oversight: Peter expressly cites the book of Psalms and the demand that another take the office of oversight. In Acts this supports the replacement of Judas, as the surrounding speech states.

Psalms 108:9
Let his sons become orphans and his wife a widow.
  Exodus 22:21-23 — Widowhood and orphanhood in judgment: The requested fate of a wife becoming a widow and sons becoming orphans repeats the consequences threatened against those who mistreat widows and orphans in Exodus. The psalm is an imprecation; Exodus is a legal warning.

Psalms 108:10
Let his sons be shaken and migrate, and let them beg; let them be cast out from their house sites.
  Psalms 36:25 — Contrasting pictures of descendants in need: The curse asks that the offender's sons wander and beg. Psalm 36's speaker says he has not seen a righteous person's offspring seeking loaves; the contrast concerns the described descendants and does not label every hungry child guilty.

Psalms 108:11
Let a lender search out everything he possesses; let strangers plunder his toils.
  Deuteronomy 28:33 — Strangers consume a person's labor: The request that strangers plunder the offender's toils corresponds to Deuteronomy's warning that an unknown nation will consume the land's yield and all the people's labors. The psalm's lender is an additional feature, not supplied by the target.

Psalms 108:12
Let no helper exist for him, nor anyone compassionate toward his orphans.
  James 2:13 — Mercilessness and judgment without mercy: Read with Psalm 108:16, the request for absence of compassion follows the man's refusal to show mercy. James explicitly connects judgment without mercy to failure to show mercy; the psalm's extension to his orphans is not attributed to James.

Psalms 108:13
Let his children be destroyed; in one generation let his name be erased.
  Psalms 36:28 — Destruction of an ungodly line: The requested destruction of the offender's children corresponds to the destruction of the ungodly person's offspring in Psalm 36. The additional erasure of his name belongs to Psalm 108's wording.

Psalms 108:14
May his fathers’ lawlessness be remembered before the Lord, and may his mother’s sin not be erased.
  Exodus 20:5 — Remembered wrongdoing across generations: The imprecation asks that parental sins remain before the Lord. Exodus describes the fathers' sins repaid upon later generations of those who hate God; both expressly connect wrongdoing across generations, with different forms and conditions.

Psalms 108:15
Let them be before the Lord continually, and may their memorial be destroyed from the earth,
  Psalms 33:17 — Remembrance erased from the earth: Both speak of the destruction of evildoers' remembrance from the earth. Psalm 108 requests it as part of the imprecation; Psalm 33 describes the Lord's opposition to those doing evil.

Psalms 108:16
because he did not remember to show mercy and pursued a poor and needy person and one pierced in heart, to put him to death.
  Psalms 36:14 — Violence against the poor and needy: The merciless offender pursues a poor and needy person to kill him. Psalm 36 describes sinners arming themselves to cast down the poor and needy and slaughter the upright, a particular parallel of predatory violence.

Psalms 108:17
He loved a curse, and it will come to him; he did not desire blessing, and it will be far from him.
  Psalms 108:28 — Same-chapter curse and blessing contrast: The offender's loved curse comes to him and blessing remains distant. Later the speaker contrasts enemies' cursing with God's blessing; the repeated curse/blessing opposition distinguishes the two positions within this psalm.

Psalms 108:18
He clothed himself in a curse like a garment; it entered his innards like water and his bones like oil.
  Psalms 72:6 — Moral evil pictured as clothing: The offender clothes himself in a curse, while Psalm 72's arrogant people wrap themselves in injustice and ungodliness. Both make a harmful moral condition into clothing, while the conditions themselves differ.

Psalms 108:19
Let it become for him like a garment he puts around himself and like a belt he continually fastens.
  Isaiah 11:5 — Contrasting belts as moral imagery: The curse is to become a continually fastened belt. Isaiah describes the ruler's loins and sides bound with righteousness and truth; the shared clothing image carries sharply contrasting moral contents.

Psalms 108:21
But Thou, Lord, Lord, show mercy with me for Thy name’s sake, for Thy mercy is good.
  Psalms 78:9 — Mercy requested for God's name: Both petitions appeal to God's name as the ground of help or rescue. Psalm 108 requests mercy for His name's sake, while Psalm 78 links rescue and propitiation of sins to the glory of that name.

Psalms 108:22
Rescue me, for I am poor and needy, and my heart is troubled within me.
  Psalms 39:18 — The poor and needy speaker seeks God's aid: Both speakers expressly identify themselves as poor and needy and seek God's rescue or care. Psalm 108 adds the troubled heart; Psalm 39 names God as helper and defender.

Psalms 108:23
Like a shadow as it declines, I was taken away; I was shaken off like locusts.
  Psalms 101:12 — A declining shadow as an image of frailty: The speaker taken away like a declining shadow corresponds to days bent like a shadow in Psalm 101. The locust image remains particular to Psalm 108, and is not supplied by the target.

Psalms 108:24
My knees grew weak from fasting, and my flesh was altered because of oil.
  Hebrews 12:12 — Weakened knees: The psalm reports knees weakened by fasting, while Hebrews urges the straightening of weakened knees. The particular bodily image corresponds; the psalm's separate phrase about flesh altered because of oil is not rewritten or explained by this link.

Psalms 108:25
I became a reproach to them; they saw me and shook their heads.
  Matthew 27:39 — Mockery by head-shaking: The speaker becomes a reproach as observers shake their heads. Matthew describes passersby reviling the crucified Jesus while shaking their heads, a clear parallel of the mocking gesture.

Psalms 108:26
Help me, Lord my God; save me according to Thy mercy,
  Psalms 6:5 — Salvation requested because of mercy: Both petitions explicitly ask the Lord to save on account of His mercy. Psalm 108 says according to Thy mercy, while Psalm 6 says for the sake of Thy mercy.

Psalms 108:27
and let them know that this is Thy hand and that Thou, Lord, hast done it.
  1 Samuel 6:9 — Recognition of the Lord's hand in an event: The psalm asks observers to recognize that the Lord's hand has acted. The foreigners' advisers propose watching the ark's route to distinguish His hand from chance. This parallels recognition of divine agency without equating the events or their means of recognition.

Psalms 108:28
They will curse, but Thou wilt bless; let those rising against me be ashamed, but Thy servant will rejoice.
  Numbers 23:7-12 — Human requests for curses met by blessing: The psalm contrasts enemies' curses with God's blessing. Balak complains that Balaam blessed those he summoned him to curse, and Balaam says he must speak what God gives him; this supplies a concrete curse/blessing reversal.

Psalms 108:29
Let those slandering me wear humiliation and wrap themselves in their shame like a double cloak.
  Psalms 34:26 — Opponents clothed in shame: Both petitions ask that hostile people wear shame and humiliation. Psalm 108 extends the clothing image to a double cloak; Psalm 34 identifies the opponents as those rejoicing in the speaker's troubles.

Psalms 108:30
I will greatly thank the Lord with my mouth and praise Him among many,
  Psalms 34:18 — Public thanksgiving after opposition: Both speakers promise to thank and praise the Lord among a large company. Psalm 34 explicitly names the great assembly, corresponding to Psalm 108's praise among many.

Psalms 108:31
for He stood at a poor person’s right hand, to save from those pursuing my soul.
  Psalms 108:6 — Same-chapter reversal at the right hand: The psalm closes with the Lord at the poor person's right hand to save, contrasting the earlier request for a slanderer at the accused person's right hand. The repeated position now holds a rescuer instead of an adversary.

Psalms 109:1
Of David. A psalm. The Lord said to my lord: Sit at My right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.
  Matthew 22:44 — Explicit quotation in a discussion of the Christ: Jesus cites David's address about the Lord's invitation to sit at His right and the subjection of enemies beneath the feet. Matthew places the citation in the question of how David calls the Christ Lord.
  Mark 12:36 — Explicit quotation attributed to David: Mark's Jesus attributes to David in the Holy Spirit the invitation to sit at the Lord's right until enemies are put beneath the feet. Both clauses correspond directly to this psalm's opening.
  Luke 20:42-43 — Explicit quotation from the Psalms: Luke expressly names David speaking in the book of Psalms and quotes both the right-hand invitation and the footstool clause. The multi-verse quotation is one target passage.
  Acts 2:34-35 — Explicit quotation in Peter's speech: Peter quotes the right-hand invitation and enemy-footstool promise while distinguishing David from the exalted Jesus in context. The link records that stated application in Acts.
  Hebrews 1:13 — Explicit quotation contrasting the Son and angels: Hebrews cites the right-hand invitation and enemy-footstool promise while asking which angel ever received it. The shared words form the stated comparison in Hebrews, rather than a new identification supplied by this tracker.

Psalms 109:2
The Lord will send out the rod of your power from Zion. Exercise dominion in the midst of your enemies.
  Psalms 2:6-9 — Royal authority from Zion and a rod: Both associate appointed royal authority with Zion and a rod. Psalm 109 sends the rod of power from Zion into the midst of enemies; Psalm 2 names the king on Zion and an iron rod over the nations.

Psalms 109:3
With you is rule on the day of your power, in the splendors of the holy ones; from the womb, before the morning star, I begot you.
  Psalms 2:7 — Divine begetting language in royal speech: Both royal passages contain the Lord's declaration I begot you. Psalm 109 specifies from the womb before the morning star, while Psalm 2 says today; the parallel preserves these different temporal formulations.

Psalms 109:4
The Lord swore and will not change His purpose: You are a priest for ever according to the order of Melchizedek.
  Hebrews 5:6 — Explicit quotation of the Melchizedek priesthood: Hebrews quotes the declaration of priesthood according to Melchizedek's order in its explanation of Christ's appointment as high priest. Its for the age and the psalm's for ever retain their source wording.
  Hebrews 6:20 — The Melchizedek declaration applied to Jesus: Hebrews names Jesus as the forerunner who entered within the veil and became high priest according to Melchizedek's order. That named order and enduring priesthood directly recall the psalm's declaration.
  Hebrews 7:17 — Explicit testimony concerning enduring priesthood: Hebrews introduces as testimony the declaration that the addressed person is a priest for the age according to Melchizedek's order. It repeats the psalm's distinctive priesthood formula.
  Hebrews 7:21 — Explicit quotation of the divine oath: Hebrews quotes both the Lord's oath without change of mind and the enduring priesthood declaration. The target emphasizes the oath as the distinction from priests appointed without one.
  Genesis 14:18 — Named priestly antecedent: Genesis names Melchizedek king of Salem and priest of God Most High. This supplies the explicit narrative priesthood associated with the name used in the psalm, without adding an identity claim beyond those texts.

Psalms 109:5
The Lord at your right hand crushed kings on the day of His anger.
  Psalms 2:10-12 — Kings confronted by the Lord's anger: The crushing of kings on the day of anger corresponds to Psalm 2's instruction to kings to learn and serve the Lord, lest He become angry and they perish from a righteous way. The shared kings-and-divine-anger connection preserves that target wording alongside the source's explicit crushing of kings.

Psalms 109:6
He will judge among the nations; He will fill the field with corpses; He will crush heads over the land of many.
  Isaiah 34:2-3 — Judgment of nations and their dead: Both picture judgment among nations accompanied by slaughter and dead bodies. Isaiah names the Lord's wrath against the nations and their dead thrown out; the psalm speaks of a field filled with corpses and crushed heads.

Psalms 110:1
Hallelujah! I will give Thee thanks, Lord, with my whole heart, in the council of the upright and the congregation.
  Psalms 137:1 — Wholehearted thanksgiving: Both speakers promise to thank the Lord with their whole heart. Psalm 110 places that thanksgiving among the upright and the congregation; Psalm 137 adds praise before angels, preserving the different settings.

Psalms 110:2
Great are the works of the Lord, searched out for all His purposes.
  Psalms 91:6 — The greatness of God's works: Both explicitly proclaim the greatness of the Lord's works. Psalm 110 adds that they are searched out for His purposes, while Psalm 91 joins them to the depth of His reasonings.

Psalms 110:3
Thanksgiving and magnificence are His work, and His righteousness remains for an age of an age.
  Psalms 111:3 — The enduring-righteousness formula: The statement that righteousness remains for an age of an age recurs in Psalm 111. Psalm 110 speaks of God's righteousness, while the other psalm describes the God-fearing man's house and righteousness.

Psalms 110:4
He made remembrance of His wonders; the Lord is merciful and compassionate.
  Exodus 34:6 — Merciful and compassionate divine character: The psalm names the Lord merciful and compassionate while recalling His wonders. Exodus' proclamation of the divine name likewise expressly pairs compassion and mercy among the Lord's attributes.

Psalms 110:5
He gave food to those who fear Him; He will remember His covenant for ever.
  Psalms 104:40-42 — Food and covenant remembrance: The psalm joins provision of food with God's enduring remembrance of His covenant. Psalm 104 recalls bread from heaven and water, followed by remembrance of His holy word to Abraham; the pairing of provision and covenant memory is particular to both.

Psalms 110:6
He announced the strength of His works to His people, to give them an inheritance of nations.
  Deuteronomy 4:37-38 — Divine strength and an inheritance of nations: The psalm links the strength of God's works to giving His people an inheritance of nations. Deuteronomy recalls His great strength in the exodus and His giving the land of mightier nations as an inheritance.

Psalms 110:7
The works of His hands are truth and judgment; all His commandments are faithful,
  Psalms 18:8-10 — Trustworthy commandments and true judgments: Both praise the reliability of God's revealed instruction. Psalm 110 calls His commandments faithful and His works truth and judgment; Psalm 18 calls His testimony trustworthy, His ordinances upright and His judgments true.

Psalms 110:8
established for an age of an age, made in truth and uprightness.
  Psalms 118:151-152 — Instruction established for ever in truth: The established commandments made in truth correspond to Psalm 118's declaration that all God's commandments are truth and His testimonies were established for ever.

Psalms 110:9
He sent redemption to His people; He commanded His covenant for ever; holy and fearsome is His name.
  Luke 1:68-72 — Redemption linked to the covenant: The psalm places redemption sent to the people beside God's lasting covenant. Zechariah's prophecy likewise joins redemption accomplished for His people to mercy and remembrance of His holy covenant.

Psalms 110:10
The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord; good understanding belongs to all who practice it. His praise remains for an age of an age.
  Proverbs 9:10 — Fear of the Lord as wisdom's beginning: Both explicitly state that the beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord, and both continue with understanding. The psalm relates understanding to practice, while Proverbs names the counsel of holy ones.

Psalms 111:1
Hallelujah! Blessed is the man who fears the Lord; he will greatly delight in His commandments.
  Psalms 1:1-2 — The blessed person delights in God's instruction: Both open with the blessed man and associate his life with willing devotion to the Lord's instruction. Psalm 111 names delight in His commandments; Psalm 1 names the will set on His law and continual meditation.

Psalms 111:2
His seed will be powerful in the land; a generation of the upright will be blessed.
  Proverbs 20:7 — Blessed descendants of the righteous: The blessed generation of the upright corresponds to the proverb that one living blamelessly in righteousness leaves blessed children. Both explicitly extend the described blessing to descendants.

Psalms 111:3
Glory and riches are in his house, and his righteousness remains for an age of an age.
  Psalms 111:9 — Same-chapter enduring-righteousness refrain: The statement that his righteousness remains for an age of an age appears with household glory and riches, then recurs with giving to the poor. The repeated formula connects resources and generosity in the same portrait without making a claim that all wealth proves righteousness.

Psalms 111:4
Light sprang up in darkness for the upright— merciful, compassionate and righteous.
  Isaiah 58:10 — Light in darkness alongside compassion: The psalm's light for the upright is accompanied by mercy, compassion and righteousness. Isaiah promises light rising in darkness when a person feeds the hungry and satisfies a humbled life, connecting the same light image with compassionate conduct.

Psalms 111:5
Kind is the man who has compassion and lends; he will manage his words with judgment.
  Psalms 36:26 — Compassion expressed through lending: Both describe the good or righteous person's compassion and lending together. Psalm 111's further statement about managing words with judgment is preserved rather than rewritten as a statement about managing money.

Psalms 111:6
For he will never be shaken; the righteous person will be an everlasting memorial.
  Proverbs 10:7 — An enduring favorable remembrance: The righteous person's everlasting memorial corresponds to the proverb that the memory of the righteous is accompanied by praises. Both contrast the lasting standing of the righteous with the fate of the impious in their respective contexts.

Psalms 111:7
He will not fear an evil report; his heart is ready to hope in the Lord.
  Proverbs 3:25-26 — Confidence in the Lord amid threatening news: The established hope in the Lord prevents fear of an evil report. Proverbs likewise promises freedom from fear of an approaching fright or ungodly assault because the Lord supports the person's way.

Psalms 111:8
His heart is established; he shall certainly not fear, until he looks upon his enemies.
  Psalms 26:3 — A heart unafraid before enemies: Both describe a heart that will not fear in the presence of enemies. Psalm 26 supplies the concrete setting of an approaching army and war, while Psalm 111 speaks of the established heart looking upon enemies.

Psalms 111:9
He scattered gifts; he gave to the poor. His righteousness remains for an age of an age; his horn will be raised in glory.
  2 Corinthians 9:9 — Explicit quotation about generosity: Paul introduces as written the scattering of gifts to the poor and the permanence of righteousness. The citation supplies Scripture within his discussion of generous giving.

Psalms 111:10
A sinner will see and be angry; he will gnash his teeth and waste away; the desire of sinners will perish.
  Psalms 36:12 — A sinner gnashes teeth at the righteous: Both depict a sinner looking at the righteous person and gnashing his teeth. Psalm 111 adds anger and wasting away, while Psalm 36 names hostile watchfulness.

Psalms 112:1
Hallelujah! Praise the Lord, servants; praise the name of the Lord.
  Psalms 134:1 — Servants summoned to praise the Lord's name: Both summon the Lord's servants or slaves to praise Him and His name. The paired address and object of praise recur as the opening of Psalm 134.

Psalms 112:2
May the name of the Lord be blessed from now and for ever.
  Daniel 2:20 — The divine name blessed for ever: Both explicitly bless the Lord's name for ever. Daniel's declaration occurs as thanksgiving for the revealed mystery, while the psalm issues the blessing as a worship formula.

Psalms 112:3
From the sun’s risings to its settings, praise the name of the Lord.
  Malachi 1:11 — The Lord's name from sunrise to sunset: Both place the Lord's praised or glorified name across the sun's rising and setting. Malachi adds worship among the nations, while the psalm's geographical sweep is stated through the sun's course.

Psalms 112:4
The Lord is high above all nations; His glory is above the heavens.
  Psalms 96:9 — Divine exaltation over nations and gods: Psalm 112 declares the Lord high above all nations and His glory above the heavens. Psalm 96 calls Him Most High over all the earth and exalted above all gods; the shared exaltation is clear while nations, gods and heavens remain different objects of comparison.

Psalms 112:5
Who is like the Lord our God, who dwells in high places,
  Exodus 15:11 — A question proclaiming divine incomparability: Both praise through the question Who is like the Lord. Exodus places the comparison among gods and adds holiness and wonders; the psalm adds His dwelling in high places.

Psalms 112:6
and looks upon lowly things in heaven and on earth?
  Isaiah 57:15 — The high-dwelling Lord attends to the lowly: Read with Psalm 112:5, the Lord dwelling high nevertheless looks upon lowly things. Isaiah likewise describes the Most High dwelling in the heights and giving patience to the fainthearted and life to the brokenhearted.

Psalms 112:7
He raises the poor from the ground and lifts the needy from the dung heap,
  1 Samuel 2:8 — The needy raised from earth and dung heap: Hannah's song repeats the paired raising of the poor or needy from the earth and dung heap. Both depict divine reversal of extreme lowliness through the same concrete images.

Psalms 112:8
to seat him with rulers, with the rulers of His people.
  1 Samuel 2:8 — The raised poor seated with rulers: The purpose of seating the lifted person with rulers repeats the next clause in Hannah's song. Samuel adds inheritance of a throne of glory, while the psalm specifies rulers of His people.

Psalms 112:9
He settles a barren woman in a house as a mother of children rejoicing.
  1 Samuel 2:5 — The barren woman becomes a mother: Both portray barrenness reversed into motherhood. The psalm names a rejoicing mother settled in a house, while Hannah's song says the barren woman bore seven; the different details are retained.

Psalms 113:1
Hallelujah. At Israel’s departure from Egypt, the house of Jacob from a foreign-speaking people,
  Exodus 12:41-42 — Israel's departure from Egypt: The psalm's opening recalls Israel leaving Egypt. Exodus narrates the Lord's host going out and the night vigil commemorating that departure; the psalm also characterizes the people from whom Israel departed as foreign-speaking.

Psalms 113:2
Judea became His sanctuary, Israel His authority.
  Psalms 77:68-69 — Judah and the divine sanctuary: The psalm associates Judea with God's sanctuary. Psalm 77 describes the choice of Judah and Zion and the building of the sanctuary; the link does not place that building at the same time as the departure mentioned in Psalm 113:1.

Psalms 113:3
The sea saw and fled; the Jordan turned backward.
  Exodus 14:21-22 — The sea withdraws at the exodus: The psalm personifies the sea as seeing and fleeing. Exodus narrates the Lord driving it back, splitting its waters and allowing Israel to cross on dry ground, supplying the narrative counterpart to that poetic image.
  Joshua 3:15-17 — The Jordan's flow is interrupted: The psalm's Jordan turning backward corresponds to Joshua's account of the incoming waters standing and the downstream flow ceasing as Israel crosses. The poetic turning and the narrative description retain their different forms.

Psalms 113:4
The mountains skipped like rams, and the hills like lambs of sheep.
  Isaiah 55:12 — Mountains and hills pictured leaping: Both personify mountains and hills as leaping. The psalm compares them to rams and lambs, while Isaiah describes their joyful welcome; the particular landscape movement is shared without identifying the occasions.

Psalms 113:5
What is it with you, sea, that you fled, and with you, Jordan, that you withdrew backward?
  Psalms 113:3 — Same-chapter question echoes the water imagery: The question to the sea and Jordan repeats the preceding statement that the sea fled and the Jordan turned backward. This same-chapter poetic echo changes the description into direct address.

Psalms 113:6
Mountains, that you skipped like rams, and hills, like lambs of sheep?
  Psalms 113:4 — Same-chapter question echoes the animal similes: The direct question to mountains and hills repeats their skipping like rams and lambs. It is a deliberate same-chapter repetition of the landscape similes in interrogative form.

Psalms 113:7
Before the Lord the earth was shaken, before the God of Jacob,
  Judges 5:4-5 — Earth and mountains shake before the Lord: Deborah and Barak's song describes the earth and mountains shaking at the Lord's going forth. The psalm similarly states that the earth was shaken before the Lord, the God of Jacob.

Psalms 113:8
who turned the rock into pools of water and the sheer rock into springs of water.
  Exodus 17:6 — Water from the rock: The psalm describes the rock becoming a source of water. Exodus gives the particular narrative of the rock at Horeb producing water when Moses strikes it at God's command, without requiring the psalm to name that location.
  Numbers 20:11 — Another narrated provision of rock water: Numbers narrates abundant water coming from the rock for the congregation and livestock. It supplies a second explicit rock-water passage corresponding to the psalm's image, without conflating it with the Horeb account.

Psalms 113:9
Not to us, Lord, not to us, but only to Thy name give glory, because of Thy mercy and Thy truth,
  Ezekiel 36:22-23 — God acts for His name rather than the people: The psalm asks that glory go to God's name, not to us. Ezekiel says God's action is not for Israel but for His holy name among the nations; the contrast between the people and the divine name is explicit in both.

Psalms 113:10
lest the nations say, “Where is their God?”
  Joel 2:17 — The nations ask where Israel's God is: The same hostile question, Where is their God, appears in Joel's priests' plea against national reproach. Both prayers appeal to God in view of what surrounding nations might say.

Psalms 113:11
But our God is in heaven above; in the heavens and on the earth He has done everything He wished.
  Psalms 134:6 — God does what He wills in heaven and earth: Both declare that the Lord did everything He wished in heaven and on earth. Psalm 134 extends the list to seas and deeps, while Psalm 113 begins by affirming God's heavenly place.

Psalms 113:12
The idols of the nations are silver and gold, works of human hands.
  Psalms 134:15 — Repeated description of manufactured idols: Both describe the nations' idols as silver and gold and the works of human hands. The material and human workmanship recur together.

Psalms 113:13
They have a mouth and will not speak; they have eyes and will not see.
  Psalms 134:16 — Repeated speechless mouths and sightless eyes: Both passages give idols mouths that cannot speak and eyes that cannot see. The identical paired faculties form part of the two psalms' extended idol comparison.

Psalms 113:14
They have ears and will not hear; they have noses and will not smell.
  Psalms 134:17 — Repeated deaf ears and unresponsive noses: The idols' ears that do not hear and noses that do not smell recur in Psalm 134's longer unit. Both retain the bodily faculties and their failure to function.

Psalms 113:15
They have hands and will not feel; feet, and will not walk; they will not sound with their throat.
  Psalms 134:17 — Repeated powerless hands, feet and throat: Psalm 134's longer unit repeats the hands that do not touch, feet that do not walk and throat that produces no sound. These bodily faculties are powerless in both idol portraits.

Psalms 113:16
May those who make them become like them, and all who have put their trust in them.
  Psalms 134:18 — Repeated fate of idol makers and their trusters: Both ask that those making the idols and those trusting them become like them. The shared consequence completes the preceding description of the idols' inability to act.

Psalms 113:17
The house of Israel hoped in the Lord; He is their helper and their defender.
  Psalms 134:19 — The house of Israel in corresponding worship lists: Both explicitly address or describe the house of Israel in a sequence that also names Aaron's house. Psalm 113 describes its hope in the Lord, while Psalm 134 commands it to bless Him.

Psalms 113:18
The house of Aaron hoped in the Lord; He is their helper and their defender.
  Psalms 134:19 — The house of Aaron in corresponding worship lists: Aaron's house follows Israel's house in both passages. The psalm describes its hope and the Lord's help, while Psalm 134 calls the same named house to bless the Lord.

Psalms 113:19
Those who fear the Lord hoped in the Lord; He is their helper and their defender.
  Psalms 134:20 — Those fearing the Lord in corresponding worship lists: Those who fear the Lord appear after the named houses in both psalms' worship sequences. Here they hope in the Lord as helper and defender; Psalm 134 summons them to bless Him.

Psalms 113:20
The Lord remembered us and blessed us; He blessed the house of Israel; He blessed the house of Aaron.
  Psalms 113:17-18 — Same-chapter hope answered by blessing: The houses of Israel and Aaron, previously described as hoping in the Lord, reappear together as recipients of His remembrance and blessing. This same-chapter progression connects the named groups across the two stanzas.

Psalms 113:21
He blessed those who fear the Lord, the small together with the great.
  Psalms 113:19 — Same-chapter God-fearing group receives blessing: The people who fear the Lord, already described as hoping in Him, now receive blessing, small together with great. The repeated group links trust and blessing while adding explicit inclusion of the small and great.

Psalms 113:22
May the Lord add to you, to you and to your sons.
  Deuteronomy 1:11 — A blessing of divine increase: The request that the Lord add to the people and their sons corresponds to Moses' wish that the Lord make the people a thousand times more numerous and bless them. Both explicitly pray for increase from God.

Psalms 113:23
You are blessed by the Lord, who made heaven and earth.
  Genesis 14:19 — Blessing by the Maker of heaven and earth: The psalm identifies the blessing's giver as the Lord who made heaven and earth. Melchizedek likewise blesses Abram by God Most High, who created heaven and earth, coupling blessing with the same creative scope.

Psalms 113:24
The heaven of heaven belongs to the Lord, but the earth He gave to the sons of men.
  Genesis 1:28 — The earth entrusted to human beings: The earth given to human beings corresponds to God's blessing commissioning humanity to fill and subdue it and exercise rule over its creatures. Genesis describes the human commission rather than repeating the psalm's separate heaven-of-heaven clause.

Psalms 113:25
The dead will not praise Thee, Lord, nor any who descend into Hades,
  Isaiah 38:18 — The dead and those in Hades do not praise: Hezekiah's prayer expressly says those in Hades do not praise and the dead do not bless God. It corresponds to the psalm's denial of praise by the dead and those descending into Hades.

Psalms 113:26
but we, the living, will bless the Lord from now and for ever.
  Isaiah 38:19 — The living bless the Lord: The psalm contrasts the living who bless the Lord with the preceding dead. Hezekiah's prayer makes the same contrast by saying the living will bless God, as he himself does.

Psalms 114:1
Hallelujah. I loved, for the Lord will hear the voice of my petition,
  Psalms 65:19-20 — The voice of petition heard: Psalm 114 anticipates the Lord hearing the voice of petition. Psalm 65 reports that God heard and attended to the sound of petition and did not turn prayer away; the future expectation and reported answer remain distinct.

Psalms 114:2
for He inclined His ear to me, and in my days I will call upon Him.
  Psalms 85:1 — The Lord inclines His ear: The report that the Lord inclined His ear corresponds to Psalm 85's request that He incline His ear and hear. Both use the same listening image in a personal appeal.

Psalms 114:3
The pangs of death surrounded me; the dangers of Hades found me; I found affliction and pain.
  Psalms 17:5-6 — Death and Hades surround the speaker: Both describe death and Hades as surrounding dangers. Psalm 17 names birth pangs of death and Hades and traps of death, closely paralleling the psalm's pangs, dangers and affliction.

Psalms 114:4
And I called on the name of the Lord: “O Lord, rescue my soul!”
  Psalms 17:7 — Calling to the Lord from distress: After describing death's surrounding dangers, both speakers call upon the Lord. Psalm 114 gives the request to rescue the soul, while Psalm 17 recounts the cry being heard.

Psalms 114:5
Merciful is the Lord, and righteous; and our God shows mercy.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — Divine mercy and righteousness: The psalm calls the Lord merciful and righteous and says God shows mercy. Exodus' proclamation describes Him as compassionate and merciful, preserving righteousness and showing mercy, joining the same mercy and righteousness themes.

Psalms 114:6
The Lord guards the little children; I was humbled, and He saved me.
  Matthew 18:10 — Divine care for little ones: The Lord's guarding of little children corresponds to Jesus' warning not to despise little ones because their angels behold His Father's face. The connection concerns divine regard for the little ones, without replacing the psalm's children with another edition's wording.

Psalms 114:7
Return, my soul, to your rest, for the Lord has benefited you,
  Matthew 11:28-29 — Rest for the soul: The address to the soul to return to rest corresponds to Jesus' invitation to the burdened and His promise of rest for their souls. The psalm describes received benefit, while Matthew presents an invitation to learn from Jesus.

Psalms 114:8
for He rescued my soul from death, my eyes from tears, and my feet from slipping.
  Psalms 55:14 — Rescue from death and feet from slipping: Both explicitly describe the soul rescued from death and feet kept from slipping. Psalm 114 adds eyes rescued from tears, retaining an additional detail not found in the target.

Psalms 114:9
I will be well pleasing before the Lord in the land of the living.
  Psalms 55:14 — Being pleasing before God among the living: Both connect deliverance with being pleasing before God among the living. Psalm 114 says land of the living and Psalm 55 says light of the living; those distinct expressions are preserved.

Psalms 115:1
Hallelujah. I believed; therefore I spoke. But I was greatly humbled.
  2 Corinthians 4:13 — Explicit quotation linking belief and speech: Paul introduces as written I believed, therefore I spoke and applies the sequence to his own believing and speaking. This directly cites the opening clause of the psalm's unit.

Psalms 115:2
I said in my bewilderment, “Every man is a liar.”
  Romans 3:4 — Every human being a liar: Romans repeats the statement that every human being is a liar while affirming God's truth. The psalm places it in the speaker's bewilderment; Romans' following formally introduced quotation is a separate clause about God's justification.

Psalms 115:3
What shall I repay to the Lord for everything He has repaid to me?
  1 Chronicles 29:14 — Returning to God what comes from Him: The psalm asks what can be repaid to the Lord for His benefits. David's thanksgiving asks how the people could offer so willingly and answers that everything is God's and their gift comes from His own; both frame human giving as a response to divine provision.

Psalms 115:6
Precious before the Lord is the death of His holy ones.
  Wisdom of Solomon 3:1-4 — The righteous in death remain valued before God: The psalm calls the death of God's holy ones precious before Him. Wisdom contrasts the foolish view of the righteous person's departure as destruction with their peace in God's hand and hope of immortality, offering a particular divine-versus-human valuation of that death.

Psalms 115:7
O Lord, I am Thy slave; I am Thy slave and the son of Thy slave woman. Thou hast torn apart my bonds.
  Psalms 85:16 — God's servant and the son of His maidservant: Both speakers identify themselves through service to God and their mother's status as His servant. Psalm 115 reports bonds torn apart, while Psalm 85 asks for saving might; the shared servant-and-mother formula is explicit.

Psalms 115:8
To Thee I will sacrifice a sacrifice of praise.
  Hebrews 13:15 — Sacrifice of praise: The promised sacrifice of praise corresponds to Hebrews' instruction to offer God a sacrifice of praise through Jesus, described there as lips confessing His name.

Psalms 115:9
I will repay my vows to the Lord before all His people,
  Psalms 21:26 — Vows repaid before the worshiping people: Both speakers promise to repay vows publicly. Psalm 115 specifies all the Lord's people, while Psalm 21 places the vows before those who fear Him in a great assembly.

Psalms 115:10
in the courts of the Lord’s house, in your midst, Jerusalem.
  Psalms 99:4 — Thanksgiving within the Lord's courts: The location of Psalm 115's praise and vow payment is the Lord's courts in Jerusalem. Psalm 99 explicitly calls worshipers to enter His courts with hymns and thanksgiving, a corresponding setting and act of worship.

Psalms 116:1
Hallelujah. Praise the Lord, all nations; laud Him, all peoples,
  Romans 15:11 — Explicit quotation inviting all nations to praise: Romans quotes the call for all Gentiles and peoples to praise the Lord in its discussion of Gentiles glorifying God for mercy. Both universal groups in the psalm's summons are retained.

Psalms 116:2
for His mercy has grown strong over us, and the Lord’s truth remains for ever.
  Psalms 99:5 — Mercy paired with enduring truth: Both pair the Lord's mercy with the permanence of His truth. Psalm 116 says truth remains for ever, while Psalm 99 extends it through generation after generation.

Psalms 117:1
Hallelujah. Give thanks to the Lord, for He is good, for His mercy is for ever.
  1 Chronicles 16:34 — Shared thanksgiving and enduring-mercy formula: Both call for thanksgiving to the Lord and affirm His mercy for ever. The psalm's He is good and Chronicles' it is good are preserved as different wordings in the shared formula.

Psalms 117:2
Let the house of Israel now say that He is good, that His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 113:17 — Israel's house in a repeated worship sequence: Israel's house begins a sequence followed by Aaron's house and those who fear the Lord in both psalms. Psalm 117 asks it to proclaim enduring mercy; Psalm 113 describes its hope in the Lord.

Psalms 117:3
Let the house of Aaron now say that He is good, that His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 113:18 — Aaron's house in a repeated worship sequence: Aaron's house occupies the corresponding place after Israel in both lists. Here it is summoned to affirm the Lord's enduring mercy; in Psalm 113 it hopes in Him as helper and defender.

Psalms 117:4
Let all those who fear the Lord now say that He is good, that His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 113:19 — God-fearers in a repeated worship sequence: Those fearing the Lord follow the named houses in both sequences. Psalm 117 calls them to declare His enduring mercy, and Psalm 113 describes their hope in His help.

Psalms 117:5
In affliction I called upon the Lord, and He answered me with room to breathe.
  Psalms 17:19-20 — Affliction changed into spaciousness: The Lord's answer gives the afflicted speaker room to breathe. Psalm 17 likewise joins affliction to the Lord's support and being brought into a spacious place, using spatial relief to describe rescue.

Psalms 117:6
The Lord is my helper; I will not fear what a man will do to me.
  Hebrews 13:6 — Explicit quotation of fearless trust: Hebrews expressly quotes the Lord as helper and the refusal to fear what a human being will do. The quoted confession reproduces this verse's assurance.

Psalms 117:7
The Lord is my helper, and I will look upon my enemies.
  Psalms 53:9 — Rescue followed by seeing enemies: The psalm joins the Lord's help with looking upon enemies. Psalm 53 similarly joins rescue from affliction with the speaker's eye having looked upon enemies; the future and reported actions remain distinct.

Psalms 117:8
Better to trust in the Lord than to trust in a man.
  Jeremiah 17:5-7 — Trust in the Lord contrasted with trust in humans: The preference for trusting the Lord over a human corresponds to Jeremiah's contrasting curse on human-dependent hope and blessing on trust in the Lord. Both make the alternatives explicit.

Psalms 117:9
Better to hope in the Lord than to hope in rulers.
  Psalms 145:3-5 — Hope in God rather than rulers: Both contrast reliance on rulers with hope in God. Psalm 145 explains the rulers' lack of salvation and mortality, then calls happy the person whose helper and hope is God.

Psalms 117:10
All nations surrounded me, and in the name of the Lord I warded them off.
  Psalms 117:11 — Same-chapter hostile-surrounding refrain: The surrounding enemies and defense in the Lord's name recur immediately with an intensified doubling of surrounded. This is a same-chapter refrain, not an identification of a particular national battle.

Psalms 117:11
Surrounding me, they surrounded me, and in the name of the Lord I warded them off.
  Psalms 21:17 — Hostile encirclement of the speaker: The speaker surrounded by attackers corresponds to Psalm 21's encirclement by a gathering of evildoers. Psalm 21 uses dog imagery, while Psalm 117:11 emphasizes warding attackers off in the Lord's name; their attackers are not identified as one group.

Psalms 117:12
They surrounded me like bees a honeycomb, and they blazed up like fire among thorns, and in the name of the Lord I warded them off.
  Deuteronomy 1:44 — Enemies compared to bees: Both compare hostile action to bees. The psalm describes surrounding enemies warded off in the Lord's name; Deuteronomy recalls Amorites pursuing and wounding disobedient Israel, so the shared simile does not imply a shared outcome.

Psalms 117:13
Pushed, I was overturned so as to fall, and the Lord took hold of me.
  Psalms 36:24 — The Lord supports a falling person: The speaker describes being pushed over so as to fall and the Lord taking hold of him. Psalm 36 describes a fall without being dashed down because the Lord supports the person's hand. Both link falling with the Lord's supporting hold.

Psalms 117:14
The Lord is my strength and my hymn, and He has become my salvation.
  Isaiah 12:2 — The Lord becomes the speaker's salvation: Both join praise of the Lord with the declaration that He became the speaker's salvation. Psalm 117 names strength and hymn; Isaiah names glory and praise, retaining the related but different descriptors.
  Exodus 15:2 — Salvation celebrated in song: The song at the sea says God became a helper and shelter for salvation and promises to glorify and exalt Him. This corresponds to the psalm's Lord as strength, hymn and salvation without importing a different edition's strength-and-song wording into Exodus.

Psalms 117:15
A voice of exultation and salvation is in the tents of the righteous: “The Lord’s right hand has done a mighty work;
  Exodus 15:6 — The Lord's mighty right hand praised: The rejoicing tents proclaim the Lord's right hand doing a mighty work. The sea song similarly praises His right hand glorified in strength and shattering enemies.

Psalms 117:16
the Lord’s right hand lifted me high; the Lord’s right hand has done a mighty work.”
  Exodus 15:6 — Repeated praise of the powerful right hand: The Lord's right hand doing mighty work corresponds again to the sea song's right hand glorified in strength. The psalm also says it lifted the speaker high, a clause not supplied by Exodus.

Psalms 117:17
I will not die, but live, and tell in full the works of the Lord.
  Isaiah 38:19 — Surviving to tell God's works: The speaker expects to live and recount the Lord's works. Hezekiah's prayer after illness describes the living blessing God and children announcing His righteousness, joining continued life to testimony.

Psalms 117:18
The Lord disciplined me with discipline, and did not hand me over to death.
  2 Corinthians 6:9 — Disciplined but not put to death: Paul's description of being disciplined yet not put to death closely matches the psalm's discipline without being handed over to death. Both explicitly distinguish affliction from a fatal outcome.

Psalms 117:19
Open for me the gates of righteousness; entering through them, I will give thanks to the Lord.
  Isaiah 26:2 — Gates opened for righteous entry: Both call for gates to open and associate entry with righteousness. The psalm's speaker wishes to enter and thank the Lord; Isaiah invites a people keeping righteousness and truth.

Psalms 117:20
This is the gate of the Lord; the righteous will enter through it.
  Isaiah 26:2 — The righteous enter the gate: The statement that the righteous will enter the Lord's gate corresponds to Isaiah's gate opened for a people keeping righteousness and truth. The shared entry qualification is explicit.

Psalms 117:21
I will thank Thee, for Thou heardest me and becamest my salvation.
  Psalms 117:28 — Same-chapter thanksgiving reprise: The thanks for being heard and for God becoming salvation reappears near the psalm's end. The same-chapter repetition links the entry-through-the-gates sequence with the concluding personal praise.

Psalms 117:22
The stone the builders rejected— this has become the head of the corner.
  Matthew 21:42 — Explicit quotation of the rejected stone: Jesus quotes the rejected stone becoming the head of the corner after the vineyard parable. Matthew's single verse also includes the next psalm verse's declaration of the Lord's marvelous action.
  Mark 12:10 — Explicit quotation of the rejected stone: Mark's Jesus asks whether His hearers have read this Scripture and quotes the builders' rejected stone becoming the head of the corner. The citation follows the rejected-son vineyard parable.
  Luke 20:17 — Explicit quotation of the rejected stone: Luke introduces the statement as written and quotes the stone the builders rejected becoming the head of the corner. Its place after the vineyard parable is supplied by the target's context.
  Acts 4:11 — The stone saying applied to Jesus: Peter explicitly identifies Jesus as the stone despised by the builders that became the cornerstone. The target makes the application itself, rather than leaving the tracker to infer a new identity.
  1 Peter 2:7 — Explicit citation of the rejected stone: The letter repeats the stone rejected by builders becoming the head of the corner within its account of Christ as a living stone. The quotation supplies a direct literary connection.

Psalms 117:23
This has come from the Lord, and is marvelous in our eyes.
  Matthew 21:42 — Explicit quotation of the Lord's marvelous action: Matthew's quotation includes the statement that this came from the Lord and is marvelous in our eyes, directly corresponding to this source unit after the rejected-stone saying.
  Mark 12:11 — Explicit quotation of the Lord's marvelous action: Mark completes the stone quotation with this came from the Lord and is marvelous in our eyes. Both attribute the astonishing outcome to the Lord.

Psalms 117:24
This is the day the Lord has made; let us exult and be glad in it.
  Isaiah 25:9 — A day of joy in divine salvation: The psalm invites rejoicing in the day the Lord made. Isaiah describes what will be said on that day and rejoicing in salvation from the God in whom the people hoped; the relation is celebratory salvation language, not an identification of a date.

Psalms 117:26
Blessed is the one who comes in the name of the Lord. We have blessed you from the house of the Lord.
  Matthew 21:9 — Entry acclamation quoting the blessing: The crowds acclaim Jesus with the blessing on the one coming in the Lord's name. This directly reproduces the psalm's first clause in Matthew's Jerusalem-entry narrative.
  Mark 11:9 — Entry acclamation quoting the blessing: Mark's crowds before and behind Jesus repeat Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord. The shared blessing is explicit, while the surrounding Hosanna wording remains as given.
  Luke 19:38 — Royal entry acclamation using the blessing: Luke's disciples bless the King who comes in the Lord's name. The psalm's blessing formula is retained with the target's explicit royal title, which is not inserted into the source text.
  John 12:13 — Entry acclamation using the blessing: John's palm-bearing crowd blesses the one coming in the Lord's name and calls Him King of Israel. The first blessing clause corresponds directly to the psalm.
  Matthew 23:39 — Jesus cites the blessing concerning future recognition: Jesus tells Jerusalem it will not see Him until it speaks the blessing on the one coming in the Lord's name. The target itself places the psalm's formula within that declaration.
  Luke 13:35 — Jesus cites the blessing concerning future recognition: Luke likewise records Jesus telling Jerusalem it will not see Him until the time when it says Blessed is He who comes in the name of the Lord. The correspondence is the explicit cited blessing.

Psalms 117:27
The Lord is God, and He has shone upon us. Arrange a festival among the thickly covering boughs, up to the horns of the altar.
  Leviticus 23:39-43 — Festival rejoicing with leafy branches: The psalm's festival among thickly covering boughs corresponds to Leviticus' festival using palm fronds and dense tree branches in rejoicing before the Lord. This is a limited ritual-image parallel; it does not identify the psalm's festival or explain its altar-horn detail.

Psalms 117:28
Thou art my God, and I will thank Thee; Thou art my God, and I will exalt Thee. I will thank Thee, for Thou heardest me and becamest my salvation.
  Isaiah 25:1 — My God addressed in promised exaltation: Both address the Lord as my God and promise glorification or exaltation and praise. Isaiah links that response to wondrous works, while the psalm links it to being heard and receiving salvation.

Psalms 117:29
Give thanks to the Lord, for He is good, for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 117:1 — Same-chapter opening and closing refrain: The final thanksgiving for the Lord's goodness and enduring mercy repeats the psalm's opening. This same-chapter framing refrain encloses the intervening account of help, praise and blessing.

Psalms 118:1
Hallelujah. One: Alph. Blessed are the blameless in the way, those who walk in the Lord’s law.
  Psalms 1:1-2 — Blessed conduct defined by the Lord’s law: Psalm 118 opens by blessing those blameless in their way who walk in the Lord’s law. Psalm 1 likewise opens with a blessing, contrasts the sinner’s way with the blessed person’s conduct, and locates his will and meditation in the Lord’s law.

Psalms 118:2
Blessed are those who search out His testimonies; with their whole heart they will seek Him.
  Deuteronomy 4:29 — Seeking the Lord with the whole heart: The psalm blesses those who search the testimonies and says they will seek God with their whole heart. Deuteronomy promises that the scattered people will find the Lord when they seek Him with all their heart and soul in distress; the shared wholehearted seeking does not identify the psalm’s circumstances with that dispersion.

Psalms 118:4
Thou didst command Thy commandments to be kept diligently.
  Deuteronomy 6:17 — Commanded diligence in keeping commandments: The psalm says God commanded His commandments to be kept diligently. Deuteronomy directly commands diligent keeping of the Lord’s commandments, testimonies and ordinances, providing a close formulation of the obligation acknowledged in the poem.

Psalms 118:5
If only my ways were directed to keep Thine ordinances!
  Psalms 36:23 — Divine direction of a person’s way and steps: The speaker wishes that his ways might be directed to keep God’s ordinances. Psalm 36 states that a person’s steps are directed by the Lord; the desired direction in Psalm 118 is expressly toward obedience, while the target states the Lord’s direction of the person’s steps.

Psalms 118:6
Then I will certainly not be ashamed when I look upon all Thy commandments.
  Psalms 118:80 — Obedience and freedom from shame (same-chapter): Verse 6 anticipates not being ashamed when looking to all the commandments. Verse 80 asks for a blameless heart in the ordinances so as not to be ashamed. The later petition returns to the earlier connection between attention to all commandments or a blameless heart in ordinances and freedom from shame.

Psalms 118:7
I will give thanks unto Thee, Lord, with uprightness of heart, when I have learned Thy righteous judgments.
  Psalms 118:171 — Learning instruction followed by praise (same-chapter): Verse 7 promises upright-hearted thanksgiving when the speaker learns God’s righteous judgments. Verse 171 similarly asks that a hymn pour from the lips when God teaches His ordinances. The two passages expressly connect learning or being taught with the ensuing praise, while distinguishing heart and lips.

Psalms 118:9
Two: Beth. By what will the younger man set his way right? By keeping Thy words.
  Proverbs 4:20-27b — Words kept inwardly and a young person’s directed way: The psalm asks how a younger man can set his way right and answers with keeping God’s words. Proverbs addresses a son, commands attention to words and their keeping in the heart, and then commands straight tracks and avoidance of an evil way. Its final native continuation says God will make the tracks straight; the parental instruction is compared without identifying the two speakers.

Psalms 118:11
In my heart I hid Thy sayings, so that I might not sin against Thee.
  Psalms 36:31 — Divine instruction in the heart guards conduct: Psalm 118 hides God’s sayings in the heart so as not to sin against Him. Psalm 36 places God’s law in the righteous person’s heart and says his steps will not be tripped up. Both connect inwardly held instruction with guarded conduct, using sin and stumbling respectively.
  Deuteronomy 6:6 — Commanded words placed in the heart: The speaker’s hiding of God’s sayings in his heart corresponds to Deuteronomy’s command that the words be in the hearer’s heart and soul. Psalm 118 adds the particular purpose of not sinning against God; Deuteronomy continues with teaching and speaking the words in daily life.

Psalms 118:14
In the way of Thy testimonies I delighted, as in all wealth.
  Psalms 18:11 — Divine instruction valued against material riches: The speaker delighted in the way of God’s testimonies as in all wealth. Psalm 18 calls the Lord’s judgments desirable beyond gold and much precious stone. Both explicitly compare the value of divine instruction with riches, while one speaks of delight as in wealth and the other of greater desirability.

Psalms 118:18
Uncover my eyes, and I will consider Thy wonders from Thy law.
  Luke 24:44-45 — Opened perception for understanding sacred writings: The psalm asks God to uncover the speaker’s eyes to consider wonders from His law. Luke says Jesus opened the disciples’ mind to understand the Scriptures after naming the Law of Moses, Prophets and Psalms. Opened eyes and opened mind are distinct images of enabled understanding; no identification of the psalm’s speaker with the disciples is required.

Psalms 118:19
I am a sojourner on the earth; do not hide Thy commandments from me.
  1 Chronicles 29:15 — Earthly life described as sojourning before God: The speaker calls himself a sojourner on earth while asking that the commandments not be hidden. David’s prayer in Chronicles calls the people resident strangers and sojourners before God and compares their earthly days to a shadow. The common sojourner description is retained without assigning David as this psalm’s speaker.

Psalms 118:21
Thou didst rebuke the arrogant; accursed are those who turn away from Thy commandments.
  Deuteronomy 27:26 — A curse upon departure from divine instruction: The psalm calls those turning away from God’s commandments accursed and mentions His rebuke of the arrogant. Deuteronomy pronounces a curse upon everyone who does not continue in all the law’s words to do them. Both attach the curse to failure to remain with the commanded instruction.

Psalms 118:22
Remove reproach and contempt from me, for I sought Thy testimonies.
  Psalms 118:39 — Removal of reproach requested twice (same-chapter): Verse 22 asks that reproach and contempt be removed because the speaker sought the testimonies. Verse 39 returns to removal of dreaded reproach and declares the judgments good. The repeated request is compared with its different stated grounds, without treating either petition as an accomplished removal.

Psalms 118:23
For rulers also sat and were speaking against me, but Thy servant was pondering Thine ordinances.
  Psalms 118:161 — Rulers’ opposition beside attention to divine words (same-chapter): Verse 23 sets rulers sitting and speaking against the speaker beside his pondering of God’s ordinances. Verse 161 sets rulers’ causeless pursuit beside the heart’s fear of God’s words. The paired scenes contrast rulers’ opposition with the speaker’s response to divine instruction, without identifying the rulers or equating speaking against with pursuit.

Psalms 118:24
For Thy testimonies are also my meditation, and Thine ordinances are my counsels.
  Proverbs 6:20-22 — Instruction serving as counsel and a speaking companion: The psalm calls the testimonies its meditation and the ordinances its counsels. Proverbs tells the son to retain parental laws and ordinances and presents instruction as a companion that accompanies, guards and converses with him. The comparison concerns instruction’s advisory role while preserving Proverbs’ parental setting.

Psalms 118:25
Four: Delth. My soul clung to the ground; give me life according to Thy word.
  Psalms 43:26 — Clinging to the ground in lament: Psalm 118 says the speaker’s soul clung to the ground and asks for life according to God’s word. Psalm 43 says the people’s soul was humbled to dust and their belly clung to the earth. The shared ground-clinging lament differs in its bodily subject and in singular versus communal voice.

Psalms 118:27
Give me understanding of the way of Thine ordinances, and I will ponder Thy wonders.
  Psalms 118:18 — Understanding and consideration of wonders in instruction (same-chapter): Verse 27 asks for understanding of the ordinances’ way and promises pondering of God’s wonders. Verse 18 asks for uncovered eyes to consider wonders from the law. Both petitions seek enabled comprehension of wonders, with understanding and uncovered eyes as distinct formulations.

Psalms 118:28
My soul dripped away from weariness; strengthen me by Thy words.
  Isaiah 40:30-31 — Weariness answered with renewed strength: The psalm describes a soul dripping away from weariness and asks strengthening through God’s words. Isaiah contrasts young men growing weary and chosen men lacking strength with those enduring for God exchanging strength and running without weariness. The comparison concerns weariness and renewed strength; it does not substitute the familiar but absent wording about giving strength to the weary in Isaiah 40:29.

Psalms 118:31
I clung to Thy testimonies; Lord, do not put me to shame.
