  Psalms 118:25 — Two objects of clinging in the Delth stanza (same-chapter): Within the same stanza, verse 25 says the soul clung to the ground, whereas verse 31 says the speaker clung to the testimonies. The repeated clinging language shifts from a condition prompting the plea for life to adherence prompting the plea against shame; the objects and petitions remain distinct.

Psalms 118:32
I ran the way of Thy commandments when Thou didst enlarge my heart.
  1 Kings 5:9 — Divine enlargement or abundance of heart: The speaker reports running in the commandments’ way when God enlarged his heart. First Kings says the Lord gave Solomon discernment, wisdom and abundance of heart like the sand beside the sea. Both describe divinely given expansiveness of heart, but the poem concerns running in obedience and the account concerns Solomon’s wisdom.

Psalms 118:33
Five: Ē. Instruct me, Lord, in the way of Thine ordinances, and I will seek it continually.
  Psalms 24:4 — Teaching the divine way: Psalm 118 asks the Lord to instruct the speaker in the way of His ordinances and says he will seek it continually. Psalm 24 asks the Lord to make His ways known and teach His paths. The common formulation joins divine teaching with way or path imagery; the psalm’s continual seeking of that way is not supplied to the target.

Psalms 118:36
Bend my heart toward Thy testimonies, and not toward greed.
  Jeremiah 22:17 — The heart’s direction toward instruction or greed: The speaker asks God to bend his heart toward the testimonies and not greed. Jeremiah condemns eyes and heart directed toward greed, innocent blood and wrongdoing. The texts contrast the requested turning away from greed with an explicitly greedy orientation, without identifying the psalmist with Jeremiah’s addressee.

Psalms 118:37
Turn away my eyes so that they do not see futility; give me life in Thy way.
  Psalms 100:3 — Turning the eyes from futility or lawlessness: Psalm 118 asks God to turn the eyes away from seeing futility. Psalm 100 reports not setting a lawless matter before the eyes. Both concern what is placed before one’s sight, while distinguishing futility from lawlessness and a petition from reported conduct.

Psalms 118:38
Establish Thy saying for Thy servant, for the fear of Thee.
  2 Samuel 7:25 — Establishing the word spoken for the servant: The psalm asks God to establish His saying for His servant, for fear of Him. David’s prayer in 2 Samuel asks that the word concerning the servant and his house be confirmed for ever. The servant-and-established-word formulation is shared; the poem does not name a house or identify its saying as the dynastic promise.

Psalms 118:39
Remove my reproach, which I dreaded, for Thy judgments are good.
  Psalms 118:22 — Reproach-removal refrain with differing grounds (same-chapter): Verse 39 asks that dreaded reproach be removed because God’s judgments are good. Verse 22 similarly asks removal of reproach and contempt after seeking the testimonies. The repeated appeal is a literary link; the goodness of judgments and the speaker’s seeking are different stated grounds.

Psalms 118:41
Six: Ouau. May Thy mercy also come upon me, Lord, Thy salvation according to Thy saying.
  Psalms 84:8 — Mercy and salvation requested together: Psalm 118 asks for God’s mercy to come and for salvation according to His saying. Psalm 84 asks the Lord to show mercy and give salvation. Both petitions explicitly pair mercy with salvation, without turning either request into a report of completed rescue.

Psalms 118:42
And I will answer those who reproach me with a word, for I hoped in Thy words.
  1 Peter 3:15-16 — An answer grounded in hope amid reproach: The psalm anticipates answering reproachers with a word because the speaker hoped in God’s words. First Peter urges readiness to give an account of hope and joins that defense with good conscience under slander. The psalm’s reproachers and the letter’s questioners are not equated; the comparison joins hopeful speech with a context of hostile accusation.

Psalms 118:43
And do not remove the word of truth from my mouth utterly, for I placed my hope in Thy judgments.
  Isaiah 59:21 — Divine words remaining in the mouth: Psalm 118 asks that the word of truth not be removed utterly from the speaker’s mouth. Isaiah’s covenant declaration says the words put in the mouth will never fail from that mouth or the offspring’s mouth. The requested retention and promised persistence share the concrete mouth-and-word formulation, while Isaiah expressly extends it to offspring.

Psalms 118:45
And I was walking in a broad place, for I sought Thy commandments.
  Psalms 17:20 — Walking or being brought into spaciousness: The speaker reports walking in a broad place because he sought God’s commandments. Psalm 17 reports God bringing the speaker into a spacious place and adds future rescue from enemies. The spacious-place image is shared; Psalm 118 grounds its walking in seeking commandments and does not itself narrate the target’s rescue event.

Psalms 118:46
And I was speaking of Thy testimonies before kings, and I was not ashamed.
  Matthew 10:18-20 — Testimony spoken before kings: The speaker reports speaking God’s testimonies before kings without shame. Jesus tells His messengers they will be brought before governors and kings as a testimony and that speech will be supplied to them. Both put testimony in a royal audience, with different speakers and past versus future settings.

Psalms 118:48
And I raised my hands toward Thy commandments, which I loved, and I was pondering Thine ordinances.
  Psalms 62:5 — Lifted hands in relation to divine commandments or name: The speaker raised his hands toward the commandments he loved. Psalm 62 promises to lift hands in God’s name. The shared raised-hand gesture is particular, but the different expressions toward commandments and in the name are preserved rather than treated as identical objects.

Psalms 118:50
This comforted me in my humiliation, for Thy saying gave me life.
  Psalms 118:92 — Life sustained amid humiliation (same-chapter): Verse 50 recalls comfort in humiliation because God’s saying gave life. Verse 92 says that without the law as meditation the speaker would have perished in humiliation. The two verses connect the same condition with life-preserving divine instruction, using a completed benefit and a counterfactual respectively.

Psalms 118:51
The arrogant were acting lawlessly to an extreme, but I did not turn away from Thy law.
  Psalms 118:69 — Arrogant lawlessness contrasted with fidelity (same-chapter): Verse 51 says the arrogant acted lawlessly to excess while the speaker did not turn from the law. Verse 69 says the injustice of the arrogant multiplied against him, but he will search out the commandments with his whole heart. The repeated opponent-and-response pattern preserves the different forms of the speaker’s fidelity.

Psalms 118:52
I remembered Thy judgments from of old, Lord, and I was comforted.
  Romans 15:4 — Earlier revelation as a source of comfort: The speaker remembered God’s judgments of old and was comforted. Romans says what was written beforehand serves instruction so that perseverance and the encouragement of the Scriptures might bring hope. Both connect earlier revelation with present encouragement, without asserting Romans quotes this verse.

Psalms 118:53
Despondency seized me because of sinners who forsake Thy law.
  Ezra 9:3-10 — Distress at the forsaking of divine commandments: Psalm 118 reports despondency because sinners forsake God’s law. Ezra’s account reports agitation and torn garments at covenant-breaking, then confesses that the community abandoned God’s commandments. The emotional responses and circumstances differ; the link is specifically distress over abandoned divine instruction, not identification of the two crises.

Psalms 118:54
Thine ordinances were songs to me in the place of my sojourning.
  Psalms 136:3-4 — Song in sojourning or foreign land: Psalm 118 calls God’s ordinances songs in the place of the speaker’s sojourning. Psalm 136 reports captors demanding Zion’s songs and asks how to sing the Lord’s song on foreign land. Both join song with sojourning or life on foreign land, but Psalm 118 does not name captivity and reports songs where Psalm 136 voices a difficulty.

Psalms 118:55
I remembered Thy name in the night, Lord, and kept Thy law.
  Psalms 62:7 — Remembrance during the night and dawn meditation: The speaker remembered God’s name at night and kept His law. Psalm 62 speaks conditionally of remembering God upon the bed and meditating on Him in the dawn hours. Both connect remembrance with these hours, while the name-and-law formulation is distinguished from remembrance of God upon the bed.

Psalms 118:57
Eight: Ēth. Thou art my portion, Lord; I said that I would keep Thy law.
  Psalms 15:5 — The Lord as the speaker’s portion: Psalm 118 declares the Lord to be the speaker’s portion and reports having said that he would keep the law. Psalm 15 calls the Lord the portion of the speaker’s inheritance and cup and the restorer of his inheritance. The shared portion language is retained without flattening the target’s fuller inheritance-and-cup expression.

Psalms 118:58
I entreated Thy face with my whole heart; have mercy on me according to Thy saying.
  Psalms 26:8-9 — Heart and face in seeking divine favor: Psalm 118 says the speaker entreated God’s face with his whole heart and asks mercy according to the saying. Psalm 26 has the heart say that the speaker’s face sought God, then expresses future seeking of the Lord’s face and asks that it not be turned away. Both combine heart, face and appeal, with different requests and no claim of identical circumstances.

Psalms 118:59
I considered Thy ways and turned my feet toward Thy testimonies.
  Proverbs 4:26-27b — The feet redirected along a right way: The speaker considered God’s ways and turned his feet toward the testimonies. Proverbs commands straight tracks, right direction of ways and turning the foot from an evil way, then says God will straighten tracks. The comparison is the concrete direction of feet and ways, preserving the psalm’s completed turn and Proverbs’ instruction and promise.

Psalms 118:61
Sinners’ cords entangled me, and I did not forget Thy law.
  Psalms 139:6 — Hostile cords as a trapping image: Psalm 118 says sinners’ cords entangled the speaker, though he did not forget the law. Psalm 139 describes the proud hiding a trap and stretching cords as traps for his feet. The shared hostile-cord image does not require the sinners and proud to be the same group or the trapping scenes to be one event.

Psalms 118:62
At midnight I would rise to give thanks unto Thee for Thy righteous judgments.
  Acts 16:25 — Worship at midnight: The psalm speaks of rising at midnight to thank God for His righteous judgments. Acts places Paul and Silas praying and singing hymns to God about midnight in prison. Midnight worship is the particular link; Acts does not say they were reciting this psalm, and the psalm does not name a prison.

Psalms 118:63
I am a companion of all who fear Thee and of those who keep Thy commandments.
  Psalms 14:4 — Chosen association with those who fear the Lord: The speaker calls himself a companion of all who fear God and of those who keep His commandments. Psalm 14 describes the acceptable sojourner as honoring those who fear the Lord. Companionship and honor are different forms of positive association with the explicitly named God-fearing group.

Psalms 118:64
The earth is full of Thy mercy, Lord; teach me Thine ordinances.
  Psalms 32:5 — The earth full of the Lord’s mercy: Psalm 118 declares the earth full of God’s mercy before asking instruction. Psalm 32 uses the same earth-full-of-mercy declaration after saying the Lord loves mercy and judgment. The shared claim is distinct from the instructional petition added in Psalm 118.

Psalms 118:66
Teach me kindness, discipline and knowledge, for I trusted Thy commandments.
  Proverbs 1:2-4 — Learning discipline and knowledge: Psalm 118 asks to be taught kindness, discipline and knowledge because the speaker trusted the commandments. Proverbs announces learning wisdom and discipline and giving perception and thought to a young child. The texts share an instructional concern with discipline and knowing, but their lists and the psalm’s stated trust are not made identical.

Psalms 118:67
Before I was humbled, I did wrong; therefore I kept Thy saying.
  Deuteronomy 8:2-5 — Affliction connected with learning obedience: The speaker says that before being humbled he did wrong and therefore kept God’s saying. Deuteronomy recalls God’s afflicting and testing the people to reveal whether they would keep His commandments, then compares His chastening to a father’s. The personal account of wrongdoing before humbling and keeping the saying is compared with the wilderness discipline without identifying the experiences.

Psalms 118:69
The injustice of the arrogant multiplied against me, but I will search out Thy commandments with my whole heart.
  Psalms 118:51 — Arrogant injustice and steadfast response (same-chapter): Verse 69 sets the injustice of the arrogant multiplying against the speaker beside his promised wholehearted search for the commandments. Verse 51 similarly opposes the arrogant’s excessive lawlessness to his not turning from the law. This is a repeated two-part opposition, rather than a claim that every reference to the arrogant describes one incident.

Psalms 118:70
Their heart curdled like milk, but I meditated on Thy law.
  Job 10:10 — Milk and curdling applied to human subjects: Psalm 118 says the opponents’ heart curdled like milk. Job asks whether God milked him out like milk and curdled him like cheese in the formation of his body. The distinctive milk-and-curdling imagery is shared; bodily formation is not equated with the psalm’s description of the opponents’ heart, nor is fatness substituted for curdling.

Psalms 118:71
It is good for me that Thou didst humble me, so that I might learn Thine ordinances.
  Hebrews 12:10-11 — A painful experience described as beneficial training: The speaker says it was good to be humbled so that he might learn the ordinances. Hebrews describes divine discipline as beneficial for sharing holiness and says it yields righteousness after present sorrow. The shared beneficial-training pattern does not turn every affliction into punishment or identify the two experiences.

Psalms 118:72
The law of Thy mouth is better for me than thousands of gold and silver.
  Psalms 18:11 — Divine instruction preferred to precious wealth: Psalm 118 values the law of God’s mouth above thousands of gold and silver. Psalm 18 calls God’s judgments desirable beyond gold and much precious stone. Both explicitly rank instruction above precious wealth, while retaining the psalm’s thousands and silver and the target’s precious stone.

Psalms 118:73
Ten: Iōth. Thy hands made me and formed me; give me understanding, and I will learn Thy commandments.
  Job 10:8 — God’s hands forming the person who prays: Psalm 118 says God’s hands made and formed the speaker and asks understanding to learn commandments. Job likewise addresses God as the One whose hands molded and made him, but then accuses Him of changing and striking him. The formation-by-hands language is shared while the contrasting ensuing appeals remain distinct.

Psalms 118:74
Those who fear Thee will see me and rejoice, for I placed my hope in Thy words.
  Psalms 33:3 — Others’ rejoicing in connection with the speaker: Psalm 118 expects those fearing God to see the speaker and rejoice because he hoped in God’s words. Psalm 33 says the speaker’s soul will be praised in the Lord and invites the meek to hear and rejoice. Seeing and hearing, and those fearing God and the meek, remain distinct in this comparison of the speaker with others’ rejoicing.

Psalms 118:75
I knew, Lord, that Thy judgments are righteousness, and that in truth Thou didst humble me.
  Deuteronomy 8:2 — God’s humbling and the keeping of commandments: Psalm 118 acknowledges righteous judgments and says God humbled the speaker in truth. Deuteronomy attributes the wilderness affliction and testing to God and explicitly connects it with whether the people would keep commandments. The shared divine humbling is interpreted within each text’s own setting, not as proof the psalm narrates the wilderness.

Psalms 118:76
Let Thy mercy now be for my comfort, according to Thy saying to Thy servant.
  Psalms 118:50 — Comfort requested after comfort remembered (same-chapter): Verse 76 asks for mercy as comfort according to God’s saying to His servant. Verse 50 remembers being comforted in humiliation because the saying gave life. The later request echoes the earlier reported comfort and common appeal to the saying, without turning the request itself into a completed event.

Psalms 118:79
Let those who fear Thee turn to me, and those who know Thy testimonies.
  Psalms 118:63 — The God-fearing community and the speaker (same-chapter): Verse 79 asks those fearing God, and those knowing the testimonies, to turn toward the speaker. Verse 63 calls him a companion of those fearing God and of those keeping commandments. The paired descriptions are related to the speaker through requested turning and declared companionship; knowing and keeping are distinguished, and identical membership of the groups is not asserted.

Psalms 118:80
Let my heart be blameless in Thine ordinances, so that I may not be ashamed.
  Psalms 118:1 — Blamelessness in way and heart (same-chapter): The opening verse blesses those blameless in their way who walk in the law. Verse 80 asks for a blameless heart in the ordinances so as not to be ashamed. The petition applies the opening blamelessness motif inwardly, distinguishing a blessed way from a requested condition of the heart.

Psalms 118:81
Eleven: Chaph. My soul fails with longing for Thy salvation, and I placed my hope in Thy word.
  Psalms 118:174 — Longing for salvation beside attention to instruction (same-chapter): Verse 81 describes the soul failing in longing for salvation while hoping in God’s word. Verse 174 again declares longing for salvation and calls the law the speaker’s meditation. The repeated longing-for-salvation formulation has different accompanying responses, and only verse 81 describes the failing soul.

Psalms 118:82
My eyes failed with longing for Thy saying, saying, “When wilt Thou comfort me?”
  Psalms 118:123 — Eyes failing while awaiting the divine saying (same-chapter): Verse 82 says the eyes failed in longing for God’s saying and asks when comfort will come. Verse 123 repeats the failing-eyes image in longing for salvation and the righteous saying. The bodily image and awaited saying recur, with comfort and salvation distinguished.

Psalms 118:83
For I became like a wineskin in frost; I did not forget Thine ordinances.
  Job 13:28 — Wineskin similes with differing physical conditions: Psalm 118 compares the speaker to a wineskin in frost while he does not forget the ordinances. Job 13:28 says that it grows old like a wineskin or moth-eaten garment. The comparison is the unusual wineskin simile; it preserves frost versus aging and does not resolve the target’s pronoun or substitute smoke for frost.

Psalms 118:86
All Thy commandments are truth; they pursued me unjustly—help me.
  Psalms 118:150-151 — True commandments beside approaching lawless pursuers (same-chapter): Verse 86 calls all commandments truth and asks help against unjust pursuit. Verses 150–151 set lawless pursuers drawing near, though far from the law, beside the Lord being near and all His commandments being truth. The repeated truth claim appears in both pursuit settings, with the later near-and-far contrast adding a distinct development.

Psalms 118:88
Give me life according to Thy mercy, and I will keep the testimonies of Thy mouth.
  Deuteronomy 8:3 — Life and utterance from God’s mouth: Psalm 118 asks for life according to mercy and declares that the speaker will keep the testimonies of God’s mouth. Deuteronomy says a person lives by every word proceeding through God’s mouth. Both join life with divine utterance from the mouth, while the psalm combines a request for life with an undertaking to keep testimonies and Deuteronomy identifies that by which a person lives.

Psalms 118:89
Twelve: Labd. For ever, Lord, Thy word remains in heaven.
  Isaiah 40:8 — God’s word remaining for ever: Psalm 118 declares that God’s word remains for ever in heaven. Isaiah contrasts withered grass and fallen flower with the word of God remaining for ever. The same permanence claim is shared; the heavenly location and the grass-and-flower contrast belong to their respective passages.

Psalms 118:90
Thy truth is from generation to generation; Thou didst found the earth, and it remains.
  Psalms 88:2-3 — Truth extending through generations: Psalm 118 declares God’s truth from generation to generation and joins it with the earth He founded remaining. Psalm 88 promises to proclaim His truth from generation to generation and reports God’s saying that truth will be prepared in the heavens. The generational truth expression is shared without equating the earthly and heavenly statements.

Psalms 118:91
By Thine arrangement the day remains, for all things are Thy servants.
  Job 38:12-13 — The day or morning ordered by God: Psalm 118 says the day remains by God’s arrangement and all things are His servants. In the Lord’s speech in Job, He asks whether He ordered morning brightness in Job’s time and describes the dawn-bearer’s appointed place. Both place daylight under divine ordering; the psalm’s all-things-as-servants claim is not inserted into Job’s question.

Psalms 118:92
If Thy law had not been my meditation, then I would have perished in my humiliation.
  Psalms 118:50 — Instruction sustaining life under humiliation (same-chapter): Verse 92 says the speaker would have perished in humiliation without the law as meditation. Verse 50 recalls comfort in humiliation because God’s saying gave life. The counterfactual and remembered event share humiliation and life sustained through instruction, without being made one narrated occurrence.

Psalms 118:93
I will certainly never forget Thine ordinances, for by them Thou gavest me life, Lord.
  Proverbs 4:20-22 — Remembered instruction associated with life: Psalm 118 resolves never to forget the ordinances because God gave life through them. Proverbs commands attention to words and keeping them in the heart, explaining that there is life for those finding them and healing for all flesh. Both give life as a reason for retaining instruction, with divine ordinances and parental words distinguished.

Psalms 118:94
I am Thine; save me, for I sought Thine ordinances.
  Isaiah 43:1 — Belonging to God and appeal to rescue: The speaker says he is God’s and asks to be saved, having sought the ordinances. Isaiah’s divine address to Jacob and Israel says God redeemed them, called by name and declared them His. The belonging-and-rescue connection is explicit in both, with petition and completed redemption, and individual and national address, distinguished.

Psalms 118:95
Sinners waited for me to destroy me; I understood Thy testimonies.
  Psalms 36:32 — A sinner’s watch directed toward another’s death: Psalm 118 says sinners waited to destroy the speaker while he understood the testimonies. Psalm 36 describes the sinner observing the righteous and seeking to put him to death. Waiting or observation directed toward killing is the particular parallel; no identity of the attackers is asserted.

Psalms 118:97
Thirteen: Mēm. How I loved Thy law, Lord! It is my meditation all day.
  Psalms 1:2 — Delight in the law and sustained meditation: Psalm 118 exclaims over love of the law and calls it meditation all day. Psalm 1 places the blessed person’s will in the Lord’s law and says he will meditate on it day and night. Both combine inward attachment with an explicitly sustained rhythm of meditation, preserving all day versus day and night.

Psalms 118:98
Thou madest me wiser than my enemies in Thy commandment, for it is mine for ever.
  Deuteronomy 4:6 — Commandments and wisdom before others: Psalm 118 says God made the speaker wiser than enemies in His commandment. Deuteronomy tells Israel to keep and do the ordinances because this is their wisdom and understanding before other nations. Both connect commanded instruction with wisdom visible in relation to others, without equating enemies with all nations.

Psalms 118:99
I understood more than all who teach me, for Thy testimonies are my meditation.
  Psalms 118:100 — Comparative understanding beyond teachers and elders (same-chapter): Verse 99 says the speaker understood more than all his teachers because the testimonies are his meditation. Verse 100 similarly claims understanding beyond elders because he sought the commandments. The consecutive comparisons distinguish teachers from elders and meditation from seeking rather than treating those groups as identical.

Psalms 118:100
I understood more than elders, for I sought Thy commandments.
  Job 32:7-9 — Understanding not measured simply by age: Psalm 118 claims understanding beyond elders because the speaker sought commandments. Elihu recounts expecting years to convey wisdom, then says the Almighty’s breath teaches and that the long-lived are not wise and elders do not know judgment. Elihu’s speech is identified as his argument, not made the narrator’s unqualified verdict on the debate.

Psalms 118:101
I restrained my feet from every evil way, so that I might keep Thy words.
  Proverbs 4:27 — The foot restrained or turned from an evil way: Psalm 118 reports restraining the feet from every evil way to keep God’s words. Proverbs commands turning the foot away from an evil way and not turning right or left. The shared foot-and-evil-way language connects restraint with moral direction while preserving report and command.

Psalms 118:103
How sweet are Thy sayings to my throat, beyond honey and honeycomb to my mouth!
  Psalms 18:11 — Divine sayings sweeter than honey: Psalm 118 calls God’s sayings sweet to the throat beyond honey and honeycomb to the mouth. Psalm 18 likewise describes the Lord’s judgments as sweeter than honey and honeycomb. The distinctive double honey comparison is shared, with throat and mouth explicit in Psalm 118.
  Ezekiel 3:3-4 — Divine utterance experienced as honey in the mouth: Psalm 118 describes God’s sayings as sweeter than honey to the mouth. Ezekiel reports eating the given scroll, which became like sweet honey in his mouth, before being sent to speak God’s words. Figurative sweetness of sayings and the narrated eating of a scroll remain distinct.

Psalms 118:104
From Thy commandments I gained understanding; therefore I hated every way of injustice, for Thou didst instruct me.
  Psalms 118:128 — Commandments joined to hatred of the unjust way (same-chapter): Verse 104 derives understanding from commandments and therefore hatred of every unjust way. Verse 128 says the speaker was set right toward all commandments and hated every unjust way. The repeated rejection of the unjust way is paired with gaining understanding from commandments and being set right toward them, without assigning the latter phrase an unstated cause.

Psalms 118:105
Fourteen: Noun. Thy word is a lamp to my feet and a light to my paths.
  Proverbs 6:23 — Instruction as lamp, light and a guide for the way: Psalm 118 calls God’s word a lamp for the feet and a light for paths. Proverbs calls the commandment of law a lamp and a light and associates correction and discipline with life’s way. The lamp-and-light image is shared, with feet and paths explicit in the psalm and life’s way in Proverbs.
  2 Peter 1:19 — The word compared with a shining lamp: Psalm 118 describes the word as a lamp for feet and light for paths. Second Peter compares attention to the prophetic word with attention to a lamp shining in a dark place until day dawns. The shared word-and-lamp image has different settings and does not turn the psalm’s path into the letter’s daybreak expectation.

Psalms 118:106
I have sworn and established it: to keep Thy righteous judgments.
  Nehemiah 10:30 — An oath to keep divine judgments: The speaker says he swore and established an undertaking to keep God’s righteous judgments. The account in Nehemiah records entry into a curse and oath to walk in God’s law and keep His commandments and judgments. The oath-and-observance connection is explicit, while a personal resolution and communal undertaking remain distinct.

Psalms 118:107
I was humbled to an extreme; Lord, give me life according to Thy word.
  Psalms 118:50 — A plea for life beside remembered life in humiliation (same-chapter): Verse 107 describes extreme humiliation and asks for life according to God’s word. Verse 50 recalls comfort in humiliation because God’s saying gave life. The later petition repeats the earlier humiliation-and-life pattern, while completed comfort is not attributed to the current request.

Psalms 118:108
Please accept the freewill offerings of my mouth, Lord, and teach me Thy judgments.
  Hebrews 13:15 — Speech presented as an offering to God: Psalm 118 asks acceptance of the mouth’s freewill offerings. Hebrews urges offering through Jesus a sacrifice of praise, defined as fruit of lips confessing God’s name. Both present speech through an offering image; the freewill offerings are not made identical to the letter’s explicitly Christ-mediated sacrifice.

Psalms 118:109
My soul is in my hands continually, and I did not forget Thy law.
  Judges 12:3 — Life or soul in one’s own hand: Psalm 118 says the speaker’s soul is continually in his hands and says he did not forget the law. Jephthah reports putting his life in his hand when he crossed against Ammon without a deliverer. The distinctive hand-and-life expression is shared, with continual condition and a particular military action distinguished.

Psalms 118:110
Sinners set a trap for me, and I did not wander from Thy commandments.
  Psalms 139:6 — A trap laid by hostile people: Psalm 118 says sinners set a trap while the speaker did not wander from commandments. Psalm 139 describes the proud hiding a trap and setting cords and a stumbling block along the path. The concrete trap imagery connects the passages without supplying the target’s cords to Psalm 118:110 or identifying the hostile groups.

Psalms 118:111
I inherited Thy testimonies for ever, for they are my heart’s rejoicing.
  Deuteronomy 33:4 — Divine instruction as an inheritance: The speaker inherited God’s testimonies for ever and calls them the heart’s rejoicing. Deuteronomy calls the law commanded by Moses an inheritance for Jacob’s assemblies. Both name instruction as inheritance, while the personal joy and perpetual possession are explicit only in the psalm.

Psalms 118:112
I bent my heart to perform Thine ordinances for ever, for a recompense.
  Psalms 18:12 — Keeping instruction with recompense in view: Psalm 118 says the speaker bent his heart to perform ordinances for ever, for recompense. Psalm 18 says God’s servant keeps the judgments and that in keeping them there is great recompense. The shared keeping-and-recompense formulation preserves the psalm’s stated heart orientation and the target’s greatness of reward.

Psalms 118:113
Fifteen: Samch. I hated lawbreakers, and I loved Thy law.
  Psalms 118:163 — Hatred and love contrasted around the law (same-chapter): Verse 113 opposes hatred of lawbreakers to love of the law. Verse 163 opposes hatred and abhorrence of injustice to love of the law. The repeated contrast preserves the important difference between lawbreakers as persons and injustice as the object rejected.

Psalms 118:115
Turn away from me, evildoers, and I will search out my God’s commandments.
  Psalms 6:9 — A direct command that evildoers depart: Psalm 118 tells evildoers to turn away and declares that the speaker will search God’s commandments. Psalm 6 orders all workers of lawlessness to stand away because the Lord heard the weeping. The direct dismissal of wrongdoers is shared; the source joins it with a promised search, while the target grounds it in the hearing of weeping.

Psalms 118:116
Uphold me according to Thy saying, and I will live; and do not put me to shame in my expectation.
  Romans 5:5 — Expectation or hope not ending in shame: Psalm 118 asks to be upheld according to God’s saying and not be shamed in expectation. Romans states that hope does not put believers to shame because God’s love has been poured into their hearts through the Holy Spirit. The expectation-and-shame connection is retained without equating the petition’s grounds with the letter’s stated explanation.

Psalms 118:118
Thou didst despise all who depart from Thine ordinances, for their thought is unjust.
  Psalms 118:21 — Divine rejection of those departing instruction (same-chapter): Verse 118 says God despised those departing His ordinances because their thought is unjust. Verse 21 reports rebuke of the arrogant and calls those turning away from commandments accursed. The shared departure from instruction and adverse divine response are compared while despising, rebuke and curse retain their distinct wording.

Psalms 118:120
Nail down my flesh through fear of Thee, for I feared Thy judgments.
  Galatians 5:24 — Flesh nailed down or crucified in distinct spiritual settings: Psalm 118 asks God to nail down the speaker’s flesh through fear of God, adding that he feared God’s judgments. Galatians says those belonging to Christ Jesus crucified the flesh with its passions and desires. The connection is the concrete fastening or crucifying image applied to flesh, not an assertion that the psalm names a cross or describes the same completed act.

Psalms 118:122
Receive Thy servant for good; do not let the arrogant oppress me with false accusations.
  Psalms 118:134 — Protection from false accusation (same-chapter): Verse 122 asks God to receive His servant for good and prevent the arrogant from oppressing him with false accusations. Verse 134 asks redemption from people’s oppression by false accusation and promises to keep commandments. The same specific hostile action prompts different protective requests; the people are not thereby proved to be one identified group.

Psalms 118:123
My eyes failed with longing for Thy salvation and for Thy righteous saying.
  Psalms 118:82 — The repeated image of eyes failing in longing (same-chapter): Verse 123 says the eyes failed in longing for salvation and God’s righteous saying. Verse 82 similarly says the eyes failed longing for the saying, then asks when comfort will come. The repeated bodily image and awaited saying connect the petitions while preserving their different expressed hopes.

Psalms 118:126
It is time to act for the Lord; they scattered Thy law.
  Ezra 9:14 — Scattering divine law or commandments: Psalm 118 announces a time to act for the Lord and says that they scattered His law. Ezra’s prayer confesses that the community returned to scattering God’s commandments and intermarrying. The unusual scattering language for disobedience is shared; the poem does not identify its they with Ezra’s community or turn time to act for the Lord into a different supplied subject.

Psalms 118:127
Therefore I loved Thy commandments more than gold and topaz.
  Job 28:19 — Instruction or wisdom valued above gold and topaz: Psalm 118 says the speaker loved the commandments above gold and topaz. Job’s wisdom poem says Ethiopian topazion cannot equal wisdom and pure gold cannot be weighed with it. The gold-and-topaz comparison is particular, while commandments and wisdom and the target’s qualifiers remain distinct.

Psalms 118:128
Therefore I was set right toward all Thy commandments; I hated every unjust way.
  Psalms 118:104 — A repeated rejection of every unjust way (same-chapter): Verse 128 joins being set right toward all commandments with hatred of every unjust way. Verse 104 joins understanding through the commandments with the same hatred of every unjust way. The explicit repeated conclusion makes the link, without collapsing set right into understood.

Psalms 118:129
Seventeen: Phē. Thy testimonies are wonderful; therefore my soul searched them out.
  Psalms 118:18 — Wonders in instruction prompting inquiry (same-chapter): Verse 129 calls the testimonies wonderful and therefore says the soul searched them. Verse 18 asks for uncovered eyes to consider wonders from the law. The wonderful character of instruction is linked with searching or enabled consideration, while testimony and law retain their terms.

Psalms 118:130
The disclosure of Thy words will give light and understanding to the simple.
  Psalms 18:8-9 — Instruction giving light and understanding to the simple: Psalm 118 says the disclosure of God’s words will give light and understanding to the simple. Psalm 18 calls the testimony trustworthy and making infants wise, and the commandment shining and giving light to eyes. The combined light-and-understanding pattern is explicit, without equating simple people exactly with infants.

Psalms 118:133
Direct my steps according to Thy saying, and let no lawlessness rule over me.
  Romans 6:14 — A plea or assurance against sin’s dominion: Psalm 118 asks for directed steps according to God’s saying and that no lawlessness rule over the speaker. Romans says sin will not rule over the addressees because they are under grace rather than law. The dominion language is shared, but petition and assurance and their different stated grounds are preserved.
  Psalms 36:23 — The Lord’s direction of steps: Psalm 118 asks God to direct the steps according to His saying and prevent lawlessness from ruling. Psalm 36 states that a person’s steps are directed by the Lord. The common Lord-directed steps image does not supply the psalm’s explicit protection from lawlessness to the target.

Psalms 118:134
Redeem me from people’s oppression through false accusation, and I will keep Thy commandments.
  Psalms 118:122 — Two requests concerning false accusation (same-chapter): Verse 134 asks redemption from people’s oppression by false accusation and promises that the speaker will keep commandments. Verse 122 asks that the arrogant not oppress God’s servant with false accusations. The specific oppression-through-false-accusation wording recurs, distinguishing rescue from prevention and people from the arrogant.

Psalms 118:135
Shine Thy face upon Thy servant, and teach me Thine ordinances.
  Numbers 6:25 — The shining divine face in blessing and petition: Psalm 118 asks God to make His face shine upon His servant and teach ordinances. The priestly blessing in Numbers asks that the Lord make His face shine upon the recipient and have mercy. The same shining-face formulation connects the personal petition with the prescribed blessing, without equating their recipients or added requests.
  Psalms 30:17 — God’s face shining upon His servant: Both passages ask God to make His face shine upon His servant. Psalm 118 adds instruction in ordinances, whereas Psalm 30 adds salvation in mercy. The exact face-and-servant petition is preserved with those different accompanying requests.

Psalms 118:136
Streams of water ran down from my eyes, since they did not keep Thy law.
  Lamentations 3:48 — Water pouring from the eye in grief: Psalm 118 describes streams from the eyes because they did not keep God’s law. Lamentations says the eye will bring down outpourings of water at the shattering of the daughter of the people. Both use abundant water from the eye for grief, distinguishing disobedience from devastation and reported streams from the target’s future outpouring.

Psalms 118:138
Thou didst command Thy testimonies as righteousness and exceeding truth.
  Psalms 18:10 — Truth and righteousness attributed to divine judgments: Psalm 118 says God commanded His testimonies as righteousness and exceeding truth. Psalm 18 calls the Lord’s judgments true and justified together. The paired truth-and-righteousness description connects the instruction terms without converting testimonies into a quotation of the target.

Psalms 118:139
Zeal for Thy house melted me away, for my enemies forgot Thy words.
  Psalms 68:10 — Zeal for God’s house consuming or melting the speaker: Psalm 118 says zeal for God’s house melted the speaker because enemies forgot God’s words. Psalm 68 says zeal for God’s house devoured the speaker and that reproaches against God fell on him. The distinctive house-zeal and consuming effect are shared, with melting and devouring and the accompanying statements distinguished.
  John 2:17 — House-zeal language recalled around Jesus’ temple action: Psalm 118 uses zeal for God’s house with a melting effect on the speaker. John records the disciples remembering the written saying that zeal for God’s house will consume Jesus. The shared house-zeal formulation is compared without claiming John expressly quotes Psalm 118 rather than the closer wording of Psalm 68:10.

Psalms 118:140
Thy saying has been thoroughly refined by fire, and Thy servant loved it.
  Psalms 11:7 — Divine sayings refined by fire: Psalm 118 calls God’s saying thoroughly refined by fire and loved by the servant. Psalm 11 describes the Lord’s sayings as pure, like silver tried by fire and purified sevenfold. The refining image is shared; silver and sevenfold purification belong explicitly to Psalm 11.
  Proverbs 30:5 — God’s words refined by fire: Psalm 118 describes God’s saying as thoroughly fire-refined. Proverbs states that all God’s words have been refined by fire and adds His shielding of those who revere Him. The close fire-refining statement stands without supplying the target’s shielding clause to the source.

Psalms 118:141
I am younger and despised; I did not forget Thine ordinances.
  1 Timothy 4:12 — Youth, contempt and fidelity in conduct: Psalm 118 describes the speaker as younger and despised and says he did not forget the ordinances. First Timothy tells its recipient not to let anyone despise his youth and to be a pattern in word and conduct. The youth-and-contempt conjunction is specific, with reported contempt and an exhortation against it distinguished.

Psalms 118:142
Thy righteousness is righteousness for ever, and Thy law is truth.
  John 17:17 — Divine law or word expressly called truth: Psalm 118 says God’s righteousness lasts for ever and His law is truth. Jesus’ prayer in John asks sanctification in truth and declares the Father’s word truth. The direct truth predication is shared, while law, word, lasting righteousness and sanctification retain their different textual roles.

Psalms 118:143
Affliction and constraint found me; Thy commandments are my meditation.
  Psalms 118:92 — Law as meditation amid distress (same-chapter): Verse 143 sets affliction and constraint finding the speaker beside the commandments as his meditation. Verse 92 says that without the law as meditation he would have perished in humiliation. The recurrent conjunction of distress and sustaining meditation is compared, without attributing the latter counterfactual to verse 143 itself.

Psalms 118:147
I rose early at an untimely hour and cried out; I placed my hope in Thy words.
  Mark 1:35 — Rising unusually early for prayer: Psalm 118 reports rising early at an untimely hour and crying out, and says the speaker placed hope in God’s words. Mark says Jesus rose very early while it was still dark, went to a deserted place and prayed. Unusually early rising with prayer is shared; the psalm does not specify Jesus, a deserted place or the target’s darkness.

Psalms 118:148
My eyes were awake before dawn to meditate on Thy sayings.
  Psalms 62:7 — Dawn hours and meditation: Psalm 118 says the eyes were awake before dawn to meditate on God’s sayings. Psalm 62 conditionally connects remembering God upon the bed with meditation on Him in the dawn hours. The timing-and-meditation conjunction is specific, while before dawn and during dawn hours, and sayings and God Himself, remain distinct.

Psalms 118:150
Those who pursue me with lawlessness drew near, but they were far from Thy law.
  Psalms 118:151 — Near pursuers, distant law and the near Lord (same-chapter): Verse 150 says lawless pursuers drew near although they were far from God’s law. Verse 151 immediately declares the Lord near and His commandments truth. The consecutive near-and-far contrast opposes threatening proximity to divine proximity without saying the Lord or the pursuers have the same relation to the law.

Psalms 118:151
Thou art near, Lord, and all Thy commandments are truth.
  Psalms 118:150 — The near Lord contrasted with near lawless pursuers (same-chapter): Verse 151 declares God near and all His commandments truth. The preceding verse describes pursuers drawing near but being far from His law. These adjacent statements differentiate physical or threatening nearness from fidelity to instruction and the asserted nearness of God.

Psalms 118:152
From the beginning I knew from Thy testimonies that Thou didst establish them for ever.
  Psalms 110:7-8 — Testimonies or commandments established for ever: Psalm 118 says from the beginning the speaker knew from God’s testimonies that He established them for ever. Psalm 110 calls the commandments faithful, established for an age of an age and made in truth and uprightness. The shared establishment and permanence claim preserves the psalm’s personal from-the-beginning knowledge.

Psalms 118:153
Twenty: Rēs. See my humiliation and rescue me, for I did not forget Thy law.
  Psalms 24:18 — God asked to see the speaker’s humiliation: Psalm 118 asks God to see humiliation and rescue, adding that the speaker did not forget the law. Psalm 24 similarly asks God to see humiliation and toil but asks forgiveness of all sins. The repeated see-my-humiliation petition is linked while rescue and forgiveness and their surrounding professions remain distinct.

Psalms 118:154
Judge my case and redeem me; give me life because of Thy word.
  Psalms 42:1 — Divine adjudication of the speaker’s case and rescue: Psalm 118 asks God to judge the speaker’s case and redeem him, followed by a request for life because of the word. Psalm 42 asks God to judge the speaker’s case against an unholy nation and rescue him from an unjust, deceitful person. The judicial case-and-rescue appeal is shared without supplying the target’s specified opponents to the source.

Psalms 118:155
Salvation is far from sinners, for they did not seek Thine ordinances.
  Proverbs 15:29 — Distance of salvation or God from the impious: Psalm 118 says salvation is far from sinners because they did not seek the ordinances. Proverbs says God is far distant from the impious but hears righteous people’s prayers. Both use distance for the impious person’s relation to divine help, with salvation and God as the respective grammatical subjects.

Psalms 118:158
I saw those who break faith and melted away, for they did not keep Thy sayings.
  Psalms 118:139 — Melting in response to neglected divine words (same-chapter): Verse 158 says the speaker saw people breaking faith and melted because they did not keep God’s sayings. Verse 139 says zeal for God’s house melted him because enemies forgot the words. The repeated melting and others’ neglect of divine speech link the passages, with seeing breach of faith and zeal for the house distinguished.

Psalms 118:160
The beginning of Thy words is truth, and all Thy righteous judgments endure for ever.
  John 17:17 — Truth predicated of divine speech: Psalm 118 says the beginning of God’s words is truth and all His righteous judgments last for ever. Jesus’ prayer declares the Father’s word truth. The comparison preserves beginning rather than substituting sum or totality, and does not make John’s statement include the psalm’s everlasting-judgments clause.

Psalms 118:161
Twenty-one: Sen. Rulers pursued me without cause, and my heart was afraid of Thy words.
  1 Samuel 24:12-15 — Royal pursuit answered with a claim of innocence: Psalm 118 says rulers pursued the speaker without cause while his heart feared God’s words. David tells Saul he has done no evil or sin against him and asks whom the king is pursuing. The comparison concerns royal pursuit asserted to be unjust; it does not identify this anonymous psalm’s speaker as David or its rulers as Saul.

Psalms 118:162
I will rejoice in Thy sayings like one who finds much spoil.
  Isaiah 9:2 — Joy compared with obtaining or dividing spoil: Psalm 118 anticipates rejoicing over God’s sayings like one finding much spoil. Isaiah compares the people’s future rejoicing with harvest rejoicing and those dividing spoils. The distinctive spoil-and-joy comparison is shared, while finding and dividing and the different occasions remain distinct.

Psalms 118:163
I hated and abhorred injustice, but I loved Thy law.
  Psalms 118:113 — Law loved beside hatred of differing objects (same-chapter): Verse 163 says the speaker hated and abhorred injustice but loved the law. Verse 113 says he hated lawbreakers but loved the law. The repeated contrast distinguishes hatred of injustice from hatred of lawbreakers rather than silently equating an act with people.

Psalms 118:164
Seven times a day I praised Thee for Thy righteous judgments.
  Psalms 118:62 — Timed praise because of righteous judgments (same-chapter): Verse 164 reports praise seven times in the day because of righteous judgments. Verse 62 speaks of rising at midnight to thank God because of righteous judgments. The recurring reason and specified times make the link, without adding the two statements into an unstated numerical schedule.

Psalms 118:165
Great peace belongs to those who love Thy law, and there is no stumbling block for them.
  Isaiah 48:18 — Peace associated with regard for commandments: Psalm 118 declares great peace for those loving God’s law. Isaiah says that if the addressee had listened to God’s commands, peace would have become like a river. The law-and-peace connection is shared while the psalm’s declaration and Isaiah’s counterfactual are kept distinct.
  Psalms 36:31 — Divine law and the absence of stumbling: Psalm 118 says those loving God’s law have great peace and no stumbling block. Psalm 36 places God’s law in the righteous person’s heart and says his steps will not be tripped up. Love of law and law in the heart are related through the explicit absence-of-stumbling image, not treated as identical statements.

Psalms 118:168
I kept Thy commandments and Thy testimonies, for all my ways are before Thee, Lord.
  Proverbs 5:21 — All human ways before God: Psalm 118 says the speaker kept commandments and testimonies because all his ways are before God. Proverbs says a man’s ways are before God’s eyes and He watches all his tracks. The shared all-seeing relation to human ways is particular; the psalm’s obedience and Proverbs’ warning context remain distinct.

Psalms 118:171
May my lips pour forth a hymn when Thou teachest me Thine ordinances.
  Psalms 118:7 — Praise arising when instruction is learned (same-chapter): Verse 171 asks that lips pour out a hymn when God teaches ordinances. Verse 7 promises upright-hearted thanksgiving when the speaker learns righteous judgments. The matching when-taught-or-learned structure frames praise as a response to instruction, without asserting the two acts of praise already occurred.

Psalms 118:172
May my tongue utter Thy saying, for all Thy commandments are righteousness.
  Psalms 36:30 — Tongue speaking judgment or divine saying: Psalm 118 asks that the tongue utter God’s saying and declares all His commandments righteousness. Psalm 36 says the righteous person’s mouth will meditate on wisdom and his tongue will speak judgment. The explicit tongue-and-just-speech pattern is shared while a divine saying and the righteous person’s judgment are distinguished.

Psalms 118:174
I longed for Thy salvation, Lord, and Thy law is my meditation.
  Psalms 118:81 — Repeated longing for salvation (same-chapter): Verse 174 declares longing for God’s salvation and identifies the law as meditation. Verse 81 describes the soul failing while longing for salvation and hoping in the word. The repeated longing is linked with two different responses to instruction, and the failing-soul condition is confined to verse 81.

Psalms 118:175
My soul will live and praise Thee, and Thy judgments will help me.
  Psalms 113:25-26 — Living and praising God: Psalm 118 anticipates the soul living and praising God, and God’s judgments helping the speaker. Psalm 113 contrasts the dead and those descending to Hades, who will not praise, with the living who will bless the Lord. The explicit life-and-praise connection is shared without making Psalm 118 itself state the target’s description of the dead.

Psalms 118:176
I wandered like a lost sheep; seek Thy servant, for I did not forget Thy commandments.
  Ezekiel 34:16 — The wandering or lost sheep sought by God: The psalm ends with the speaker as a lost sheep asking God to seek His servant, while professing not to forget commandments. Ezekiel has God promise to seek the lost and bring back the wandering among His sheep. The shared seeking-and-lost-sheep pattern does not erase the psalm’s statement that the speaker did not forget commandments.
  Luke 15:4-7 — Seeking and finding a lost sheep: Psalm 118 asks God to seek the speaker who wandered like a lost sheep. Jesus’ parable describes going after a lost sheep until finding it and interprets the joy in relation to a repentant sinner. The common search image is retained without importing the whole parable’s repentance framework into the psalm’s profession of remembered commandments.
  Isaiah 53:6 — Human wandering described through sheep: Psalm 118 describes the speaker as wandering like a lost sheep. Isaiah describes all of us as sheep gone astray, each in his own way, before speaking of the Lord delivering the suffering figure over to our sins. The shared sheep-and-wandering image is limited to that comparison; the psalm’s servant is not identified with Isaiah’s suffering figure.
  1 Peter 2:25 — Wandering sheep and return to a shepherd: Psalm 118 asks God to seek the wandering speaker pictured as a lost sheep. First Peter describes the addressees as formerly wandering sheep who have now returned to the Shepherd and Overseer of their souls. The psalm requests seeking, whereas the letter reports a completed return; neither is turned into the other’s chronology.

Psalms 119:1
A song of the ascents. To the Lord, when I was afflicted, I cried, and He heard me.
  Jonah 2:3 — Affliction, crying to the Lord and being heard: Both describe affliction followed by crying to the Lord and His hearing the speaker. Jonah places these words in prayer from the sea creature's belly; the psalm does not identify its affliction with that episode.

Psalms 119:2
Lord, rescue my soul from unjust lips and from a deceitful tongue.
  Psalms 11:3-4 — Rescue from deceitful speech: The psalm asks rescue from unjust lips and a deceitful tongue. Psalm 11 describes deceitful lips and asks the Lord to destroy them along with a boastful tongue. The paired organs of harmful speech make the connection particular.

Psalms 119:5
Alas for me, for my sojourn has been prolonged; I have encamped among the tents of Kedar.
  Song of Songs 1:5 — The tents of Kedar: The psalmist reports encamping among Kedar's tents during a prolonged sojourn. The Song's speaker compares her black and beautiful appearance with Kedar's tents and Solomon's tent coverings. The named tents connect residence and simile without identifying their speakers or occasions.

Psalms 119:7
With those who hate peace I was peaceable; whenever I spoke to them, they fought me without cause.
  Romans 12:18-21 — Peaceful conduct amid hostility: The speaker was peaceable with people who hated peace and fought him without cause. Romans instructs its readers to live peaceably if possible and as far as it depends on them and to answer an enemy's need with good. The comparison joins peaceful conduct with continued hostility, preserving report and instruction as different forms.

Psalms 120:1
A song of the ascents. I lifted my eyes to the mountains. From where will my help come?
  Jeremiah 3:23 — Help, mountains and the Lord: The speaker lifts his eyes to mountains and asks where help will come from; the next unit answers that help is from the Lord. Jeremiah calls the hills and mountains' power false and locates Israel's salvation in the Lord. The relation of mountains to divine help is compared without accusing the psalmist of worshiping mountains.

Psalms 120:2
My help is from the Lord, who made heaven and earth.
  Psalms 123:8 — Help from the maker of heaven and earth: Both identify the source of help with the Lord who made heaven and earth. Psalm 120 says help is from the Lord, while Psalm 123 locates help in His name; the distinctive creator-and-help formula is shared.

Psalms 120:3
Do not give your foot over to shaking, and may your Keeper not slumber.
  Psalms 65:9 — Keeping a foot from shaking: The source says not to give the foot over to shaking and wishes that the Keeper not slumber. Psalm 65 credits God with not giving the speaker's feet over to shaking. The foot-and-shaking expression corresponds; the sleep clause is additional to the source.

Psalms 120:4
Behold, the One guarding Israel will neither slumber nor sleep.
  1 Kings 18:27 — The contrast with a sleeping deity: The psalm denies both slumber and sleep of Israel's guardian. Elijah's mockery suggests that Baal might be asleep. The particular contrast concerns a deity's sleeping, with Elijah's words retained as mockery rather than a factual account of Baal.

Psalms 120:5
The Lord will guard you; the Lord is your shelter at your right hand.
  Psalms 15:8 — The Lord at the right hand: The Lord will guard the addressee and is shelter at the right hand. Psalm 15 places the Lord at the speaker's right hand so that he may not be shaken. The divine position and protective result correspond, without adding shelter wording to Psalm 15.

Psalms 120:6
By day the sun will not scorch you, nor the moon by night.
  Isaiah 49:10 — Protection from the sun's heat: The psalm promises that the sun will not scorch by day, alongside the moon by night. Isaiah promises that scorching heat and the sun will not strike those led by the merciful One. Protection from solar heat is shared; the moon clause remains particular to the psalm.

Psalms 120:7
The Lord will guard you from all evil; He will guard your soul.
  John 17:15 — Guarding against evil: The Lord will guard the soul from all evil in the psalm. Jesus asks the Father to keep His disciples from the evil one in John. Both concern divine guarding against evil, while all evil and the evil one are retained as different expressions and scopes.

Psalms 120:8
The Lord will guard your coming in and your going out, from now and for ever.
  Deuteronomy 28:6 — Coming in and going out under divine care: The psalm places coming in and going out under the Lord's perpetual guarding. Deuteronomy blesses the addressee when coming in and going out within its conditional covenant blessing. The paired movements correspond, without importing the psalm's duration into the target.

Psalms 121:1
A song of the ascents. I rejoiced over those who said to me, “We will go to the Lord’s house.”
  Isaiah 2:3 — Going to the Lord's house: The speaker recounts rejoicing over those who said they would go to the Lord's house. Isaiah portrays many nations inviting one another to go up to the Lord's mountain and the house of Jacob's God. Both concern a journey to God's house, with the reported resolve and the invitation distinguished.

Psalms 121:2
Our feet were standing in your courts, Jerusalem.
  Psalms 133:1 — Standing in the courts of God's house: The source locates the speakers' standing feet in Jerusalem's courts. Psalm 133 addresses those standing in the Lord's house and its courts. With the Lord's house already named in Psalm 121:1, both describe worshipers standing in the sacred courts.

Psalms 121:4
for there the tribes went up, the tribes of the Lord—a testimony for Israel— to give thanks to the Lord’s name.
  Deuteronomy 16:16 — Going up to the appointed place of worship: The psalm recounts the Lord's tribes going up to give thanks to His name. Deuteronomy requires the males to appear before the Lord at the chosen place on three annual feasts. The ascent for worship is compared with the prescribed gathering, without identifying the psalm's visit with one particular feast.

Psalms 121:5
For there thrones sat for judgment, thrones over David’s house.
  Jeremiah 21:11-12 — David's house and judgment: The psalm places judgment thrones over David's house in Jerusalem. Jeremiah addresses the house of Judah's king and David's house, commanding morning judgment and rescue of the wronged. David's house and the duty of judgment connect the passages.

Psalms 121:6
Ask, then, for the things that bring Jerusalem peace, and prosperity for those who love you.
  Luke 19:41-44 — The things belonging to Jerusalem's peace: The source asks for the things that bring Jerusalem peace. Jesus weeps over the city and speaks of the things that lead to peace that it failed to know. Jerusalem and its peace are particular to both, with the psalm's appeal and Jesus' lament kept distinct.

Psalms 121:7
May there be peace within your strength, and prosperity within your fortified towers.
  Isaiah 60:17-18 — Peace within the city's defenses: The psalm wishes peace within Jerusalem's strength and prosperity within its fortified towers. Isaiah promises rulers in peace, the end of destruction within the borders, and walls called Salvation. Both associate the city's defenses with peace; Isaiah's walls and the psalm's towers are not treated as identical wording.

Psalms 122:1
A song of the ascents. To Thee I lifted my eyes, Thou who dwellest in heaven.
  John 17:1 — Lifting eyes toward heaven in prayer: The psalmist lifts his eyes to the One dwelling in heaven. John describes Jesus lifting His eyes to heaven and addressing the Father. The bodily orientation of prayer is shared without identifying the prayers or their speakers.

Psalms 122:3
Have mercy on us, Lord, have mercy on us, for we have been filled with contempt to excess,
  Nehemiah 3:36 — A prayer amid contempt: The repeated request for mercy follows being filled with contempt. Nehemiah's prayer asks God to hear because the speakers have become objects of mockery. Both address God in response to communal contempt, without dating the psalm to the wall-building episode.

Psalms 123:1
A song of the ascents. Had the Lord not been among us— let Israel now say—
  Psalms 128:1 — The call for Israel to speak: Both poems interrupt their opening recollection with the summons for Israel now to speak. Psalm 123 introduces a condition about the Lord's presence, whereas Psalm 128 introduces repeated hostility from youth. The shared congregational cue connects their forms.

Psalms 123:2
had the Lord not been among us when people rose against us,
  Romans 8:31 — God's presence and human opposition: The psalm considers what would have happened without the Lord among the speakers when people rose against them. Romans asks, if God is for us, who is against us. Both set divine support against human opposition, preserving the conditional retrospect and rhetorical question.

Psalms 123:3
then they would have swallowed us alive when their fury was kindled against us.
  Proverbs 1:12 — Swallowing a living victim: The psalm describes hostile people conditionally swallowing the speakers alive. Proverbs quotes a murderous invitation to swallow a righteous man alive like Hades. The unusual image of swallowing a living victim connects the texts; the target's singular victim and Hades comparison remain distinct.

Psalms 123:4
Then the water drowned us; our soul passed through a torrent.
  Psalms 68:2-3 — The soul overwhelmed by waters: Within the opening conditional sequence, the source says water drowned the speakers and their soul passed through a torrent. Psalm 68 speaks of waters reaching the soul, entry into the sea's depths and a tempest drowning the speaker. The water-and-soul peril image corresponds without equating the afflictions.

Psalms 123:5
Then our soul passed through the irresistible water.
  Isaiah 43:2 — Passing through dangerous waters: The soul's passage through irresistible water belongs to the psalm's condition about the Lord's presence. Isaiah joins crossing water with God's presence and a promise that rivers will not flood over Israel. Divine presence and passage through threatening waters connect the contrasting formulations.

Psalms 123:6
Blessed be the Lord, who did not give us as prey to their teeth.
  Job 29:17 — The image of predatory teeth: The psalm blesses the Lord for not giving the speakers as prey to enemies' teeth. Job recounts crushing unjust people's molars and tearing plunder from between their teeth. The shared predatory-teeth image links the Lord's withholding of prey and Job's removal of plunder, with their objects kept distinct.

Psalms 123:7
Our soul was rescued like a little bird from the trap of hunters; the trap was shattered, and we were rescued.
  Proverbs 6:5 — A bird escaping a hunter's trap: The rescued soul is compared with a little bird escaping a shattered hunters' trap. Proverbs urges escape from a guarantor's danger like a bird from a trap. The bird-and-trap image is shared, while the psalm credits rescue and Proverbs gives an instruction.

Psalms 123:8
Our help is in the Lord’s name, who made heaven and earth.
  Psalms 120:2 — Help from the creator of heaven and earth: The source locates help in the name of the Lord who made heaven and earth. Psalm 120 likewise names the creator of heaven and earth as the source of help. The repeated formula connects the ascent songs while preserving the source's emphasis on the name.

Psalms 124:1
A song of the ascents. Those trusting in the Lord are like Mount Zion; the one dwelling in Jerusalem will never be shaken.
  Psalms 45:5-6 — The city and freedom from shaking: Those trusting in the Lord are compared with Mount Zion, and the Jerusalem dweller will never be shaken. Psalm 45 describes God's city as unshaken because God is in her midst. Both connect the city with freedom from shaking, but the source speaks of its dweller and the target of the city itself; the source also compares trusting people with Mount Zion.

Psalms 124:2
Mountains are around her, and the Lord is around His people, from now and for ever.
  Zechariah 2:9 — The Lord surrounding His people: Mountains around Jerusalem illustrate the Lord's surrounding His people. Zechariah promises that the Lord will be a wall of fire around Jerusalem and glory within her. Both use a surrounding defense to depict the Lord's protection, with mountains and a fiery wall distinguished.

Psalms 124:3
For He will not leave the rod of sinners upon the lot of the righteous, so that the righteous may not stretch out their hands in lawlessness.
  Isaiah 14:5 — God ending sinners' domination: The Lord will not leave sinners' rod upon the righteous person's lot. Isaiah says God shattered the sinners' and rulers' yoke. Both depict God ending sinful domination represented by an instrument of oppression; rod and yoke remain different images.

Psalms 124:4
Do good, Lord, to the good and to those upright in heart.
  Psalms 72:1 — God's goodness to the upright in heart: The source asks the Lord to do good to the good and upright in heart. Psalm 72 declares God's goodness to Israel and to those upright in heart. Divine goodness and the same description of its recipients connect petition and declaration.

Psalms 124:5
But those turning aside into tangled ways— the Lord will lead them away with workers of lawlessness. Peace upon Israel.
  Galatians 6:16 — Peace upon Israel: The source ends its judgment concerning tangled ways with peace upon Israel. Galatians pronounces peace and mercy upon those who will keep in step with its rule and upon the Israel of God. The explicit peace blessing connects the passages without equating their preceding judgments or defining their groups beyond the wording.

Psalms 125:1
A song of the ascents. When the Lord turned back Zion’s captivity, we became like those comforted.
  Jeremiah 37:18 — The turning of captivity: The psalm reports the Lord turning back Zion's captivity and the speakers becoming like those comforted. Jeremiah promises the return of Jacob's deportees, mercy on his captivity and rebuilding a city. Restoration from captivity connects report and promise without claiming one dated fulfillment.

Psalms 125:2
Then our mouth was filled with joy and our tongue with exultation. Then they will say among the nations, “The Lord has done great things with them.”
  Job 8:21 — A mouth filled with joy: The psalm reports a mouth filled with joy and a tongue with exultation. Bildad forecasts God filling the mouth of true people with laughter and their lips with thanksgiving. The common image of a filled mouth is compared without endorsing Bildad's explanation of Job's suffering.

Psalms 125:3
The Lord has done great things with us; we became glad.
  Luke 1:49 — The Lord doing great things: The source says the Lord has done great things with the speakers and they became glad. Mary says the Mighty One has done great things for her. The declaration of great divine acts is shared, with the communal and individual recipients distinguished.

Psalms 125:4
Turn back our captivity, Lord, like torrents in the south.
  Psalms 125:1 — Same-chapter petition after reported restoration: This same-chapter comparison places the petition to turn captivity beside the earlier report that the Lord turned Zion's captivity. The move from remembered restoration to a fresh request is explicit; the southern-torrent image is not supplied by the earlier unit.

Psalms 125:5
Those sowing in tears will reap with exultation.
  Galatians 6:8-9 — Sowing, perseverance and future reaping: The psalm promises joyful reaping for those sowing in tears. Galatians uses sowing and future reaping to encourage perseverance in doing good. The harvest image supports the comparison, while tears and exultation are not equated with Galatians' contrast between flesh and Spirit.

Psalms 125:6
Going, they went and wept, carrying their seeds; but coming, they will arrive with exultation, carrying their sheaves.
  Psalms 125:5 — Same-chapter expansion of the sowing and reaping image: This same-chapter expansion gives the sowing-and-reaping saying a fuller scene: departure with tears and seeds, then expected arrival with exultation and sheaves. The neighboring unit states the pattern more briefly. Past going and weeping are preserved alongside future arrival.

Psalms 126:1
A song of the ascents. For Solomon. Unless the Lord builds a house, those building it have labored in vain. Unless the Lord guards a city, the watchman has kept awake in vain.
  1 Chronicles 28:20 — Building with the Lord's aid: The heading associates the psalm with Solomon, and its opening makes successful building depend on the Lord. David tells Solomon that God is with him until he completes the ministry in the Lord's house. Solomon, building and divine aid connect the passages without restricting the psalm's house and city to the temple project.

Psalms 126:2
It is vain for you to rise early, to get up after sitting down, you who eat bread of pain, when He gives sleep to His beloved ones.
  Ecclesiastes 2:22-23 — Painful labor and nighttime rest: The psalm describes vain exertion and bread of pain, then the sleep God gives His beloved ones. Ecclesiastes describes painful toil and a heart that does not sleep even at night. Labor, pain and lost or granted rest form the particular comparison.

Psalms 126:3
Behold, sons are the Lord’s inheritance, the reward of the fruit of the womb.
  Genesis 33:5 — Children associated with God's favor: The source calls sons the Lord's inheritance and the reward of the fruit of the womb. Jacob identifies his children as those with whom God has shown mercy to His servant. Both associate children with divine benefit, while the source's inheritance and reward remain its own terms.

Psalms 126:4
Like arrows in a mighty man’s hand, so are the sons of those who have been cast out.
  Isaiah 49:2 — People compared with arrows: Sons of those who have been cast out are compared with arrows in a mighty man's hand. Isaiah's speaker describes being made a chosen arrow sheltered in God's quiver. People pictured as arrows connect the texts; neither the people nor the possessors of the arrows are equated.

Psalms 127:1
A song of the ascents. Happy are all who fear the Lord, who walk in His ways.
  Deuteronomy 10:12 — Fearing the Lord and walking in His ways: The beatitude joins fear of the Lord with walking in His ways. Deuteronomy likewise joins fearing the Lord and walking in all His ways among what He requires of Israel. The paired descriptions connect blessing and covenant instruction.

Psalms 127:2
You will eat the labors of your wrists; happy are you, and it will be well with you.
  Isaiah 65:21-22 — Enjoying the fruit of one's own labor: The source promises eating the labors of one's wrists and being well. Isaiah describes people planting vineyards and eating their own produce, without others taking the benefit of their work. Enjoyment of one's own labor is the particular connection.

Psalms 127:3
Your wife is like a flourishing vine in the sides of your house; your sons are like newly planted olive trees around your table.
  Ezekiel 19:10 — A woman compared with a fruitful vine: The wife is compared with a flourishing vine at the house. Ezekiel's lament over Israel's ruler compares the addressed person's mother with a vine planted by water. The woman-and-vine image is shared, while wife, mother and the lament's subsequent ruin remain distinct.
  Jeremiah 11:16 — People pictured as olive trees: The sons are compared with newly planted olive trees around the table. Jeremiah describes the Lord naming the people a beautiful olive tree, then recounts its damaged branches. The comparison is limited to people represented as olive trees, with the family blessing and judgment scene distinguished.

Psalms 127:4
Behold, thus will the person be blessed who fears the Lord.
  Psalms 111:1 — The blessed person who fears the Lord: Both describe the blessed person through fear of the Lord. Psalm 127 summarizes the household blessing in its surrounding units, while Psalm 111 begins a beatitude and adds delight in the commandments. The explicit fear-and-blessing formula is shared.

Psalms 127:5
May the Lord bless you from Zion, and may you see Jerusalem’s good things all the days of your life.
  Psalms 133:3 — A blessing from Zion: The source wishes for the Lord's blessing from Zion, while Psalm 133 declares that He will bless from Zion. Psalm 127 additionally wishes that the person see Jerusalem's good things through life; Psalm 133 additionally identifies the Lord as maker of heaven and earth. The shared blessing formula remains precise.

Psalms 127:6
And may you see sons of your sons. Peace upon Israel.
  Job 42:16 — Seeing children's children: The source wishes that the addressee see sons of his sons. Job's conclusion reports that he saw his sons and his sons' sons, a fourth generation. The experience of seeing descendants connects wish and narrative; Job's stated lifespan is not transferred to the psalm.

Psalms 128:1
A song of the ascents. Many times they fought against me from my youth— let Israel now say—
  Psalms 123:1 — The summons for Israel to speak: Both openings summon Israel now to speak within a recollection of danger. Psalm 128 recalls repeated fighting from youth, while Psalm 123 asks what would have happened without the Lord's presence. The particular congregational summons connects the songs.

Psalms 128:2
many times they fought against me from my youth, and indeed they did not prevail over me.
  Jeremiah 1:19 — Fighting without prevailing: The source reports repeated fighting in which the opponents did not prevail. God tells Jeremiah that people will fight him but will certainly not prevail, because He is with him to rescue him. The paired fighting-and-not-prevailing language connects communal report and prophetic assurance.

Psalms 128:3
On my back sinners worked; they prolonged their lawlessness.
  Isaiah 50:6 — Hostile acts against the speaker's back: The source places sinners' work on the speaker's back and describes prolonged lawlessness. Isaiah's speaker gives his back to scourges. The location of hostile action on the back is shared; plowing or furrows are not imported into the supplied psalm.

Psalms 128:5
May all who hate Zion be put to shame and turn backward.
  Psalms 34:4 — Shame and turning back for enemies: Both wish shame and a backward turning upon adversaries. Psalm 128 identifies them as those hating Zion; Psalm 34 identifies them as those seeking the speaker's soul and devising evils. The paired outcomes connect distinct descriptions of enemies.

Psalms 128:6
May they become like grass on roofs, which dried up before it was pulled out,
  2 Kings 19:26 — Rooftop grass as an image of weakness: The source wishes Zion's enemies to become rooftop grass that dried up before it was pulled out. The oracle concerning Sennacherib compares weakened city inhabitants with rooftop grass among other images. The unusual rooftop-grass comparison is shared, with different subjects and the psalm's drying clause preserved.

Psalms 128:8
And passersby have not said, “The Lord’s blessing be upon you; we have blessed you in the Lord’s name.”
  Ruth 2:4 — A contrasting blessing at the harvest: The source says passersby have not spoken the divine blessing, following the barren grass image. Ruth narrates Boaz greeting reapers with the Lord's presence and their reply asking the Lord to bless him. The harvest setting and divine blessing create a contrast between an absent greeting and an exchanged one.

Psalms 129:1
A song of the ascents. From the depths I have cried unto Thee, Lord.
  Jonah 2:3-4 — Crying from the depths: The psalm cries to the Lord from the depths. Jonah's prayer combines crying from the belly of Hades with being cast into the sea's depths. The cry from a depth of peril connects the prayers without identifying their occasions.

Psalms 129:2
Lord, hear my voice; let Thine ears be attentive to the voice of my petition.
  2 Chronicles 6:40 — Ears attentive to prayer: The source asks that the Lord's ears attend to the voice of petition. Solomon asks that God's ears be attentive to the supplication of this place. The attentive-ear prayer formula is shared; the temple location belongs to Solomon's prayer.

Psalms 129:3
If Thou dost closely watch lawless deeds, Lord, Lord, who will withstand?
  Ezra 9:15 — Who can stand with lawless deeds: The source asks who could withstand if the Lord closely watched lawless deeds. Ezra's confession says there is no standing before God because of the people's offenses. Lawless conduct and inability to stand before God connect question and confession.

Psalms 129:4
For with Thee is atonement.
  Psalms 64:4 — Atonement for lawless deeds: The source says atonement is with the Lord. Psalm 64 says God will make atonement for the speakers' impieties after words of lawless deeds overpowered them. The shared atonement claim connects both passages' response to lawlessness.

Psalms 129:5
Because of Thy law I waited for Thee, Lord; my soul waited for Thy word.
