  Psalms 118:81 — The soul waiting for God's word: The source speaks of waiting for the Lord and His word. Psalm 118 describes the soul failing with longing for salvation and hope placed in God's word. Waiting and hoping are distinguished, while the soul's dependence on the divine word is particular to both.

Psalms 129:6
My soul hoped in the Lord from the morning watch until night; from the morning watch let Israel hope in the Lord.
  Psalms 130:3 — Israel exhorted to hope in the Lord: Both expressly call Israel to hope in the Lord. Psalm 129 frames the call with the soul's hope from the morning watch until night; Psalm 130 extends the communal exhortation from now and for ever. The temporal forms are preserved.

Psalms 129:7
For with the Lord is mercy, and with Him is abundant redemption,
  Ephesians 1:7 — Redemption and abundant divine favor: The source joins the Lord's mercy with abundant redemption. Ephesians describes redemption through Christ's blood according to the riches of His grace. Redemptive provision and its abundance connect the passages, while blood and forgiveness are explicit features of Ephesians.

Psalms 129:8
And He will redeem Israel from all its lawless deeds.
  Titus 2:14 — Redemption from all lawlessness: The source says He will redeem Israel from all its lawless deeds. Titus describes Christ giving Himself to ransom us from all lawlessness. The redemptive action and comprehensive lawlessness clause correspond, with Israel, us and Christ's self-giving kept as the respective texts state them.

Psalms 130:1
A song of the ascents. For David. Lord, my heart was not exalted, nor were my eyes raised high; I did not walk among great things nor among wonders beyond me.
  Romans 12:16 — Rejecting exalted thinking: The source denies an exalted heart, high-raised eyes and pursuing things beyond the speaker. Romans instructs readers not to set their minds on lofty things or become wise in their own eyes. Rejected self-exaltation connects the report and instruction.

Psalms 130:3
Let Israel hope in the Lord, from now and for ever.
  Psalms 129:6 — The call for Israel to hope in the Lord: The source calls Israel to hope in the Lord from now and for ever. Psalm 129 also calls Israel to hope in the Lord after describing the soul's hope from morning watch until night. The repeated communal exhortation is the connection, not an equation of their time expressions.

Psalms 131:1
A song of the ascents. Remember David, Lord, and all his gentleness,
  2 Chronicles 6:42 — Remembering David: The source asks the Lord to remember David and his gentleness. Solomon asks remembrance of David's mercies in Chronicles. The intercession naming David and asking remembrance is shared; gentleness and mercies remain different supplied terms.

Psalms 131:2
how he swore to the Lord, vowed to the God of Jacob:
  1 Chronicles 22:7 — David's resolve concerning God's house: The vow introduced here concerns finding the Lord a dwelling in verses 3-5. David tells Solomon that building a house for the Lord's name came upon his soul. David's resolve concerning God's dwelling connects the texts, without presenting Chronicles as a quotation of this vow.

Psalms 131:3
“I will not enter the dwelling of my house, nor climb onto my bed’s couch;
  2 Samuel 7:1-2 — David's dwelling and the ark's lodging: The source vows to withhold entry into David's own house and bed until a place is found for the Lord. The account in 2 Samuel contrasts David already dwelling in a cedar house with the ark remaining in a tent. Both juxtapose David's lodging and God's dwelling, with the differing circumstances preserved.

Psalms 131:4
I will not give sleep to my eyes, slumber to my eyelids, or rest to my temples,
  Proverbs 6:4 — Refusing sleep to eyes and eyelids: Both use the specific refusal of sleep to the eyes and slumber or dozing to the eyelids. The psalm makes it part of David's vow about God's dwelling; Proverbs gives the instruction amid a guarantor's need for escape. Their motives are not conflated.

Psalms 131:5
until I find a place for the Lord, a dwelling for the God of Jacob.”
  1 Chronicles 15:1 — Preparing a place for the ark: The vow seeks a place for the Lord, a dwelling for Jacob's God. Chronicles reports David preparing a place for God's ark and making a tent for it. In the psalm's ark context at verse 8, the preparation of a divine dwelling is a concrete comparison.

Psalms 131:7
We will enter His dwelling places; we will bow toward the place where His feet stood.
  Psalms 98:5 — Bowing at the place of God's feet: The speakers say they will bow toward the place where God's feet stood. Psalm 98 commands bowing at His footstool. Both locate worship through the image of God's feet, with place and footstool distinguished.

Psalms 131:8
Rise, Lord, to Thy rest, Thou and the ark of Thy holiness.
  2 Chronicles 6:41 — The Lord and His ark rising to rest: Both address the Lord with a call to rise to His rest together with His ark. The source calls it the ark of His holiness, while Solomon's prayer calls it the ark of His strength. The paired rising-and-rest formula is retained with that difference.

Psalms 131:9
Thy priests will clothe themselves in righteousness, and Thy holy ones will exult.
  2 Chronicles 6:41 — Priests clothed and holy ones rejoicing: The source says priests will clothe themselves in righteousness and holy ones will exult. Solomon asks priests to clothe themselves with salvation and sons to rejoice in good things. The paired priestly clothing and rejoicing clauses correspond, preserving the differing garments and groups.

Psalms 131:10
For the sake of David Thy slave, do not turn away the face of Thine anointed.
  2 Chronicles 6:42 — David and the face of the anointed: Both ask that God not turn away the face of His anointed and connect the appeal with David His servant. Psalm 131 leads with David's sake; Solomon's prayer follows the appeal with remembrance of David's mercies.

Psalms 131:11
The Lord swore truth to David, and He will certainly not annul it: “From the fruit of your belly I will set one on your throne.
  Acts 2:30 — The sworn Davidic succession: Peter describes David knowing that God swore an oath to seat one from the fruit of his loins on his throne. The psalm likewise joins the divine oath to David, bodily offspring and enthronement. Belly and loins remain the respective terms; Acts subsequently relates the promise to Christ's resurrection.

Psalms 131:12
If your sons keep My covenant and these testimonies of Mine that I will teach them, their sons too will sit for ever on your throne.”
  1 Kings 8:25 — The condition attached to David's descendants: The source conditions the descendants' enduring throne on keeping God's covenant and testimonies. Solomon's prayer repeats a promise of Davidic succession conditioned on the children guarding their ways and walking before God. The condition on the descendants is shared, with the formulations preserved.

Psalms 131:13
For the Lord chose Zion; He selected her as His dwelling:
  Psalms 77:68-69 — Zion chosen for God's dwelling: The source identifies Zion as the Lord's chosen dwelling. Psalm 77 recounts the choice of Judah and loved Mount Zion, followed by construction of His sanctuary. Divine choice of Zion and a sacred dwelling connect the passages.

Psalms 131:14
“This is My rest for age upon age; here I will dwell, for I selected her.
  Psalms 67:17 — God's selected dwelling for ever: The source declares the selected place God's rest and dwelling for ages. Psalm 67 asks the curdled mountains about the mountain in which God was pleased to dwell, then affirms that the Lord will dwell to the end. The dwelling and its duration correspond without resolving the target's mountain question into a geographic identity.

Psalms 131:15
I will surely bless her game; I will satisfy her poor with loaves.
  Psalms 21:27 — The poor eating their fill: God promises to satisfy Zion's poor with loaves. Psalm 21 says the needy will eat and be filled. Feeding the needy to satisfaction is shared; the source's separate promise to bless her game is preserved without interpreting it through the target.

Psalms 131:16
I will clothe her priests with salvation, and her holy ones will surely exult.
  Psalms 131:9 — Same-chapter priestly clothing and rejoicing: This same-chapter return to priestly clothing and the holy ones' exultation recalls verse 9. Here God promises to clothe the priests with salvation; verse 9 says they will clothe themselves in righteousness. The repeated pattern and changed garment terms are explicit.

Psalms 131:17
There I will make a horn spring up for David; I have prepared a lamp for My anointed.
  Luke 1:69 — A horn raised for David's house: The source promises a horn springing up for David. Zechariah praises God for raising a horn of salvation in David's house. The horn-and-David association is particular, while salvation and the house formulation are explicit in Luke.
  2 Chronicles 21:7 — God's promise of a lamp for David: The source says God prepared a lamp for His anointed within the promise for David. Chronicles recalls God's covenant with David and promise to give him and his sons a lamp always. The divine provision of a Davidic lamp connects the passages.

Psalms 131:18
I will clothe his enemies with shame, but upon him My holy sign will blossom.”
  Psalms 108:29 — Opponents clothed with shame: The source promises to clothe the anointed one's enemies with shame. Psalm 108 asks that slanderers wear humiliation and wrap themselves in shame like a cloak. The shame-as-clothing image is shared; the source's blossoming holy sign is not interpreted as a crown.

Psalms 132:1
A song of the ascents. For David. Behold, what is good or what is delightful except for brothers to dwell together?
  Acts 2:44-47 — The good of life together: The psalm celebrates brothers dwelling together as good and delightful. Acts depicts believers together, sharing possessions and meals with gladness. Shared life and its positive evaluation connect the passages without identifying the groups or prescribing the same household arrangement.

Psalms 132:2
Like ointment upon the head, descending upon the beard, Aaron’s beard, descending upon the border of his garment;
  Leviticus 8:12 — Oil poured upon Aaron's head: The image names ointment upon Aaron's head, descending upon his beard and his garment's border. Leviticus narrates Moses pouring anointing oil on Aaron's head and consecrating him. Aaron, oil and the head identify the connection; the ointment's descent upon the beard and garment's border is a detail of the psalm.

Psalms 132:3
Like Hermon’s dew descending upon Zion’s mountains; for there the Lord commanded the blessing and life for ever.
  Hosea 14:6 — Dew as an image of divine blessing: The source compares the blessing scene with Hermon's dew descending upon Zion's mountains, where the Lord commanded blessing and life. Hosea depicts the Lord as dew to Israel, followed by blossoming and rooting. Dew associated with life and blessing from God connects the images without equating their geography.

Psalms 133:1
A song of the ascents. Behold, bless the Lord, all slaves of the Lord, who stand in the Lord’s house, in the courts of the house of our God.
  Psalms 134:2 — Those standing in God's house and courts: Both address those standing in the Lord's house and in the courts of God's house. Psalm 133 includes the summons to bless and the designation of the Lord's slaves. The nearly repeated standing-and-courts description connects the neighboring psalms.

Psalms 133:2
By night lift your hands toward the holy places and bless the Lord.
  Psalms 62:5 — Raised hands and blessing the Lord: The source commands raised hands toward the holy places and blessing the Lord by night. Psalm 62 joins blessing God with lifting hands in His name. The paired acts correspond, while the night and sacred-direction clauses remain distinct.

Psalms 133:3
The Lord will bless you from Zion, who made heaven and earth.
  Psalms 127:5 — The Lord's blessing from Zion: Both locate the Lord's blessing from Zion. Psalm 133 names the creator of heaven and earth; Psalm 127 additionally wishes lifelong sight of Jerusalem's good things. The shared Zion blessing is the specific connection.

Psalms 134:1
Hallelujah! Praise the Lord’s name; praise the Lord, slaves,
  Psalms 112:1 — The Lord's servants praising His name: Both summon the Lord's servants to praise Him and praise His name. The two praise objects and the addressed servants are closely repeated, while the source begins with the name and then the servants.

Psalms 134:2
you who stand in the Lord’s house, in the courts of our God’s house.
  Psalms 133:1 — Standing in the courts of God's house: The source addresses those standing in the Lord's house and its courts. Psalm 133 includes the same standing-and-courts description within a summons to bless. The repeated worship location and posture connect the texts.

Psalms 134:3
Praise the Lord, for the Lord is good; make music to His name, for it is beautiful.
  Psalms 91:2 — Making music to the Lord's name: The source commands making music to the Lord's name after declaring the Lord good. Psalm 91 calls it good to give thanks to the Lord and make music to His name. The specific name-and-music phrase is shared, while the goodness clauses have different grammatical subjects.

Psalms 134:4
For the Lord chose Jacob for Himself, Israel as His special possession.
  Deuteronomy 7:6 — Israel chosen as a special possession: The source says the Lord chose Jacob and Israel as His special possession. Deuteronomy describes God's choosing Israel as a holy people for His own possession. The election-and-possession combination identifies the relationship.

Psalms 134:5
For I knew that the Lord is great, and our Lord is above all the gods.
  Exodus 18:11 — Knowing the Lord is above all gods: The psalmist declares knowledge that the Lord is great and above all gods. Jethro says that now he knows the Lord is greater than all gods after hearing about the deliverance from Egypt. Knowledge and the comparative claim are closely shared, with their speakers and settings distinguished.

Psalms 134:6
Everything the Lord willed, He did, in heaven and on earth, in the seas and all the deeps.
  Psalms 113:11 — The Lord doing all He wills: Both say God has done everything He wished or willed and include heaven and earth. Psalm 134 additionally names seas and deeps. The assertion of divine will carried into action is closely parallel.

Psalms 134:7
He brings clouds up from the earth’s extremity; He made lightning for rain; He brings winds out of His treasuries.
  Jeremiah 10:13 — Clouds, lightning and treasuries: Both describe God bringing clouds from the earth's extremity and making lightning for rain. The source says He brings winds from His treasuries; Jeremiah says He brought light from His treasuries. The shared sequence is retained with the different final objects and verb forms.

Psalms 134:8
He struck Egypt’s firstborn, from man to beast.
  Exodus 12:29 — Egypt's firstborn, humans and animals: The source summarizes the Lord's striking Egypt's firstborn from human to beast. Exodus narrates the midnight blow against firstborn, from Pharaoh's firstborn to the captive woman's, and includes the firstborn of livestock. The firstborn judgment and its human-and-animal scope are particular.

Psalms 134:9
He sent out signs and wonders into your midst, Egypt, upon Pharaoh and all his slaves.
  Exodus 7:3-5 — Signs and wonders in Egypt: The source recalls signs and wonders sent into Egypt upon Pharaoh and his slaves. Exodus's divine speech promises multiplied signs and wonders in Egypt while describing Pharaoh's refusal and God's action. The Egyptian setting and signs correspond, with the psalm's slaves clause left as its own detail.

Psalms 134:10
He struck many nations and killed powerful kings,
  Joshua 12:7-8 — The defeat of many kings: The psalm credits the Lord with striking nations and killing powerful kings. The account in Joshua introduces the kings whom Joshua and Israel killed and the land allotted to Israel. The conquest summary corresponds, preserving the psalm's divine attribution and the narrative's human agents.

Psalms 134:11
Sihon king of the Amorites, Og king of Bashan, and all Canaan’s kingdoms.
  Joshua 12:1-5 — Sihon and Og named among defeated kings: Both name Sihon king of the Amorites and Og king of Bashan among conquered rulers. The account in Joshua specifies their territories, while the psalm also includes all Canaan's kingdoms. The two named kings and realms give a precise historical connection.

Psalms 134:12
He gave their land as an inheritance, an inheritance to Israel His people.
  Deuteronomy 4:38 — Land given as an inheritance: The source says God gave the conquered land as Israel's inheritance. Deuteronomy recalls God's bringing Israel in and giving the nations' land to inherit. The land gift following the removal of nations is shared.

Psalms 134:13
Lord, Thy name is for ever; Lord, Thy memorial is from generation to generation.
  Exodus 3:15 — The perpetual name and memorial: Both join the Lord's enduring name with His memorial from generation to generation. Exodus presents the formula within God's instructions to Moses concerning His name. The name-and-memorial combination is closely repeated.

Psalms 134:14
For the Lord will judge His people and be comforted concerning His slaves.
  Deuteronomy 32:36 — Judging His people and responding concerning His servants: Both say the Lord will judge His people and then describe His response concerning His servants. The psalm says He will be comforted concerning His slaves; Deuteronomy says He will be moved concerning His servants. The paired clauses correspond with those wording differences preserved.

Psalms 134:15
The nations’ idols are silver and gold, works of human hands.
  Psalms 113:12 — Idols of silver and gold made by human hands: Both describe the nations' idols as silver and gold and works of human hands. Material and manufacture are repeated together, giving a direct literary parallel.

Psalms 134:16
They have a mouth but will not speak; they have eyes but will not see.
  Psalms 113:13 — Mouths and eyes that do not function: Both enumerate a mouth that will not speak and eyes that will not see. The same paired nonfunctioning organs continue the shared idol description.

Psalms 134:17
They have ears but will not listen; they have noses but will not smell; they have hands but will not touch; they have feet but will not walk; they will not sound in their throat, for there is no breath in their mouth.
  Psalms 113:14-15 — The idols' unresponsive senses and limbs: Both enumerate unresponsive ears, noses, hands and feet, ending with an inability to sound in the throat. Psalm 134 adds the absence of breath in the mouth. The extended organ sequence is shared without importing that final clause into Psalm 113.
  Jeremiah 10:14 — Idols without breath or spirit: The source says the idols have no breath in their mouth. Jeremiah describes falsely cast objects with no spirit in them. Both portray manufactured idols as lacking animating capacity, with breath in the mouth and spirit in them retained as distinct expressions.

Psalms 134:18
May those who make them become like them, and all who trust in them.
  Psalms 113:16 — Makers and trusters becoming like idols: Both wish the idol makers and those trusting in the idols to become like them. Makers, trusters and the desired likeness form the same concluding judgment.

Psalms 134:19
House of Israel, bless the Lord; house of Aaron, bless the Lord.
  Psalms 113:20 — Blessing and the houses of Israel and Aaron: The source summons the houses of Israel and Aaron to bless the Lord. Psalm 113 reports the Lord blessing the same two houses. The paired groups correspond, while the direction of the blessing is reversed.

Psalms 134:20
House of Levi, bless the Lord; you who fear the Lord, bless the Lord.
  1 Chronicles 23:30 — The Levites' praise: The source summons Levi's house to bless the Lord before addressing those who fear Him. Chronicles assigns Levi's sons the morning and evening work of standing to praise and thank the Lord. Levi's service of praise is the particular connection; the psalm's further group is not equated with the Levites.

Psalms 134:21
Blessed be the Lord from Zion, He who dwells in Jerusalem.
  Psalms 131:13-14 — The Lord dwelling in Zion: The source blesses the Lord from Zion and says He dwells in Jerusalem. Psalm 131 describes Zion chosen as His dwelling and enduring rest. The divine dwelling in Zion is explicit in both.

Psalms 135:1
Hallelujah! Give thanks to the Lord, for He is kind; for His mercy is for ever.
  1 Chronicles 16:34 — Thanksgiving and enduring mercy: Both summon thanksgiving to the Lord and repeat that His mercy endures for ever. The psalm calls Him kind, whereas Chronicles says for it is good. The thanksgiving and mercy refrain connect the texts without silently harmonizing those clauses.

Psalms 135:2
Give thanks to the God of gods; for His mercy is for ever.
  Deuteronomy 10:17 — God of gods: The source calls for thanks to the God of gods. Deuteronomy names Israel's Lord as God of gods and Lord of lords. The distinctive superlative divine title is the connection.

Psalms 135:3
Give thanks to the Lord of lords; for His mercy is for ever.
  1 Timothy 6:15 — Lord of lords: The source addresses the Lord of lords. First Timothy's doxology describes the Sovereign as King of those who reign and Lord of those who rule. The particular title of lordship over rulers corresponds, with the phrasing preserved.

Psalms 135:4
To Him who alone does great wonders; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 71:18 — The One who alone works wonders: Both describe the Lord as the One who alone does wonders. Psalm 135 calls them great and adds its mercy refrain; Psalm 71 blesses Israel's God. The exclusivity of the wonder-working is shared.

Psalms 135:5
To Him who made the heavens with understanding; for His mercy is for ever.
  Jeremiah 10:12 — The heavens made with understanding: The source attributes the heavens' making to understanding. Jeremiah says the Lord stretched out heaven by His understanding after describing earth and the inhabited world. The heaven-and-understanding creation claim corresponds, with the verbs distinguished.

Psalms 135:6
To Him who made the earth firm upon the waters; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 23:1-2 — The earth founded upon waters: The source says God made earth firm upon waters. Psalm 23 says He founded the earth upon seas and prepared it upon rivers. The particular foundation-upon-water image is shared.

Psalms 135:7
To Him who alone made great lights; for His mercy is for ever.
  Genesis 1:16 — The making of great lights: The psalm praises the One who made great lights. Genesis describes God making two great lights and the stars. The making and great-light description correspond, without adding Genesis's number to the source.

Psalms 135:8
the sun for authority over the day; for His mercy is for ever.
  Genesis 1:16 — The sun's authority over the day: The source names the sun for authority over the day. Genesis assigns rule of the day to the greater light. The day-governing role connects them, while sun is explicit in the psalm and greater light in Genesis.

Psalms 135:9
the moon and stars for authority over the night; for His mercy is for ever.
  Genesis 1:16-18 — The lights ruling over night: The source names moon and stars for authority over the night. Genesis assigns night rule to the lesser light, mentions stars, and describes the lights' governing function. The night-light pattern corresponds without replacing Genesis's descriptive name with the psalm's moon wording.

Psalms 135:10
To Him who struck Egypt with their firstborn; for His mercy is for ever.
  Exodus 12:29 — The firstborn struck in Egypt: The source recalls the striking of Egypt with their firstborn. Exodus describes the Lord striking Egypt's firstborn at midnight, including human and animal groups. The firstborn blow is the concrete historical connection.

Psalms 135:11
And to Him who brought Israel out from their midst; for His mercy is for ever.
  Exodus 13:3 — Israel brought out of Egypt: The source praises the One who brought Israel out from the Egyptians' midst. Moses tells the people to remember the day the Lord brought them out of Egypt's bondage. The named exodus and God's bringing out are shared.

Psalms 135:12
with a mighty hand and a raised arm; for His mercy is for ever.
  Deuteronomy 4:34 — The mighty hand and raised arm: Both describe the Egyptian deliverance through a mighty hand and a raised or uplifted arm. Deuteronomy places the formula among the signs and acts by which God took a nation from another nation's midst.

Psalms 135:13
To Him who divided the Red Sea into divisions; for His mercy is for ever.
  Exodus 14:21 — The Red Sea divided: The source praises the division of the Red Sea into divisions. Exodus narrates Moses' stretched hand, the Lord driving back the sea, and the water being split. The divided sea is shared; the source does not specify the target's south wind.

Psalms 135:14
And to Him who led Israel through its midst; for His mercy is for ever.
  Exodus 14:22 — Israel passing through the sea: The source credits God with leading Israel through the sea's midst. Exodus describes Israel entering its midst on dry ground between walls of water. The route and people identify the connection, while the detailed terrain belongs to Exodus.

Psalms 135:15
And to Him who shook Pharaoh and his force into the Red Sea; for His mercy is for ever.
  Exodus 14:27 — The Egyptians shaken into the sea: The source names Pharaoh and his force as those shaken into the Red Sea. Exodus says the Lord shook the Egyptians into the sea's midst. The unusual shaking-into-the-sea expression corresponds; that target clause does not separately name Pharaoh.

Psalms 135:16
To Him who led His people through the wilderness; for His mercy is for ever. To Him who brought water out of a sheer rock; for His mercy is for ever.
  Deuteronomy 8:15 — Wilderness guidance and water from rock: This complete source unit includes both wilderness guidance and water brought from a sheer rock, each with its refrain. Deuteronomy likewise joins guidance through the fearful waterless wilderness with a spring brought from the sheer rock. Both source clauses are supported together.

Psalms 135:17
To Him who struck great kings; for His mercy is for ever.
  Joshua 12:7-8 — Great kings struck down: The source praises the striking of great kings. The account in Joshua introduces kings whom Joshua and Israel killed beyond the Jordan and lists the lands involved. The comparison concerns the defeat of kings, preserving the source's divine attribution and the narrative's named human agents.

Psalms 135:18
And to Him who killed mighty kings; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 134:10 — Powerful kings killed: Both credit the Lord with killing mighty or powerful kings. Psalm 134 couples that action with striking many nations; Psalm 135 gives it a separate mercy refrain.

Psalms 135:19
Sihon king of the Amorites; for His mercy is for ever.
  Numbers 21:23-24 — Sihon's defeat: The source names Sihon king of the Amorites within the account of kings struck down. Numbers describes Sihon's refusal of passage, his force at Jahaz and Israel striking him and taking his land. The named ruler and the battle account give the historical connection.

Psalms 135:20
and Og king of Bashan; for His mercy is for ever.
  Numbers 21:33-35 — Og's defeat: The source names Og king of Bashan in the defeated-kings sequence. Numbers describes Og's advance at Edrei, the Lord's assurance to Moses and his defeat. The named king and kingdom make the connection precise, without transferring other kings' battles to Og.

Psalms 135:21
And to Him who gave their land as an inheritance; for His mercy is for ever.
  Numbers 32:33 — The conquered land assigned as inheritance: The source says the defeated rulers' land was given as an inheritance. Numbers specifies Moses' assignment of Sihon's and Og's kingdoms to Gad, Reuben and half of Manasseh. The land of the kings just named in the psalm supplies the link, with the agents and recipient descriptions retained.

Psalms 135:22
an inheritance to Israel His slave; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 134:12 — Israel receiving an inheritance: Both call the land an inheritance for Israel. The source calls Israel His slave, while Psalm 134 calls Israel His people. The closely repeated inheritance formula is preserved with that difference.

Psalms 135:23
For in our humiliation the Lord remembered us; for His mercy is for ever.
  Exodus 2:23-25 — Divine remembrance amid humiliation: The psalm says the Lord remembered the speakers in humiliation. Exodus describes Israel's labor and groaning and God remembering His covenant with the patriarchs. Divine remembrance amid affliction is compared, without identifying the psalm's humiliation as that one episode or replacing the object of remembrance.

Psalms 135:24
And He redeemed us from our enemies; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 106:2 — Redemption from enemies: Both describe people redeemed by the Lord from an enemy or enemies. The psalm speaks as the redeemed community; Psalm 106 summons the redeemed to say so. The redemption-from-enemies formula is shared.

Psalms 135:25
He gives food to all flesh; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 144:15-16 — Food given to all living things: The source says God gives food to all flesh. Psalm 144 describes all eyes hoping in Him, His gift of food at the proper time, and His filling every living creature. Universal dependence on God's feeding connects the descriptions.

Psalms 135:26
Give thanks to the God of heaven; for His mercy is for ever. Give thanks to the Lord of lords; for His mercy is for ever.
  Psalms 135:3 — Same-chapter return to the opening title: The second clause of this complete final unit repeats the earlier call to thank the Lord of lords with the enduring-mercy refrain. This same-chapter closing return links verse 26 to verse 3, while preserving the preceding thanksgiving to the God of heaven.

Psalms 136:1
For David. By Babylon’s rivers, there we sat and wept when we remembered Zion.
  Psalms 125:1-3 — Zion in captivity and restored gladness: Weeping beside Babylon’s rivers at the remembrance of Zion contrasts with the comfort, joyful mouths and gladness when Zion’s captivity is turned back. The comparison follows the named Zion and captivity setting without making the two poems one occasion.

Psalms 136:2
Upon the willows in her midst we hung our instruments.
  Lamentations 5:14-18 — Silent music in mourning over Zion: Hanging the instruments amid Babylon’s captivity corresponds to chosen men ceasing from songs, joy ending and dancing turning to mourning because Mount Zion was destroyed. Lamentations does not mention willows or hung instruments; the specific link is the silence of music in the lament for Zion.

Psalms 136:3
For there those who took us captive asked us for words of songs, and those who led us away for a hymn: “Sing us some of Zion’s songs.”
  Psalms 125:1-2 — Captive songs and the joy of restored Zion: Captors demand songs of Zion here; the return of Zion’s captivity there fills the people’s mouths and tongues with joy. The speakers and circumstances differ, making the requested captive song and restored expression a concrete contrast.

Psalms 136:4
How can we sing the Lord’s song on foreign land?
  Psalms 125:1-2 — The Lord’s song abroad and restored Zion’s voice: The question about singing the Lord’s song on foreign land contrasts with the joyful mouth and tongue when Zion’s captivity is turned back. This does not supply a named location for Psalm 125 beyond Zion’s stated captivity.

Psalms 136:5
If I forget you, Jerusalem, may my right hand be forgotten.
  Isaiah 49:14-16 — Refusing to forget Jerusalem or Zion: The speaker binds himself not to forget Jerusalem; Isaiah answers Zion’s complaint of being forgotten with the Lord’s promise not to forget her and the image of her walls on His hands. Human self-imprecation and divine assurance remain distinct.

Psalms 136:6
May my tongue cling to my throat if I do not remember you, if I do not set Jerusalem first at the beginning of my gladness.
  Psalms 21:16 — A tongue stuck to the throat: The conditional curse for failing to remember Jerusalem uses the tongue clinging to the throat, while Psalm 21 describes that condition amid mortal distress. The shared bodily image does not identify the occasions.

Psalms 136:7
Remember, Lord, Edom’s sons on Jerusalem’s day, those saying, “Empty her, empty her, down to the foundation within her!”
  Obadiah 1:11-14 — Edom’s participation in Jerusalem’s calamity: The psalm asks remembrance of Edom’s sons urging Jerusalem’s destruction. Obadiah’s address to Idumea describes opposition when strangers entered Jerusalem and forbids rejoicing, entering and attacking on the day of destruction. The specific quotations and actions are not collapsed into one speech.

Psalms 136:8
Daughter Babylon, miserable one, happy is the one who will repay you the repayment you gave us.
  Jeremiah 27:28-29 — Babylon repaid according to what she did: The psalm calls happy the one repaying Babylon the repayment she gave; Jeremiah commands that Babylon be repaid according to her works and treated as she treated others. Both explicitly name Babylon and reciprocal recompense.

Psalms 136:9
Happy is the one who will seize your infants and dash them against the rock.
  Isaiah 13:16-19 — Children dashed in judgment against Babylon: The psalm’s violent blessing concerning Babylon’s infants corresponds to Isaiah’s Babylon oracle describing their children dashed before them. Isaiah does not specify the psalm’s rock.

Psalms 137:1
For David. I will thank Thee, Lord, with all my heart, for Thou heardest the words of my mouth, and before angels I will make music unto Thee.
  Psalms 9:2-3 — Wholehearted thanksgiving and music to God: Both first-person prayers promise thanksgiving with the whole heart and music or sung praise to God. Psalm 137 adds heard speech and angels; Psalm 9 adds the recounting of wonders.

Psalms 137:2
I will bow toward Thy holy temple and thank Thy name for Thy mercy and Thy truth, for Thou hast magnified Thine utterance above every name.
  Psalms 5:8 — Bowing toward the holy temple: Both speakers bow toward God’s holy temple in a context naming His mercy. Psalm 137 adds thanksgiving for mercy and truth; Psalm 5 describes entering the house and bowing in fear.

Psalms 137:3
On whatever day I call Thee, hear me quickly; Thou wilt greatly attend to me with strength in my soul.
  Psalms 101:3 — Hear quickly on the day of calling: The petition on whatever day the speaker calls, asking to be heard quickly, closely repeats Psalm 101’s concluding plea. Psalm 137’s strengthening of the soul is an additional element.

Psalms 137:4
Let all earth’s kings thank Thee, Lord, for they heard all the words of Thy mouth.
  Psalms 101:16 — All earth’s kings respond to the Lord: The summons for all earth’s kings to thank the Lord after hearing His words corresponds to all the kings of the earth fearing His glory in Psalm 101. Thanksgiving and fear are distinguished.

Psalms 137:5
And let them sing in the Lord’s ways, for great is the Lord’s glory.
  Psalms 95:3-4 — Singing in view of the Lord’s great glory: Singing in the Lord’s ways because His glory is great corresponds to announcing His glory and declaring the Lord great and greatly to be praised. The passages unite the stated glory, greatness and sung praise without treating ways and nations as the same object.

Psalms 137:6
For the Lord is high and watches the lowly, and the lofty He knows from afar.
  Psalms 112:5-7 — The high Lord attends to the lowly: The Lord’s height and regard for the lowly parallel His high dwelling and looking upon lowly things, followed by raising the poor. Psalm 137’s knowledge of the lofty from afar is not imported into the target.

Psalms 137:7
If I walk amid affliction, Thou wilt keep me alive; against my enemies’ anger Thou didst stretch out Thy hand, and Thy right hand saved me.
  Psalms 17:18-20 — Preservation amid affliction and hostile power: Psalm 137 anticipates being kept alive if the speaker walks amid affliction and recalls God stretching His hand against the enemies’ anger and saving him with His right hand. Psalm 17 recalls enemies overtaking him on the day of affliction and the Lord becoming his support and bringing him into a spacious place; it also expresses future assurances of rescue. The conditional expectation and remembered acts remain distinct; the right-hand image belongs to Psalm 137.

Psalms 137:8
The Lord will repay on my behalf. Lord, Thy mercy is for ever; do not neglect the works of Thy hands.
  Job 10:8-12 — A plea concerning the work of God’s hands: The plea not to neglect the works of God’s hands can be compared with Job’s appeal that those hands made him, followed by his complaint of being struck and remembrance of life and mercy. The speakers’ differing expectations are preserved.

Psalms 138:1
For the end. A psalm for David. Lord, Thou didst test me and know me.
  Psalms 25:2 — Divine testing of the speaker: Psalm 138 begins by affirming that the Lord tested and knew the speaker; Psalm 25 asks the Lord to test and try the speaker’s kidneys and heart. An accomplished testing and a requested testing are distinguished.

Psalms 138:2
Thou knewest my sitting and my rising; Thou didst understand my reasonings from afar.
  Psalms 93:11 — The Lord knows human reasonings: Knowledge of the speaker’s reasonings from afar corresponds to the Lord knowing human reasonings in Psalm 93. The latter calls them futile, a judgment not stated in this source unit.

Psalms 138:3
Thou didst trace my path and my measuring cord, and Thou didst foresee all my ways.
  Proverbs 5:21 — Human ways and tracks before God: God traced the speaker’s path and foresaw all his ways; Proverbs places a man’s ways before God’s eyes and says He watches all his tracks. The psalm’s measuring cord is not supplied to Proverbs.

Psalms 138:5
behold, Lord, Thou knewest all things, the last and the first; Thou didst form me and place Thy hand upon me.
  Job 10:8 — God’s forming hands upon the person: The Lord’s forming the speaker and placing His hand upon him corresponds to Job’s assertion that God’s hands molded and made him. Job’s subsequent complaint that God changed and struck him gives the comparison a different emotional setting.

Psalms 138:6
Thy knowledge was made wondrous beyond me; it grew strong—I cannot reach it.
  Job 42:3 — Wonderful divine knowledge beyond the speaker: The speaker cannot reach God’s wondrous knowledge; Job acknowledges great and wonderful things he did not understand. Neither statement is expanded into knowledge of an unstated event.

Psalms 138:7
Where can I go from Thy Spirit, and where can I flee from Thy face?
  Jeremiah 23:23-24 — No hiding from God’s presence: The questions about fleeing God’s Spirit and face correspond to the Lord’s question whether someone hiding can escape His sight and His filling heaven and earth. The different terms Spirit, face and sight remain intact.

Psalms 138:8
If I ascend to heaven, Thou art there; if I descend into Hades, Thou art present.
  Amos 9:2 — Heaven and Hades cannot remove divine reach: Both passages explicitly pair ascent to heaven and descent into Hades with God’s presence or reach. The psalm affirms presence; Amos announces inescapable judgment.

Psalms 138:9
if I take up my wings at dawn and settle at the sea’s farthest reaches,
  Jonah 1:3-4 — Departure across the sea cannot escape the Lord: Within the psalm’s question about fleeing God’s face, settling at the sea’s farthest reaches can be compared with Jonah’s attempt to sail to Tarshish from the Lord’s presence and the Lord’s action upon the sea. No geographical identity for the psalm’s destination is asserted.

Psalms 138:10
indeed, even there Thy hand will guide me, and Thy right hand will hold me fast.
  Psalms 72:23-24 — Divine handholding and guidance: Both passages combine divine holding and guidance. Psalm 138 says God’s right hand will hold the speaker; Psalm 72 says God grasped the speaker’s right hand and guided him in counsel.

Psalms 138:12
For darkness will not be dark from Thee, and night will be lit up like day; as its darkness, so also its light.
  Daniel 2:22 — Darkness does not exclude divine knowledge: The psalm says darkness is not dark from God and night is lit like day; Daniel says He reveals dark things and knows what is in darkness and light. The comparison preserves Daniel’s actual knowledge wording rather than adding a different concluding light clause.

Psalms 138:13
For Thou didst acquire my kidneys, Lord; Thou didst support me from my mother’s womb.
  Psalms 70:6 — Divine support from the mother’s womb: Both speakers explicitly describe being supported from the womb. Psalm 138 adds the acquiring of kidneys; Psalm 70 names shelter from the mother’s belly.

Psalms 138:14
I will thank Thee, for I was made fearfully wonderful; wondrous are Thy works, and my soul knows very well.
  Job 10:8-11 — The person wonderfully fashioned by God: Thanksgiving for the speaker’s wonderful making corresponds to Job’s description of God molding him and clothing or threading his body together. The relation is bodily divine workmanship; Job’s distressed protest is not turned into thanksgiving.

Psalms 138:15
My bone was not hidden from Thee, which Thou madest in secret, and my substance in earth’s lowest places.
  Ecclesiastes 11:5 — Hidden bodily formation and God’s works: The psalm says the speaker’s bone and secretly made substance were not hidden from God; Ecclesiastes uses bones in a pregnant woman’s womb to illustrate human ignorance of God’s works. Divine knowledge and human ignorance are contrasted without equating earth’s lowest places with the womb.

Psalms 138:19
If Thou dost kill sinners, God— men of blood, turn away from me,
  Psalms 25:9 — Separation from sinners and men of blood: The source commands men of blood to turn away while addressing God concerning sinners; Psalm 25 asks not to have the speaker’s life destroyed together with ungodly people and men of bloodshed. Desired separation is shared, while the source’s conditional opening is preserved.

Psalms 138:21
Did I not hate those who hate Thee, Lord, and melt away over Thine enemies?
  Psalms 25:5 — The speaker’s aversion to God’s opponents: The speaker’s hatred of those hating the Lord corresponds to hatred of an assembly of evildoers and refusal to sit with the ungodly. This is a comparison of stated opposition, not an equation of every named group or person.

Psalms 138:22
I hated them with complete hatred; they became enemies to me.
  Psalms 118:113 — Declared hatred of God’s opponents or lawbreakers: Complete hatred of the Lord’s enemies can be compared with the declared hatred of lawbreakers alongside love of His law. The target identifies lawbreakers; it does not repeat the source’s complete-hatred wording.

Psalms 138:23
Test me, God, and know my heart; examine me and know my paths,
  Psalms 25:2 — Testing the inward person: Both petitions ask God to test the speaker and examine the heart. Psalm 138 adds knowledge of paths, while Psalm 25 names kidneys and trying by fire.

Psalms 138:24
and see whether a way of lawlessness is in me, and guide me in an eternal way.
  Psalms 5:9 — Guidance away from a wrong way: The request to detect a way of lawlessness and guide in an eternal way corresponds to the request for divine guidance in righteousness and a straight way before the speaker. Eternal duration is stated only in Psalm 138.

Psalms 139:2
Rescue me, Lord, from an evil person; deliver me from an unjust man,
  Psalms 17:49 — Rescue from an unjust man: The plea to be delivered from an unjust man closely corresponds to Psalm 17’s assurance that the Lord will rescue the speaker from an unjust man. The source also names an evil person.

Psalms 139:3
those who devised injustices in their heart and were arraying wars all day.
  Psalms 55:6 — Continual hostile thought against the speaker: The source’s people devise injustices in their heart and array wars all day; Psalm 55 describes all-day hostility toward the speaker’s words and thoughts against him for evil. Literal war is not added to the target.

Psalms 139:4
They sharpened their tongue like a serpent’s; asp venom is beneath their lips. Diapsalma.
  Romans 3:13 — Asp venom beneath the lips: Romans’ written-text sequence includes the poison of asps under their lips, corresponding closely to this psalm’s asp venom beneath their lips. The psalm’s sharpened serpent-like tongue differs from the target’s deceitful tongues.

Psalms 139:5
Guard me, Lord, from a sinner’s hand; rescue me from unjust people, those who devised to trip my steps.
  Psalms 55:7 — Hostile attention to the speaker’s feet: The plea against unjust people who devise to trip the speaker’s steps corresponds to concealed adversaries watching his heel and waiting for his soul. The relation rests on predatory pursuit expressed through steps or heel.

Psalms 139:6
The proud hid a trap for me and stretched cords, traps for my feet; along the path they set a stumbling block for me. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 141:4 — A trap concealed on the speaker’s path: Both passages explicitly place an enemy-hidden trap on the path where the speaker walks. Psalm 139 adds cords, feet and a stumbling block.

Psalms 139:7
I said to the Lord, “Thou art my God.” Listen, Lord, to the voice of my petition.
  Psalms 30:15 — The confession Thou art my God: Both speakers say to the Lord, Thou art my God, while trusting Him amid surrounding adversaries. Psalm 139 adds a petition to listen; Psalm 30 states the speaker’s hope.

Psalms 139:9
Do not hand me over to a sinner, Lord, through my desire; they schemed against me; do not abandon me, lest they rise high. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 26:12 — Do not hand the speaker over to wrongdoers: Both petitions ask not to be handed over to hostile wrongdoers. Psalm 139’s through-my-desire clause remains uninterpreted; Psalm 26 specifies unjust witnesses and afflicters.

Psalms 139:10
The head of their encirclement— the trouble of their lips will cover them.
  Psalms 7:17 — Trouble returning upon the hostile head: The trouble of the encirclers’ lips covering them corresponds to trouble returning on an evildoer’s head and injustice descending upon his crown. The source’s compressed head-of-encirclement wording is preserved rather than replaced with a named leader.

Psalms 139:11
Coals will fall upon them; in fire Thou wilt cast them down; in miseries they will certainly not withstand.
  Psalms 10:6 — Fire among the punishments of sinners: The source describes coals falling and being cast into fire; Psalm 10 names fire and sulfur in the sinners’ cup portion after raining traps. The target is not said to rain coals or fire.

Psalms 139:12
A talkative man will not be set straight on the earth; evils will hunt an unjust man to destruction.
  Proverbs 5:22 — Evil or lawless acts hunt a person: Evils hunting the unjust man to destruction correspond to lawless acts hunting a man in Proverbs. The target adds bondage by his own sins; the source also judges the talkative man.

Psalms 139:13
I knew that the Lord will act for the poor person’s judgment and the needy people’s cause.
  Psalms 71:2-4 — Judgment for poor and needy people: The Lord’s action for the poor person’s judgment and needy people’s cause corresponds to the royal prayer for judgment of the poor and rescue of the children of the needy. The Lord and the king receiving His judgment are not treated as identical agents.

Psalms 139:14
Indeed, the righteous will thank Thy name, and the upright will dwell with Thy presence.
  Psalms 16:15 — Righteousness in God’s presence: The upright dwelling with God’s presence corresponds to the first-person hope of appearing before His face in righteousness. The thanksgiving clause and satisfaction at appearing glory are distinct additions.

Psalms 140:1
A psalm for David. Lord, I have cried unto Thee; hear me. Attend to the voice of my petition when I cry unto Thee.
  Psalms 141:2 — Crying and pleading to the Lord with the voice: The source’s cry and plea for attention to the voice of petition closely correspond to the twice-stated voice crying and pleading to the Lord in Psalm 141.

Psalms 140:2
Let my prayer be directed as incense before Thee, the lifting of my hands as an evening sacrifice.
  Luke 1:9-10 — Prayer compared with incense and prayer at the incense hour: The comparison of prayer with incense can be read alongside the people praying at the hour when the priest burns incense. The psalm is a prayer-image, whereas Luke narrates simultaneous temple actions.
  Exodus 29:39-42 — Raised hands compared with the evening sacrifice: The psalm compares the lifting of hands to an evening sacrifice; Exodus prescribes the second daily lamb in the evening as a continual offering. The hands are a comparison, not a claim that the speaker performs the prescribed lamb offering.

Psalms 140:3
Set a guard, Lord, for my mouth and a restraining door around my lips.
  Psalms 38:2 — A guard over the mouth against sinful speech: The request for a guard over the mouth and restraining door around lips corresponds to the speaker placing a guard upon his mouth so as not to sin with his tongue. Petition and stated self-restraint differ.

Psalms 140:4
Do not turn my heart toward words of wickedness, to offer excuses for sins with people who practice lawlessness, and I will certainly not join with their chosen ones.
  Proverbs 4:23-24 — Guarding heart and speech from wickedness: The petition not to turn the heart toward wicked words and sinful excuses corresponds to guarding the heart and removing crooked mouth and unjust lips. Proverbs’ instructions are not represented as the same excuse-making scene.

Psalms 140:5
A righteous man will discipline me in mercy and correct me, but let a sinner’s oil not enrich my head, for still my prayer is amid their good pleasures.
  Proverbs 27:5-6 — Beneficial correction contrasted with an enemy’s pleasant treatment: The righteous person’s merciful discipline and correction, contrasted with a sinner’s oil, can be compared with open rebuke and trustworthy wounds of a friend against an enemy’s willing kisses. Oil and kisses are distinct images, and the psalm’s final prayer clause is not resolved by the comparison.

Psalms 140:7
Like a clod of earth broken upon the earth, our bones were scattered beside Hades.
  Psalms 21:15 — Scattered bones in the speaker’s distress: The source’s scattered bones beside Hades correspond to the scattered bones in Psalm 21’s bodily distress. The clod-of-earth image and location beside Hades are not added to the target.

Psalms 140:8
For my eyes are toward Thee, Lord, Lord; in Thee I hoped—do not take away my soul.
  Psalms 122:1-2 — Eyes directed toward the Lord in dependence: The source directs its eyes toward the Lord and says it hoped in Him; Psalm 122 lifts eyes to Him and compares that attention with servants looking to their masters’ hands, continuing until divine compassion.

Psalms 140:9
Guard me from the trap they assembled for me and from the stumbling blocks of those practicing lawlessness.
  Psalms 118:110 — Protection from sinners’ traps: The plea to be guarded from the assembled trap and lawless stumbling blocks corresponds to sinners setting a trap while the speaker remains with God’s commandments. The target states fidelity rather than explicitly repeating the source’s protection request.

Psalms 140:10
Sinners will fall into his casting net; I am alone until I pass by.
  Psalms 7:15-16 — Wrongdoers caught in a destructive device: Sinners falling into his casting net correspond to the evildoer falling into the pit he dug. The source’s his is left unassigned, and a net is not called a pit.

Psalms 141:1
Of understanding, of David, when he was in the cave. A prayer.
  Psalms 56:1 — David and a cave in psalm headings: Both headings place David in a cave. Psalm 56 explicitly names escape from Saul; Psalm 141 does not name the pursuer or specify which cave. The shared heading information does not prove one historical occasion.

Psalms 141:2
With my voice I cried to the Lord; with my voice I pleaded with the Lord.
  Psalms 76:2 — Repeated voice crying to the Lord: Both lines repeat with my voice in describing the cry to the Lord. Psalm 141’s second action is pleading; Psalm 76 continues with God attending to the speaker.

Psalms 141:3
I will pour out my plea before Him; I will declare my affliction before Him.
  Psalms 61:9 — Pouring out the inward plea before God: The speaker will pour out his plea before God and declare affliction; Psalm 61 tells the assembly to pour out their hearts before Him. The individual plea and the communal heart image are distinguished.

Psalms 141:4
When my spirit was failing within me, Thou knewest my paths. On this way where I was walking, they hid a trap for me.
  Psalms 142:4 — The failing or despondent spirit within: The spirit failing within the speaker closely corresponds to the spirit growing despondent within and the troubled heart in Psalm 142. The source continues with God’s knowledge and a trap, details not supplied to the target.

Psalms 141:5
I looked to the right and observed, for there was no one recognizing me. Escape perished from me, and there is no one seeking my soul.
  Psalms 68:21 — No human companion or comforter found: The speaker looks and finds no one recognizing or seeking him; Psalm 68 waits for someone to grieve with him and finds no comforter. Seeking the speaker’s soul is retained as written, not turned into an enemy pursuing him.

Psalms 141:6
I cried unto Thee, Lord; I said, Thou art my hope, my portion in the land of the living.
  Psalms 15:5 — The Lord as the speaker’s portion: Calling the Lord the speaker’s portion in the land of the living corresponds to the Lord as the portion of inheritance and cup. Neither passage identifies the portion as a literal land allotment.

Psalms 141:7
Attend to my plea, for I was brought very low. Rescue me from those pursuing me, for they grew stronger than I.
  Psalms 17:18 — Rescue from pursuers stronger than the speaker: The plea for rescue because pursuers grew stronger than the speaker closely corresponds to Psalm 17’s rescue from powerful enemies who grew stronger than him.

Psalms 141:8
Bring my soul out of prison, that I may thank Thy name, Lord. The righteous will wait for me until Thou repayest me.
  Psalms 106:13-16 — Release from confinement followed by thanksgiving: The request to bring the soul out of prison in order to thank God corresponds to rescue, broken bonds and a summons to acknowledge His mercies. The psalm’s soul-prison language is not assigned the target’s particular captivity or cause.

Psalms 142:1
A psalm of David, when his son pursues him. Lord, hear my prayer; give ear to my plea in Thy truth. Answer me in Thy righteousness.
  2 Samuel 15:13-14 — David endangered by his son: The heading identifies David pursued by his son; the narrative describes David fleeing the danger from Absalom, identified earlier as his son. It supplies a concrete comparable father-son crisis without proving the poem’s composition date or equating every detail.

Psalms 142:2
Do not enter into judgment with Thy servant, for no one living will be justified before Thee.
  Romans 3:20 — No living person justified before God: The plea not to enter judgment because no living person will be justified before God corresponds to Romans’ statement that no flesh will be justified before Him by works of law. The law qualification belongs to Romans, not this psalm’s wording.
  Job 9:2-3 — Human righteousness before God’s judgment: Job asks how a mortal can be righteous before the Lord and considers entering judgment with Him; the psalm asks God not to enter judgment with His servant because no living person will be justified before Him. Job’s actual denied-hearing wording is preserved.

Psalms 142:3
For the enemy pursued my soul, brought my life low to the ground, made me sit in dark places like those long dead.
  Lamentations 3:6 — Dwelling in darkness like the long dead: The enemy’s making the speaker sit in dark places like those long dead closely corresponds to Lamentations’ dwelling in dark places like the dead of ages. The surrounding agents differ and are not equated.

Psalms 142:4
My spirit grew despondent within me; within me my heart was troubled.
  Psalms 76:4 — The spirit loses heart amid distress: The source’s despondent spirit and troubled heart correspond to Psalm 76’s spirit losing heart after extended speech. Remembering God and gladness in the target are not omitted to claim identical emotional sequences.

Psalms 142:5
I remembered ancient days and meditated on all Thy works; I was pondering the works of Thy hands.
  Psalms 76:12-13 — Remembering and meditating on God’s works: Both speakers remember the Lord’s works and meditate on all His works. Psalm 142 also names ancient days, while Psalm 76 names wonders from the beginning.

Psalms 142:6
I spread out my hands unto Thee; my soul is like a waterless land for Thee. Diapsalma.
  Psalms 62:2 — The soul’s longing expressed through waterless land: The soul like a waterless land for God corresponds to the soul thirsting for Him and flesh longing in a waterless wilderness. The source compares the soul to land; the target situates longing in that landscape.

Psalms 142:7
Quickly hear me, Lord; my spirit is spent. Do not turn Thy face away from me, or I shall become like those descending to the pit.
  Psalms 27:1 — Hear lest the speaker resemble those entering the pit: Psalm 142 asks quick hearing and an unaverted face lest the speaker become like those descending to the pit; Psalm 27 asks that God not remain silent lest he become like those descending into a pit.

Psalms 142:8
Make Thy mercy heard by me in the morning, for I hoped in Thee. Make known to me, Lord, the way I shall walk, for unto Thee I lifted my soul.
  Psalms 24:1-5 — The lifted soul asks to know God’s way: Both prayers combine lifting the soul to God, trusting or hoping in Him, and asking to know the way to walk. Psalm 142’s morning mercy is an additional specification.

Psalms 142:9
Take me out from my enemies, Lord, for I fled unto Thee for refuge.
  Psalms 17:3-4 — Refuge in God and rescue from enemies: The plea for rescue from enemies because the speaker fled to God for refuge corresponds to the confession of God as refuge and rescuer followed by salvation from enemies. No particular flight is inferred.

Psalms 142:10
Teach me to do Thy will, for Thou art my God. Thy good Spirit will lead me on level ground.
  Nehemiah 9:19-20 — God’s good Spirit teaches and guides: The request to be taught God’s will and the affirmation that His good Spirit will lead the speaker correspond to the account of wilderness guidance and the gift of His good Spirit for understanding. Level ground is stated only in the psalm.

Psalms 142:11
For Thy name’s sake, Lord, Thou wilt give me life; in Thy righteousness Thou wilt bring my soul out of distress.
  Psalms 22:3 — Name and righteousness in divine guidance or restoration: The source expects life for God’s name’s sake and deliverance in His righteousness; Psalm 22 describes restoration of the soul and guidance in paths of righteousness for His name’s sake. The particular restoration and direction images differ.

Psalms 142:12
In Thy mercy Thou wilt utterly destroy my enemies and destroy all who distress my soul, for I am Thy servant.
  Psalms 53:6-7 — God’s support and destruction of the speaker’s enemies: The source expects the destruction of enemies and afflicters in God’s mercy; Psalm 53 joins divine support of the soul with turning evils on enemies and a petition to destroy them in truth. Mercy and truth remain the respective grounds stated.

Psalms 143:1
Of David, concerning Goliath. Blessed is the Lord, my God, who trains my hands for battle array, my fingers for war.
  Psalms 17:35 — God trains hands for war: The blessing of God who trains the speaker’s hands for battle closely repeats the training of hands for war in Psalm 17. The source adds fingers and its Goliath heading; the target adds the bronze bow.
  1 Samuel 17:4-10 — Goliath’s challenge in the named heading: The source heading explicitly concerns Goliath; the account in 1 Samuel names Goliath and records his challenge to Israel for single combat. This identifies the named narrative figure without requiring every poetic battle image to be a literal detail of the encounter.

Psalms 143:2
My mercy and my refuge, my Helper and my Rescuer, my Defender, in whom I hoped, who subjects my people beneath me.
  Psalms 17:3 — A cluster of divine refuge and rescue titles: Both lines call God the speaker’s refuge, helper, rescuer and protector or defender, and express hope in Him. Psalm 143’s subjecting of the people is a further clause, not part of this selected target.

Psalms 143:3
Lord, what is man, that Thou becamest known to him, or a son of man, that Thou takest account of him?
  Psalms 8:5 — What is man or a son of man to God: Both questions pair man and son of man with God’s attention. Psalm 143 asks why God became known to him and takes account of him; Psalm 8 asks why He remembers and visits him.

Psalms 143:4
A man was likened to futility; his days pass like a shadow.
  Job 14:1-2 — Human life passes like a shadow: The speaker likens a man to futility and his passing days to a shadow; Job describes the mortal’s short life as fleeing like a shadow. Job’s additional flower image is not imported into the psalm.

Psalms 143:5
Lord, bend Thy heavens and descend; touch the mountains, and they will smoke.
  Psalms 17:8-10 — Heaven bent down in a smoking divine descent: The request that the Lord bend heaven and descend, touching mountains so they smoke, corresponds to Psalm 17’s mountain shaking, smoke in divine anger and heaven bent in descent. The source’s touch is not asserted in the target.

Psalms 143:6
Flash lightning, and Thou wilt scatter them; send out Thine arrows, and Thou wilt throw them into confusion.
  Psalms 17:15 — Lightning and arrows scatter adversaries: Both lines combine divine lightning and arrows with scattering and throwing opponents into confusion. The request and expected action in Psalm 143 correspond to the narrated action of Psalm 17.

Psalms 143:7
Send out Thy hand from on high; take me out and rescue me from many waters, from the hand of foreigners.
  Psalms 17:17 — Rescue from many waters from on high: The plea to send a hand from on high and take the speaker out of many waters closely corresponds to God sending from on high, taking the speaker and removing him from many waters.

Psalms 143:8
Whose mouth spoke futility, and whose right hand is a right hand of injustice.
  Psalms 143:11 — Same-chapter repetition of mouth and right-hand wrongdoing: This description of foreigners whose mouth spoke futility and whose right hand is a right hand of injustice returns in the later rescue petition. The exact repeated characterization links the petition to the previously described adversaries.

Psalms 143:9
God, I will sing a new song unto Thee; on a ten-stringed psaltery I will make music unto Thee.
  Psalms 32:2-3 — A new song with a ten-stringed psaltery: The promise of a new song and music on a ten-stringed psaltery corresponds to the paired commands for that instrument and a new song in Psalm 32.

Psalms 143:10
To Him who gives salvation to kings, who redeems David His servant from an evil sword.
  Psalms 17:51 — Salvation of kings and David the servant: God’s saving kings and redeeming David His servant corresponds to magnifying His king’s salvations and showing mercy to His anointed, David. Psalm 143’s evil sword is not added to the target.

Psalms 143:11
Rescue me and take me out from the hand of foreigners, whose mouth spoke futility and whose right hand is a right hand of injustice.
  Psalms 143:7-8 — Same-chapter return to rescue from the foreigner’s hand: The later petition repeats rescue from the foreigners’ hand and reproduces their mouth-and-right-hand description from the earlier two verses. This is an internal repeated plea rather than a second historical event.

Psalms 143:12
Whose sons are like young plants established in their youth, their daughters beautified, adorned all around like the likeness of a sanctuary.
  Psalms 127:3 — Sons compared with young plants: The source compares their sons to young plants established in youth; Psalm 127 compares sons to newly planted olive trees around the table. The source’s whose remains attached to its own context, and neither household nor daughter imagery is equated with the target’s wife and children.

Psalms 143:15
They called happy the people who have these things; happy the people whose God is the Lord.
  Psalms 32:12 — Happiness of the people whose God is the Lord: The closing blessedness of the people whose God is the Lord closely corresponds to the blessed nation whose God is the Lord in Psalm 32. Psalm 143 distinguishes this from what they called happy in the preceding prosperity description.

Psalms 144:1
A praise of David. I will exalt Thee, my God, my King, and bless Thy name for the age and for the age of the age.
  Psalms 112:2 — Blessing the Lord’s name for ever: The speaker promises to bless God’s name for the age and age of the age; Psalm 112 blesses the Lord’s name from now and for ever. The royal address of Psalm 144 remains an additional feature.

Psalms 144:2
Every day I will bless Thee and praise Thy name for the age and for the age of the age.
  Psalms 33:2 — Continual blessing and praise: Blessing God every day and praising His name corresponds to blessing the Lord at every time and praise continually in the mouth. Both explicitly describe the ongoing practice, with different time expressions.

Psalms 144:3
Great is the Lord and greatly to be praised; His greatness has no limit.
  Psalms 95:4 — The Lord great and greatly to be praised: The same declaration that the Lord is great and greatly to be praised occurs in both passages. Psalm 144 adds limitless greatness, while Psalm 95 adds fearfulness above all gods.

Psalms 144:4
Generation after generation will praise Thy works, and they will declare Thy power.
  Psalms 77:4-6 — God’s works and power told to successive generations: Generation praising God’s works and declaring His power to generation corresponds to announcing mighty acts and wonders so another generation and its children will know. The target gives the intergenerational teaching setting explicitly.

Psalms 144:5
They will speak of the magnificence of the glory of Thy holiness, and they will recount Thy wonders.
  Psalms 95:3 — Declaring divine glory and wonders: Speech about the magnificence of God’s holy glory and recounting His wonders corresponds to the paired command to announce His glory and wonders among nations and peoples.

Psalms 144:6
They will speak of the power of Thy fearsome acts, and they will recount Thy greatness.
  Psalms 65:3 — Speaking of fearsome works and divine power: Psalm 144 anticipates speech about the power of God’s fearsome acts and His greatness. Psalm 65 commands speech to God calling His works fearsome, then says His enemies will lie to Him through the abundance of His power. The fearsome-works and power language is shared, while the target’s consequence for enemies is distinct.

Psalms 144:7
They will pour forth remembrance of the abundance of Thy kindness and rejoice in Thy righteousness.
  Psalms 30:20 — The abundance of divine kindness: Pouring forth remembrance of abundant kindness corresponds to the exclamation over the great multitude of the Lord’s kindness. Psalm 30 specifies those fearing and hoping in Him; Psalm 144 adds rejoicing in righteousness.

Psalms 144:8
Compassionate and merciful is the Lord, patient and abundant in mercy.
  Exodus 34:6 — Compassionate and merciful, abundant in mercy: The divine attributes compassionate, merciful and abundant in mercy closely parallel the proclamation in Exodus. The psalm says patient, while Exodus says slow to anger and adds true.

Psalms 144:9
The Lord is kind to all, and His compassions are over all His works.
  Wisdom of Solomon 11:23-26 — Mercy extending to all God’s works: The Lord’s kindness to all and compassion over all His works correspond to mercy on all and love for all existing things He made. The comparison follows the expressly universal scope of both passages.

Psalms 144:10
Let all Thy works give thanks unto Thee, Lord, and let Thy holy ones bless Thee.
  Psalms 102:22 — All God’s works summoned to bless or thank Him: The summons for all God’s works to thank Him corresponds to all His works being told to bless Him throughout His dominion. Psalm 144 separately calls His holy ones to bless.

Psalms 144:12
to make known to the sons of men Thy might and the glory of the magnificence of Thy kingdom.
  Psalms 70:18 — Making divine might known to people: Making God’s might known to the sons of men corresponds to the speaker’s desire to announce His arm and power to the coming generation. Psalm 144 further specifies the glorious kingdom.

Psalms 144:13
Thy kingdom is a kingdom of all ages, and Thy lordship endures through every generation.
  Daniel 7:14 — Everlasting dominion and an enduring kingdom: God’s kingdom of all ages and lordship through every generation correspond to the vision’s everlasting authority and kingdom never destroyed. The comparison concerns their explicit duration while preserving Daniel’s given-authority setting.

Psalms 144:13a
Faithful is the Lord in His words, and holy in all His works.
  Psalms 32:4 — Faithfulness in divine words and works: The Lord’s faithfulness in His words and holiness in His works correspond to the Lord’s upright word and all His works in faithfulness. The attributes are related but assigned differently in the two sentences.

Psalms 144:14
The Lord supports all who are falling and raises up all who have been broken down.
  Psalms 145:8 — The Lord raises the broken down: The source’s raising all who have been broken down closely repeats the Lord’s raising those broken down in Psalm 145. Psalm 144 additionally names support for the falling.

Psalms 144:15
The eyes of all hope in Thee, and Thou givest their food at the right time.
  Psalms 103:27 — Creatures wait for food at the right time: The eyes of all hoping in God and receiving food at the right time correspond to all waiting for Him to give food at the right time. The target’s creature context is read without limiting the source’s all to a different unstated group.

Psalms 144:16
Thou openest Thy hand and fillest every living creature with good pleasure.
  Psalms 103:28 — The open divine hand fills living things: The open hand filling every living creature with good pleasure corresponds to God opening His hand and all things being filled with goodness. Good pleasure and goodness are preserved as the respective wording.

Psalms 144:17
The Lord is righteous in all His ways and holy in all His works.
  Deuteronomy 32:4 — Righteousness and holiness in God’s ways and works: The Lord’s righteousness in all His ways and holiness in all His works correspond to true works, ways that are judgments, and the declaration that the Lord is righteous and holy.

Psalms 144:18
The Lord is near to all who call on Him, to all who call on Him in truth.
  Deuteronomy 4:7 — The Lord near when people call: The Lord’s nearness to those calling in truth corresponds to the rhetorical question about the Lord drawing near in all things in which His people call upon Him. The psalm’s truth qualification remains explicit.

Psalms 144:19
He will fulfill the will of those who fear Him; He will hear their plea and save them.
  Psalms 33:18 — Hearing a plea and rescuing the faithful: God hearing the plea and saving those who fear Him corresponds to the righteous crying, the Lord hearing and rescuing them. The source also promises fulfillment of their will, which is not supplied to the target.

Psalms 144:20
The Lord guards all who love Him, and all sinners He will utterly destroy.
  Psalms 96:10 — The Lord guards those who love Him: Guarding all who love the Lord corresponds to the address to those loving Him followed by His guarding the souls of His holy ones. The source destroys sinners; the target rescues the guarded people from sinners’ hands.

Psalms 144:21
My mouth will speak the Lord’s praise, and let all flesh bless His holy name for the age and for the age of the age.
  Psalms 144:1 — Same-chapter closing return to blessing the name for ever: The final summons to bless God’s holy name for the age and age of the age returns to the opening promise to bless His name with the same duration. The close extends the speaker’s praise to all flesh.

Psalms 145:1
Hallelujah. Of Haggai and Zechariah. Praise the Lord, my soul.
  Psalms 102:1 — The soul summoned to praise or bless the Lord: Praise the Lord, my soul closely parallels Bless the Lord, my soul. The Haggai-and-Zechariah heading is preserved without using this verbal comparison to infer authorship.

Psalms 145:2
I will praise the Lord throughout my life; I will make music to my God as long as I exist.
  Psalms 103:33 — Lifelong praise and music as long as the speaker exists: Both speakers promise praise or singing throughout life and music to their God as long as they exist. The source’s praise and target’s sing are not treated as identical verbs.

Psalms 145:3
Do not trust in rulers and in sons of men who have no salvation.
  Psalms 117:8-9 — Do not place trust in humans or rulers: The warning against trusting rulers and sons of men corresponds to the preference for trust and hope in the Lord over a man or rulers. Psalm 145 expressly grounds the warning in their lack of salvation.

Psalms 145:4
His spirit will go out, and he will return to his earth; on that day all their reasonings will perish.
  Ecclesiastes 12:7 — Departure of spirit and return to earth: The spirit going out and the person returning to his earth correspond to dust returning to earth and spirit returning to God. Ecclesiastes gives the spirit’s destination; the psalm adds the perishing of reasonings.

Psalms 145:5
Happy the one whose Helper is the God of Jacob, whose hope is in the Lord his God,
  Psalms 45:8 — Jacob’s God as helper or supporter: The happiness of having Jacob’s God as helper corresponds to Jacob’s God being the community’s supporter. The individual hope and communal confession differ.

Psalms 145:6
who made heaven and earth, the sea and all that is in them, who keeps truth for ever,
  Exodus 20:11 — Maker of heaven, earth, sea and their contents: The named creation of heaven, earth, sea and all in them closely corresponds to the same creation list in the Sabbath command. The psalm’s truth kept for ever and Exodus’ six days are distinct additional clauses.

Psalms 145:7
who does justice for those wronged, who gives food to the hungry. The Lord releases those in fetters.
  Psalms 102:6 — The Lord does judgment for the wronged: The Lord doing justice for those wronged corresponds closely to His deeds of mercy and judgment for all who are wronged. The source also feeds the hungry and releases prisoners.
  Isaiah 61:1 — Release for those held captive: The Lord’s release of those in fetters corresponds to the commissioned speaker proclaiming release to captives. The Lord as releasing agent and His anointed messenger as proclaimer are distinguished.

Psalms 145:8
The Lord raises up those broken down; the Lord makes the blind wise; the Lord loves the righteous.
  Psalms 144:14 — Raising people who have been broken down: The Lord raising those broken down directly corresponds to Psalm 144’s raising all who have been broken down. The source’s making the blind wise is retained without changing it to the restoration of eyesight.

Psalms 145:9
The Lord guards the newcomers; He will take up the orphan and widow, and He will obliterate the way of sinners.
  Deuteronomy 10:18 — God’s care for sojourner, orphan and widow: The Lord’s guarding newcomers and taking up orphan and widow corresponds to His judgment for sojourner, orphan and widow and love for the sojourner. Different descriptions of care remain distinct.

Psalms 145:10
The Lord will reign for ever, your God, Zion, through every generation.
  Exodus 15:18 — The Lord reigns for ever: The promise that the Lord will reign for ever corresponds to the song’s declaration that He reigns for ever and ever and beyond. Psalm 145 adds the address to Zion and every generation.

Psalms 146:1
Hallelujah. Of Haggai and Zechariah. Praise the Lord, for a psalm is good; may praise be pleasing to our God.
  Psalms 134:3 — A praise summons with differing goodness clauses: Both summon praise of the Lord and follow it with a goodness clause. Psalm 146 calls a psalm good and asks that praise please God; Psalm 134 calls the Lord good and commands music to His name, adding “for it is beautiful.” The repeated form is compared without equating the subjects of good or resolving the final it.

Psalms 146:2
The Lord builds Jerusalem, and He will gather the dispersed of Israel.
  Isaiah 11:12 — Gathering Israel’s dispersed people: The Lord gathering Israel’s dispersed corresponds to His gathering Israel’s lost ones and Judah’s scattered ones from earth’s extremities. The building of Jerusalem is additional in the psalm.

Psalms 146:3
He heals the brokenhearted and binds up their fractures.
  Isaiah 61:1 — Healing the brokenhearted: The Lord healing the brokenhearted closely corresponds to the commissioned speaker sent to heal the brokenhearted. Binding fractures is additional in the psalm, and the agents are not confused.

Psalms 146:4
He numbers multitudes of stars and calls them all by names.
  Isaiah 40:26 — Numbering and naming the heavenly multitude: Numbering multitudes of stars and naming them corresponds to the heavenly array brought out by number and called by name. The psalm explicitly names stars; Isaiah’s upward-looking context supplies its heavenly setting.

Psalms 146:5
Great is our Lord, and great is His strength; His understanding is beyond number.
  Isaiah 40:28 — Divine understanding beyond measure or discovery: The Lord’s understanding beyond number corresponds to the Creator’s understanding that cannot be discovered. The comparison concerns the explicit limit of human measurement or discovery, not a claim that the terms are synonymous.

Psalms 146:6
The Lord takes up the meek, but brings sinners low to the ground.
  1 Samuel 2:7-8 — The Lord raises the lowly and brings low: The Lord taking up the meek and bringing sinners to the ground corresponds to His humbling and raising high, followed by lifting the needy from earth. The psalm identifies sinners as those brought low; Samuel’s selected statement does not.

Psalms 146:7
Begin singing to the Lord with thanksgiving; make music to our God with a lyre,
  Psalms 32:2 — Thanksgiving with the lyre: The command to begin with thanksgiving and make music to God with a lyre closely corresponds to giving thanks to the Lord with a lyre. Psalm 32 additionally names a ten-stringed psaltery.

Psalms 146:8
who clothes heaven with clouds, who prepares rain for the earth, who makes grass sprout on the mountains and green growth for the service of humans,
