  Psalms 103:13-14 — Rain, mountains, grass and growth for human service: Preparing rain and making grass sprout on mountains and green growth for humans corresponds to watering mountains and making grass and green plants spring up for livestock and human service.

Psalms 146:9
who gives the cattle their food and to the young ravens who call upon Him.
  Job 38:41 — Food for young ravens calling to the Lord: God feeding young ravens calling upon Him corresponds to the question who prepared a raven’s food, with its nestlings crying to the Lord and seeking food. Cattle are an additional group in the psalm.

Psalms 146:10
He will not delight in the horse’s might, nor does He take pleasure in a man’s shins.
  Psalms 32:16-17 — Horse and human strength contrasted with dependence on God: The Lord’s lack of delight in a horse’s might or human shins corresponds to the denial that great human force or a powerful horse saves. Divine pleasure and capacity to save are related contrasts, not the same assertion.

Psalms 146:11
The Lord takes pleasure in those who fear Him and in those who hope in His mercy.
  Psalms 32:18 — Those fearing God and hoping in His mercy: The Lord’s pleasure in those fearing Him and hoping in His mercy corresponds to His eyes being upon those described with the same paired expressions.

Psalms 147:1
Hallelujah. Of Haggai and Zechariah. Commend the Lord, Jerusalem; praise your God, Zion.
  Psalms 134:21 — Praise addressed from Zion and Jerusalem: Jerusalem and Zion are summoned to praise their God; Psalm 134 blesses the Lord from Zion and identifies His dwelling in Jerusalem. This compares the named city and its worship without dating the headings.

Psalms 147:3
He makes your borders peaceful and fills you with the finest wheat.
  Psalms 80:17 — God feeds with the finest wheat: The Lord filling the city with the finest wheat closely corresponds to His feeding them with the finest wheat. The source’s peaceful borders and target’s honey are distinct additional provisions.

Psalms 147:5
of Him who gives snow like wool, who sprinkles mist like ashes,
  Isaiah 1:18 — Snow and wool in paired imagery: The psalm compares snow with wool, while Isaiah compares the whitening of sins with snow and wool. The shared pair supports a limited image comparison; mist like ashes and moral cleansing remain different subjects.

Psalms 147:6
who casts His ice like morsels; who will withstand His cold?
  Job 38:29-30 — Ice and frost within divine rule over water: God casting ice like morsels and the question who withstands His cold correspond to Job’s questions about the origin of ice and frost and the fixing of the abyss’s face. The target’s actual flowing-water wording and questions are retained; it does not repeat the morsels image.

Psalms 147:7
He will send His word and melt them; He will blow His wind, and waters will flow.
  Job 37:10 — Divine breath or wind controls frost and water: Psalm 147 says God will send His word and melt them, blow His wind, and waters will flow. Elihu says frost will be given from the Strong One’s breath and He steers water as He wishes. Divine wind or breath and control of water connect the passages, while frost-giving and melting remain different actions.

Psalms 147:8
He declares His word to Jacob, His ordinances and judgments to Israel.
  Psalms 77:5 — Word and law declared to Jacob and Israel: God declaring His word, ordinances and judgments to Jacob and Israel corresponds to raising a testimony in Jacob and setting a law in Israel. The named people and divine instruction coincide, with distinct terms for that instruction.

Psalms 147:9
He did not do so for any nation, and He did not disclose His judgments to them.
  Deuteronomy 4:7-8 — Israel’s distinctive divine judgments among nations: The claim that no other nation received this disclosure of judgments corresponds to the rhetorical question whether another great nation has such righteous ordinances and judgments. The comparison preserves the distinction between assertion and question.

Psalms 148:1
Hallelujah. Of Haggai and Zechariah. Praise the Lord from the heavens; praise Him in the highest places.
  Psalms 68:35 — The heavens summoned to praise: The summons to praise from the heavens and highest places corresponds to the summons for heavens and earth to praise. The target extends the range to sea and its creeping creatures.

Psalms 148:2
Praise Him, all His angels; praise Him, all His powers.
  Psalms 102:20-21 — All angels and powers summoned to worship: The paired summons to all God’s angels and powers to praise Him corresponds to the paired commands that all His angels and powers bless Him. Psalm 102 adds their doing His word and will.

Psalms 148:3
Praise Him, sun and moon; praise Him, all the stars and the light.
  Genesis 1:14-18 — The heavenly lights called to praise their maker: Sun, moon, stars and light are summoned within the psalm’s creation praise; Genesis describes the two great lights and stars set in heaven. The psalm’s named sun and moon are not substituted into Genesis’ wording.

Psalms 148:4
Praise Him, heavens of heavens, and the water above the heavens.
  Genesis 1:6-8 — Waters above heaven: The praise summons naming water above the heavens corresponds to the creation account’s water above the firmament, which God names Heaven. The heavens of heavens remain the psalm’s additional expression.

Psalms 148:5
Let them praise the Lord’s name, for He spoke, and they came to be; He commanded, and they were created.
  Psalms 32:9 — Creation by divine speech and command: The reason for praise repeats the sequence that He spoke and they came to be, He commanded and they were created. This is a close verbal correspondence.

Psalms 148:6
He established them for the age and for the age of the age; He set a command, and it will not pass away.
  Psalms 118:89-91 — Creation endures under God’s established word: The enduring establishment and command that will not pass away correspond to God’s word remaining in heaven, the founded earth remaining, and the day remaining by His arrangement. The exact subjects and time expressions remain distinct.

Psalms 148:7
Praise the Lord from the earth, dragons and all deeps,
  Psalms 68:35 — Earth and its watery realm summoned to praise: The summons from earth to dragons and deeps corresponds to heavens, earth, sea and everything creeping in them being summoned to praise. Dragons are not identified with a particular creature in the target.

Psalms 148:8
fire, hail, snow, ice, storm wind—all fulfilling His word,
  Psalms 103:4 — Fire and wind in divine service: Fire and storm wind fulfilling God’s word can be compared with His angels as winds and ministers as burning fire. The relation is the explicit fire-and-wind service imagery, without deciding whether the two passages identify the same beings.

Psalms 148:9
mountains and all hills, fruit-bearing trees and all cedars,
  Psalms 95:12-13 — Trees rejoicing or praising before the Lord: The summons to fruit trees and cedars to praise corresponds to all woodland trees exulting before the coming Lord. The psalm’s mountains and hills are not inserted into the target.

Psalms 148:10
wild animals and all cattle, creeping things and winged birds,
  Genesis 1:24-25 — Land creatures in creation and praise: The praise list of wild animals, cattle and creeping things corresponds to those kinds named in the creation of land animals. Winged birds are additional in the source, not in the selected target.

Psalms 148:11
kings of the earth and all peoples, rulers and all judges of the earth,
  Psalms 101:22-23 — Peoples and kingdoms assembled for the Lord’s praise: Earth’s kings, peoples, rulers and judges are summoned to praise; Psalm 101 joins the declaration of His praise to peoples and kingdoms gathering to serve Him. The target does not separately name judges.

Psalms 148:13
Let them praise the Lord’s name, for His name alone was exalted; His praise is over earth and heaven.
  Psalms 82:19 — The Lord’s name and unique exaltation: The Lord’s name alone being exalted corresponds to the declaration that His name is Lord and He alone is Most High over all the earth. The source extends His praise over earth and heaven.

Psalms 148:14
He will raise a horn of His people— a hymn for all His holy ones, for the children of Israel, a people drawing near to Him.
  Psalms 74:11 — A horn raised for God’s people or the righteous: Raising a horn of His people corresponds to the righteous person’s horns being raised alongside the promise to break sinners’ horns. The image is shared without identifying a particular ruler or event.

Psalms 149:1
Hallelujah. Sing a new song to the Lord, His praise in the assembly of holy ones.
  Psalms 95:1 — The summons to sing a new song: Both passages command a new song to the Lord. Psalm 149 places praise in the assembly of holy ones; Psalm 95 summons all earth and includes a different heading.
  Psalms 88:6 — Praise in the assembly of holy ones: Praise in the assembly of holy ones corresponds to acknowledgment of the Lord’s truth in an assembly of holy ones. The heavens are explicitly named in Psalm 88, without being inserted as the source assembly’s identity.

Psalms 149:2
Let Israel rejoice in the One who made it, and let Zion’s children exult in their King.
  Isaiah 44:1-6 — Israel’s maker and king: The summons for Israel to rejoice in its maker and Zion’s children in their king corresponds to the Lord who made and formed chosen Israel and identifies Himself as Israel’s King. The target’s Jacob address and womb imagery remain its own.

Psalms 149:3
Let them praise His name in a chorus; with tambourine and psaltery let them make music to Him.
  Exodus 15:20-21 — Timbrel and collective song to the Lord: Praising in a chorus with tambourine and psaltery can be compared with Miriam and the women using timbrels and dances as she leads the song to the Lord. Instruments, chorus and dancing are not treated as identical terms or one performance.

Psalms 149:4
For the Lord takes pleasure in His people, and He will exalt the meek in salvation.
  Psalms 146:6 — The meek lifted by the Lord: The Lord exalting the meek in salvation corresponds to His taking up the meek in Psalm 146. The source also states His pleasure in His people, and the target contrasts sinners brought low.

Psalms 149:5
Holy ones will boast in glory and rejoice upon their beds.
  Psalms 62:7-8 — Rejoicing and remembrance upon the bed: The holy ones rejoicing upon their beds can be compared with remembrance of God upon the speaker’s bed and exultation in the shelter of His wings. The target does not explicitly place all of its exultation on the bed.

Psalms 149:6
Exaltations of God in their throat, and two-edged swords in their hands,
  Hebrews 4:12 — Two-edged swords in literal and comparative use: The holy ones hold two-edged swords while exalting God; Hebrews describes God’s word as sharper than any two-edged sword. The distinctive weapon expression is shared without making the psalm’s held swords a metaphor for the word.

Psalms 149:7
to execute vengeance among the nations, reproofs among the peoples,
  Deuteronomy 32:41-43 — Vengeance and judgment upon enemies among nations: The purpose of executing vengeance and reproof among nations can be compared with the Lord’s declared repayment to enemies and vengeance for His sons’ blood. The psalm’s holy ones and Deuteronomy’s divine agent are distinguished; no specific written judgment is identified.

Psalms 149:8
to bind their kings in fetters and their glorious ones in iron handcuffs,
  2 Kings 25:6-7 — Kings subjected to judgment and fetters: The poetic binding of kings in fetters can be compared with the narrated judgment and fettering of King Sedekias. The historical captors are Babylonian, not identified as the psalm’s holy ones, and this is not asserted to fulfill the psalm.

Psalms 150:1
Hallelujah. Praise God among His holy ones; praise Him in the firmament of His power.
  Psalms 88:6 — Praise in heavenly and holy assembly language: Praise among God’s holy ones and in the firmament of His power corresponds to the heavens acknowledging His wonders and His truth in an assembly of holy ones. This does not resolve the identity of every holy one.

Psalms 150:2
Praise Him for His mighty acts; praise Him according to the abundance of His greatness.
  Psalms 144:3-6 — Praise according to greatness and mighty acts: Praise for mighty acts and abundant greatness corresponds to the Lord’s limitless greatness and the generations declaring His power and fearsome acts. The connection follows the explicit grounds for praise.

Psalms 150:3
Praise Him with the sound of a trumpet; praise Him with psaltery and lyre.
  1 Chronicles 16:4-6 — Trumpets and stringed instruments in praise: The summons to praise with trumpet, psaltery and lyre can be compared with appointed praise before the ark using harps, lyres and trumpets. The psaltery is not equated with every instrument name in Chronicles, and no performance date for the psalm is inferred.

Psalms 150:4
Praise Him with tambourine and chorus; praise Him with strings and instrument.
  Psalms 149:3 — Tambourine and chorus in praise: The summons to praise with tambourine and chorus closely corresponds to praising God’s name in a chorus and making music with tambourine and psaltery. Each passage retains its different additional instruments.

Psalms 150:5
Praise Him with well-sounding cymbals; praise Him with cymbals of a shout.
  1 Chronicles 15:16-19 — Sounding cymbals in joyful praise: The command to praise with sounding cymbals corresponds to David’s appointment of singers and instruments to sound with gladness, including bronze cymbals. The source’s cymbals of a shout are not treated as a technical name found in Chronicles.

Psalms 150:6
Let every breath praise the Lord. Hallelujah.
  Psalms 144:21 — The closing summons extends praise to all living voices: Let every breath praise the Lord corresponds to the closing summons for all flesh to bless His holy name. The collective scope and closing praise function are shared, while breath and flesh remain distinct expressions.

Psalms 151:1
This psalm is personally written concerning David and is outside the number, when he fought Goliath in single combat. I was small among my brothers and younger in my father’s house; I was shepherding my father’s sheep.
  1 Samuel 16:10-12 — David the youngest brother tending the flock: The psalm’s small and younger David shepherding his father’s sheep corresponds to Jesse’s remaining youngest son tending the flock before being summoned. The narrative does not repeat every adjective in the psalm.
  1 Samuel 17:4-10 — Goliath and the single-combat setting: The full heading places David’s fight with Goliath in single combat; the account in 1 Samuel identifies Goliath and records his challenge for two men to fight in single combat. The psalm’s outside-the-number statement remains its own heading metadata.

Psalms 151:2
My hands made an instrument; my fingers fitted a psaltery.
  1 Samuel 16:18-23 — David’s hands and musical instruments: David’s making an instrument and fitting a psaltery with his hands and fingers can be compared with his skill in playing and his hand playing the kinyra before Saul. Making and playing are different acts, and the instrument names are not equated.

Psalms 151:4
He Himself sent out His angel and took me from my father’s sheep and anointed me with the oil of His anointing.
  1 Samuel 16:11-13 — David taken from the sheep and anointed: The account of being taken from the father’s sheep and anointed with oil corresponds to David summoned from the flock and anointed by Samuel. Psalm 151 names His angel while the Samuel narrative names Samuel; their identity is not resolved by the supported shepherd-to-anointing sequence.

Psalms 151:5
My brothers were handsome and tall, but the Lord took no pleasure in them.
  1 Samuel 16:6-10 — Appearance does not secure the brothers’ election: The handsome and tall brothers in whom the Lord took no pleasure correspond to the warning not to regard Eliab’s appearance or height and the rejection of Jesse’s sons passing before Samuel. The narrative’s appearance warning is directed particularly at Eliab, not asserted of every brother.

Psalms 151:6
I went out to meet the foreigner, and he cursed me by his idols.
  1 Samuel 17:42-45 — The foreigner curses David by his gods: The source’s going out to the foreigner and being cursed by his idols corresponds to Goliath confronting David and cursing him by his gods. The different idols and gods wording is preserved.

Psalms 151:7
But I drew the sword he carried, cut off his head, and removed reproach from the sons of Israel.
  1 Samuel 17:51 — David beheads the foreigner with the foreigner’s sword: Both passages describe David taking the opponent’s sword and removing his head. The psalm adds the removal of Israel’s reproach; the narrative records the foreigners fleeing when their champion died.
